BACKGROUNDS OF AMERICAN LIFE 

W. P. 4 

MADE IN U.S.A.

PREFACE 

H. G. Wells has said, "I fear for western civilization and particularly for the United States. It will be a race between catastrophe and education." There are people who believe that "loose thinking in the adult citizen is at once a character of American democracy and a menace to its development." 

The deduction from these statements is that we of today live in a most complicated political, social, scientific, and economic age. The responsibilities of the present and the oncoming generations are and will be increasingly difficult. A more intelligent and more ethical citizenship, based on a broad knowledge of our social heritage, is necessary if civilization is to progress, and, perhaps, if our democratic institutions are to survive. 

It is with these thoughts in mind that the authors have written Backgrounds of American Life. The history of the ages is presented in an organized way so that its student-readers may understand and appreciate present conditions and needs. Eight "backgrounds" or units, introduced by stimulating previews and subdivided into twenty-five chapters, tell the story of the history of the world in a straightforward, appraising manner. 

In this review of world affairs the authors have attempted to consider events in their true light and in their relative importance for young American students. Through it all the literature, science, art, civics, economic and social problems, geography, and episodes of character are woven and integrated. 

To promote and insure its complete grasp, the text is couched in a style and language that are within the easy understanding of the pupils. Yet, at the same time, to augment the vocabulary, new words and phrases are introduced. In addition enough detail is given to secure a treatment that is replete with life, color, and vividness.

vi 

PREFACE 

Reference material at the end of each chapter is carefully selected with the thought of provoking a wider interest in historical literature. Particular attention is given to individual pupil-differences. The activities and problems include fact and thought questions, projects, map work, charts, suggestions for graphs, dramatization, and floor talks. 

Backgrounds of American Life is an integrated unit text. It will serve as a preparatory course for advanced world history in the senior high school and as a survey course for the many students who may never have the opportunity for further study in world history.

BACKGROUND ONE PREHISTORIC TIMES 

Let us imagine that we are going to take a long journey in a great dirigible airship. We shall start off away back in the "land of time." We shall see first the earth long before the coming of man. This was ages and ages ago; no one knows when. Among other interesting things during this prehistoric period we shall see the great ice fields creeping slowly back and forth over the earth's surface. These times when the ice fields moved were known as the glacial periods in the story of the earth.

We shall see the first straggling, waving plants appear in the waters. We shall witness the first signs of animal life, then we shall watch those huge, clumsy, and ugly but interesting animals who lived on the land and in the sea. We shall see, also, great swamp forests, dark and dismal in those days, but supplying man and the world today with a coal supply. In these forests we shall find enormous reptiles. 

Later, yes much later, we shall meet the men of the Old Stone Age, wandering from place to place, living in caves, and searching for nuts, berries, and small animals.

And then, still later, we shall observe the men of the New Stone Age inventing their first durable tools and weapons and building their first homes. We shall see those homes on the summit of hills and on small islands close to the shore. We shall watch those men of long ago caring for their flocks and fields, while the women spin and weave and make their first crude pottery. 

These and many other things we shall observe until we arrive at the time when man first learned to write. That place in history is known as the beginning of historic times, and there our view will change.

CHAPTER I THE STORY OF THE EARTH 

Modern Times. - We of today are living in the age of the steam engine, the gas engine, and the electric dynamo. We have the telegraph, the telephone, and the radio. We are living in the age of the talking motion picture. We have beautiful operas, paintings, and sculpture. We have books, magazines, and newspapers. 

We have cathedrals, churches, schools, hospitals, scientific laboratories, banks, stores, and government buildings. All of these things add to our pleasures, comforts, and conveniences. They make possible our many different industries and numerous factories. 

The Present and the Past. Of course we know that these things exist. They are such common everyday affairs that we Early man lived in caves like these. They protected him from cold weather and served as hiding places from wild animals and enemies. 2

THE STORY OF THE EARTH 

3 give them scarcely a moment's thought. But far back in the dim distant past man did not live, think, work, and spend his leisure time as he does today. His home was a tree, a pit, or a cave. 

His food was such as he could find-nuts, berries, raw meat, and fish. His clothing was made from suitable plants or from the skins of animals. He had few comforts and few pleasures. But we are indebted to those men and women of the past for everything that we have today. Slowly through the ages mankind has struggled and sacrificed until at last our present life became what it is. 

History Defined. History is the story of those struggles and sacrifices. These qualities form the background for most of the world's history. And it is only as the story unfolds that we are able to understand and to appreciate the civilization we now enjoy. History is not merely an account of wars, kings, queens, and conquerors. It tells anything about man that is interesting and helpful in explaining his present life. It includes the story of his language, his music, his arithmetic, his religion, his government, his beliefs and customs, and his methods of making a living. In this brief story of world history we shall start at the very beginning.

Geological Epochs. - Geologists (persons who study the rocks) have divided the earth before the coming of man into several great epochs or periods of time. The first of these epochs lasted for millions of years. It was a time when there was probably no life at all upon the earth. During the second epoch the first signs of life began to appear. The first plants were clusters of straggling, waving weeds that appeared in the waters. The first animals were hideous, creeping, crawling creatures. There were scorpions, some of which measured eight and nine feet long. 

Other animals, called trilobites and shaped like giant wood lice, crawled slowly over the land or swam clumsily through the water. 

The world was a dismal place at this time. These first two epochs were followed by the age of fish and great swamp forests.

PREHISTORIC TIMES 

American Museum of Natural History 

A restoration of one of the largest prehistoric reptiles that ever lived. The 

Allosaurus was forty-seven feet long. 

Millions of years later a new age dawned, and there appeared upon the earth enormous reptiles. This is known as the age of reptiles. Another long period followed, and finally we hear of the age of mammals (animals that suckle their young). The remains of a few of these prehistoric animals, and they were enormous monsters, can be seen in some of our museums. Their age was also the age when grass and great land forests came into existence. The forests of that long-ago yesterday have become the source of the world's coal supply. 

Glacial Periods. The geologists tell us, also, the interesting story of the four glacial periods of the earth's history. For several reasons, they explain, portions of the earth have become very cold from time to time, and great ice fields have crept slowly down from the northern regions and have covered the southland. 

The last glacial period probably occurred 50,000 years ago. Ice covered portions of southern Europe, Asia, and North America. 

It was during the glacial periods, particularly the last one, that we begin to get our first evidence of man living in Europe. Many interesting stories have been written about the men and women of that so-called "Ice Age."

Sources of Knowledge. 

far-away times by means known as sources. These sources are in the form of skeleton remains, Scholars or the imprint of plant or animal life on rocks. depend upon pictures, finds, and writings for their sources of information. If these sources are the genuine paintings or tools or writings of the people in question, then they are called original or primary sources. If we have only a copy of these things, then we have what the historian calls a secondary source. On these sources depend whether or not we can furnish proof in history. Which would you say offered the most valuable 

American Museum of Natural History 

Part of a fossil backbone. form of proof for study-primary or secondary sources? 

The Prehistoric Period. To learn the story of events which took place before there were any written records, scholars depend upon the impression of plant and animal life that can be seen in the layers of rocks. Then, too, they have found tools, weapons, and pictures and carvings on tools and in caves. All of these finds have been pieced together and studied. Much of this study, such as dates and the everyday life of the people, is guesswork. It has been within the past century only that scientists began to make a careful study of these things. The story that the rocks, caves, and the tools have to tell deals with a time in history called "prehistoric times." 

Man's Heritage. Thus we see that it took and ages ages to make this world a fit place in which man could dwell. We do not know when the first human being appeared upon this earth.

PREHISTORIC TIMES 

These pictures were carved on the wall of a cave in southern France. A buffalo, a horse, and a stag are shown. Early man used these animals for food and for beasts of burden.

But when he did appear, he found a favorable climate and good soil. These conditions enabled him to live and to secure the wild fruits, berries, and nuts which grew so abundantly. He found a world inhabited by many strange and interesting animals, some of which have served him in many ways. He used their skins for clothing, their meat for food, and their bones for tools and weapons. Man was superior to all living things. 

Our next chapter will tell about the earliest people of whom we have any knowledge- the men of the Old Stone Age.

CHAPTER II  

Neanderthal Man. In the year 1857 there was found in the famous Neanderthal (nä-än'der-täl) cave, near Düsseldorf (düs'ěl-dôrf), Germany, a man's skull, upper arm, rib, and thigh bones. These remains were pieced together, and from them we get some idea of that man's appearance. He was a short, thickset, brutish-looking animal. It is supposed that this Neanderthal man, as he has been called, lived about 50,000 years ago during the fourth glacial period. For thousands of years, it appears, the race to which he belonged wandered over Europe unmolested by any other type of man. 

The Cro-Magnon Race. The next story that the caves have to tell deals with the discovery of three skeletons and parts of two others. They were found in the Cro-Magnon (krō-må-nyôn') cave of France in 1868. They were the remains of one woman, a child, an old man, and two boys. These Cro-Magnon people were tall and in appearance resembled modern man. They were more clever than the Neanderthal man. They are supposed to have entered Europe about 25,000 years ago. At that time the great ice sheets were moving northward again. The climate in southern Europe was getting warmer. Plant and animal life was becoming more abundant. 

The Paleolithic or Old Stone Age. Scholars have discovered many tools and weapons that were used by the men of long ago, and it is from these that we learn much about early man and woman. At first man must have used nothing but his bare hands with which to fight or defend himself. Later he probably used sticks and stones. Many of his stone tools are crude, rough, and unpolished. For this reason his period of civilization is called and known as the Paleolithic (pa-lê-ô-lith'ik; rough) or 

Old Stone Age.

Civilization. We must remember that civilization began when man first learned how to use the materials with which nature had provided him; when he learned new and better methods of securing food, clothing, and shelter; and when he began to invent things that made his life and work easier. We shall read in this book how man gradually developed these things and improved civilization. There were many stages of civilization. We think of ours as the highest and that of primitive peoples as the lowest of these stages. 

Problems of Early Man. Like modern folk, early man's greatest problem was that of getting a living. How was he to procure his food, clothing, and shelter? 

Food. Man, at first, wandered about looking for wild fruits, vegetables, and nuts. He searched for small animals in the forests and for dead fish along the water shores. He ate these raw, for as yet he did not know about fire. Later he learned how to split the bones of animals and suck the marrow from the broken pieces.

Clothing. The clothing of early man depended upon the climate of the land in which he lived. If it was extremely warm, he did what many savages do today in warm climates. He went naked or wore a little clothing merely for display and ornament. 

If it was cold, and in some places it was bitterly cold, he wore the skins of animals. 

Shelter. One of man's first shelters from climate, enemies, and wild animals was the cave. Proof of this is found in the Unpolished weapons (knives and hatchets) of the Old Stone Age.

RA 2

IO 

PREHISTORIC TIMES 

pictures, small statues, and sculpture work discovered in such caves. This art, representing animals and people, was certainly the handiwork of the early cave dwellers. The bison, horse, ibex, cave bear, and reindeer are all pictured. In some cases the 

Early man painted this hunting animals were done in beautifully colored paintings. The colors were made by grinding up red, yellow, and brown oxides of iron. These caves were the first art galleries in human history.

Cave Life. 

The earliest cave dwellers, however, did not live within the depths of these caves.

They had not learned the use of fire and had no torches. It must be remembered that their caves were dark, unventilated, and the home of wild animals. Man, at first, lived in the grottoes, the outer shelterings of the caves. 

Early Inventions. Thus we see that early man's food, clothing, shelter, and his method of procuring them were far different from our present-day life. But that ancestor of ours had other handicaps. He could only communicate with his fellows by grunt, growl, grimace, or gesture. His only method of traveling was by walking or swimming. It was necessary, as we have seen, for him to be a hunter. He was in danger of being destroyed by the larger, wilder animals. He could not fight them with his bare hands, so he invented weapons. scene on the wall of a cave in Spain sometime during the Old 

Stone Age.

His first inventions were imitations. His club was shaped like the human arm with the doubled fist. His sling was used to throw rocks and stones in much the same way that he threw them with his hand and arm. His drinking gourd was probably suggested to him by its cup-shaped appearance, resembling his two hands cupped together as he drank from some clear pool. In the water he learned to float on a drifting log, and his first invention

II Civilization began when man first learned to use the things he found about him. 

Here you see one of the first boats. How was it made? in water transportation must have been a log, dug out in the center so that he could sit in it. Crude as this last invention seems to us, it was a great achievement in human progress. 

This "log dugout" was the forerunner of our modern ocean liners. man. 

Fire. Fire was to play a very important part in the life of 

The first fires were started, it is thought, by volcanoes or by lightning. Man, at first, probably feared fire, but as time went on he found that it could be made very useful. It kept him warm and protected him from the wild animals, who still feared it. 

Later he learned how to make fire by rubbing sticks, striking flints together, or by the boring process. This invention was handed down through the ages. It was improved very little until the middle of the nineteenth century when matches were invented. 

Language. The greatest invention of this period was language, for although man had the power to speak, he had to make his own language. We have already noticed that there

PREHISTORIC TIMES 

was a time when only gesture, grimace, and grunt could express man's ideas. It was then that man imitated the sounds that he heard. Gradually certain sounds came This way of fire-making was begun by early man. 

It has been used thousands of years. Did early man know what made the sticks burn? Do you know? to mean certain things, and so slowly a language was formed. 

Music. Did early man enjoy music?

He had a voice, but at first he must have used it in a vague way to express his emotions. He howled with pleasure and moaned with pain. It took him long ages to acquire tone and quality of voice. One of his first musical instruments was likely a river reed from which he made a reed pipe. His first drum must have been made by stretching parchment (animal skin) over the head of a hollow log. 

Later, after he had invented the bow and arrow, the thrumming of the hand on the bowstring might have produced a low musical sound. This gave him an idea, and the first primitive violin with a bow and gut strings came into existence. These crude sounds and inventions are the beginnings of our fine operas and melodious orchestras. 

Mirrors. The still water in ponds and lakes reflected man's image, and these were his only mirrors for countless ages. This may seem trivial, but let us carry the history of the mirror down to the present. "Mirrors of Brass" are mentioned in the Bible.

Bronze mirrors were used by the Egyptians (ê-jip'shănz), the Greeks, and the Romans. It was not until 1300 A.D. that lookingglasses were first made in Venice (ven'is), and just a little over two hundred years ago that they were first introduced into England. 

Mathematics. - Early man's arithmetic must have given him considerable trouble. He expressed quantity by the number of his fingers and toes. He was lost beyond the number ten, but this method of counting was the beginning of our digit system in

mathematics. It was the start of that science which today is partly responsible for our difficult engineering feats.

Early men traveled in groups for 

Such wandering groups probably which was ruled over by the oldest This was the earliest form of gov- 

Wars between Tribes. protection and for company. belonged to the same family, and the strongest member. ernment. The life of the groups was a hard one. Often they wandered for days and days and found nothing to eat. The weakest members of the tribe perished. A tribe might come upon another tribe occupying territory rich in food supply. 

War to the death resulted. No prisoners were taken and no slaves were made, for these brought the added burden of getting food for more people. Later, tribes joined together and became very powerful. 

Religion. Very early in history man, Man's first weapons were rocks and stones. it appears, had a religion and believed in a life after death. We believe this because, buried with his dead, are often found the tools and weapons that the dead man used on earth. It was thought that these would aid him in that other world that he had visited so often in his dreams.

Nature, too, played an important part in his religion, because early man worshiped things that he did not understand or that he feared. Thunder, lightning, daylight, and dark were deified (made gods of) by nearly all peoples when we first find them in authentic history. It is thought that the cave man's pictures and statues were often connected with his religious rites. If this was the case, then some of these caves were to early man what the cathedrals and churches are to modern man. 

A Cave-Man Scene. We have told enough at this point to enable us to picture a scene which took place long ago. One evening a group of cave men, women, and children sat around their fire. An old man told a story of a famous hunter of the past

PREHISTORIC TIMES 

and of his brave deeds. He told of some tribal war that had taken place in his youth, or of a bitterly cold winter when food had been scarce and many persons had died. The 

The group listened attentively, and, after the old man had finished, they discussed their adventures of the day. They told of the things that they intended to do on the morrow, what crude instruments they hoped to finish, and what strange place they intended to visit. These conversations served those people in much the same way as books, magazines, and newspapers serve us. Their night fires, too, were useful as a signal for some lost tribesman who had failed to reach home before dark. This fire signal is our first knowledge of any form of wireless telegraphy.

The Neolithic or New Stone Age. - Years rolled on, and the time arrived when man learned to grind the edges of his tools During the Neolithic Stone Age man took pride in chipping, grinding, and polishing his weapons. Arrow heads like these were commonly used during that time. 

Notice how carefully the arrow head was bound to the arrow. and to smooth and sharpen his stone implements. He built wooden homes and provided them with a few simple comforts. 

The women learned how to harden clay in the fire, how to care for plants and animals, and how to spin and weave. 

This stage in prehistoric times has been called the Neolithic (nē-ô-lith'ik) or 

New Stone Age. Our next backward glance at prehistoric times will give us a picture of these men of the New Stone Age who lived about 

12,000 years ago.

As we look back on the life of these people, it seems crude and simple, and it was. But you will find that it was much better than the life of the Old Stone Age. We, today, profit from the works of both ages.

CHAPTER III  

Maiden Castle." Maiden Castle," in England, is the remains of a village once occupied by people who lived in the New Stone Age. These people built their huts on the summit of a hill and surrounded them with a high earthen wall. Beyond the wall, and surrounding it, they dug several ditches to make it difficult for enemies to reach their homes. The plan of this ancient British camp was the same as that used later by the medieval knights. They often built their castles on some high cliff and surrounded them with walls and moats. 

Lake Dwellers. - Other men of this age built their villages on islands in the middle of lakes. Sometimes they made their own islands. This was done by piling stones, trees, and brushwood Lake dwellings. Many small villages were built on lakes. How was this village connected with the land? (A restoration.) 16

together to make a foundation in some favorable section of the lake. The remains of such a village was found in Lake Zurich (zoo'rik), Switzerland, in 1854. The foundations of wooden platforms were discovered. These platforms were supported by long rows of piles, made from the split trunks of trees and driven close together in the bed of the lake. The walls of the dwellings, it seems, were fastened together by woven branches. 

The roofs were thatched. Small platforms connected the huts, and a larger platform connected the village with the land. 

How the Lake Dwellers Lived. — This method of living by the 

Lake Dwellers in Switzerland had several advantages. First of all, the garbage and refuse which collected in the homes were thrown in the lake through trapdoors in the hut floors. It was among this refuse that many things were found which showed how these people lived. The remains of dogs, oxen, sheep, and goats have been found on the lake bottoms. These animals, therefore, must have been tame and used by man in his work and to supply him with meat and milk. Nuts, wheat, cereals, and barley have been found also, which indicates a knowledge of farming.

In the second place, over the long platform which connected their homes with the land, the Lake Dwellers could drive their domesticated (tamed) animals out in the morning and let them pasture during the day. Then they could bring them home at night, where they would be safe from prowling enemies. 

The Lake Dwellers' Tools. The tools and weapons unearthed in this rubbish include bone pins, stone axes, horn buttons, wooden bowls, and spear heads. The Lake Dwellers used linen, could spin and weave, and wore leather shoes. Many of their weapons were made of bronze, and from this we know that they lived in what has been called the age of metals. Fish hooks, made from the bones of animals, point to another industry, that of fishing. It is things such as these that enable scientists to get more light on the past. Many finds in other parts of the world are making the story even clearer.

PREHISTORIC TIMES 

Burton Holmes, from Ewing Galloway

This modern pastoral scene shows that, although man adopts new ways of making a living, he does not drop entirely the old methods. 

Stonehenge. In the southern part of England, near Salisbury (sôlz'ber-1), stands a primitive monument of the early 

Britons, known as Stonehenge (stōn'henj; hanging stones). This consists of huge, upright stones which form two large circles, one within the other. The perpendicular columns are partly connected at the top by large flat stones. The structure is one of the mysteries of history. How was it erected by a people who apparently knew little of mechanics? For what purpose was it built? To the latter question many students answer that it is the remains of an old temple, one of the places where the early inhabitants practiced that strange religion called Druidism (droo'ĭd-iz'm). 

Druidism. - The Druids were priests who studied and worshiped the stars, the moon, and the sun. They taught the young and old. They erected their altars in the dark gloomy forests; the mistletoe and the oak were worshiped, and human sacrifices

were offered to appease the gods. The Druids believed in life after death, and the warrior's weapons were buried with him, as well as food, drink, and dishes. Many of our early American 

Indians buried their dead in the same way. 

Pastoral Stage. It was another step in the progress of civilization when man learned to tame and domesticate certain wild animals. Among the first were the dog, horse, and cow. 

Instead of hunting the animals for food, he now took them with him. Thus he needed pasture land, for the tamed animals needed food. Sometimes, like the Lake Dwellers, he was able to lead a settled life. But more often he had to wander from place to place looking for suitable land to pasture his flocks. 

When one place gave out, he traveled on to another, taking his family, household goods, and tent with him. However, in this second stage of economic development, it took less land to support a family than it did in the hunting and fishing stage. 

The Old Testament story of Abram and Isaac furnishes a good example of a pastoral people. 

Agricultural Stage. When man learned how to plant the wild grain and till the soil, he took another step forward. It is thought that woman, whose time was taken up with home duties, was the very first agriculturist (one skilled in farming). 

Crude plows were man's first tools with which he tilled the soil. 

Then agriculture increased when man used his fellowman as power to pull the plow. 

A great step forward was made when man learned to use animals for his heavy work.

PREHISTORIC TIMES 

Remaining at home to care for the children, to watch the fire, and to prepare the meals, she became interested in some flower or plant near her home. She watched it grow. She saw seeds scattered by it. She saw other plants grow from these seeds. In this way she learned to plant and to care for grains and fruits. 

Now man settled down and began to plant much of his food. 

Agriculture in this stage was crude, and the implements were of the simplest kind. A stick was used to dig up the soil. Early man knew nothing of irrigation and the rotation of crops. 

Slavery. It is thought that slaves were first used during the pastoral stage. Prisoners, taken in battle, were placed in charge of the herds. But for this few people were needed, and so slavery did not become a well-organized movement until the agricultural stage. The work of caring for the fields was hard, and since early men disliked work and considered it a disgrace, prisoners of war had to do all the disagreeable labor. 

The Metals. The Lake Dwellers, as we know, were familiar with bronze. Copper was one of the first metals used by primitive man, who found out by accident how to get the copper from its ore. But copper is soft, and tools made from it will not last. In many places copper ore and tinstone are found together. By accident, perhaps, these two metals were united, and the ancient smelters had a new metal, bronze. Bronze is harder and more durable than either copper or tin. The use of bronze and, a little later, of iron brought about a great change in the life of those early people. These changes will be described in later chapters. 

Weapons like these were made during the Bronze Age. Metal weapons made of bronze were better than those made of polished stone. Man could fashion this metal which was a mixture of copper and tin to suit his many purposes.

REYNS 

This Egyptian writing is read from right to left. It is made up of pictures, combination pictures, and syllables. This sentence was on the Rosetta Stone, which was found in 1799. It took years to learn to read these characters. 

But what we know of ancient Egypt dates from the time when scholars learned to translate sentences like this one. 

Writing. Another great invention of early man was writing. 

Authentic history depends upon written documents. These of course must be true. It is believed that man first expressed his ideas in writing by the picture method. He drew pictures to show what he wanted to say. (Such was the writing of the 

Mayas and of the Aztec and Inca Indians when conquered by the Spanish under Cortez and Pizarro.) After a time the pictures came to represent ideas, and still later they were changed so as to form characters, which represented sounds. For example, in our language a picture of a bee and a leaf might come to represent the sound belief. The Egyptians had invented such a sound writing as this. It is called hieroglyphics. 

The story of early Egypt remained hidden until the beginning of the nineteenth century A.D. At that time a young officer in the army of Napoleon discovered a block of black basalt (volcanic rock) at Rosetta (ro-zět'à), near the mouth of the Nile River. This is known today as the Rosetta Stone. It proved to be the key to the Egyptian hieroglyphics, for on it were three kinds of writing. Jean Champollion (shän-pol-yôn′), a French-. man, worked with these writings for twenty years and then discovered how to read what was inscribed on the stone. 

Through his work we learned much of the story of those first dwellers along the Nile. The history of those people and other ancient Oriental (Eastern) nations is told and described in our next chapter.

BACKGROUND TWO  

Our huge airship leaves the scenes of prehistoric times. The great ice fields, the wandering hordes of the Old Stone Age men, the cave dwellers, and the homes of the men of the New Stone 

Age fade in the distance.

A new scene presents itself. We see the "Background of 

Ancient Oriental Civilization" appear below us. 

We pass slowly over the Nile River in Egypt and the Tigris and Euphrates (ú-frā'tēz) rivers in Babylonia (băb-i-lō'nĬ-ȧ) and Assyria (ă-sir'i-a). We head northeast and then south along the Mediterranean Sea. There we catch our first glimpse of the Phoenicians (fé-nish'ǎnz) and Hebrews. 

In Egypt we shall see the overflow of the Nile, the irrigation of the fields, and the first attempts at surveying. We shall see the slave bending to his toil, the taskmaster with his lash, the trader, merchant, and craftsman at his work, the priest in his temples, the child in his school, the noble at his hunt, and the king in his splendor as an absolute monarch. We shall see the 

Egyptian making the first alphabet and preparing a written language, listening to the tale of his Cinderella, and inventing a calendar, an inclined plane, and a lever. All of these he will pass on to civilization.

In the Tigris-Euphrates Valley we shall see the city of Babylon in the days of Hammurabi (hảm-oo-rä’bė). We shall see the 

Armenian boatman and the Egyptian camel driver wending their way with others to this great commercial center of the long ago. 

We shall see the Tower of Babel and the Hanging Gardens of Babylon; the latter, one of the seven wonders of the ancient world. We shall see builders who will give us an idea for our modern steeples; priests or Magi (mā'jī) preparing for us our knowledge of astronomy, the twelve signs of the zodiac, the idea RA-3 25

of sixty seconds in a minute, sixty minutes in an hour, and 360° in a circle.

In Assyria we shall see the beautiful library of the Emperor Ashurbanipal (ä'shoor-bä-ne-päl). We shall read from one of the clay tablets the great epic of their hero, Gilgamesh (gil'ga-měsh).

In the Phoenician ports and cities we shall see ships and caravans from all over the world. We shall see the busy merchants and clerks preparing bills of lading and using the calendar which has been passed on to modern times. 

We shall see the Hebrew nation with the Bible, the Ten Commandments, and the idea of one God. All of these have influenced the people of today. 

And finally we shall witness the conquering armies of Cyrus the Great and the crumbling of ancient Oriental civilization. 

But, as our dirigible turns towards Greece, we are impressed with the fact that these peoples contributed much to our present life. 

Herod's temple.

CHAPTER IV EGYPT 

The Nile. Let us examine this wonderful river. The Nile is one of the longest rivers in the world. It rises in the central part of Africa near the equator and flows northward into the Mediterranean Sea. Fed by the rains and melting snows in the far south, it winds its way through thickly wooded regions and deep valleys. It tumbles over many cataracts, and eventually it emerges upon the plains of Egypt. There it flows lazily along at the rate of about three miles an hour. 

As the floods recede, a rich black loam, which the Nile has gathered on its long journey, is deposited over the land. It is this natural irrigation that made Egypt possible. We pause a moment to reflect. This is the Egypt often called the “Cradle of Civilization”; Egypt, with its monuments and tombs, with its mysteries and achievements; Egypt, whose history carries us back through the centuries more than seven thousand years ago. No wonder this river was called "Father Nile" by those 'dawn people,” the ancient Egyptians. 66 

Life along the Nile. — Long, long ago, beyond the knowledge of any living person, man wandered into this fertile valley in search of food. He found plenty to eat by hunting and fishing. 

In time the region harbored numerous wandering tribes. Later the descendants of those groups passed from the hunting and fishing stage to the agricultural stage. They ceased to roam and began to cultivate the soil. They learned to raise flax and wheat, to weave beautiful linens, and to make pottery. In their leisure time the men hunted lions, elephants, and wild oxen. In their light papyrus canoes they sped lightly over the waters of the Nile and speared the hippo and the crocodile. With a clever hand and a crooked stick they broke the neck of the waterfowl as it sought food among the reeds at the river edge. 27

EGYPT 

29 Irrigation. Where the Nile flows into the Mediterranean 

Sea, there is a rich triangular plain called the Delta because it is shaped like the Greek letter of that name (A): The Delta is about one hundred miles wide. The fertile Nile valley, somewhat narrower, extends as far south as the First Cataract. 

About 4000 B.C. Egypt was a series of great farming communities throughout the Delta and the fertile valley. In the spring of the year those settlements looked like many small islands because of the flood season. This occurs during the months of 

August, September, and October. The remaining nine months make up the Egyptian summer. There is no fall or winter, and the sun shines nearly every day of the year.

As the water and loam rushed on towards the sea, those early settlers found it necessary to build dikes to protect themselves against the flood. They used the same dikes as dams to hold back the water with its rich deposits of earth. In this way it could be retained and used during the dry summer season. When the river became very low and the land very dry, it was found necessary to lift the water by means of levers and buckets from the river to the ditches above. So the Egyptians began a system which we call irrigation. This system has been used in every civilized country from their day to ours. - - The Home. The island communities consisted of many onestory mud huts surrounded by water. The roofs were made of plaited straw. The doors and windows were open spaces without covering. The furniture was very simple. It probably consisted of a bench, a stool, and a bed, made of soft weeds, in the corner. 

The kitchen utensils generally included a stone for grinding grain, several pots made of clay, and a crude stove for baking and cooking.

Farm Life. 

The early Egyptian scattered his grain over the muddy fields and frequently had it trampled in the ground by driving oxen over the newly sown field. They cut their grain with a sickle, and then separated it from the chaff with a heavy flail. In spite of these crude methods Egypt was able to feed

an ever-increasing population. It became known as the "land of plenty."

Many years later, when a great famine occurred throughout the 

Mediterranean world, Joseph of Bible fame became prime minister. During the years of plenty he had placed the surplus grain in vast storehouses. Then came the days of want and hunger and suffering. The care Joseph had taken saved the situation not only for the Egyptians but for surrounding peoples. Still later in history, during the height of the Roman Empire, Egypt was the granary of the world. She sent her grain to Rome to feed the unemployed people and to keep them from revolting. 

The Slave. Most of the farm work was done by the slave. 

His dress was simple. Whether he worked in the fields or in the quarries, he wore a simple garment extending from his waist to his knees. He went to work at daybreak and carried his noonday meal of wheat cake and oil with him. Over him was placed a master who represented the noble or owner of the land. The master carried a strap and frequently used it whether the slave needed it or not. It was believed that the slave worked best when flogged most. The slave paid his taxes in the form of grain to the noble, who in turn paid taxes to the king.

The womenfolk did all the spinning and weaving for the family. 

In the mud huts life was very simple but very busy. In them were made the coarse homespun garments for the field and the highgrade linen for religious festivals. The vast majority of these women were the wives of slaves and, of course, were themselves slaves. This lot was hard, and like their husbands they could not rise above their station in life. 

The Middle Class. While the slave class consisted of farmers, herdsmen, and quarry workers, the freemen or middle class consisted of traders and manufacturers. This group accumulated a large amount of wealth in the form of land. They could marry out of their class and enter the nobility. They have left but few records because their trading accounts were kept on paper, which of course is perishable. Their houses were of wood and

The daily life of the Egyptian worker. These groups are working at different trades wood carving, glass making, and sandal making. 

Taken from a wall. painting in a tomb near Thebes. 31

sun-dried bricks. These homes usually lasted only during the life of the owner. The wealthy members of this group probably led the way in encouraging education. It furnished scribes as secretaries to the nobles, and from its ranks officers and men for the army were recruited. 

The Upper Classes. - There was a certain amount of overlapping among the freemen and the upper classes. The former were continually striving to become one of the three upper groups. 

These consisted of the priests, the nobles, and the military leaders. 

The Priests. The priests were agents of the king and a highly privileged class. They were free from taxation. Their chief duty was to aid in carrying on the religion of the state. Many persons belonged to this class who were not engaged in religious work, such as the poets, historians, lawyers, and physicians. 

All learning was in their hands, however, and as a result they were able to wield a powerful influence and sometimes curb the of a king. power

The Nobles. The noble's wealth, which he inherited, consisted mostly of land. He had a life of leisure. He journeyed far to hunt wild game and was often accompanied by his family. 

The family was the unit of Egyptian life, and the family shared the noble's pleasure. The wife of a noble was highly respected.

There was a great personal attachment between father and son. 

It was customary for the father to rear and educate the son, and it was the duty of the son to take care of the father's tomb.

The Military Class. At one time Egypt had an army of over 400,000 militia. The militia was called out to fight only when needed. The government gave each soldier six acres of land to work as his own. This land was tax free, yet the life of a common soldier was very hard. He was flogged as often as the slave in the field. It was no pleasure to join a military expedition and march away to a foreign war. Drafting was the common method of raising an army. Each town had to furnish its share. When a young man was drafted and left home for war, his relatives

EGYPT 

33 Egyptian soldiers preparing for an attack. The men with the bows and arrows lead the way. They are followed by other men who carry light wooden shields in their left hands and battle axes in their right hands. mourned him as dead. The life of an officer was pleasant, however, and parents sought those positions for their sons. Many young men looked forward to a military career. 

Government. At an early date Egypt included two separate divisions. One division occupied the Delta and was known as 

Lower Egypt. Its capital was situated at Memphis. South of the Delta was Upper Egypt with its capital at Thebes (thēbz). 

Race. According to the Bible, the Egyptians were descendants of Ham, the son of Noah. Therefore they were classed as a Hamitic race, and the name still is applied to them. They were probably a branch of the early white race with a slight mixture of Negro blood, received from the contact with the inhabitants of Ethiopia (e-thi-o'pi-à). The people of the Delta were more of a mixed race because they were frequently attacked by desert tribes, and their land was overrun with invaders from Asia. "Nomes." Such was Egypt, with its mixed races, its social classes, and its various occupations. It was composed of about thirty-five communities called "nomes." Over each nome the king placed one of his favorites as governor. This officer was responsible to the king only. He collected the taxes in the form of grain and sent it to the king's granary, where it was stored for future use. Many civil wars took place between the people of 

Upper and Lower Egypt until about 3400 B.C. At that time a powerful ruler by the name of Menes (mē'nēz) united the two countries and made Thebes the capital, 

---

Menes. Menes ruled the united Egypts and established a monarchy with himself as king, or pharaoh. He had the power of life and death over his governors and his people. No orders could be given nor gods worshiped without his consent. One of his difficult problems was to find enough food for his people. 

Menes increased the grain supply by compelling the people to coöperate. This was done by encouraging them to raise as much as possible in good years. Granaries were built to store the grain which was not used. Menes knew that lean years were sure to come years when the Nile would remain low and the land would become dry and hot. 

The Hyksos. Nearly two thousand years passed, and Egypt became a great kingdom, with a long history of kings, queens, wars, trade, commerce, science, and progress. And then about 

1800 B.C. a rude, warlike people from Syria, called the Hyksos (hik'sōs), with very little civilization, conquered Egypt. The 

Hyksos introduced the horse and the chariot into the land of the Nile. The new masters ruled with an iron hand for several hundred years. Finally the Egyptians learned to unite against This Egyptian war chariot is now in the Metropolitan Museum of Art, New York, their common foe. They arose in rebellion against them and threw off the yoke of submission the days of the Hyksos or "shepherd kings" were over.

EGYPT 

35 Queen Hatshepsut. - One of the most famous rulers of ancient 

Egypt was Queen Hatshepsut (about 1500 B.C.). She ruled first with her brother, later with her husband, and during the last twenty years of her life, she ruled alone. Under Queen Hatshepsut's rule Egypt became a great commercial nation. A canal was dug connecting the Nile with the Red Sea. Vessels, manned by oars and with gayly painted sails, plied back and forth between the various ports on the 

Mediterranean and the Red seas. Ivory, precious stones, and leopard skins from the coast of eastern Africa were exchanged for the products of the Nile Valley. Festivals and celebrations were held to welcome the vessels home. 

The Empire. After their experience with the Hyksos the 

Egyptians became a powerful military nation. 

Great honors Queen Hatshepsut. From her temple near Thebes. were given to successful generals. Egypt began to expand, to conquer, and to govern subject nations. Such a government is called an "empire." 

Rameses II (1292-1225 B.C.). Rameses II (răm´ê-sēz), one of the renowned pharaohs (kings) of Egypt, reorganized the army. 

It included charioteers, bowmen, and foot-soldiers, armed with short, heavy knives. The Egyptians conquered Syria and parts of the bordering lands. The first treaty known in world history was made between Rameses II and the Hittite king. 

The victorious monarch returned to his capital, Thebes. He brought back thousands of captives to be sold as slaves, trophies of war, precious stones, and valuable works of art. Rameses did not devote all of his time to war and to preparing for it. Under his direction numerous and large irrigation canals connecting all

parts of the valley plain were made, and other public works were encouraged.

Religion. Religion was the most important part of Egyptian life. Egyptians believed in a life after death and worshiped many gods. Their leading god was Ra (rä), who represented the sun. 

The sun had a powerful and mysteri- Egyptians usually worshiped their gods in groups of three.

In one such group was Osiris, god of the underworld, Isis his wife, and Horus, their son.

In this picture you see Horus, who represented the rising sun. The hawk was his emblem. ous influence over the minds of the 

Egyptians. They, like primitive man, marveled at its splendor and its lifegiving qualities of heat and light. 

Next in importance to Ra, the sun god or god of gods, was Osiris (ō-sī′ris).

He dwelt in the underworld and weighed in a scale the good and the bad qualities of a soul seeking admittance into heaven. 

The

The Nile: Giver of Life." 

Nile River was praised in song and prayer. As a deity it ranked high in the minds of the Nile dwellers. They felt its power of destruction at flood time and appreciated the crops made possible by its black and fertile loam. 

Many prayers started, "O Nile, thou giver of life, every good thing that we have comes from thee."

Local Gods. Every community had its own local god in addition to the many national gods. 

Every home had its altar. Birds, animals, trees, and anything that the Egyptians could not understand were respected and worshiped.

Burials. In time religion became so important that life in this world was devoted in a large way in preparation for the next. 

Great care was taken in the burial of the dead. Everything that a person had used in this world was buried with him so that

EGYPT 

37 he could use them in the next. A record of the person's good deeds, written on papyrus, accompanied the double or spirit on his journey to the next world. This record was to help the spirit in case it became confused in the presence of the great Osiris.

The body of the dead received special preparation for burial. 

It was prepared in a liquid pitch and was called a "mummy," from the name of the pitch used. The mummy was then wrapped tightly in many yards of linen and placed in a grave to await the call of the Great Spirit.

The grave was either simple or elaborate, depending on the wealth and position of the deceased. The graveyards were always located west of the town and faced west. Stones were piled on the graves to prevent the body from being disturbed and the valuables stolen. Later the Egyptians vied with one another to see who could build the largest stone pile. 

The Pyramids. — This attempt resulted in the building of the pyramids, which were the tombs of the pharaohs. The largest Colonnade of the Temple of Luxor built by Tutankamen, whose tomb was discovered a few years ago. It was beautifully decorated by this king.

and most famous of these was that built by King Cheops (ke'ops) near the present city of Cairo (ki'rō). This pyramid covers thirteen acres and is over four hundred fifty feet high. It contains about 2,300,000 blocks of limestone, each weighing more than two tons. It is supposed to have taken 120,000 men twenty years to build it. Near this pyramid stands the Sphinx (sfinks), which has the head of a human being and the body of a lion.

Temples. Temples were erected in honor of the gods. The great temple of Karnak was dedicated to Ammon, the god of Thebes. There lived the priests and there came the pharaoh to worship and sacrifice to the gods. He came also to be worshiped by his people as the god's representative on earth. The approach to this famous temple was a broad, well-paved avenue. Giant sphinxes, which faced each other, lined both sides. Two tall obelisks with gilded tops were located at the entrance to the temple. The structure contained the largest colonnaded hall ever built. One hundred men could stand on the head of one of the columns, which were brightly painted and beautifully and artistically carved.

Schools. Egyptian boys and girls attended school. There were no books, and no homework was assigned. Yet their lessons in reading, writing, and arithmetic kept them busy. They learned to read "hieroglyphics" from papyrus rolls, and they copied lessons prepared for them by their teachers. The pupils were taught addition and subtraction. A crude form of multiplication was used, but division was unknown for a long time. 

Discipline was very strict, and flogging was a daily occurrence and a part of the educational system. 

The Graduates. After finishing school, a boy, if he wanted to become a keeper of accounts, was apprenticed to a commercial or business house. If a boy wanted to follow a military career, he was sent to a military school which was controlled by the government. A future scribe sought employment from a noble, from whom he learned the duties of a secretary. A youth looking

Orient and Occident 

The Pyramids of ancient Egypt look down on the Nile Valley of today.

39

forward to the priesthood was sent to one of the many temples dedicated to the gods. Whether he entered the commercial world, became a military leader, scribe, or priest, the boy often looked down on his less-fortunate brothers and sisters. They did not care about this, however, but were proud and boastful of the fact that a brother of theirs had gained honor and distinction. 

Literature. The early Egyptians, like all people, were fond of stories. Some of their literature, which was written by the 

Egyptians on stone and papyrus, have been handed down to the present. This includes stories of their gods and early tribal kings, who performed miraculous deeds. The most important book of the Egyptians was the "Book of the Dead." This told about the religion of the people, that is, how life was to be carried on in this world in preparation for the next. It gave the rules which guaranteed a safe journey to the "judging spirit," Osiris. 

Many copies of this book were made by the scribes. No burial was complete unless the body was accompanied by it. A few magic words from its pages, and evil spirits would become charmed.

Another part of the "Book of the Dead" is known as the "negative confession." Each statement in this part began with, "I did not." Some examples are: "I did not steal," "I did not lie," "I did not murder," "I did not blaspheme a god," "I did not revile my father." The above confessions closely resemble our Ten Commandments, except that "I did not" is used for งงงง The "Book of the Dead" was found in many ancient Egyptian homes. The animal gods are presenting the human spirit to Osiris. Notice the scales in which the good and bad qualities of the spirit were weighed.

EGYPT 

4I "Thou shalt not." If the soul had been faithful to the negative confession, it was immediately admitted into heaven. If not, it had to return to earth again in the form of some animal. Then, after it had passed through many different lives on this earth, the spirit might ask for another chance to enter heaven. 

The Original Cinderella Story. - 

One of the old Egyptian stories tells about Nitokris and how she became queen. It is called the original Cinderella story and was written about 4000 years ago. The name of the story is 

Rhodopis and Her Gilded Sandals. 

Rhodopis was a rosy-cheeked girl who went down to the Nile one day to bathe. She left her gilded sandals on the shore. While she was swimming, a royal eagle flew overhead. It spied the glistening sandals among the papyrus reeds, swooped down to earth, and seized one of them. Then the eagle soared into the heavens where it was lost among the bright beams of the sun god, Ra. At the same time 

Pharaoh was holding court in the square at Memphis. This mighty king, who wore the crowns of 

Upper and Lower Egypt, had found no woman worthy to be his queen. And as he sighed about his own unhappiness, the royal eagle dropped the sandal in his lap. 

Bronze statue of Isis.

The king became curious. He wanted to meet the owner of this tiny slipper. For some reason he felt that the maiden whose foot it fitted was worthy to be his queen. He called on Isis, the goddess of love, to guide him to her. The king stopped all government business. His chief scribe sent notices to all parts of 

RA 4

Egypt requesting maidens to come to the Great House, Pero, and try their foot in the gilded sandal. Many came from far and near, but no one could wear it. 

After many months the king was in despair. Finally there appeared before Pharaoh a poor man who had been forgiven the royal tax. He told the king that a beautiful maiden appeared at daybreak every morning at the great Sphinx near the pyramids and that she sang a song of welcome to the sun god, Ra, as he flooded the desert suddenly with light.

The next morning the king and his scribe were hiding near the 

Sphinx when Rhodopis appeared to meet the dawn with song and dance. She was more lovely than the great king had thought her to be. Finally he persuaded the shy maiden to try her foot in the tiny sandal. As her foot slipped into the gilded slipper, the mighty king asked Rhodopis to be his queen. She gave her consent as she took from her girdle another little slipper which had not been worn since the royal eagle appeared overhead one bright sunny day along the Nile.

Political History. After the rule of Rameses II the Egyptian 

Empire gradually began to crumble. By the twelfth century 

B.C. it had fallen before invaders from the north. Later it was conquered by Assyria, and in the year 525 B.C. it became a part of the Persian Empire. It was then under the rule of various kings and peoples until 1922, when it was permitted to govern itself.

How This Early Egyptian Civilization Affects the Present. 

Many things that we know and have today we owe to those early Egyptians. They were one of the first peoples to write down their thoughts. They invented a crude alphabet, and later generations improved it.

Paper. They needed paper, pen, and ink, so they invented them. Some genius went down to the Nile, took a reed plant which grew in the marshes, and split its leaves with a very fine needle. These leaves were from six to twelve inches wide and were taken from the papyrus plant, hence our word "paper."

EGYPT 

43 Pens were made by taking small pieces of reed and making them into the form of a brush. Ink was made by mixing charcoal and water. 

The Calendar. The rise and fall of the Nile created a desire for some accurate measure of time. So a calendar was invented in 4241 B.C., which is the earliest recorded date in history. It consisted of twelve months of thirty days each with a festival period of five days at the end of the year. Later an additional day was added every four years by Julius Caesar. This early 

Egyptian calendar has lived through six thousand years of history and is the basis for our calendar today. 

Geometry and Surveying. The Nile, too, was responsible for the science of surveying. Every year the spring floods covered the landmarks. The Egyptians found it necessary to devise some system whereby they could locate a point which was covered by mud and water from some place on dry land. Geometry was used in making these measurements. Thus it is one of our earliest mathematical sciences and owes its origin to the Egyptians. 

The Lever and Inclined Plane. To Egyptians we owe the principle of irrigation explained on a previous page. They invented the lever used in raising water from a lower to a higher level. They built their pyramids by applying the principle of the inclined plane.

Such was the valley of the Nile. And such were the people, with their government, religion, art, literature, science, and everyday life.

We leave the land of the pyramids and temples, the obelisks, and the Sphinx. We bid farewell to the peaceful, life-giving Nile.

But we shall journey to another river valley -the Tigris- Euphrates one that sheltered a civilization as old perhaps as that of Egypt.
