CHAPTER V  I. THE OLD BABYLONIAN EMPIRE 

The Fertile Crescent. - About one hundred fifty miles east of the Delta in Asia Minor, there begins a fringe of fertile land. This territory extends in a semicircle from the peninsula of Sinai (si'nī) in the west to the Persian Gulf in the east. The backbone of this grassy fringe consists of high mountains and plateaus, which gradually give way to barren hills and the hot Arabian Desert. This semicircle of fertile territory is known as the "Fertile Crescent."

The Lands to Be Visited. Along the Mediterranean coast is 

Palestine, the home of the early Hebrews. To the north is 

Phoenicia. Opposite these countries, on the other side of the semicircle, are Babylonia and Assyria. These are the lands we must visit to complete our story of ancient Oriental civilization. 

Mesopotamia: the Land between the Rivers. At the top of the semicircle in the snow-capped mountains of Armenia near Mt. Ararat (ăr'a-răt), where Noah's Ark is supposed to have landed, two rivers have their origin. They are the Tigris and 

Euphrates, and they flow towards the southeast. Sometimes their courses bring them very close together, at other times far apart, but eventually they unite and empty as one river into the 

Persian Gulf. Like the Nile, when the Tigris and Euphrates overflow their banks and then recede, they deposit a rich black soil in the valley plain. The fertile strip of land thus formed extends for several hundred miles north of the gulf and was called by the Greeks Mesopotamia (měs-o-pō-tā'mĭ-à), meaning "between the rivers."

The Sumerians. Before Menes united Upper and Lower 

Egypt into a kingdom (3400 B.C.

), there was a civilization in 47

Mesopotamia. The first inhabitants about whom we know anything were called Sumerians (su-mē'ri-ănz), and they were members of the white race. They built their houses of sundried brick and surrounded their towns with thick brick walls as a defense against savage tribes. They wrote on clay tablets with a stick. They used a system of wedge or V-shaped characters, which was later called "cuneiform" by the Latins. They cultivated the soil, were excellent farmers and herdsmen, and probably carried on a brisk trade with cities along the Persian Gulf. 

The Land of Sumer Conquered by Sargon I. Then, about the time of Menes, wandering and warlike tribes from the Arabian Desert began to settle along the Tigris and Euphrates rivers. 

These tribes were called Semitic (se-mit'ik) after another son of Noah, by the name of Shem. Eventually the Semites as well as the Sumerians were conquered and united by a great Semitic leader called Sargon I (sär'gon). inso Much that we know about the Fertile Crescent was learned from clay tablets. 

This one shows a group of Sumerian soldiers. 

The Old Babylonian Empire. - The Semites under Sargon I 

acquired the civilization of the Sumerians; they became farmers, merchants, and skilled artisans. They became the leaders of their adopted land. Many years passed, and finally all the

Sargon I used this seal to stamp his official documents. city-states of Mesopotamia were united into an empire with the capital at Babylon in the south. About 2100 B.C. Hammurabi became emperor of the Babylonian Empire. In order to avoid confusion, let us explain that there were two Babylonian empires. 

The first, called the Old Babylonian Empire, extended from the time of Hammurabi to the year 728 B.C. The second is known as the New Babylonian or Chaldean (kǎl-de'ăn) Empire, and it dated from 625 B.C. to 530 B.C. We shall follow first the story of the old empire.

The City of Babylon in the Days of Hammurabi. Let us examine the city of Babylon during the reign of Hammurabi. 

It was located on a broad plain and was surrounded by a wall thirty feet high. The wall was so wide that chariots could pass on its top. Beyond the walls, on the outside, a deep moat encircled the city. It is said that the Babylonians used the mud from the moat to make their bricks. They had no mortar in those days, so a thick pitch called bitumen was used. The Euphrates divided the city, but the walls were so built that the shores of the river were protected, and there was no danger of an invasion from that source. Within, the city teemed with life. Soldiers drilled, and government officials, priests, merchants, artisans, and slaves were kept busy. The courts and schools were in session, and trade and commerce were brisk. Let us follow the ships and the caravans as they journey to Babylon.

The Armenian Boatmen. The river Euphrates brought commerce to Babylon from Armenia (är-me'ni-à). The Armenians were Semites from the north. Their boats were shaped like shields, and the framework of each was made from willow, over which skins were tightly drawn. These craft were capable of carrying several tons. Many of the boats which came to Babylon had strange cargoes. For example, a boat might be laden with wooden casks filled with wine, while near the stern several donkeys might stand. In Babylon the Armenians marketed their wine, then dismantled and sold their boat because the current 

Photo by J. B. Shackelford, American Museum of Natural History 

Transportation in the Near East a camel train with its burdens winding its way across the desert.

was too swift to paddle upstream. They rode their donkeys home and rebuilt new boats for the next journey. 

A Camel Caravan from Egypt and a Tariff Argument. - A 

camel caravan from Egypt was ready to enter the main gate of the city. The leader of the caravan was arguing with the gate keeper about the high tariff he had to pay before he could enter. 

Sentries stationed on the high wall, their spears and helmets blazing in the setting sun, watched the proceedings with curiosity and amusement. The customs duty was finally fixed. The heavy cedar gates, covered with metal, were opened. The caravan train, laden with costly linen, ivory, cups of gold and silver, and precious stones, passed in. It was greeted by the ambassadors from Egypt, who escorted the train to a hotel. 

No wonder the Babylonian officials made these people pay a high tariff. They needed the money to carry on their government and to pay their soldiers. The Egyptians, on the other hand, added the tariff charges to the price of their goods and still made a handsome profit. So, in the end, the Babylonians who purchased the products from the Egyptians really paid the tariff or customs duty.

A Babylonian Crowd. A motley crowd followed the caravan to find out what the merchants would offer for sale in the market place on the morrow. They differed greatly from a crowd in the business section of Thebes. The Babylonians had shaggy heads of hair covered with heavy turbans, while their whiskers were long and plentiful. They wore two armless cloaks, a thin linen one covered over by a gayly decorated thick one. On their feet they wore either sandals or high boots. They presented quite a contrast to the Egyptians, who had smoothly shaven heads and faces and whose garments were very plain, reaching from their waists to their knees. 

The Skilled Workmen of Babylon and Where They Lived. 

Babylon was divided into many sections. The workers of the same trade lived in the same section. The seal cutters, those skilled workers who made signatures for the populace, lived in

These show how ancient architecture has influenced the present. On the left is a temple like those built by the Sumerians; on the right is a modern church. one group.

Others who lived in the same manner were the weavers, who were known wherever traders assembled, the dyers, who could compete with any on the Mediterranean coast, and the smiths, who made armor and forged weapons and tools. The goldsmiths and jewelers in their section were proud of the fact that their work rivaled the best that Egypt produced. — - 

A Babylonian Temple and the Tower of Babel. Near the main gate stood a beautiful temple, seven stories high. Each story was larger than the one above it, and the last was covered with a large gilded dome. Since the days of the Sumerians it had been the custom for the people of each city to build a high tower in connection with their religion. In the old days when they lived on the hills, they sought always the highest point when offering sacrifices to their gods. They wanted to get as near the sun, moon, and stars as possible. Those heavenly bodies represented a few of their deities. When they moved to the plain, they missed their hills and so they built their own high places. The story is told that once, while a Sumerian tribe

was in the midst of building one of these towers, a Semitic tribe called Hebrews came upon the scene, decided that these builders were trying to reach heaven, and called it the Tower of Babel. 

Our present-day custom of building steeples in connection with our churches and cathedrals is supposed to have originated with these ancient temple towers of Mesopotamia. 

Babylonian Gods and Goddesses. - The Babylonian temple just described was dedicated to the god Marduk (mär'dook). 

He was supposed to be all-powerful and was the god of war. 

There were, however, many other gods, such as Sin, the moon god, Ea (a'ä), the earth god, and Ishtar, the goddess of love. 

The Priests or Magi; the Three Wise Men of the East. - 

The temples were in charge of priests or Magi. These Magi also read the future by studying the stars, and prescribed remedies for sickness by watching the entrails of animals. The three wise men or Magi who journeyed from the East to Bethlehem, when Jesus was born, were either Chaldeans (men of the New Babylonian Empire) or Persians. 

The Astrology of the Magi Becomes the Astronomy of Today. 

The planets and the stars represented many gods. The priests, who were the interpreters for the gods, spent most of their time searching the heavens to deter- mine whether good or bad luck was in store for people. This method of determining the future by watching the movements of the planets is called "astrology." It was the forerunner of our modern science of astronomy, which is the scientific study of the heavenly bodies.

The Twelve Signs of the molim nu me 10 11 صدا 12 1 

Zodiac. - All Babylonians be- Twelve Signs of the Zodiac. What are lieved in astrology, which they and what do they mean?

began at a very early date. Many fortune tellers today ply their trade by this old Babylonian method. The Magi at a later period mapped the heavens in the same manner as they drew maps of the earth. They divided the heavens into twelve parts or signs. Each part represented a figure of power. They 444 Notice the little wedge-shaped charac- 

This letter was found at Tellters. el-Amarna. are called the "Twelve Signs of the Zodiac."

A Lucky Number. - Seven was considered a lucky number in the days of Hammurabi. The leading temples were seven stories high. The week was divided into seven days. No work was allowed on the seventh day (which was called Sabbath by the 

Babylonians, from whom the 

Hebrews got the term and handed it on to us). In case of sickness a magic thread was wound around the body seven times to cure the disease.

A Babylonian School. - 

But let us get back to the city. Near the temple stood a schoolhouse. (One of these schoolhouses is so well preserved that from it and other evidence we know how it was used in those days of long ago.) It is entered through a long hall which leads into an open court. Here, at one time, boys and girls of your age laughed and talked. At a signal everything became quiet. The children formed in groups and entered their classroom through a door which faced the court. They had to learn to read nearly three hundred cuneiform characters that made up their language. They wrote these wedge-shaped characters on a clay tablet with a stylus, a stick about the size of a lead pencil.

Clay was furnished to the pupils in the form of a ball. When their lessons were assigned, they flattened the ball according to instructions and work began. If a mistake was made, all that they had to do was to smooth over the surface. After they finished their schooling here, the boys could continue their education as apprentices (learners) to weavers, dyers, goldsmiths, scribes, or business accountants. Or they could enter the army or study for the priesthood. 

Babylonian Arithmetic and Calendar. - The priests were the most highly educated people of Babylon. If we could have visited one of the classes, we might have seen one of the Magi lecturing to a group of young men. Perhaps he drew a circle on the sand and explained that there are 360° in the circumference (or distance around it). He told them there are sixty seconds in a minute, sixty minutes in an hour, and twelve hours in a day. Twelve was taught as a unit. Thus the faces of our The Babylonians began the use of the arch. This is the oldest known arch in history. It was found about forty years ago at Nippur, an ancient city south of Babylon.

modern clocks and watches are still marked with twelve instead of with twenty-four hours.

The Magus explained also the system of weights and measures (a pound was called a "mena") and the basis for their calendar. 

The Babylonian month began with each new moon. At the end of each year or twelve moons an additional month was added to complete the year. In some parts of Arabia this calendar is used today. 

The Babylonian Arch. If we had stood at the temple and looked down a long narrow street, we should have seen block after block of plain walls. Upon a closer view we should have noticed many arched gateways. If we had entered one of these gates, we should have found ourselves in a courtyard upon which all the rooms of the house faced. Every door of these rooms was arched. 

The Babylonians began the use of the arch. The Egyptians used only a straight line above their doors. The arch was copied by every following civilization. The triple arch of the 

Assyrians and the far-famed 

Roman arch began here. 

The Sundial and Water 

Clock. Some historians give the Babylonians credit for both the sundial and the water clock. The water clock was an improvement over the sundial because it could be used A water clock. This was used by the on a cloudy day. The water 

Babylonians to check their twelve-hour clock consisted of a vessel filled with a certain amount of water day. at a definite time. It was so regulated that a certain amount trickled out in an hour. The changing levels of water could be compared with the graduated (or hour-marked) vessel. Thus the time could be seen at any hour of the day. It was generally

customary to have a sundial in the courtyard and a water clock in the library. Naturally this was the case only in the homes of the wealthy.

A Babylonian Day Begins: Pastures, Groves, Canals. — When the sundial or sun clock, located near the temple, cast a shadow, it meant that the day had begun. The gates, which had been locked the night before at sundown, were thrown open. The herdsmen then led their flocks to the pasture lands beyond the beautiful groves of date and palm trees. Fields of grain, which waved in the morning sun, were located between the groves and the pastures. Irrigation canals crossed each other at regular intervals. High banks hid the slowly receding Euphrates from view. Man's genius had conquered nature. A hot, barren desert had been converted into a garden. 

A Day with Hammurabi the Postal System. Let us follow the daily life of the great King Hammurabi. He had built up a powerful empire and had organized a postal system to keep in touch with the governors in all of his provinces. His letters were written on clay tablets over which a dry clay powder was sprinkled. This kept the wet clay envelope from sticking to the letter. Then it was sent to the oven to be baked. In a short time a messenger sped the letters on their way. Fifty-five of 

Hammurabi's letters have been preserved. Some deal with taxation, some with the calendar, while others have statements referring to religion.

The Code of Hammurabi. - Hammurabi administered justice in an open court near the temple. Every one admired the great 

Hammurabi. He had collected all the laws of their forefathers, added new ones, and had them chiseled on a stone shaft about eight feet tall. At the top of the shaft was a picture of Hammurabi receiving the laws from the sun god. This is the oldest code of laws in history and is called the Code of Hammurabi. 

Although this code is more than 4000 years old, some parts of it may be applied in our courts today. It made special provisions for widows and orphans and punished fraud and forgery by

RA-5

death. Portions of the code have been described as "an eye for an eye and a tooth for a tooth" law. For example, if a son struck his father, his hand was cut off. If a person was attacked and lost an eye, the assailant lost an eye. 

2. ASSYRIA The City of Assur Is Founded. About the time of Ham murabi, a warlike Semitic tribe left Babylon and followed the course of the Tigris River until it reached the uplands. There the hills were barren, but the valleys were fertile. Snow fell during the winter months, but the summers were warm, and the country beautiful. They built a city and named it after their principal god, Assur. These people of Assur, or Assyrians as they are called, copied many things from their fellow Babylonians. 

They inclosed their city with walls of sun-dried brick. The foundations were made of limestone, which was found in limited amounts. The walls were covered with alabaster upon which beautiful designs could be fashioned. 

The Assyrian Empire: Sennacherib. As time went on, the little walled city of Assur extended its power in all directions and finally organized much of the eastern world into the Assyrian Empire. As such it was hated and feared from the Ganges 

River in India to the Nile in Egypt. Emperors followed each other in rapid succession, each more powerful than his predecessor. About the year 700 B.C. Sennacherib (se-năk'er-ĭb) became ruler of Assyria.

He was noted as a war- 

The Conquests of Sennacherib. 

rior. Under him the Assyrian armies invaded Babylon, killed many of the inhabitants, demolished the once proud city, and left the surrounding country a barren waste. He compelled the king of the powerful Hittite Empire to pay tribute. He invaded and conquered the Arameans (ăr-à-mēʼănz), who were the commercial leaders of that day. Damascus (da-măs'kus), their principal city, was on the caravan route to and from the chief cities and countries of the Orient.

Assyrian war chariots. Taken from an Assyrian bas-relief. 

The kingdom of Israel and its capital, Samaria, were plundered and laid waste. The men, women, and children were carried into captivity. Judah and its capital, Jerusalem, were saved from a similar fate when Sennacherib's army was struck by a plague. 

The Bible mentions this last incident. Soldiers were sent to the forests of Lebanon to prepare cedar to be used for the heavy gates of the new city at Nineveh. This city was built by Sennacherib on the Tigris River above Assyria, and became his capital. 

The Celebrations at Nineveh. After each conquest messengers would return to Nineveh with the news. Sculptors would begin immediately to carve the story on rocks and on the walls of the palace so the emperor could read them on his return. The city would be full of excitement. Every day a band of captives arrived, bringing their wealth, and driving their herds before them. At the head of the columns marched the priests carrying the heads of conquered leaders. Celebrations lasted for weeks, after which the inhabitants returned to the daily routine. The captives became slaves, and the emperor would again take up his sport of lion hunting. No wonder the armies of Sennacherib were feared and hated. 

The Assyrian Builders: the Triple Arch. The Assyrians were great builders.

Each emperor built for himself a palace

more splendid than that of his father. These buildings con- 

The walls were covered with slabs of alabaster on which were carved figures of human-headed bulls with wings, lions, and tigers. The arch of the Babylonians was used in triple form in the construction of magnificent palace doorways. sisted of many halls built around an open court. 

The Library of Ashurbanipal. - Ashurbanipal, the grandson of Sennacherib, contributed more to civilization than any of his predecessors. He built a large public library. It was the first of its kind in the ancient world. He wanted people to use it. 

The books were composed of little clay tablets. There must have been many, many thousands of them.

Many were so well pre- Writing wedge-shaped characters on a clay served when found a few tablet with a stylus. years ago that scholars were able to read the little wedge-shaped characters. Some of these characters are so small that a magnifying glass must be used to see them. The clay tablets found here and at other places deal with history, geography, astronomy, and literature. Beautiful poems and stories are included also. 

The Story of Gilgamesh, the Epic of the Semites of the Tigris- Euphrates Valley. One of these stories goes back many, many years, before the time of Assyria. It is the great epic (narrative poem of a national hero) of the Semitic races, who lived in the Tigris-Euphrates Valley. It tells of the hero Gilgamesh, who probably lived a long time before the Sumerians wandered into 

Mesopotamia. The story is as follows: 

The people living in the town of Erech (e'rěk) were oppressed. 

Gilgamesh was their ruler and was supposed to be their champion, but he was very hard and strict with the people. He forced

all the young men into his army and compelled all the young women to labor in his palace. The people of Erech appealed to the goddess Aruru (ä-roo'roo), who had created Gilgamesh, and asked her to create some one who could overcome him. She promised to do so and made a savage being, half man and half beast, whose body was covered with hair. He was called 

Eabani (ā-ä-bä’nē), and he roamed about with the animals of the field.

Gilgamesh realized what had taken place and refused to meet Eabani in battle. He sent his huntsman to spy on Eabani and snare him, but he failed. Then Gilgamesh sent Ukhat (σo'kät), a beautiful girl. Eabani fell in love with her and wanted to follow her everywhere. He forgot that he had been created to kill Gilgamesh. Ukhat persuaded Eabani to leave the beasts of the field and return to town with her. Eabani met Gilgamesh, and they became devoted friends. Together they conquered the land of Elam, which lay east of the Euphrates. Upon their return they were honored by the people of Erech. 

The goddess of love, Ishtar, fell in love with Gilgamesh and wanted to marry him. But Gilgamesh realized the fate of any human being who tried to marry a goddess, and so he refused her offer.

Ishtar was highly insulted and rushed to her father, the god of gods, asking revenge upon Gilgamesh. Her father created a fierce bull, which was sent to destroy the land of Gilgamesh and Eabani. The two champions easily killed the bull. Ishtar was very angry and placed a curse upon the two heroes. his boldness defied the goddess and insulted her. 

Eabani in

For this offense Eabani could not hope to escape punishment. 

He was stricken by the gods and, after much suffering, died. 

While lamenting the fate of his friend, Gilgamesh was stricken also. When nearly dead, he remembered his great ancestor 

Ut-napishtim (oot-nä-pish'tim), who of all mortals had been allowed to escape death. Gilgamesh determined to go to him for advice.

Now Ut-napishtim lived far beyond the joining place of the great waters. Many dangers blocked the way. Gilgamesh started the journey and overcame one difficulty after another until he came to the shore. Here the Princess Sabitu ruled. 

Gilgamesh, after much persuasion, gained her help. Finally, she sent him to a boatman who decided to make the month's journey with him. They made the crossing in three days. 

Ut-napishtim and his wife saw Gilgamesh coming and met him at the shore. They were curious to know how he had crossed the "waters of death." Without moving from his boat, Gilgamesh related the sad story about Eabani. Then he wanted to know how to escape the same fate. But Ut-napishtim sadly told Gilgamesh that every one must die. 

Gilgamesh asked his ancestor how he and his wife had happened to escape death. Ut-napishtim told the story of a flood which had covered the earth because of the wickedness of the people. He and his wife had escaped and could live forever like the gods.

Ut-napishtim pitied Gilgamesh, had him put to sleep, and then gave him some magic food which cured him of his illness. He then told Gilgamesh where he could find a magic plant which would always keep him young. He found it, but on the return journey he stopped to drink from a cool spring and a serpent snatched it from his hand. He lost the magic plant but had been cured of his disease. Now he concluded sadly that there was no escape from death.

Gilgamesh wanted the gods to bring Eabani back so that he could find out what death was like. Finally the god of the dead brought Eabani face to face with his old friend. Gilgamesh asked Eabani what death was like, but Eabani refused to tell him.

The only thing that Gilgamesh could do was to lead the proper kind of life, make his people happy, and prepare for his own burial. He, like Ponce de Leon in Florida, had failed in his search for eternal youth.

3. THE CHALDEAN EMPIRE 

The Fall of Assyria. - Ashurbanipal was the last of the great 

Assyrian rulers. At last the empire came to an end. Too many men had been taken from the fields and workshops and forced into the army. Many of these men never returned, and the population dwindled. The conquered provinces took advantage of this condition and rebelled against their tyrannical masters. 

The Chaldean Empire 625 B.C. Nebuchadnezzar. The 

Chaldeans, another Semitic people, besieged Nineveh and brought about its complete downfall. A new leader, Nebuchadnezzar (něb-u-kăd-něz'ar), reëstablished the Babylonian Empire and rebuilt the old city of Babylon. He erected temples to the leading gods. He copied Assyrian sculpture and surpassed those 

www An imaginative reconstruction of the Hanging Gardens of Babylon. From a painting in an old Bible. Notice the temple towers.

people in Oriental luxury. He built the famous Hanging Gardens of Babylon for his homesick wife, who longed for the mountains and scenes of her homeland. These gardens were called one of the seven wonders of the ancient world by the Greeks. 

The Feast of Belshazzar. Nebuchadnezzar's armies marched to the west. They captured Jerusalem and sent many of the inhabitants back to Babylon as captives. The fourth chapter of the book of Daniel in the Old Testament tells of Nebuchadnezzar and his dream. Under his rule art, literature, science, and architecture flourished. The Chaldean Empire, however, was shortlived. About one hundred years later (538 B.C.), in the midst of a gay and boisterous feast, King Belshazzar saw the handwriting on the wall that predicted the downfall of his nation (see the Book of Daniel, Chapter V). During that same year Babylon was overthrown by the Persians under Cyrus the Great. 

4. THE PHOENICIANS 

The Phoenicians. — In the meantime we must follow briefly the fortunes of those two Semitic peoples in the west- the Phoenicians and the Hebrews. The Phoenicians at a very early date wandered in from the desert and settled on the eastern coast of the Mediterranean Sea. The country that they occupied was very small. It was about one hundred fifty miles long and about forty miles wide. It lay between the mountains of Lebanon and the sea. The Phoenicians became the greatest traders and colonizers of the ancient Oriental world. Their ships or caravans were known in all the ports and inland cities of that day as far away as Britain, India, and southern Africa. 

The chief product of the Phoenicians was a purple dye, which was in great demand throughout the Orient. Robes of Phoenician purple became a mark of wealth and later of royalty in many lands. The dye was extracted by a very slow process from a small shellfish called the murex. The Phoenician fishermen were ever moving westward in search of new fishing grounds.

Phoenician traders used boats with oars and sails. These fast-moving ships carried goods as far west as the Atlantic Ocean and the British Isles. 

As they went, they established colonies all along the Mediterranean coast. 

The most important of those colonies was Carthage (kär'thaj), which years later was to rival Rome. We read in the Bible how 

Hiram, king of Tyre (tir), a Phoenician city-state, sent workmen and lumber made from the cedars of Lebanon to help the Jewish king, David, build his palace at Jerusalem. 

The Alphabet. -The chief contribution of the Phoenicians to civilization was the alphabet. They needed a simple method to keep their trading accounts and bills of lading for the many colonies and other foreign ports. The clay tablets of the 

Babylonians were too bulky and the writing too difficult. So they borrowed alphabet, paper, and pen from the Egyptians. They improved upon the work of the Egyptians by having a letter stand for a sound instead of a syllable. They used twenty-two

letters. This alphabet was passed on to the Greeks, who made several changes in it and then passed it on to the Romans. 

The Romans changed it to the present-day alphabet of twentysix letters, and thus it was handed on to us. 

5. THE HEBREWS 

The Hebrews. - The Phoenicians, like all the people studied so far, worshiped many gods. Their chief god was Baal (bā'ǎl) or Moloch (mō'lõk). He was represented in metal as a human being with a bull's head. He was the sun god or god of fire, and human sacrifices were made to him. 

South of Phoenicia were the Hebrews. They believed in one god Jehovah. The traditional founder of their race was Abraham, who left the city of Ur about the time of Hammurabi. He objected to the idol worship of his homeland and traveled west to the "promised land" of Canaan (kā'năn). There he hoped to live, found a nation, and worship the one true God. Many years were to pass, and the descendants of Abraham were to wander, suffer, and struggle before his dream came true. 

Great leaders arose, such as Joseph, Moses, and others. They played their parts in the history of the race. But it was not until 

1025 B.C. that the Philistines were conquered and Saul became the first of the Hebrew kings.

Our Debt to the Hebrews. To the Hebrews we are indebted today for the Bible, the Ten Commandments, and the idea of one God. They, like the Phoenicians, were not powerful enough to withstand the successive invasions of the Assyrians and Chaldeans. In 538 B.C. many of them were still held captives in 

Babylon. But Cyrus the Great, after he had conquered Babylon, proved a friend of the Hebrews. He permitted them to return home and rebuild their city of Jerusalem, which had been destroyed by Nebuchadnezzar. This city will play an important part in the history of the days of Jesus of Nazareth, the Roman Empire, the Crusades, and the World War of 1914.

6. THE PERSIANS 

The Persians. The Persians over whom Cyrus ruled were an Indo-European people, the race from which the Greek and Romans as well as the present nations of western Europe and America have sprung. Previous to the conquest of Babylon, Persia had united with the Medes. They formed a powerful union whose kings ruled their people with an iron hand. One of the famous sayings which has come down to us today is: "The laws of the Medes and Persians do not change." 

Trouble arose between the two nations; the Persians conquered the Medes and then became masters of the Fertile Crescent. Cyrus the Great allowed the people under his control more freedom than was customary. He built a fine system of roads which connected all his provinces, and messengers carried information from one province to another. We shall meet the successors of Cyrus in later chapters when they come in contact with the Greeks. 

Zoroaster. When the Persians invaded the Tigris-Euphrates

Valley, they brought with them the doctrines of a religion which is still followed in some parts of the world. Zoroaster (zō-rô-ăs′tēr) was the founder of their religion, and their sacred book was the 

Zend-Avesta. Zoroaster taught that there were two great forces in the world, one good and the other evil. The good god, whose aiding spirit was light, they called Ahura-Mazda (ä'hoo-rȧmȧz'da). The evil god, whose aiding spirit was darkness, they called Ahriman (ä'ri-măn). There was continual strife between the two, but Zoroaster taught that eventually good would triumph.

Our Debt to the Ancient Oriental Civilization. - We have followed briefly the rise and fall of nations along the Fertile Crescent. We have noted their everyday life, their religions, laws, governments, customs, art, literature, and science. And we have emphasized their contributions to our modern civilization. We are better able to understand why some things in our life are as

they are. We now turn to a new land and a new people, whose accomplishments have often been referred to as the "glory that was Greece."

BACKGROUND THREE GREEK CIVILIZATION 

We leave the land of the Nile and the Fertile Crescent. The pyramids, the temples, the Hanging Gardens, the caravan trains, the moving armies, and the busy ports appear as mere specks, far below our lookout. 

The Egyptians, Babylonians, Assyrians, Hebrews, Phoenicians, and Persians are left behind. However, their literature, their science, their architecture, and their forms of government will be seen in other lands. In many instances these will be improved by the men of a new age.

We pass over the Mediterranean Sea, stop at the island of Crete (krēt), and then move on to Greece. 

In Crete we shall see the old Cretan sea kings, the palace of Cnossus (nos'us), and the beautiful art and sculpture of the workmen. We shall see how these Cretans adapted the civilization of the ancient Orient and passed it on to Greece. 

We shall see wandering tribes of Hellenes (hěl'enz) on the plains and in the valleys of Greece. They will overrun the country, conquer it, and mingle with the Cretan and Aegean (ê-jē'ăn) people. This new race the Greeks will organize their city-states. They will discard the idea of an absolute monarch. Instead they will establish democratic governments which will permit the people to assemble and to make their own laws.

The Greek sculptors and architects will be imitated down through the ages. The literature, the dramas, and the theaters of the Greeks will serve as models for succeeding generations, down to and including our own times. The work of Greek philosophers, historians, geographers, and scientists will live to influence modern civilization. Because the people of Greece lived, our life was to be and is more worth living. 71

CHAPTER VI  

Crete. The island of Crete is located in the eastern part of the Mediterranean Sea. It is about one hundred sixty miles long and at some places forty miles wide. Crete and the islands of the Aegean Sea formed the connecting link between the Greek civilization of Europe and that of Egypt and the 

Orient. 

Cretan Civilization. About 3000 B.C. Egyptian merchants began to trade with the people of Crete. A thousand years later the Cretans had developed a civilization that rivaled that of Egypt. They became skilled artisans and shrewd traders. 

Finally they entered the Delta of the Nile. 

The 

Egyptian noblemen preferred Cretan art to their own. vases of Crete were the most beautiful to be found in any market. 

The pictures of plants on their pottery seemed to wave with the wind. The carvings of human beings on their stone and metal jars stood out like objects in real life, and gold cups made by Cretan goldsmiths have never been excelled. Many of these articles have been found in Egyptian tombs and show in what high regard Cretan art was held by the dwellers along the Nile. 

Writing. At a very early date the Cretans used a form of picture-writing, which proved unsatisfactory as trade and commerce developed. Under Egyptian influence an entirely new system of writing was devised. The crude picture-symbols evolved into an alphabet, but no one as yet has been able to decipher and read this Cretan writing. Thousands of little clay tablets lie waiting to unfold to a modern world many things which we cannot explain now about these people of old Crete. 

The Cretan Sea Kings. - Cretan civilization was at its height about 1500 B.C. This is known as the "Grand Age of Crete.” 

Large galleys with oars and sails traveled back and forth between 72

Greece, Asia Minor, the Black Sea, and Sicily. They brought home tin, copper, silver, and gold. The Cretans exported many manufactured articles that appealed to the peoples of those distant lands. The Cretans of this period were truly called "Sea 

Kings." They developed a powerful navy. Piracy in the 

Mediterranean Sea was destroyed, and Cretan ships sailed unmolested in search of trade and commerce.

The Palace of Cnossus. - Commerce brought wealth to the ruling classes, and much of it was used to build beautiful palaces. 

The largest and most luxurious palace was located at Cnossus on the northern coast of Crete. This palace covered five acres and at first consisted of a few rooms around an open court. Later, many rooms were added, and as a result it acquired a very odd shape. The numerous halls which connected the buildings trailed in and out here and there in a very peculiar manner. 

The palace was divided into sections. One of these was given over to the goldsmiths who made the beautiful cups which were used at the royal banquets. Another section was allotted to the metal workers who made helmets and breastplates for the soldiers. In another portion of the palace potters made huge clay jars in which the king's grain was stored. 

Huge vases for oil and grain found in the palace of Cnossus. RA-6

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

There were special rooms for the queen and ladies of the court. 

In the official room of the king was located the oldest throne in Europe. Special rooms were set aside as sanctuaries or places of worship; in these many little gold and ivory snake goddesses were assembled. The whole series of buildings had a system of drainage and sanitation which was not equaled from that day to the nineteenth century.

The City of Cnossus. 

The city of Cnossus was located on Many paved roads led out from a hillside facing the plain. the city in all directions; one led to the harbor, another to an inland town. But the road that was used most led by the arena in the plain below. The palace had many roof gardens with receding terraces. These gardens formed excellent galleries from which the nobles and their ladies looked down upon the arena sports. 

The Sports of the Cretans. The bull was the sacred animal of the Cretans, and bull-leaping and boxing were their favorite sports. Young men and maidens entered the arena to meet the charging bull. They were without weapons and watched for a chance to seize the bull by the horns, turn a somersault over his back, and leap to the ground in the rear. It is generally believed that these young people were forced to take part in this dangerous sport. They were probably captives taken as tribute from a conquered city-state. 

When captives were brought to the city, a holiday was declared. The king invited the rich nobles from their country estates to enjoy the spectacle from the palace terraces. The freemen left their workshops, and masters allowed their slaves to join the holiday crowd. The people of the poorer classes crowded one another in order to find a place from which they could see well. The results and incidents of the brutal bullleaping remained a topic of conversation until the sea king's return with a new group of unfortunates from a foreign land. 

Colonies: Mycenae and Tiryns. - The Cretans extended their civilization first to the many small islands in the Aegean Sea. In

this manner, step by step, their influence reached the mainland of Greece. The remains of two cities in Greece - Mycenae (mi-se'ne) and Tiryns - show evidence of Cretan and Egyptian civilization. These cities had paved roads and luxurious palaces. The latter were divided into sections like the palace of 

Cnossus, and tradesmen occupied quarters according to their profession. Bath rooms with a modern drainage system were used. 

The cities were surrounded by high stone walls for protection against savage tribes. The remains of their art, literature, and religion show traces of Cretan influence. 

Legendary Crete. The early history of Crete is hidden in legend and myth. Just where legend ends and history begins is difficult to say. We think of a legend as a beautiful story based on fancy or imagination, a story which tries to explain something that early peoples wanted to know. Today, however, we realize that many of these legends contain a few facts, and that, as the years went by, they had been exaggerated and added to by ancient peoples. The following is a legendary story of Crete. you read it, see if you can recognize any historical facts that have been woven into the tale. As 

Minos. Minos (mi'nos) was a legendary king of Crete. He was supposed to have been the son of the god of gods, Zeus. 

Crete, under the rule of Minos, became very powerful. This king has been called the "Cretan Moses" by some writers. It was told that he went high up into the mountains, where he met his father, Zeus, in a cave. Many laws, which he later used to govern his subjects wisely and well, were given to him on that occasion.

The Story of Theseus and the Minotaur. - Sometimes Minos is pictured as being very cruel and revengeful. A legendary story called Theseus (thē'sūs) and the Minotaur (min'ô-tôr) brought out this side of his character. Theseus was the son of 

Aegeus, king of ancient Athens. He underwent many hardships and performed many daring deeds in order to be recognized by his father as a worthy son and heir.

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

Now it happened that, a few years before the time of Theseus, a son of King Minos came to Athens to take part in an athletic carnival. His ability was so great that he easily won the distinction of being the best athlete among all the contestants.

King Aegeus, jealous of his laurels, sent him against the fire-spitting bull of Marathon. The son of King Minos was killed, and Minos, in order to avenge his death, laid siege to Athens. He was unable to capture the city, so he called upon Zeus for aid. Zeus sent disease and starvation to

Athens. The Athenians called on the oracles for advice. 

They were told to submit to the terms of King Minos. This meant that every ninth year Athens had to send to King Minos seven young men and seven young women from the most influential families as tribute. 

The Labyrinth of Daedalus. Now let us go to the island of 

Crete and the palace of Cnossus. The king had ordered his engineer, Daedalus (děd'à-lus), to construct underneath the palace a perfect maze called a labyrinth. Once a person entered this labyrinth, he became so confused with its many winding tunnels that he could never find his way out again. It was certain death to the person who entered. The maze was constructed, also, as a stable for a horrible creature called the Minotaur. This monster had the body of a giant and the head of a furious bull. This was the place where the youths of Athens gave up their lives every ninth year to satisfy the revenge of the 

Cretan king. 

Theseus Volunteers. The Athenians knew the fate that awaited their children. And there was much sorrow on a particular spring day when the time came to pick the hostages. 

Theseus volunteered to make the journey. King Aegeus reluctantly gave his consent, for he was very fond of his son. Theseus knew that it would be difficult to escape from the labyrinth, so he prayed to the sea god, Neptune, for help. The oracle at 

Delphi in Greece foretold that Aphrodite (ǎf-rô-di'tė), the goddess of love, would rescue him.

Ariadne Aids Theseus. The fourteen youths landed in 

Crete. They marched from the harbor by the palace and towards the jail. Aphrodite had ordained that King Minos and his daughter Ariadne (ǎr-i-ǎd'nē) watch them pass. The young princess saw Theseus and fell in love with him at once. She determined to save him. In the morning she visited the jail and asked to see the Athenian prisoners. The jailor did not notice that she carried a short sword and a ball of twine. 

These she gave to Theseus. 

The Athenian Youths Escape. The Athenian captives entered the labyrinth at noon to meet their doom. Theseus carried the twine and the sword under his cloak. He secretly tied one end of the twine to the mouth of the cave, and allowed it to unravel as they wandered deeply into the dark, winding passageways. Far within the tunnel they heard a roaring noise. 

Two bloodshot eyes like balls of fire pierced the darkness. In a fierce struggle Theseus first broke the Minotaur's leg and then pierced his breast. The captives retraced their steps by following the string. Ariadne met them at the mouth of the tunnel. 

All escaped in the boat in which they had come. 

Theseus Deserts Ariadne. Theseus was not loyal to the princess to whom he owed his life. The Athenians stopped at the island of Naxos (năk'sos) which was sacred to Dionysus (di-o-ni'sus), the god of wine. Here Theseus was told in a dream that his princess was to marry a god. He deserted her and sailed away with his companions. Ariadne sorrowed much for Theseus, but later married Dionysus. 

The Death of Aegeus. On the way home Theseus anchored his ship at the island of Delos and offered a sacrifice to Apollo (the sun god) in gratitude for his escape. But tragedy still remained.

When they had left Athens for Crete on their sorrowful journey, their boat had a black sail. King Aegeus doubted that he would see his son again, although Theseus assured him that he would soon return. The father told his son that in case of success he

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

was to change his black sail to a white one. While Theseus was gone, the king went daily to a high cliff and watched for the return of the boat. But the returning party, happy and eager to be on their way home, forgot to change sails. The old king spied the black sail far out on the blue sea. He thought his son was dead and jumped into the sea to drown. From that time on this body of water was called the Aegean Sea after King Aegeus of Athens.

King Theseus. Theseus returned to Athens and ruled in place of his father. He was a very good king. He united all the tribes of Attica and gave them a government elected by themselves instead of the old monarchy. He disappeared from 

Athens after doing this. Some said that he, like King Aegeus, drowned himself in the Aegean Sea. 

The Ship to Delos. The ship of Theseus was preserved, it is said, for hundreds of years. Every year it was sent to Delos with sacrifices for Apollo, a journey which took thirty days. 

During the absence of this ship no public act of any kind could take place. A thousand years later, during the authentic history of Greece, this custom was still in use. The great philosopher and teacher, Socrates (sõk'ra-tēz), of whom we shall learn more in later chapters, had his fate postponed until the return of the ship from Delos. 

Cretan Influence on Greece. This story of Theseus and the 

Minotaur is interwoven and fused with Cretan and Greek life. 

King Minos, the labyrinth, the sports, and the power of the island monarch are Cretan. King Aegeus, Athens, the gods and goddesses, the oracle, and the ship to Delos are Greek. As Dr. 

Durant says: "If you examine a map of Europe, you will see that

Greece is a skeleton-like hand, stretching its crooked fingers out into the Mediterranean Sea. South of it lies the great island of Crete from which those grasping fingers captured 2,000 years before Christ the beginnings of civilization and culture." 

Geography of Greece. - Greece is a small country about the size of the state of Virginia. Its bays are deeply indented. Its

rivers are not navigable, because they are short and swift. Its mountains are numerous and cross each other in an irregular manner. Some end abruptly at the sea. Because of these things, travel was very difficult, and people were encouraged to settle in small independent communities. 

Its climate is warm.

The summers are long with a beautiful blue sky and sunshine every day. The winters are short with a medium amount of rain. Frost and snow occur very seldom in the valley plain. In this subtropical climate, made so by the influence of the Mediterranean Sea, food, clothing, and shelter were not so difficult to secure as in colder climates. Indo-Europeans.

About the year 2000 B.C. there was a general movement of nomadic tribes around the regions of the Black and Caspian seas. They were members of the white race and are called Indo-Europeans. One of these groups, as we have noticed in Chapter V, moved down the left side of the Caspian Sea and settled north of the Persian Gulf. These were the 

Medes and the Persians. They brought to an end the Chaldean 

Empire of Belshazzar and upon its ruins developed the powerful 

Persian Empire.

The Hellenes. - Another group of these Indo-Europeans called Hellenes (after Hellen, the founder of their race) moved down the west side of the Black Sea. These shepherd tribes crossed the high Cambunian Mountains which separated Greece from the remainder of Europe. They passed by the base of 

Mount Olympus and entered the rich grasslands of Thessaly. 

Here they stopped to graze their herds and flocks. They had tamed the wild horse and used him to pull a wooden cart. As time passed, they moved southward, often camping outside of the walled towns which they discovered on their journeys. Here they met the civilized Aegeans. Wars resulted between the rude 

Hellenic invaders and the people of the walled towns. Although greatly outnumbered, the barbarian tribes had the advantage both in physical strength and methods of warfare.

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

The Dorians. From 1500 to 1200 B.C. more and more of these nomadic tribes came down from the north. All of the mainland of Greece fell under their control. The great walled cities of Mycenae and Tiryns with their rich Aegean civilization were captured. The Dorians were the most warlike of these many tribes. They conquered many of the islands in the Aegean 

Sea and passed over into Asia Minor. The civilization of Crete was destroyed almost beyond recognition. 

The Escape of the Philistines. - A group of wealthy settlers in Crete made their escape and settled in Palestine, south of Phoenicia. There we met them in Hebrew history as the Philistines and witnessed their overthrow by Saul, the first Hebrew king (Chapter V). 

The Greeks. During these years of migration and conquest the Hellenes intermarried with the Aegeans. A new people 

They were alert known as the Greeks emerged from this union. and gifted. The Aegean world became a Greek world. 

Phoenician Influence on the Early Greeks. With the down- 

Phoenicians had 

The Greeks befall of the Egyptian and Aegean power, the the Mediterranean commerce to themselves. came the pupils of the Phoenicians. They learned how to make ประ 

Prow of a Greek vessel. From a bas-relief. tools and weapons from the iron which the Phoenicians brought them from the Black Sea region. The Greek shepherd took off his goatskin and put on the chiton of the Orient.

This was a loose woolen cloak which extended to the knees and was gathered at the waist with a cord. 

The Greeks learned how to build boats from the Phoenicians and became sailors, traders, and merchants. The boat developed by the Greeks came to be called a trireme, because it had three banks of oars. There were fifty oars in each bank or deck. 

Therefore the boat was propelled with the power of one hundred fifty oarsmen. The Cretan alphabet had been destroyed, so the Greeks copied the twenty-two-letter alphabet of the Phoenicians and improved it. A new and far-reaching civilization was started. 

Greek Legends and Myths. - During the many years that the 

Hellenes and early Greeks were without an alphabet and a system of writing, marvelous stories about their heroes and gods were told. These stories, like those of Crete and other early people, were partly true. But as they were handed down from generation to generation around the family fireside, they became greatly exaggerated. The Greek believed these legends and myths. 

They influenced his life greatly and were a part of his education and religious beliefs. Therefore some knowledge of the prehistoric literature of the Greeks is necessary if we are to understand and appreciate their life and thoughts during historic times.

Heracles, the Greek National Hero. The story of Heracles or Hercules (hûr'kû-lēz) is modeled after the old Babylonian epic which tells about Gilgamesh (Chapter V). That story had been brought to the Aegean world by groups of traders from the Orient. Although many changes were made, like Gilgamesh, the Greeks assigned twelve gigantic labors to their great national hero to perform.

The Story of Heracles. — Heracles's tasks began while he was very young. Hera, the wife of Zeus, was jealous of his mother, so she sent two large serpents to destroy him. Heracles killed them with ease. Later Zeus sent Heracles to the king of 

Mycenae who became the enemy of the young giant. He was

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

compelled to remain with the king for twelve years and perform any service demanded of him. 

The Tasks of Heracles. The cowardly king assigned him what he thought were twelve impossible tasks in the hope that he would be killed in their performance. Heracles, however, conquered all forms of man and beast. For these great successes the gods made him immortal and gave him a place in their circle.

Bards. It was the custom in those early days to be entertained and amused by people who could recite or sing while strumming the lyre, an instrument like a harp. A person so gifted was called a "bard." Many poems were recited and sung from memory by those ancient poet-singers. Eventually only those poems survived that expressed the feelings of the people and aroused the imagination. The most famous bards traveled from banquet hall to banquet hall and from city to city. 

Others were content to amuse the crowds that collected on the streets.

Homer. About the year 1000 B.C. the greatest of all the bards wandered throughout the Aegean world. His name was 

Homer. Besides being the most famous of the poet singers, he is believed to be the author of two of the greatest epic poems ever written in any language. These poems are the Iliad (îl′ĭ-ăd) and the Odyssey (ŏd'i-si). They were called lyrics because they were adapted for singing to the accompaniment of a lyre. The Iliad is the story of the Trojan War. The Odyssey relates the adventures of the return journey of Odysseus and his friends after the close of the Trojan War.

The Iliad. The following is a brief account of the Iliad. 

The Trojan War was fought between the heroes of Greece and the heroes of Troy. The legendary date is from 1194 to 

1184 B.C.

The Quarrel of the Goddesses. Once upon a time there were three goddesses. Hera, whom the Romans called Juno, was the wife and sister of Zeus. Athena, whom the Romans called

Minerva, was the goddess of wisdom. Aphrodite, known to the 

Romans as Venus, was the goddess of beauty. 

In a contest held to determine who was the most beautiful goddess, it was impossible to decide among them. They went to the island of Crete and called upon Paris, a shepherd boy living on Mount Ida. Paris was the son of Priam, king of Troy. He had been exiled to Crete because his mother had dreamed that Troy would be burned to the ground if he remained in the city. 

Paris decided in favor of 

Aphrodite (Venus), who had promised him as a re- 

Iward the love of the most beautiful woman in the world. That woman was 

Helen, Zeus's daughter. 

Paris Returns to 

Troy. Time passed, and 

Paris returned to Troy and took part in an athletic carnival. His ability as an athlete was recognized and admired by his father, King Priam.

He allowed his son to remain in Troy. But Paris no sooner became settled than he desired the reward promised him by Venus. 

Statue of Paris in the Vatican, Rome. 

What does it symbolize?

Paris and Helen of Sparta. - Paris sailed from Troy and within a few days arrived in Sparta. He became the guest of 

King Menelaus (men-ê-la'us). While the king was absent, Paris and Helen fell in love, just as Venus had planned. The two eloped and sailed across the Aegean Sea to the home of King 

Priam in Troy.

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

Trojan warriors. Taken from a sculptural relief.

The Greek Heroes Rally to the Cause of Menelaus. The native kings of the Greek city-states had banded themselves together in a loose league. When the news came that Helen had been stolen, they rallied to help Menelaus avenge the insult. 

Among the leaders of the expedition was Agamemnon (ǎg-àměm'non), king of Mycenae and a brother of the Spartan king. 

There was Achilles (a-kil'ēz), the son of a sea nymph. His mother had dipped him, when an infant, in the River Styx. 

This was a river of magic waters, and any one who bathed in it could not be wounded. She had held him by the heel, and none of the magic water had touched it. Thus it was his only weak spot. Other heroes were Odysseus, one of the wisest and shrewdest of men. Ajax, a giant warrior, Diomed (dī'ô-mēd), Nestor, and many more brave men joined the Greek forces. 

The Trojan War. At last a fleet of almost twelve hundred ships was ready to sail for Troy. It had taken ten years to equip it. After days of sailing and many hardships, the Greeks were in a position to besiege the ancient city of the Trojans. The siege lasted for ten years, and many deeds of valor were performed by the heroes of Greece and by the heroes of Troy. 

Achilles was one of the most outstanding heroes of the Greeks and was in every fight.

Hector, another son of King Priam, was the hero of the Trojans. He led the troops of his father and forced the Greeks back

to their ships. Achilles, who had been sulking in his tent because 

King Agamemnon had taken from him a beautiful girl whom he had captured, took the field again to avenge the death of a close friend. Achilles killed Hector. He fastened the corpse of the 

Trojan hero to his own chariot and dragged it around the city walls. Achilles was wounded in the heel later and died.

The End of the Conflict. 

The war came to an end as a result of a shrewd plan devised by Odys seus. The Greek heroes hid within a large wooden horse which they had built. 

They burned their tents, and many departed in their ships.. 

The Trojans believed that they had left for home and had constructed the wooden horse as an offering to the gods. They pulled the wooden horse into the city. During the night a Greek spy who had been in the camp of the Trojans opened a trapdoor in the wooden horse. The Greeks emerged from their hiding place, overcame the guards, and took possession of the city. The 

Greek fleet returned, and the city was burned to the ground. 

Helen returned home with Menelaus, and the long war was ended. 

Laocoön, a priest of Troy, warned the Trojans not to pull the wooden horse into their city. Because of this two serpents crushed him and his two sons to death. (In the Vatican, Rome.)

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

Greek Gods and Goddesses; Zeus; the Olympian Council. —

The gods and goddesses mentioned in the stories of Theseus and the Minotaur and in the Iliad were only a few of the many Greek deities. When the Hellenic tribes left their northern grasslands they brought with them their great sky-god Zeus. He was the father of all the gods. He caused storms and hurled lightning 

Zeus. (Vatican, Rome.) bolts accompanied by rolls of thunder. He had a mighty throne on the snow-capped peak of Mount Olympus and was surrounded by six gods and six goddesses, who made up the Olympian Council. Each had his or her own duties connected with the life of mortals or natural forces in the universe. 

Apollo. - Apollo was the sun god who, it was thought, shot beams of light like golden arrows to ward off darkness and evil. He watched over the plowman and the herdsman. He was the Greek ideal of beauty and controlled music and poetry, but above all he was the god of prophecy. The temple of Apollo at the world-famed oracle in Delphi was consulted because of the faith in his prophecy. 

Hermes (Mercury).- Hermes (hûr'mez), who corresponded to the Latin god Mercury, was the winged-footed messenger of the gods. He was given a pair of golden sandals to speed him on his errands. Everything connected with trade and commerce was believed by the Greeks to be under his control. 

Poseidon (Neptune).- Poseidon (po-si'don), a brother of Zeus, ruled the sea. He was pictured as driving his brazen-hoofed horses over the stormy waters while standing in his chariot with a trident or three-pronged spear in his hand. The sea was supposed to have calmed when his horses galloped over the waves. 

Hephaestus (Vulcan).- Hephaestus (he-fěs'tus) was the god

of fire. He made armor and weapons of warfare and forged thunderbolts for Zeus. He had a forge under Mt. Aetna. He built every god a palace of shining brass on Mt. Olympus. Although he was lame and the ugliest of the gods, Zeus amused the gods by forcing Aphrodite (Venus) to marry him. They had a son named Eros (Cupid), who shot arrows which made people fall in love. 

De- 

Demeter (Ceres). meter (dé-mē'ter), the old 

Aegean earth-mother, was adopted by the Greeks. She was the goddess of the harvest and made the grains, the fruits, and the flowers grow. She was aided by thousands of beautiful maidens called nymphs. It was these water nymphs, the Greeks believed, who made the springs bubble and the brooks run.

The Graces. The three graces were sister goddesses who made people graceful and lovely. They taught and helped boys and girls to be friendly and pleasant. 

Greek goddess of victory. This famous statue was sculptured about the fourth century B.C. and is known for its grace and beauty. It was found on the island of Samothrace in 1863 by some French students. It is called the Winged Victory of Samothrace and is now in the Louvre in Paris. 

The Muses. The nine sister goddesses of poetry, arts, and sciences were called the muses. While the gods on Mt. Olympus feasted on ambrosia and drank nectar, Apollo played the lyre and the muses sang. 

Our word "music" is derived from muses.

The Furies. The three sister goddesses called the Furies were 

draped in black. Their heads were encircled with serpents.

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

They punished people who committed wicked deeds, sometimes following them to the doors of the underworld. 

The Fates. The three sister Fates were higher than all the gods and goddesses. Whatever they planned came to pass. 

One Fate spun the thread of life. Another decided how long the thread of life should be. And the third cut the thread at the end of life. 

Crossing the River Styx. -The old Greeks buried their dead with a coin in the mouth of the corpse and a barley cake sweetened with honey in its hand. Before it could get into Hades, which was the realm of departed spirits, it had to cross the black River Styx (stiks). The crossing was made in a ferry boat. A silent boatman named Charon rowed the boat. The spirit gave the coin to him in payment for his services. Charon brought the spirit to the gate of Hades, which was guarded by a very cross dog called Kerberos (kûr'bê-ros). The spirit threw the honeysweetened cake to the dog, whose attention was thereby diverted, and entered the land of departed ones unnoticed. 

Such in brief is the early story of the Greeks, of their country, of their migrations, of their everyday life and religious beliefs. 

We pass now to the story of the Greek city-state.

CHAPTER VII  

The City-State. The Greek city-state played a very important part in the history of Greece. Individual tribes of the early 

Indo-European invaders settled in the numerous valleys of the country. Communication with their neighbors was very difficult because of the high mountain ranges which separated them. 

As time passed, these small tribal communities grew in population. Their trade and industry developed. The inhabitants inclosed the towns with strong walls to keep out savage tribes and wild animals. Later, when conditions became more settled, people ventured beyond the city walls and established small villages and hamlets. These outlying settlements were governed by the mother city, and in times of danger their inhabitants sought refuge behind the city walls. Thus the city-state developed. It

The Government of a City-State. The city-state was supreme in itself. It owed allegiance to no other Greek government. made its own laws and supported its own army. If it happened to be a seaport town, it had both an army and a navy. city-state sent and received ambassadors from other city-states and foreign nations.

The Citizens of a City-State. 

Each

The citizens of any city-state, as a rule, were composed of a small group whose ancestors were founders of the city. They made the laws, elected the officers, and conducted the general business of the government. They were an intensely patriotic and (for themselves mostly) a libertyloving people. The worst disgrace that could happen to a citizen was to be banished from his city, because in another community he was classed as a foreigner and not far above the slaves. 

Sparta. Two of the leading city-states of ancient Greece were Sparta and Athens. Sparta was situated in the valley of the Eurotas River. It was the most important city in the 90

province of Laconia (lå-kō'ní-ȧ), in southern Greece. It was surrounded on the west, north, and east by high mountains with a few narrow passes. Because of these Sparta did not need a stone wall for protection. Nature had provided higher walls than the Spartans could build. Spartan training provided strong and brave soldiers to guard the few mountain passes against invaders. The Spartans were proud of the fact that they had no city walls and boastfully stated: "the walls of 

Sparta are the chests of her soldiers.' 

Classes of People in Sparta: the Spartans. — There were three classes of people living in Sparta. They were the Spartans, the Perioeci (pĕr-i-e'sī), and the Helots (hěl'ots). The Spartans were the rulers of the state. They were descendants of the 

Dorians, who, you will recall, had much to do with the overthrow of the Aegean civilization. The Spartans came to Laconia as conquerors and remained as oppressors. They controlled the entire Peloponnesus (pěl-ô-po-ne'sus), or the peninsula of Pelops, for several centuries. At the height of their power they only numbered about 9000 or about one tenth of the entire population of Sparta. Every Spartan was trained to be a soldier, and he devoted his life to the service of the state. 

The Perioeci. - The Perioeci, meaning "dwellers around, were free inhabitants who lived in the communities surrounding Sparta. They were the descendants of the Aegeans who had inhabited the regions before the Dorian invasions. They were now the merchants, traders, skilled workmen, and sailors who carried on business with other countries. These people were allowed to retain their homes and land upon the payment of an annual (yearly) tribute. The Spartans compelled them to furnish a certain number of soldiers for all military expeditions. The 

Perioeci traveled freely from place to place, but they were not citizens and had no share in the government. 

The Helots.-The Helots were serfs and therefore belonged to the despised class. They comprised the majority of the Spartan population. They were captives of war who formerly had lived

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

in prosperous communities. Many of them had been leaders in their own city-states. "Helots" was the name applied to all of these people, because the city of Helos in southern Greece held out to the last against Spartan oppression. The Helots planted the crops, gathered the harvest, and did all the menial work for their proud and cruel masters - the Spartans. 

Lycurgus, the Lawgiver of Sparta. - Lycurgus (li-kûr'gus), the lawgiver of Sparta, is supposed to have lived about the ninth century B.C.

Several different stories are told of his life and work. For this reason it is difficult to tell which is history and which is legend. According to one account he left Sparta and traveled in many lands. He talked with the rulers and studied the laws of the countries he visited. He was told by the Delphic oracle that some day he would give the Spartans the best laws in the world. When he returned home, Sparta was in a turmoil and greatly disorganized. The citizens wanted 

Lycurgus to be their king, but he preferred to rule only until the Spartans could rule for themselves. 

The Laws of Lycurgus. The following laws were laid down by Lycurgus. Iron was to be used as money instead of gold and silver. This was to prevent a rich class of people from developing. He ordered that all houses were to be made of rough logs, hewn by an ax. This was to prevent people from building beautiful mansions. All the people were ordered to eat simple foods at a common table and to wear a small amount of very plain clothing. Every one was compelled to take physical exercise in order to make him strong and healthy and therefore better able to defend Sparta. Lycurgus received a solemn promise from the 

Spartans that they would obey his laws and constitution unti he returned. He then left Sparta forever, thus holding th people to their vow.

Spartan Government: the Kings. - Sparta was governed by a small, privileged class of nobles who were the chief persons i the state. Such a government is called an "aristocracy." Th 

Spartans had two kings, each of whom belonged to a differen

royal family. These kings had equal authority and held office for life. But they possessed very little real power because their authority was divided and limited. 

The Senate. The senate or Council of Elders was composed of thirty members. Twenty-eight of these were elected by the assembly. The two kings were members because of their official position. A Spartan had to be sixty years of age before he was eligible for the senate. This body prepared bills which were brought before the assembly for passage. 

The Assembly. The assembly was composed of all the soldier-citizens over thirty years of age. This body was called together every month to discuss and vote on the bills proposed by the senate. It decided all policies relating to peace and war. 

The Ephors. -The assembly elected five ephors, or overseers. 

It was the duty of the ephors to see that the laws and other affairs of state were carried out. They followed the armies in the field. 

Their authority was so great that they could alter the command of either king to his soldiers or reverse the decision of a judge in the courts. It is said that the ephors, by a word or nod of the head, could change the manners and customs of the people. 

Spartan Education: Its Purpose. The Spartans believed that the education of their youth should be carefully supervised in order to maintain the army which was so necessary for the existence of the state. A strong army, they said, "depended upon strong soldiers."

Therefore, as soon as a child was born, the Council of Elders decided whether it was healthy enough to grow up and serve the state. If the infant was believed unhealthy, it was taken to a near-by mountain and left to die. 

The Education of Spartan Boys. When a boy was seven years old, he was taken from his mother and placed in the care of the state. He lived in a barrack with boys of his own age. 

Here he started a very difficult and severe course of training. 

He had to wear thin clothing in both summer and winter. His bed was made of reeds, which he gathered with his own hands along the banks of the Eurotas. In winter he was allowed to add

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

a little soft down for warmth. He helped to prepare his own meals, which he ate with his comrades around a common table. 

He was taught to steal, but if he was caught doing so, he was flogged. This was to teach him to be crafty, a trait which the 

Spartans deemed necessary for war. He was taught to endure pain without emotion, because this was a Spartan virtue. 

Contests were held to see who could receive the most severe whipping without crying or uttering a sound. Many times boys actually died rather than give way to their feelings before 

The Greeks were great runners. 

Their welldeveloped bodies were only acquired after long, careful training. This race was found painted on a vase. their comrades. In order to develop his physical body so that he would become strong and alert, the boy was trained in gymnastic exercises. He was taught running, jumping, and wrestling. 

He learned how to hurl the javelin and throw the discus. The Spartan was not interested in art and science. These were not considered necessary to preserve the state. He learned reading, writing, and arithmetic. In literature the boys were taught poems that would inspire them to excel in battle. The 

Spartans were not orators. They disliked any one who did much talking. So the Spartan boys were taught to be attentive to questions and to give short answers. This method of making statements and answering questions with a few words was typical of the Spartans and is called even today "laconic." The name is derived from Laconia, the home of the Spartans. 

The Education of Spartan Girls. The Spartan girls were permitted to remain at home with a medium amount of state supervision. They had to take physical exercises such as gymnastics, running, jumping, and javelin throwing. The Spartan state wanted to produce strong and healthy mothers. The girls

were taught to look with favor upon heroes and to condemn cowardice. They were told that no sacrifice was too great for the state and that such an action was the noblest form of patriotism. 

The following short or laconic sentence expressed the attitude of Spartan girls and Spartan mothers: "Come back with your shield or on it." A story is told of a Spartan mother who hastened to a group of returning soldiers and asked: "Who won?" When she was told that all five of her sons were killed, she exclaimed: "That is not what I wish to know. Did Sparta win?" "Yes," was the answer. "Then let us give thanks to the gods," she replied. 

Spartan Soldiers. — A Spartan soldier became a member of the assembly at thirty years of age. About this time in life he married. His family lived on a small farm which was owned by the state. He, himself, spent very little time at home. His farm was cultivated for him by Helots. The Spartan was at all times a soldier. When he was not in battle or on a campaign, he spent his time keeping physically fit, attending drill, or caring for matters of state. He was a warrior until he was sixty years old, when he retired and was eligible for the Council of Elders. 

The Phalanx. - The Spartan battle formation was known as the phalanx. It was composed of a compact body of troops, standing shoulder to shoulder, with lines eight to sixteen deep. 

Before entering a battle, the kings would sacrifice a goat to the gods. The soldiers, keeping step with the music of a flute, rushed into battle singing their war songs. Every soldier was heavily armed. 

Athens and the Acropolis. As Spartan history becomes woven into that of Greece, the facts you have just read will make the story easier to understand. We now turn to that other outstanding Greek city-state, Athens. It was located about one hundred fifty miles from Sparta, in the province of Attica. It was surrounded by high mountains on the north and east and was extremely difficult to invade from those two directions. The best opportunity for invasion was from the south by sea.

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

Athens was located on a plain at the base of a high hill called the Acropolis (a-krop'ô-lis). On this hill were all the government buildings and the temples which had been dedicated to the gods. The city received its name from its patron goddess of wisdom, Athena (Minerva). The Athenian city-state controlled all of Attica.

Athenian Government: the Archons. When Athens emerged from the legendary period, it had a king, a council of elders, and an assembly. Shortly after this period the king was overthrown by the nobles. As a result the government fell into the hands of the nobility. They elected a noble to be the ruler for life. He was called an "archon," which means ruler. (Some of our words today, such as "monarch" and "patriarch," are derived from "archon.") The archons, eventually, so abused the power intrusted to them that their term of office was limited to ten years. It was later reduced to one year, and finally nine archons were elected to rule for a one-year term. 

The Council and the Assembly. — The ex-archons formed a council which advised the archons in office concerning public affairs. The assembly consisted of all citizens who served in the heavily armed forces of the state.

Classes of People in Athens: Nobles, Freemen, and Slaves. 

As in Sparta the classes of people in Athens were the nobles, the freemen, and the slaves. The nobles, besides being the ruling class, were the wealthy landowners. To possess a large amount of land was a mark of aristocracy in Athens. The freemen were the merchants, traders, bankers, and artisans. Most of them lived on the nobles' property and were forced to pay high rents. 

In Athens the supply of slaves was always large. They were brought by slave merchants from Asia Minor and the Black Sea.

Many of them were captives of war, while some were mere children who had been sold into slavery by their poverty-stricken parents. Some were women; others were young boys who had been kidnaped on the streets of foreign cities. Some were people who had been sold into slavery for debts owed the nobility.
