A slave, or pedagogue, accompanied the Greek boys to school. Notice that the boy is facing his teacher, who is holding a book. The pedagogue is in back of the boy. (Taken from an old Greek vase painting.)

Intelligent slaves were often used as tutors for the Athenian children. They accompanied the children to and from the formal schools and were called "pedagogues." Slaves were used in the household, in the shops, on the merchant ships, and on the farms. The slaves at one time outnumbered the Athenian citizens ten to one.

A Famous Slave of Athens: Aesop (about 620 B.C.-560 B.C.).— 

About 600 B.C. a small boy was sold as a slave in Athens. He was to become one of the most renowned story-tellers in world literature. Little Aesop (e'sop) was brought to Athens, where he was to be sold in the market place as a slave. But Aesop did not appear to mind. He seemed more interested in the beautiful sights of Athens than in the fact that he would soon be sold to a strange master. He was clever and witty, and everything interested or amused him. He was sold. 

Time passed, and he grew to be a young man. He was then sold to a merchant who had many slaves. They were all about looking at the large to start on a journey. The slaves stood bundles of clothing and the heavy bales of provisions. Each one

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

was trying to figure out an easy load to carry. Aesop chose a heavy bale of bread, while his fellow slaves fought for the lighter bundles of clothing. They all laughed at the fool Aesop. 

Aesop's Load. After journeying many miles, time was announced for the noonday meal. "Aesop," cried the master, "distribute half the bread." Aesop obeyed. His load was therefore lightened by one half, while the loads of his companions seemed heavier than ever. Supper time came. "Aesop," cried the master, "distribute the remainder of the bread." Now where was Aesop's load? The other slaves saw that the joke was on them and laughed in spite of their heavy burdens. 

Aesop Purchases His Freedom; the Ambassador of King Croesus. Later Aesop bought his freedom from his master. then traveled throughout the world telling clever stories, all of which contained a fine moral. One day he arrived at the court of Croesus (krē'sus), the king of Lydia. That wealthy monarch became very much attached to Aesop and invited him to remain at his court. 

He

The Death of Aesop at Delphi. Aesop became a highly trusted ambassador of the Lydian ruler. One day he was sent to 

Delphi to pay a debt which the Delphians claimed was owed by 

Croesus to the temple of Apollo.

Aesop realized that his king did not owe this money, and he was courageous enough to tell the priests of Delphi what he thought of them. As a result the people mobbed him and cried: "Thou hast offended the god Apollo, whose temple is here at Delphi." This picture of Aesop was found painted on an old Athenian vase.

He was hurled from a near-by precipice. 

Aesop's Fables; Honored by Athens.- Books today contain many fables by this ancient author. Yet some stories credited to him are of doubtful origin because they were not written down

until two centuries after Aesop's death. His fables have come down through the ages and are enjoyed today by every people who possesses a written language. The most famous are: The 

Wolf and the Lamb; The Fox and the Raven; The Frogs Ask for a King; and The Body and the Members. The last fable, which is told in Chapter XI, played a very important part in early Roman history. The Athenians came to think so highly of Aesop's work that they erected a statue of him on the Acropolis. Thus they honored one who had been bought and sold in the slave markets of their famous city.

Draco and the Draconian Laws. - About the time of Aesop political and social conditions in Athens were very bad. The nobles or aristocrats were becoming more wealthy, while the lower classes were growing poorer and poorer. There were no written laws, and the ruling class took advantage of this fact. 

The people could see no advantage in deposing the king and substituting the harsh rule of the archons. They threatened to rebel, and finally the nobles selected Draco (dra'kō), an archon at the time, to write down the laws. 

Draco did this, but when the laws were published the people realized how cruel and unjust they were. The death penalty was inflicted for many offenses. Even a minor theft brought this punishment. The people claimed that they had been betrayed, and said: "These laws were written in blood, we thought they would be written in ink." So severe were the laws of Draco that today the word "draconic " is used to mean harsh. 

Solon, the Great Lawgiver of Athens (638-558 B.C.). — When the people were on the verge of a revolution, a remarkable man came to their rescue. His name was Solon (sõ'lon), and he soon brought about many desired changes. Although he was a poor man, he was of noble birth. He was considered one of the 

66 seven wise men of Greece."

Reforms of Solon. Solon's laws declared that all those people who had been sold into slavery for debt were free. Persons who had been exiled for the same reason were allowed to

return. The peasants were permitted to own a certain amount of land and to cultivate it without interference. The sale of Athenian women and children was forbidden. Every father was forced to teach his son a trade in order to assure Athens of useful citizens. 

Besides making these reforms, Solon reorganized the government. 

Solon Started Athens on the Road to Democracy. He made the assembly more democratic (ruled by the people) by permitting nearly all men to take part in its meetings and to help elect officials. He changed the court system and allowed every man the privilege of serving on the jury. The old council, whose members were elected because of wealth, was replaced by one which was chosen by lot. This gave the poor man as well as the rich landowner a chance to have a voice in this important body. Solon did not dispose of aristocracy altogether, but he started his people on the road to Athenian democracy. He made the people take an oath that they would obey the constitution for ten years. He then resigned as archon and traveled throughout the Aegean world.

Pisistratus, the Good Tyrant. The revolution which Solon had averted finally did occur. In the class struggle that followed, Pisistratus (pi-sis'tra-tŭs) took up the cause of the common people. In 561 B.C. he overthrew the government and made himself ruler. In those days a person who did this was called a tyrant. Pisistratus governed for more than thirty years. loaned money to the poor, appointed honest judges to office, erected public buildings, built better roads, and encouraged art and commerce. He made the first collection of Homer's poems, started a library, and invited poets and authors to Athens. The country prospered under the rule of this good tyrant. But the word "tyrant" was made hateful forever by the oppression and cruelty of his two sons who followed him. 

Education in Athens: the Athenian Oath. The education of the Athenian youth was not so severe as that of the Spartan boy. 

He entered school at the age of seven, and attended classes from sun up to sun down. He was trained for war, but his training was

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

not so severe as that of his Spartan neighbor. The Athenians laid more stress on music, art, and science. 

When he was eighteen years old, the Athenian boy completed his education, and his name was enrolled in the register of the local community as a citizen. Then he took a most solemn oath before entering the army which read: "We will never bring disgrace to this our city, by any act of dishonesty or cowardice, nor ever desert our comrade; we will fight for the ideals and sacred things of the city, both alone and with many; we will revere and obey the city laws and do our best to incite a like respect and reverence in others. We will strive unceasingly to quicken the public's sense of civic duty, that thus in all these ways we may transmit this city, greater, better, and more beautiful than it was transmitted to us."

During the next two years the young citizen was in the care of the state and received a military training. 

The Education of an Athenian Girl. The education of an 

Athenian girl was directed by her mother until she was married. 

She was taught spinning, weaving, dancing, sewing, and music. 

Before her childhood days were really over, she was betrothed by her father to some young suitor. Within a few years with a suitable dowry (gift for her husband) she was located in her own home. 

Forces Separating the Greeks. Besides Sparta and Athens there was Corinth (korʼinth), Thebes, Delphi, and other citystates. But because of geographical barriers, jealousies, and pride in their own local governments the Greeks refused to unite as a single nation. - - Bonds of Union. While these conditions tended to separate the people of Greece, there were several bonds which held them together. They all prided themselves on the fact that they were descended from one common ancestor, Hellen. They had a common language and religion. The Greeks met and had a common interest in those great national shrines and religious festivals the oracles and the Olympic games. It was these ties

that led the Greeks on several occasions of grave national danger to join their military forces until the crisis was passed. — - The Delphic Oracle. Of all the oracles in Greece the most famous was that dedicated to Apollo, the god of prophecy. It was situated in Delphi, at the foot of Mt. Parnassus. Here was a sacred spot, the Greeks believed, where human beings could receive divine guidance from the gods. 

People came from all parts of the world seeking advice and bearing costly gifts to Apollo. Questions relating to private and state affairs were brought to the priests. They were given after a great deal of ceremony and pomp to a priestess or Pythia who mumbled the sayings of Apollo. The Pythia, a young girl, was seated on a tripod (three-legged stool), which was placed over a crack in the rocks. A certain vapor, which was said to be the breath of Apollo, came from it.

Sickened by the fumes, the Pythia mumbled certain words which the priests pretended to copy and translate. The answers were very carefully stated. Double meanings could be taken from them, so that no matter what way the affair ape olduq ad ni bo daktar

The famous Delphic oracle played an important part in Greek history. It influenced the lives of men and the fate of nations.

Lydia consulted the oracle and asked if he would be successful if he waged war on the Persians. The reply was: "When

Apollo was the god of manly beauty, prophecy, art, science, and music. stroyed." He crossed the river, a great empire was destroyed, but it was his own. Of course people of today believe that the

Delphic oracle was a fraud. 

But in the light of history we must remember that it guided the life of individuals and directed the fate of nations for centuries. The Olympic Games. - A great religious festival in the form of an athletic carnival was held every four years at Olympia in Elis. 

This event was in honor of Zeus. The best athletes from every city-state took part in the games. The victors were crowned with olive leaves as a token that they had been honored by the gods. They were acclaimed as heroes upon their return home, and very often their statues were erected in the public square of their native city. The first Olympic games of which we have any record occurred in the year 776 B.C. Eventually they became mere athletic contests. Athletes were paid to represent a certain city-state, and the glory and honor of the former Olympic games disappeared. After a time they ceased to exist. The Olympic games of the modern world were renewed in 1896 at Athens. 

Master, Remember the Athenians." We now come tc the most dramatic event of Greek history -the Persian invasions. The successors of Cyrus the Great had made themselves masters of Asia Minor. Their rule was so harsh that the Ionian cities on the Asiatic coast of the Aegean Sea revolted. They called on their Greek brothers for aid. Athens and Eretria 

---

(ĕ-rē'tri-ȧ) responded by sending them a fleet. The allies were defeated by the Persians, and Darius III (da-rī'us), king of Persia, decided to punish Athens for her part in this affair. 

Lest he should forget, Darius had his servant repeat to him on numerous occasions: "Master, remember the Athenians."

The First Persian Invasion, 492 B.C. It took a long time to equip an army and a fleet, but at last, in 492 B.C., Darius was ready. The expedition was a failure because the Persian fleet was destroyed in a storm off Mt. Athos. But now Darius was more determined than ever to punish Athens. Furthermore, he determined to conquer all Greece. He sent messengers throughout the land who were to demand earth and water as tokens to signify submission to Persian rule. Most of the city-states gave these tokens, but Athens and Sparta proudly refused. 66 

The Second Persian Invasion, 490 B.C.-The new expedition was soon ready, and Datis was appointed leader. He and his Persian hosts landed on the northern coast of Attica. The eyes of all Greece were now on Athens. The city-states of Thebes and 

Corinth, because of jealousy, refused to aid their kinsmen. The terror-stricken Athenians sent a runner to Sparta, but the Spartans could not help until the moon was full and that was five days away.

Battle of Marathon. - Athens determined to fight and that alone if necessary. Miltiades was placed in charge of the army. 

His plan of battle was to march out and meet the foe and not wait until they reached the city walls. The Athenians took their stand at Marathon, twenty-four miles northeast of Athens. Here the new and untried civilization of the west was to face the old Orien tal civilization, represented by Persia and her many subject nations. Here and in following engagements was to be decided a vital question - whether Europe under the leadership of Greece with her new democracy would develop unmolested or whether it would fall under the tyranny of an Oriental monarch whose laws existed for his own benefit.

106

The Persians far outnumbered the Greeks on that sultry summer day in 490 B.C. Miltiades extended his lines as long as that of the Persians. He weakened the center and strengthened the ends. The enemy rushed the weakened center of the line which gave way, but the strong Athenian ends encircled the main Persian force. The soldiers of Datis did not expect this opposition and became confused and frightened. Many were killed. They fled to their ships. The Persians set sail for Athens in the hope that it would be turned over to them by spies and traitors. But in this they were disappointed, and their fleet returned home. 

The second attempt of the east against the west had been met at the threshold. Even the enemies of Athens praised her valor and glory. A new spirit pervaded all of Greece. At this time the leadership of Athens almost created a Greek nation. 

Pheidippides. Many heroes of Greece fought and died that day at Marathon. Statues were erected in their honor. Great orators and writers lauded their bravery. But none was more worthy of honor and praise than Pheidippides (fi-dip'ĭ-dēz).

When Athens sought the aid of Sparta, it was Pheidippides who ran the distance between the two cities in less than forty-eight hours. The Athenians said that Pheidippides, on his way home, met the goat-god Pan, who promised to aid the Athenians if that city built him a statue. This was done, and Pan caused so much confusion in the Persian ranks that they were Pan-stricken. 

Hence our modern word "panic."

When Pheidippides returned to Athens and reported the decision of Sparta, he was asked what reward he desired. He wanted to fight for Athens at Marathon and then settle down and marry. He fought hard all day on the field of Marathon. After the battle some one was needed to carry the message to the Athenians. He volunteered and ran the distance from Marathon to 

Athens. He was spared enough energy to fulfill his task and exclaim, "Athens is saved." With these words he died, but with the Athenians he always lived. And today our long-distance races are called "Marathon " in honor of this event.

The Third Persian Invasion, 480 B.C.: Themistocles. Afte the second Persian invasion ten years elapsed before Greece wa again threatened. During this period Themistocles (the-mis't klēz), a great Athenian, advocated a large and well-trained navy He met with strong opposition but finally won his point. Th government took over the silver mines of Attica, built a fleet more than two hundred war galleys, and improved the harbor Piraeus (pi-re'us), which was used by Athens. 

Darius was busy also, for he planned to lead the most powe ful army and navy ever assembled against Greece. But he di during the preparations, and the task fell to Xerxes (zûrk'sēz his son and successor. In 480 B.C. a vast Persian host land in northern Greece and started south through Thrace and Mac donia. It was accompanied by a fleet of twelve hundred vesse 

Thermopylae. - The Persians were amazed and annoyed | the lack of opposition. When they arrived at the Pass

1 Thermopylae (ther-mop'i-lē), a halt was called. This very narrow road, which lay between the mountains and the sea, could be defended by a small force. Here King Leonidas (lê-ŏn'ĭ-das), with three hundred of his Spartans and about four thousand Greek allies, took his stand. Xerxes hurled his forces against the stone wall defense of the Greeks. It gained him nothing. The death list of his own troops was enormous, while that of the Greeks was very small. Even the "Ten Thousand Immortals," the pride of the Persian army, could not shake Leonidas and his brave allies. 

The Death of Leonidas. Then a traitor told Xerxes that he could take the Persian army by a secret path across the mountains. This path met the road in the rear of the Greek troops. So for a small sum of money this traitor during the night of the second day's fighting led the "Ten Thousand Immortals" over the mountains. In the morning Leonidas discovered that his soldiers were surrounded. He excused his allies, but Xerxes, the Persian king, seated on his throne watching Athens being destroyed by the Persian hosts. Looking toward the Bay of Salamis, he was terrified to see the light boats of the Greeks destroy the powerful Persian fleet.

IIO GREEK CIVILIZATION 

ordered every Spartan to stand his ground and fight to the death. 

Leonidas and all of his brave Spartans (save one who had been blinded) were killed. But so great had been the valor and courage of Leonidas that a wave of enthusiasm and intense patriotic pride spread throughout all of Greece. Spartan courage has reëchoed through the centuries in the words dedicated to the men who made this stand: "Stranger, go tell the Spartans that we lie here in obedience to their commands."

Defeat of Persia: Salamis and Plataea. The Persians enjoyed a few temporary successes after Thermopylae. But their fleet was defeated by Themistocles in a great naval battle at Salamis (sǎl'a-mis). Their army was routed at Plataea (plà-tē’à) (479 B.C.) by the largest all-Greek force ever assembled. This was the last attempt of Persia to invade Greece. 

Athens had been the guiding spirit against the invaders. It now became the most prominent of the several city-states. The next fifty years were to be what historians have called the "Golden Age of Athens."

Historical Fiction 

Perkins, L. F. The Spartan Twins

CHAPTER VIII  

Pericles (493-429 B.C.). -Athens had been burned by the 

Persians shortly before the battle of Salamis. After the triumph of the Greeks in that engagement, the inhabitants who had fled returned. A new Athens emerged upon the ruins of the old city. 

Pericles, who ruled by persuasion rather than force. (In the Vatican, Rome.) 

A great leader arose, who, more than any one else, made possible the so-called "Golden 

Age." That leader was 

Pericles (pěr'i-klēz), the greatest statesman and orator of his day. He was the friend of the common man. 

He was interested in government, art, literature, and science, and aided and encouraged painters, sculptors, dramatists, writers, and philosophers. He erected beautiful temples and open-air theaters. He made Athens "the queen of Hellas." 

Pericles was three years old when the battle of Marathon occurred. He died as Athens was about to fall before the onslaught of her Spartan enemies. 

His life, therefore, covered the most important time in Greek history. He ruled in Athens for thirty years. And the events and people of that period are so important that this chapter is devoted to the "Age of Pericles." 

Pericles, the Strategus- the Long Walls of Athens. Pericles was elected "strategus" by the Athenians.

As a strategus he 112

was one of the ten generals voted to office each year by the citizens to govern the city-state. The people learned to trust him more than any other man and elected him year after year. One of his first acts was to strengthen the walls of Athens. 

The seaport of Athens was called the Piraeus. It was located four miles away. Both cities had been walled after the Persian invasions. Pericles reënforced the city walls and connected 

Athens and the Piraeus with two high parallel walls. They were several hundred feet apart, and each was more than sixty feet high. They were wide enough at the top for chariots to pass. 

The Athenians now could travel safely to the sea during times of grave danger. The city might lose control on land, but the "long walls," as they were called, added to its security on the sea. 

Athenian Homes: the Poor Sanitary Conditions. We must not be confused or misled by the name, "Golden Age of Athens." 

XXX

The noble's wealth was in the form of land. 

Some Athenians had homes like these. The great majority, however, lived in much poorer homes. 

Notice the family at the altar at one end of the court, which was open to the sky. The main rooms were grouped around the court. (A reconstruction.)

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

The Athenians did not enjoy the improved sanitary conditions or conveniences of a modern home. Their houses were usually built of sun-dried brick. Sometimes a kind of stucco was used to cover the brick. There were no windows on the first floor. 

When a second story was added, a few small openings were made to let in air and light. The rooms were built around a court, which was open to the skies. A sundial and a few benches were often found in the center of the court. 

The rooms in the homes of the poorer classes were almost bare. 

A few hard chairs, a couch, and a crude bed without springs or coverings were the household furniture. The wealthy classes possessed highly decorated furniture, beautiful tapestries, and soft couches and beds. Pottery-making was one of the chief occupations of the Athenians. The housewives, therefore, had many kinds of jars for various purposes. 

The homes of rich and poor alike were heated by means of a charcoal burner, which was placed in the center of a room. The smoke was supposed to escape through a hole in the ceiling. Another room held the family altar, for religious worship was a daily occurrence in the Athenian home. Waste and water of all descriptions were thrown into the streets. It is no wonder that disease, pestilence, and plague were so common in Athens. (Pericles was to die during one of the epidemics.) Water was carried to the house in jars from the springs and wells by slaves. 

Athenian Food. The Athenians were moderate eaters and drinkers. Wheat and barley were used to make cakes; olive oil was used instead of butter. Honey bees supplied sweetening material. Watered wine and goat's milk were the common drinks. 

Fish and meat were included in the evening meal, which took place after sunset and was the chief meal of the day. 

Athenian Streets and Roads. The narrow, crooked streets were difficult to travel over because of the filth and rubbish. 

During a rainy day people were forced to walk in mud up to their ankles. All the streets of Athens led to the market place, and all the roads of Attica led to Athens. These were like Athenian

streets a cause for complaint. The farmers coming from the west and north with vegetables, pigs, and chickens complained.

The sailors and merchants coming from the Piraeus with fish, tapestries, linen, and ivory complained too. Athenian homes, streets, and roads never became a part of the "glory that was Greece."

The Agora. -The Athenians called their market place the 

Agora. It was a broad, open square surrounded by government buildings, temples, and porches. These porches were covered walks, which protected the people from the sun and the rain. One was called the "royal stoa" or king's porch. There court trials were held. On the back wall were carved the laws of Draco and 

Solon (Chapter VII). Another was known as the "painted porch." Here was pictured in painting and frescoes (painting on plaster) the story of the Persian wars. The markets were open daily. Business began at sunrise and ended at noon. 

There were no newspapers in those days, so the Athenians gathered at the Agora in groups to discuss the latest events of the day, to hear the gossip about their neighbors, or to listen to some great teacher or newly arrived traveler. The Athenians went to the Agora, or market place, every day in order to learn the latest news or to discuss the problems of the time.

GREEK CIVILIZATION The Government of Athens: the Strategi. The early 

Athenian tyrants had aided the people in overthrowing the archons. During the time of Pericles the government was in charge of ten generals, called strategi. The people of each of the ten "local tribes" of Athens elected a general. He was chosen for his ability to lead men in battle and for his knowledge of public affairs. The strategi had charge of foreign affairs. 

They made treaties with foreign nations and sent and received ambassadors.

The Athenian Council. 

The Athenian council was now called the "Council of Five Hundred." It was composed of citizens over thirty years of age who were chosen by lot every year. 

This body prepared laws to be discussed and voted upon by the assembly. It was the executive department; that is, it enforced and saw that the laws of the state were obeyed. It had charge of the finances, religion, and public buildings of Athens. 

The Assembly: the Ecclesia. — The Athenian assembly or 

Ecclesia was the most important political body in the state. It was composed of all male citizens over eighteen years of age (see education of the Athenian youth, Chapter VII), and met at least forty times a year. Its members gathered on a sloping hill near the Acropolis, called the Pnyx. A high perpendicular rock formed the background for the speaker's stand, which was located on a flat rock, the "bema." Any member of the assembly could stand on the bema and address the meeting. Any member could propose a law for which he assumed full responsibility. The Ecclesia was supposed to be a pure democracy; that is, every citizen came as his own representative and helped to make the laws. In most cases the voting was done by a show of hands, but where the rights of a citizen were concerned, the voting was by ballot. 

The Jury System: the Dikastry.-The jury system of Pericles's day was different from that of today. A juryman had to be thirty years old. Six thousand were chosen by lot each year, of which one thousand were held in reserve. From the remaining five thousand, ten groups of five hundred were formed. Each group

Name as many differences as you can. was a jury or "dikastry." Although five hundred men were on a jury, frequently only half of that number heard cases. A defendant had to plead his own case. He might have prepared it himself or had a better educated person prepare it for him. A majority vote decided whether he was guilty or innocent. It was almost impossible to bribe such a large jury. To further avoid bribery, not one of the jurists knew beforehand upon what cases he would sit in judgment.

Ostracism: Plutarch's Explanation. - Ostracism was the method used by the Athenians to dispose of leaders who were thought to be dangerous to the state. The constitution provided that six thousand votes were necessary to exile a citizen for ten years. Plutarch (ploo'tärk; 46-120 A.D.), the Greek historian, tells of the ostracism of Aristides (ăr-is-tī'dēz). 

Aristides, a valiant soldier and honored citizen of Athens, opposed the naval policy of Themistocles. He was called "Aristides the Just." Plutarch's account follows: "Ostracism

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

was performed in this manner. Every one took an ostracon, that is, a sherd, or a piece of earthen ware, and carried it to a certain part of the market place surrounded by wooden rails. First the magistrates numbered all the sherds. If there were less than six thousand, the ostracism was imperfect. Then, laying every name by itself, they pronounced him whose name was written by the larger number banished for ten years without loss of the enjoyment of his estate. "As therefore, they were writing the name on the sherd, it is reported that an illiterate, clownish fellow giving Aristides his sherd, supposing him to be a common citizen, begged him to write Aristides on it; and he (Aristides) being surprised, asked if Aristides had ever done injury to him. 'Not at all,' said he, "Neither do I know the man, but I am tired of hearing him everywhere called 'the Just.' Aristides, hearing this, is said to have made no reply, but returned the sherd with his own (Aristides) name inscribed." The ruins of the Parthenon on the Acropolis. This famous temple was dedicated to the goddess Athena.

Recent Finds of Dr. Shear. Two highly valuable sherds of pottery were recently discovered by Dr. T. Leslie Shear of 

Princeton University in the market place of Athens. These inscribed votes were used in ostracism proceedings against Aristides and Themistocles and are about 2400 years old. 

Athenian Art: the Parthenon. Pericles wanted to make 

Athens the most beautiful city in all Greece. He directed the building of the Parthenon (pär'the-non), the most famous of all the temples that stood on the Acropolis. It was built of white marble, and its massive roof was supported by fortysix Doric pillars. The Doric style of architecture was the simplest and most graceful used by the Greeks in their buildings. It had no base, but the gradually sloping column had at its top a square slab of marble upon which the beam supporting the roof rested. 

The Statue of Athena. 

Goddess of wisdom, courage, and honor. The Greeks called her giant statue of the goddess, Athena. To the Romans she was thirty-nine feet high, was in the temple. The figure was made known as Minerva. This statue is in the Louvre, Paris. of ivory, the draperies were of gold, and the eyes were precious jewels. Athena held in one hand a statue of victory, and in the other a spear and a shield. At her side there was a coiled serpent which was meant to represent wisdom. A frieze, or band of sculpture, encircled the temple. This pictured a stately religious procession which took place every four years when a new robe was presented to the goddess. ― - 

The Odeon: the Athenian Music Hall. Pericles had the 

Athenians construct a beautiful music hall, called the Odeon

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

(ô-de'on). It was located on the southwest corner of the 

Acropolis and was used for musical entertainments during the religious festivals. The roof of the Odeon was sloped like the tent of the Persian king, Xerxes, which had been captured at the battle of Plataea.

The Theater of Dionysus: the Drama. Pericles rebuilt and adorned the theater of Dionysus (the god of wine). It was situated at the foot of the Acropolis. The theater was open to the sky. There were many rows of seats arranged in tiers on the side of the hill. The plain below was called the orchestra, and in front of this was the stage. This large, open-air amphitheater seated 30,000 people. The early performances consisted of singing and dancing only. Young men and boys, dressed in goatskins and called satyrs, danced and sang around an altar dedicated to Dionysus. The dancers and singers were called 

LEON CUIPON 

A reconstruction of the Greek theater of Dionysus. The Greeks were greatly influenced by the plays which were shown. Some plays dealt with war others doubted the existence of the gods. Still others criticized public officials and the jury system.

the chorus. Later, tradition says, Thespis introduced the drama. 

A drama pictures the life and character of people and deals especially with performances on the stage. 

The Tragedy and the Comedy. During the time of Pericles the Greek drama had taken the form of tragedies and comedies. 

The tragedies presented the serious side of life and usually ended with a death scene or some other unhappy circumstance. This type of play received its name from the Greek word "tragos," which means goat. It was the goat in early times which was sacrificed to the wine god. The plays which were full of fun were called comedies.

The tragedies of this period usually dealt with the lives of the gods and the deeds of ancient heroes. They were enacted in the morning. The comedy writers of Athens liked to poke fun at the great Athenians of the day. These men were pictured in every rôle of life except the one which they occupied. This form of drama met the hearty approval of the people. It helped to form public opinion and to curb unjust acts by public officials. 

The comedies were performed in the afternoon. It was not considered good taste for the women of Athens to attend the theater. But often Aspasia (ăs-pā'shĬ-à), the talented wife of 

Pericles, witnessed the plays disguised as a boy.

Sculptors and Painters: Phidias and Praxiteles. It was the sculptors, painters, and dramatists, however, who made possible the fame of the Athenian art and drama. Phidias (fid'ĭ-as) was the great artist and sculptor responsible for many of the lifelike statues found on the Acropolis. It was he who carved the renowned statue of Athena and did the frieze around the Parthenon. 

One of his finest works was a statue of the god Zeus at Olympia. 

It was regarded by the Athenians as one of the great wonders of the world.

During his later life Phidias suffered much from the persecution of his fellow citizens. He was accused of stealing some of the gold donated for use on Athena's robe. After much trouble and humiliation he was declared innocent by a jury. RA-9

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

Finally he was charged with insulting the gods by placing a likeness of himself on the shield of Athena. And Phidias, considered today the most famous sculptor that the world has ever known, died in prison. He was a victim of jealousy on the part of his own people.

Modern scholars marvel at the beauty and expression of this famous statue. (In the Museum at Olympia.) lived after the time of Phidias. 

His greatest piece of art was the statue of "Hermes with the Infant Dionysus." It was placed in the temple of 

Hera at Olympia. There it was discovered by a German excavator in 1877 A.D. 

The right arm of Hermes is missing. The statue is beautifully proportioned, and the face has a lifelike expression. It was said of 

Praxiteles that "he rendered into stone the moods of the soul." What does the statement mean?

The Story of the Statue of Hermes. This work of art was the expression of the story that the god Hermes was taking the infant Dionysus to the nymphs who were going to rear and train the child. Along the road Hermes stopped and gathered a bunch of grapes. The missing arm probably held the grapes with which Hermes was amusing the little god Dionysus. 

Greek Dramatists: Aeschylus. There were other famous sculptors and painters, but we turn now to the playwrights or dramatists. Aeschylus (es'ki-lus; 525-456 B.C.) was called the "Father of Greek Tragedy." He gained fame in the Persian wars, having fought at Marathon and Salamis. One of his first plays was called The Persae (Persians). It told the story of

these invasions in a very serious manner. He also wrote about the gods. He believed that there was a guiding power higher than they in whom the Greeks believed. His great tragedy, Prometheus Bound, brought out this idea. Aeschylus had

Prometheus, the main character, steal fire from heaven and give it to the people on earth. Zeus, the king of the gods, punished 

Prometheus by chaining him to a rock to be slowly devoured by wild birds.

Sophocles. Finally the Athenians became tired of the seriousminded Aeschylus. In a contest many dramatists gave their plays for the first time in the theater of Dionysus. The one whose work received the greatest applause was declared the victor, and his were the plays accepted. In 468 B.C. the applause was given to the young poet, Sophocles (sof'ō-klēz). His characters were very human, and he introduced the idea of having three main actors carry on a conversation. In his most famous tragedy, Antigone, whose heroine is a beautiful woman character, he tries to show that there is a guiding power in the life of men. 

Euripides. Euripides (u-rip'ĭ-dēz; 480-406 B.C.) was the third of the great tragedy writers. He was not very popular with the older people. He belonged to the new school of thought which was beginning to doubt the existence of the 

Greek gods. His play expressed his belief. Today the dramas of Euripides are played on the modern stage more than those of any other Greek dramatist. More than a dozen of his plays have come down to us. The best known today is Iphigenia. 

She was the daughter of Agamemnon, who was offered as a sacrifice in the Trojan War. Electra and Orestes were the titles of two other plays by Euripides.

Aristophanes. Aristophanes (ăr-is-tof'à-nēz) was the greatest of the comedy writers. His plays amused his fellow Athenians. He ridiculed the prominent men and institutions of his times. His comedy called the Wasps poked fun at the jury system in which men sought jobs on the jury in order to receive a day's pay without working for it.

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

Philosophy. -The philosophers were also friends of Pericles. 

The most famous philosophers were Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle, all of whom lived in Athens. Their ideas and teachings have come down to the present time to influence modern thought and actions. The word "philosopher" means a "lover of wisdom."

These philosophers were teachers. Two men, Thales and 

Pythagoras (pi-thǎg'ô-răs), had blazed the trail for Socrates and 

Plato. 

Thales. Thales was born in the city of Miletus in the sixth century B.C. He studied in Egypt and Babylon. While in Egypt he taught the Egyptians how to measure the heights of the pyramids by the length of their shadows. He observed and predicted an eclipse of the sun. When it occurred, he was pronounced a great man. But above all he taught the Greeks the value of thought. The fables and myths of the Greeks, he said, did not explain peculiar happenings on the earth or in the sky. 

These, he believed, could be explained only by natural laws and science. 

Pythagoras: the World Is Round. — Pythagoras was born about fifty years later than Thales. He started a school in Italy, where at one time he had more than two hundred pupils. He taught that the earth was round, that it turned on its axis, and that it was a part of a great system that revolved around the sun. 

Pythagoras in this theory was two thousand years ahead of his time, because the world was not ready to believe it until the days of Columbus. Pythagoras also discovered that the pitch and tone of a musical string is in exact relation to its length. The teachings of Pythagoras spread, and he gained many followers. 

Later his organization became involved in politics. His followers were attacked and his buildings destroyed. It is not known whether he was killed at this time. 

Socrates (about 470-399 B.C.). — These two men and others paved the way for Socrates, the most beloved of Athenian philosophers. His father was a stone cutter, who taught his son the trade. But Socrates did not care to work. He yearned to

follow the crowd to the market place, where he would enter into discussions with any one who cared to argue with him. He was not a handsome man by any means. His bust, which has been found, shows him as a bald-headed, big-eyed, round-faced person. 

At times a crowd would gather around him. Some would be amused and laugh at him, while others would be very much interested. His method was to ask a series of questions and, in this way, cause his listeners to reason out the answers step by step. 

This is very commonly used today and is called the "Socratic method." 

Know Thyself. - Socrates's greatest maxim was "know thyself." He wanted people to think for themselves so that they could distinguish between good and evil, right and wrong. He wanted his own followers to study their faults and try to correct them -"know thyself," he kept repeating to them. 

Socrates was a philosopher and teacher. Are any of his teaching methods used today? (In the Villa 

Albani, Rome.)

Xanthippe. Socrates seldom worked, and his wife Xanthippe (zǎn-tip'ē) was often very impatient with him. She scolded him and went to the market place time and again to bring him home. 

How the family got along, it is hard to tell. Probably they drew rations from the government. Under the orders of Pericles, Athens at this time was feeding several thousands of its citizens.

Socrates Criticizes the Government. -Yet this man was a great moral teacher and believed in the immortality of the soul. Socrates criticized the jury system and government by lot. He did

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

not approve of the manner in which rulers were selected. Concerning this last, he is reported to have said (he himself wrote nothing): "If it were a temple to be built, a statue to be sculptured, a disease to be healed, men would be employed who were expert in these things. But we who have the noblest city in the world to preserve and govern select men who know nothing about the affairs entrusted to them."

Socrates felt, too, that in the assembly many people voted without thinking. He thought and told the Athenians in the market place "that eloquent speakers could stir the emotions of the multitudes and make them vote their own destruction as well as that of their city." 

The Death of Socrates. When Socrates was very old, his enemies had him brought before the jury. He was accused of corrupting the youth by de- 

Plato. (In Rome.) 

stroying their faith in the gods. He pleaded his own case but was pronounced guilty and condemned to die. 

His fate was postponed until the return of the ship from 

Delos (Chapter VI). Then, surrounded by his friends, Socrates drank the poisonous cup of hemlock. The story of his death is one of the most tragic scenes recorded in history. 

Plato (429-347 B.C.).

Much that we know about Socrates was written by his most distinguished pupil, Plato, who was of noble birth. He could have been one of the leaders of Athens, but when he met Socrates, he gave up public affairs. 

He was an excellent soldier and a fine athlete. Speaking of his great friend and teacher, he said: "I thank God that I was

born Greek and not barbarian (foreigner); freeman and not slave; but above all that I was born in the age of Socrates."

The Academy. - When Socrates died, Plato left Athens and did not return for about twelve years. In the meantime he traveled throughout the Mediterranean world. He visited strange lands and met new people. At one time he was sold into slavery, but his friends bought his freedom. He returned to Athens and established a school of philosophy called the "Academy.' 

It was located in a grove at the edge of the city. There, for about forty years, he lectured and taught the many students who came to him in search of wisdom. He loved to discuss ideals, and he taught and wrote much about truth, goodness, and beauty. 

The Republic of Plato. One of the outstanding books by 

Plato was The Republic. In this he described what he considered an ideal government or state. The state was to have full charge of educating all the children. Each child was to be given an equal opportunity. During the first ten-year period they were taught to play and take physical exercises in order to assure them of a strong and healthy body. Music was also a part of this course.

At the age of twenty every one took an examination. This examination was practical because it tested the ability of the students to do certain things. It tested their character and attitude towards life. Those who failed were to do factory work and other kinds of manual labor. Those who passed were given ten years more of education. Then another test, more severe than the first, was taken. Those who failed this time were to be government aides and military officers. Those who passed were taught philosophy for five years. Then another test and this time, the successful persons at the age of thirty-five were given a final task. They were compelled to live and work among the common people so that they would learn the needs and problems of the state. Then at the age of fifty they were considered fit to govern. Cicero (sis'er-ō), the great Roman orator (Chapter XII), said of Plato: "If Jupiter himself had been willing

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

to adopt the language of mankind, he would have spoken as 

Plato wrote." 

Aristotle (384-322 B.C.). Aristotle was born in the province of Macedonia, north of Greece. He inherited from his father, who was a physician, a love for the beauties of nature. At a very early age Aristotle began to study birds and flowers. He acquired a love for other sciences. While still a boy he went to Athens to study under Plato. He remained there for twenty years, and then for several more years he traveled. He was called home by King Philip II of Macedonia to teach the young prince who was later to be known as Alexander the Great. 

The Lyceum. Aristotle returned to Athens but not to his old teacher. They differed too much in their ideas. Plato built things upon ideals, while Aristotle watched real things in life and tried to find out their cause and effect. He founded a new school of philosophy, which he called the "Lyceum." There for many years hundreds of students were directed by Aristotle in every subject known. Aristotle gained the reputation of having the finest mind in the ancient world. His works were so many and his knowledge so great, that for a period of 1500 years his word was never doubted. Teachers would end their lessons with the expression: "Thus saith Aristotle."

The Fall of Athens: Herodotus and Thucydides. — A friend of 

Pericles was Herodotus, who is known as the 'Father of History." 

He wrote in a simple, graphic style the first history of the Persian wars. Pericles was now growing old. The citizens were becoming careless in their responsibilities to the government. The criticisms of the dramatists, of Socrates and Plato, were justified. 

The Athenian power waned with the death of its great men. 

Civil war followed. The far-flung colonies of Athens rebelled. 

Sparta declared war on the Athenian Empire. The Peloponnesian War followed, and Sparta was victorious. A young man by the name of Thucydides (thû-sĭd'î-dēz), a friend of Herodotus (hé-rŏd'ô-tus), took part in the conflict. He wrote the history of this war and became the greatest of Greek historians. He

was known as a scientific historian because he told the facts as they actually happened without showing favor to either 

Athens or Sparta. 

But Spartan supremacy did not last long. A new leader was rising in the north, the pupil of Aristotle, Alexander the Great.

CHAPTER IX  (336-323 B.C.) A New Age and New Men. -Like Pericles, Alexander the 

Great wielded a powerful influence upon the history of his own and later times. We cannot agree with everything that he did. 

But before his death at the early age of thirty-two he had set in motion a series of events that were to alter world affairs. His native country was Macedonia; his father, its king, was Philip II. 

Both influenced his career. Therefore his story is partly their story. So with them we will begin this account of a new age which ushered in new men.

Macedonia: the Highlanders and Lowlanders. - Macedonia was located north of Greece. It was a mountainous country, wild and rugged, with several broad river valleys. The inhabitants were a branch of those early Indo-European invaders, the Hellenes. For centuries the country had been divided by two warring groups, the highlanders and the lowlanders. The people of the highlands were semi-barbarians. They hunted, farmed a little, and tended their flocks. It was said that "a 

Macedonian who had not slain a wild boar could not eat at the same table with men." Their chief occupation was fighting, but as a rule they were no match for the men of the lowlands. 

These latter people had more contact with Greek civilization and were superior to their highland neighbors. Finally the mountain tribes were conquered, and a form of national unity was established in Macedonia. The man responsible for this was 

Philip II, better known as Philip of Macedon. 

Philip of Macedon (359-336 B.C.): His Ambitions. — During the boyhood of Philip, Macedonia was defeated in a war with the Greek city-states. Philip was taken to Thebes as a hostage. 

A hostage was a person given by a conquered nation to the victors as a guarantee of peace. If the terms of the peace were 131

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

broken, the hostages lost their lives; otherwise they enjoyed many privileges.

While in Thebes Philip studied and enjoyed Greek literature, art, government, and military science. A few years later he was sent back to his native land. When his brother died, he became king. His great ambition was to unite all of the 

Greek city-states under the leadership of Macedonia with himself as king. Then he hoped to march an army into Persia and make her suffer as she had made 

Greece suffer years before. Το accomplish these things he had first to reorganize his army. 

Annoy 

Gold coins like these were used by Philip of Macedon. One side shows the head of the king, and the other shows a chariot. 

Philip's Army: the Macedonian 

Phalanx. - Philip had good material with which to build. The mountaineers and plainsmen of Macedonia lived in the great out-ofdoors. They were strong, rugged, and accustomed to all sorts of hardships. He organized these men into a strong and powerful military force. He improved upon the Greek phalanx (see 

Chapter VII). He increased the number of ranks in that formation to sixteen and had the spears of the men in the front line lengthened to eighteen feet. This formed the dreaded "Macedonian phalanx." It was to win many battles for Philip and for his famous son, Alexander. 

The Cavalry and Artillery. In addition to the phalanx 

Philip collected all the horsemen in his kingdom and formed them into a cavalry. The cavalrymen moved quickly and disorganized the enemy by attacking them from the rear. Philip selected the best horsemen as his bodyguard. These he called his "companions." Another division of this new army was the artillery. It consisted of heavy machines used to throw large rocks and to batter down stone walls. There were also machines that threw large heavy arrows a great distance. Such were the men and materials which made up the army of Macedonia.

The Conquests of Philip. Philip conquered Thrace and took possession of the gold mines in that country. These mines yielded more than a million dollars a year. With this money he was able to equip his army easily and to supply it with provisions. 

Philip as the undisputed leader of Macedonia marched south. 

He extended his conquests and authority from Mt. Olympus to the pass at Thermopylae. He was recognized as the proud defender of the oracle at Delphi. He then desired to form an alliance with Athens. To Philip,

Athens stood for everything that was great and fine in Greek civilization. But the Athenians feared the Macedonian conqueror, and they refused to join an alliance that would make a united Greece with Philip at its head. Demosthenes

This attitude was due to (dê-mos'the-nez), the great Athenian orator. He, more than any other man, roused bitter feelings against the ambitions of Philip Macedon. of Demosthenes became a famous orator because he was determined 

He loved Athens but to be one. hated Macedonia. Why? (Vatican, Rome.) 

The Days of Demosthenes: Citizenship Neglected. — The spirit of patriotism in Athens was very low during the days of Demosthenes. Her citizens were more anxious to make money than they were to preserve a state. They had become indifferent toward their duties as citizens. They were careless about the men whom they elected to office and the laws which they made (recall the complaint of Socrates, Chapter VIII). They neglected the gymnasium and sought poor forms of pleasure. They were too busy to fight for Athens, so they hired soldiers to go to the front for them. These were the conditions when Demosthenes tried to revive the spirit of old. His was a difficult task, but he prepared for the ordeal.

GREEK CIVILIZATION The Handicaps of Demosthenes. -As a boy Demosthenes was delicate in health. When he spoke, he was shy, he stammered, and twitched one shoulder higher than the other. His ambition was to become a great orator. The story is told that in his first appearance on the bema before the general assembly, he was laughed at and ridiculed. When he left the speakers' stand, a senator approached and said words to this effect: "Do not give up, young man, there is something about your speech that reminds me of Pericles." This so encouraged Demosthenes that he resolved to overcome his handicaps. 

The Grit of Demosthenes. He went down to the seashore. 

To cure himself of stuttering, he placed pebbles in his mouth.

To be heard above the noise of the assembly, he tried to speak louder than the roar of the sea. At home he practiced to keep his shoulders level. He stood beneath a sharp, pointed sword hung from the ceiling. When he forgot and twitched his shoulder, it received a painful stab from the sword point. His long and hard work was rewarded. He became one of the most famous orators in history. But still he worked. He spent days in the careful preparation of his speeches. He would shave one side of his head so that he would be ashamed to appear in public until he was prepared to deliver his speech.

The Philippics. Such was the determination of the man who now kept Athens from making an alliance with Philip. In a series of speeches he denounced Philip. He pictured him as a man who was greedy for power, as one who had come into Greece to take away the liberty of the people and to destroy their cities. 

These famous orations were called the "Philippics," because they were delivered against Philip. In our own day, especially in 

Congress when a senator or representative delivers a speech condemning somebody, we say that his oration was a "modern Philippic."

The Second Congress of Corinth (337 B.C.). - Athens and 

Thebes united against Philip, but their armies were defeated, and Greece became a part of Macedonia. Years before a congress

R. Raffius, N. Y.

The remains of the "Golden Age" of Athens. In the distance, beyond the 

Acropolis, part of modern Athens can be seen. had been held at Corinth by the Greek city-states to organize against the Persian invasions. Philip now called a second congress of these same states to meet at the same place. He organized them into a loose league with permission to manage their own government affairs. He had himself elected president. with power to declare war and make peace. He now prepared to fulfill his second great ambition, the invasion and punishment of Persia. 

Death of Philip, 336 B.C. But in this he was doomed to be disappointed. He returned home from Corinth to witness the marriage of his daughter. A great public festival was held. 

Philip was happy. He was commander of the greatest army in the world. He had united his own people and had united Greece. 

He was still young and expected soon to be on his way to Persia. 

As he moved along in the stately procession, a man leaped quickly

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

from the crowd and buried a dagger in the heart of the king. 

Alexander, just nineteen years of age, was proclaimed ruler of Macedonia.

Alexander: His Boyhood: the Taming of Bucephalus. — The boyhood of Alexander was spent among the mountains of his native land. There he received a Spartan training, for Philip wanted his son to follow in his footsteps. Alexander is described as having a reckless, daring nature, a vivid imagination, and a fiery temper. He was quick of body and mind. 

One day a group of horse dealers tried to sell Alexander's father a beautiful horse. It was unmanageable. Time and again these experienced riders tried to mount Bucephalus (bū-sĕf'à-lŭs), which was the name of the steed, but in vain. Philip decided not to buy the horse, and the dealers were leaving the field. 

Alexander had watched the whole performance and, according to 

Plutarch, said: "What an excellent horse they lose for want of address and boldness to manage him." Philip, who heard the remark, said: "Do you reproach those who are older than yourself as if you knew more and were better able to manage him than they?" "I could manage this horse," replied he, "better than others do." "And if you do not," said Philip, "what will you forfeit for your rashness?" "I will pay," answered Alexander, "the price of the horse."

The group laughed at the boy, but the wager was made. Alexander approached the horse, and patted him gently on the neck.

He then turned him around so that he faced the sun. Then, with a nimble leap, he was on the horse's back. In a short time the fiery steed realized that he had a master. The boy's keen mind had seen what the others had failed to see that the horse was afraid of its own shadow. Philip rejoiced to see his son win and said: "O, my son, look thee out a kingdom equal to, and worthy of thy self, for Macedonia is too little for thee." 

Alexander rode Bucephalus in his campaigns through Greece, Asia Minor, Egypt, and Arabia. His famous horse was buried in India.

A Pupil of Aristotle. Four years of Alexander's boyhood was spent as a pupil of Aristotle (Chapter VIII). Under this great teacher the boy became interested in Greek culture, in science, and in literature. He loved to read the works of Homer. Aristotle inspired Alexander to spread Greek culture throughout the world. He taught the young prince that it was proper for the 

Greeks to conquer "barbarians," because in this way they would benefit by Greek civilization. A great friendship arose between the master and pupil. Later, while on his campaigns in the 

Orient, Alexander sent relics and specimens of plant and animal life to his teacher at Athens. Just a short time before Alexander's death this friendship was broken. Alexander in a rash moment killed Clitus, the man who had saved his life at the battle of 

Granicus. Clitus was a relative of Aristotle. 

The Young King's Leadership Accepted. When Alexander ascended the Macedonian throne, trouble awaited him. Many of the Greek city-states felt that it would be an easy matter to overthrow this youthful king. He proved them wrong in their belief, for he crushed one rebellion after another. When Thebes took the lead in one of these revolts, Alexander burned the city to the ground. The men were killed, and the women and children were sold into slavery. The only buildings left standing were the temples and the home of the poet, Pindar. Alexander's orders were to crush a revolt and not to war against Greek civilization. His leadership was now recognized by all Greece. He was ready to carry out the plans of his father, and he prepared for the campaign into the East.

In Asia Minor: on the Plains of Troy. - Alexander crossed the Hellespont into Asia Minor. He had an army of 40,000 soldiers. He visited the plains of Troy and was stirred with emotion by the sacred places of which he had read in his beloved Homer. He offered a sacrifice to Athena and placed a wreath on the tomb of Achilles. His army was victorious wherever he went. He defeated a Persian force many times larger than his own at the river Granicus. He freed the Greek cities 

RATIO

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

along the coast from the rule of Persia. His bravery at one time nearly cost him his life, but it made his soldiers love him. Alexander always took the lead in battle and was the first to set an example. If the soldiers were hungry and thirsty, he hungered and thirsted with them. 

The Gordian Knot. Alexander's first winter in Asia Minor was spent in Gordium, a city in the province of Phrygia (frïj'ï-à). 

In that town was preserved a very old wagon. According to tradition it had belonged to a tribal chief named Gordius and his son, Midas. They had been chosen by the gods to rule Phrygia. 

A pole was anchored in the ground, and a cord attached the yoke of the wagon to this pole. This cord was made from the toughest kind of fiber. The knot was so twisted and intertwined that no one had been able to untie it. Many had tried because the oracle had stated that "Asia belonged to the one who could untie the knot." Alexander decided to try.

A large crowd of Phrygians and Macedonians followed Alexander to the ancient relic. He examined the knot closely, while the crowd looked on silently. He was puzzled. Was he who had never met defeat to be balked before this curious mob because of a twisted rope? He drew his sword and with angry impatience cut the knot in two. His Macedonian followers shouted. Alexander had solved the problem. He was hailed as the emperor of Asia.

This was typical of Alexander's actions. He desired to get to the heart of things quickly. To "cut the Gordian knot " is an expression used today. It means to get to the point at once without wasting time in unnecessary discussion or argument. 

The Battle of Issus; the Siege of Tyre (333 B.C.). — In the spring of the year 333 B.C. Alexander marched south to Syria. 

There, at the narrow pass of Issus, he met the Persian king, Darius III. Darius had an army, it is said, of 600,000 men. 

He stated boastfully that he would crush this proud pretender from Macedonia. He brought with him his mother, his wife, and children to view the great victory. The Persians were

ancient city of Sidon defeated, and before the battle ended Darius fled from the field. in a cowardly fashion. His family were captured but treated with the greatest respect by Alexander. 

The victors moved south through the and laid siege to the walled city of Tyre. This city was located on an island about one mile from the coast. Now Alexander had to display his ability as an engineer. He had no fleet. So he built a large causeway (highway) between the mainland and the island. Within seven months the flourishing commercial city of 

Tyre surrendered. Other victories followed in Asia Minor, and then Alexander decided to invade Egypt. 

Alexander in Egypt: the Founding of Alexandria. The 

Egyptians were glad to be delivered from the yoke of Persia, so they welcomed Alexander. While in Egypt he founded the city of Alexandria, which was to become one of the famous cities of the world, southwest of the Delta. Alexander helped to lay out its market place. He planned where the temples were to be built for the Greek gods and instructed that one of them was to be dedicated to the Egyptian goddess, Isis. He wanted Alexandria to be a great commercial city. It was to replace Tyre as a trading center with Carthage and Italy. A reconstruction of a street in ancient Alexandria, which was made a great city by the Ptolemies. Students from all parts of the world came to Alexandria's museum and university.

Before leaving Egypt, Alexander marched across the Libyan Desert to Sinai. There he visited the temple of Zeus Ammon. 

He asked the oracle about his father and was told that Philip was immortal and that his murderers had been punished. The oracle called him the son of Zeus and the conqueror of the world. As a result Alexander showered the oracle and the prophets with gifts. 

The Battle of Arbela (331 B.C.). — Alexander left Egypt in charge of a governor and marched east into the very center of the Persian Empire. Two years had passed since the battle of 

Issus. During that time Darius had collected a vast army which is said to have numbered a million men. Alexander could get together but 50,000. Undismayed, the young conqueror decided to fight. The battle was like that of Issus, only on a larger scale. At a critical moment Darius fled again and was later killed by one of his own soldiers. The leaderless Persian army was defeated and scattered. Persia had a new master, Alexander, now called the "Great." 

The Spoils: Susa, Babylon, Persepolis. He marched south to 

Susa (soo'så) and Babylon. Vast stores of gold and silver were captured, and many of the private soldiers gained immense fortunes. At Persepolis, the capital of ancient Persia, the spoils amounted to about $125,000,000. It took 5000 camels and mules to transport this wealth of material. Alexander avenged the burning of Athens one hundred years before by ordering the destruction of Persepolis. 

Alexander's Dream. - Alexander still longed for more worlds to conquer. He started for India, but his tired and weary army refused to go farther. He was forced to return, and he established a temporary capital at Babylon. There he dreamed of a great world empire composed of all nationalities, who would learn to know and understand each other. He put his idea into practice by making the Persians equal to the Greeks. This offended his friends, and many quarrels arose. But he replied by marrying a Persian princess and advised his friends to do the same.

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

His Death. Alexander planned a campaign into the Arabian Desert. This was to take him eventually to Carthage, Italy. and the Pillars of Hercules. But he was taken suddenly sick with a fever. His friends realized the seriousness of his illness and rushed to his bedside. He died without naming a successor. He was only thirty-two years old. No one was prepared for his death, and the world from Macedonia on the west to India on the east was without a leader. 

The Division of Alexander's Empire. The generals of Alex ander tried to carry out his plans for a campaign in the west. It ended in a complete failure. The leaders quarreled and eventually seized parts of the empire for themselves. In some instances they and their successors ruled as kings for many hundred years. This was especially true in Egypt where the kingdom of the Ptolemies (tŏl'ê-mēz) was established. Of this we shall read more later.

The Death of Demosthenes. When Demosthenes heard of 

Alexander's death, he again called upon the Athenians to throw off the Macedonian yoke. Athens responded, the people revolted, but their attempt failed. Demosthenes fled. The pursuing soldiers found him in the act of writing a letter. They did not realize that the end of the pen which he was biting contained a deadly poison. He reclined in his chair and covered his face with his cloak. The great Athenian orator and leader had killed himself.

The Achaean League; the Constitution of the United States. 

The rulers of Macedonia were becoming weaker. Eventually the Achaean League was formed. This league was to have an important bearing on the history of the United States. Both 

Alexander Hamilton and James Madison, the father of our own

Constitution, studied it. These men received many of their ideas for our present government from the methods used by this ancient union. The Achaean League was more than an alliance. It was a real federal republic. (By "federal" is meant a government with a strong central power such as we have in

Washington. In a republic the people who rule and represent the people are elected.) The league included most of the Greek states except Athens and Sparta. 

The Constitution of the Achaean League. Each city-state retained its equality and independence. Each had its own government in home affairs. Its officials were elected by the citizens. A central or federal government took care of the general powers of the league. It decided questions of war and peace, conducted other relations with foreign countries, and had charge of the army and the navy. 

The Executive Department Saw That Laws Were Obeyed. 

The federal government of the league consisted of a general (president) and a council (cabinet) of ten members. The general and his council saw that the laws were obeyed. 

The Boulé or Senate. A boulé or senate of about one hundred twenty members prepared measures for the assembly. It also managed foreign affairs. 

The Assembly. - The assembly consisted of all the people. 

Each citizen had one vote. This body passed all federal laws and elected all federal officers. 

The Shadow of Rome. The Greeks had tried to unite on many occasions. The Achaean League was their most successful effort. But the Greeks were beginning to learn when it was too late that in union there is strength - "united we stand, divided we fall." By this time Rome, the growing power in the west, had conquered Italy. It was steadily extending its power westward. In 146 B.C. Greece became a Roman province. 

The Hellenistic Age. But the period between Alexander and the Roman conquest was another great period in Greek history. 

It is known as the Hellenistic Age. The language and civilization which arose in the Orient (Syria, Egypt, and Asia Minor) because of Greek influence on native customs, laws, and institutions were called Hellenistic. Greece had remained pure Greek, and the word "Hellenic" is used to describe it. The mixture of Greek and Oriental culture, however, was called Hellenistic,

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

Ptolemy I (ruled 323-283 B.C.). — Egypt was, by far, the most important division of Alexander's empire. Of all the generals who inherited parts of his realm, Ptolemy was the most able to govern. He established a kingdom in Egypt and took the name of Ptolemy I. He had ideals similar to those of his great leader and did his best to carry them out. 

The People of Alexandria. - Ptolemy brought 100,000 Jews from Jerusalem to Alexandria. His kind treatment and honest policies won the respect of these new immigrants. He, like 

Alexander, wanted to fuse different races and bring about a better understanding among nations. Alexandria became a city of a million inhabitants, composed of Egyptians, Jews, Greeks, and other Oriental nationalities. There was a general respect for the opinions and beliefs of other people; for example, although the Ptolemies worshiped Greek gods, they permitted the religion of the Egyptians and of the Jews. 

Pharos: the Lighthouse of Alexandria. In the mind of its founder Alexandria was to be the great commercial center of the Mediterranean world. As a symbol of this belief Ptolemy constructed a gigantic lighthouse on the near-by island of Pharos. 

It was built from stone, probably taken from the same quarries used by the old pharaohs to construct their pyramids. The structure was more than four hundred feet high and was divided into three parts. The lowest part was square and about twenty stories high. The second part was eight-sided and six stories high. The top was round and ended in a peak from which a beacon light burned day and night.

The lighthouse at Pharos was a famous landmark for fifteen hundred years. It

It was destroyed by earthquakes about 1325 A.D. Pharos was included among the seven wonders of the ancient world. 

The Museum of Alexandria. - Alexandria became famous as a center of learning. A remarkable school called the "Museum, dedicated to the Muses, was built by the Ptolemies. It contained large rooms fitted out for the study of art, astronomy, and

A reconstruction of the lighthouse at Pharos which was considered one of the seven wonders of the ancient world. mathematics. There were large ponds where students could see and study many forms of sea plants and many sea animals. 

There were beautiful parks with all kinds of trees and flower gardens. The long winding walks and resting places were a delight to the scholars who gathered here from all parts of the world. 

The Library: Translation of the Hebrew Scriptures. There was also the renowned Alexandrian library. It contained more than 500,000 rolls. (Greek books were made like those of ancient Egypt, see Chapter IV.) A copy of almost any book written up to that time was found here. The Ptolemies tried to collect the complete works of all Greek authors. They paid high prices for these volumes. Valuable books, lost or

A part of the library of ancient Alexandria. Compare it with our modern libraries. It is said that an attempt was made to include a copy of every important book of the ancient world in the Alexandria library. stolen, would eventually turn up, it was said, at the library of 

Alexandria. 

Scribes were kept busy rewriting copies of scarce manuscripts. It was here that the old Hebrew scriptures were translated into Greek by seventy famous Jewish scholars. They were given a separate and private section of the city in which to live and work. This noted translation was called the "Septuagint" (sep'tû-a-jint) after the seventy scholars who accomplished the task. 

Hippocrates and the Medical School at Cos. A branch of the Museum, or University of Alexandria, was the Medical school located on the island of Cos. The greatest teacher at this school was Hippocrates (hi-pok'ra-tēz). He had aided 

Pericles during the plague at Athens. The Persian king had offered him a large sum of money to become his physician. 

But Hippocrates preferred to help his countrymen without pay. 

Hippocrates is called the "Father of Medicine," because he treated his profession as a science. He did not believe that evil

spirits caused disease and taught his students, who came from many nations, that epidemics and disease were brought about by failure to observe the laws of health. He convinced them that it was necessary to understand the organs of the body if they were to understand the causes of sickness. He compelled his students to take an oath, still known to doctors as the Hippocratic Oath.

In this oath they promised to use their knowledge to save lives and to aid mankind whenever they could. Great advances have been made in medical science since those days at Cos. But these first laws of Hippocrates laid the foundations upon which medical science has been built. 

The Daily Life of Alexandria. — The daily life of Alexandria was greatly affected by the library and the Museum with its 14,000 students. The people were alert and progressive. 

Their houses were comfortable and supplied with running water. 

The women had washing machines to lighten their burdens on "wash day." The streets were cleaned and watered. There was a fire department which used a water pressure system. 

A large clock in the public square told the time of day. It is thought that this was either an old Egyptian shadow clock or Babylonian water clock. The sanitary conditions presented quite a contrast with those of Athens. 

Euclid: "No Royal Road to Geometry." - While the people were applying the principles of practical science in their city, the students were following the lectures of several great scientists in the university. One of these was a teacher of mathematics and the founder of geometry. His book of straight lines, angles, and circles was built up step by step. He went from simple problems to more difficult ones. This remarkable book, more than 

2,000 years old, is still used as a text in many of our public and private schools. Its contents are still an aid to scientists in many fields.

Ptolemy I was so much interested in Euclid (u'klid) that he became his pupil. The affairs of state took up most of the
