Archimedes, the teacher, scientist, and engineer. He was one of the great pioneers of our modern Machine Age. king's time. Geometry required hard work and study. One day the monarch asked if there was not some easier method to learn his problems. To this Euclid replied: "There is no royal road to geometry." 

Archimedes (287-212 B.C.): Specific Gravity. - Archimedes 

(är-ki-me'dez) was a teacher of mathematics and an engineer. 

His home was at Syracuse on the island of Sicily. But he was associated with the University of Alexandria. Archimedes was a great inventor and discovered many laws of physics which have made possible some of our modern inventions. 

Several stories have been told about Archimedes and his inventions. A few of his principles were discovered in attempts to aid his friends. An interesting story deals with King Hiero of

Syracuse. This proud king suspected his goldsmith of cheapening the gold in his crown by mixing it with another metal or alloy. King Hiero realized that if any one could prove this, that person was Archimedes. The problem puzzled the scholar for a long time. Then suddenly, while taking a bath, the answer came to him. Overjoyed he rushed into the street shouting, "Eureka! Eureka!" ("I have found it! I have found it!") 

What did Archimedes find? He had noticed that his bath tub was full to the point of overflowing. When he immersed himself in the water, a certain amount overflowed. He realized that there was a relationship between his body and the amount of water it displaced. He began to weigh different metals first in the air and then in the water. He divided their weight in air by the loss of their weight in water. The result was called the "specific gravity" of the different metals. Gold was found to be 19-3 times as heavy as water and silver 10-5 times as heavy. 

Thus if the specific gravity of Hiero's crown was less than 19-3, Archimedes knew that the king's suspicions of the goldsmith were correct.

The Pulley, Lever, and Fulcrum. - On another occasion the king wanted to launch a beautiful boat. Large groups of men had failed to move it from the sandy beach. The ruler called upon Archimedes. By a system of ropes and pulleys he soon had the boat afloat on the sea. 

Later, when he discovered the law of the lever and fulcrum, n he exclaimed: "Give me a place to stand and I will move the world." 

The Revolving Screw; the 

Burning Mirrors; the 

The screw pump invented by Archimedes.

Wedge. He further aided his native city by building siege guns which threw huge stones a great distance. He invented a pump

GREEK CIVILIZATION 

to keep ships from sinking. It was based on the principle of a revolving screw, that is, a round blade placed in a cylinder. To it a handle was attached, and, as it was turned, the blade revolved, forcing the water out of the boat. It was said that by a system of powerful mirrors he was able to burn an enemy's fleet. The wedge and many other inventions began with this great scientist. 

Eratosthenes, the Geographer: Measures the Size of the 

Earth. Eratosthenes (ĕr-a-tos'the-nez) was the librarian at the University of Alexandria. He took the lead in developing scientific geography. With a large amount of material before him, he wrote a complete geography. His work included Europe, Asia, and Africa. He was the first person to draw lines on his map to show position. Thus he was the founder of our present use of longitude and latitude. He was aided by the progress which was being made in astronomy. Eratosthenes measured the circumference (distance around) of the earth. He was fairly accurate because he measured it within a few miles of its actual circumference.

How He Measured the Earth's Circumference. Eratos thenes noticed that when the sun reached its farthest point north, it shone on a certain day at noon straight down to the bottom of a well. This well was located at the First Cataract of the Nile. In Alexandria, a little less than five hundred miles away, at the same time the sun was 7.2 degrees from its zenith (or away from the vertical line). When Eratosthenes discovered this, all that remained was a simple problem in arithmetic. The 

Babylonians had taught that there were 360° in a circumference. 

Then 7 was of that circumference. Therefore, according to his reasoning, five hundred miles was of the earth's circumference. Then 50 X 500 or 25,000 miles would solve the problem.

He had come within a few miles of our modern calculations. 

Greek Civilization. There were other famous men and great events during this period. But enough has been told to show how the work of Alexander and the Hellenistic Age has affected modern times. The men who toiled and worked in those

King Fuad Street, one of the main business streets of modern Alexandria.

Old Testament has come down to us. They have made possible many of our present comforts and conveniences. This was due to their studies in medicine, architecture, geography, science, and inventions. They have given us ideas in government that are still in use. They taught the people of their own day and of future generations to reason out things that seemed unexplainable. They glorified the human body, studied it, and realized the importance of its care. The Greek ideas of truth, goodness, and beauty showed themselves in their literary works and art. 

But more important those ideals were lived by the men who have made Greece live.

BACKGROUND FOUR ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

Our giant dirigible turns westward and glides toward the Tiber River in Italy. We leave Greece with its myths and legends. We leave the land still ringing with the memories of 

Miltiades at Marathon, Leonidas at Thermopylae, and Socrates teaching in the market place. We leave the bema where Demosthenes thundered his great Philippics and the theater where the plays of Aeschylus delighted the Athenians. We leave while the empire of Alexander is still crumbling, but not before the great teachers of the University at Alexandria make their great contributions.

A heavy mist will obstruct our view of the early Romans. But through their legendary history we shall see the little trading post on the Tiber pass from a kingdom through more authentic accounts to a republic and then to an empire. We shall follow the tramp of the Roman legions over magnificently paved roads to the far-flung boundaries of the Roman world. We shall see 

Roman engineers building miles and miles of aqueducts to carry water to imperial Rome. We shall see them constructing their sewerage systems, their reservoirs (waterworks), their triumphal arches, and their massive domed buildings. We shall see these 

Romans making laws and forming governments that will be copied by nations today. 

We shall see Roman bankers, merchants, and artisans at work. 

We shall see oppressors of the people and champions of the people rise and fall. We shall note the coming of the Christians, their worship, their martyrdom, and their influence. And finally we shall see the Roman legions crushed by the invading Germanic tribes. This event will produce a new background in world history. 155

CHAPTER X  

The civilization of the Nile Valley, of the Fertile Crescent, of Crete, and of Greece was handed on to Rome. And Rome, the "Eternal City" on the banks of the Tiber River, increased her heritage and gave it to a modern world. 

Roman Influence on Modern Life. Sixty per cent of the words in the English language are derived from the Latin. Many 

English and American poems, plays, and novels have as their background Roman mythology and history. To appreciate our literature, we must understand the Romans. The architecture of ancient Rome can be traced to many buildings in America, the most famous being that of the Capitol dome in Washington Roman laws and ideals of manhood and womanhood, of courage, loyalty, patriotism, and honor have come down through the years to influence modern life. 

The Italians. About 2000 B.C. the Italians, a branch of the 

Indo-European race, settled in Italy. They were of the same stock as the Hellenes and had wandered down from central Asia. 

The Hellenes settled in Greece while the Italians pushed westward, crossed the Apennines, and occupied Italy. 

The Latins. The most important branch of the Italian race 

Iwas the Latin. The Latins settled near the Tiber River. 

They tilled the soil, cultivated the vine, and learned to press out the oil from the olive. The Latins were united either by blood ties or neighborhoods into clans. These clan villages had their own local governments but were not independent. They were a part of a larger community called a "canton." Each canton had a capitolium, that is, a strong fortress situated on some hilltop. There the clans gathered to trade and to barter, to hold their religious festivals and games, and to attend the courts of justice. Tradition states that thirty cantons were united into 156

SE Map of Italy showing its mountains, rivers, and divisions. 4 the famous Latin League. At the head of this league was Alba 

Longa (the long white city), the oldest of the Latin cantons. 

The Etruscans. To the north of the Latin League was 

Etruria (e-troo'ri-a), and its inhabitants were called Etruscans (ê-trus-kǎnz). They were a more civilized people than the 

Latins. They had a written language and built fine roads, dikes, and beautiful temples. They manufactured bronze and terracotta castings. Their ornaments of gold, ivory, and amber discovered in old tombs are proofs of skilled workmanship. Their

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

merchant ships traded with the Mediterranean world. They were familiar with the chariot and were a fierce and warlike people. 

Roma. The homes, fields, and herds of the Latins were continually endangered by the attacks from the Etruscans. So one day a brave band of adventurers set out from Alba Longa to establish an outpost against these northern invaders. They chose a site about seventeen miles from the mouth of the Tiber. 

And there, on one of the seven surrounding hills called the Palatine (păl'à-tin), they founded the fortress town of Roma. This frontier settlement was later to become "Mistress of the World.” 

The Kingdom. - The Romans in time conquered the surrounding towns, and Rome became the capital of Latium. The Albans (inhabitants of the old mother city) and the Sabines (sāʼbīnz) from central Italy were admitted into the union on equal footing with the Romans. Each of these groups represented a tribe. 

Each of the three tribes was divided into ten curiae or wards, and each curia (singular for curiae) into ten houses or gentes. 

The tribes selected a king who ruled over the kingdom for life. 

He was the head of the government, commander of the army, and the high priest of the nation. 

The Senate. The king selected the heads of the clans to advise him on matters concerning community or clan interests. 

This group was known as the senate, and its members, about three hundred in all, were called senators. They were chosen from the most influential families and held office for life. 

The Assembly. - Another council representing the ten curiae of each tribe, thirty in all, could be summoned by the king. This was called the "comitia curiata." This assembly voted by curiae, that is each curia had one vote, making a total of thirty. 

The "comitia curiata" elected the king, voted for war or peace, and enacted laws.

The Patrician. The members of the most influential families were known as "patricians." They were the privileged class.

They held the high political offices, served in the army, and administered religious rites.

The Client. Subject people, former prisoners of war called clients, cultivated the land and became merchants and tradesmen. 

The client did not necessarily live with the patrician. But he was protected by and attached to a patrician household. In return he aided and helped to support his patron. 

The Plebeian. Former inhabitants of conquered towns who did not belong to any curia were known as "plebeians." They were free and could engage in commerce or work at their trades. 

However, they had no political rights and were often oppressed. 

They could not marry into a patrician family.

The Slave. Below these three classes stood that most sorrowful figure in human history -the slave. He had no rights which any one was bound to respect. He was worked, bought, sold, and beaten at the will and pleasure of his masters. 

Early Roman History. Such were the social classes in the early Roman kingdom. But very little is known of this period since no written records were kept. Later historians had to depend upon oral traditions which had been handed down from father to son. Livy (liv'i), the great Roman historian who lived from 59 B.C. to 17 A.D., had to depend upon legends for most of his account concerning early Rome. Thus we find many conflicting tales and truth mingled with fiction in the story that follows. But even the traditions and the legendary kings of 

Rome have become a part of world literature. We shall now read some of the legends.

Aeneas and Ascanius. - After the Trojan War Aeneas, one of the heroes of Troy, fled from the burning city. He became the leader of a large company of refugees. He had many adventures and suffered many hardships. Finally, with his aged father and little son, Ascanius (ǎs-kā'nĭ-ŭs), his sailors anchored their ships on the shores of Italy. Aeneas married a Latium princess, founded a city, named it after his wife, and ruled for many years. After his death Ascanius ruled for a while, but, becoming dissatisfied, left and began a settlement on a neighboring hill which he called Alba Longa. The descendants of Ascanius

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

Aeneas, the hero of Vergil's Aeneid, carrying his father and leading his son in the flight from Troy. ruled in Latium for nearly three hundred years until the time of 

Numitor (nu-me'tor). 

Romulus and Remus. Numitor was driven from the throne by his younger brother, Amulius (ȧ-mū'li-us). Then, to make sure of his crown, Amulius murdered Numitor's son. The daughter of Numitor, Rhea Sylvia, was made a vestal, or priestess, in the temple of Vesta, the Latin goddess of the home and hearth.

To Rhea were born twin sons whose father was the war-god Mars. Amulius feared that the boys when grown would claim the throne. So he put their mother to death and ordered the babes thrown into the Tiber. They were saved by a she-wolf who carried them to her cave and nursed them. The twins were rescued finally by a shepherd, and Romulus and Remus,

as they were called, grew to manhood. They drove Amulius from the city and placed their uncle Numitor on the throne again. Then the boys decided to start a kingdom of their own.

Rome. Romulus and Remus went to the hills near the 

Tiber River where they had been rescued and suckled by the she-wolf. There the brothers and their followers quarreled about the naming and the government of the city. They agreed to let the gods decide. Romulus went to the Palatine Hill and Remus to the Aventine.

After waiting many hours, Remus and his followers saw six vultures fly overhead. A cry went up from the Aventine Hill proclaiming Remus king. But in the midst of the cheering 

Romulus saw twelve vultures, and the shepherds on the Palatine claimed him as king. Another quarrel took place. Remus claimed the throne because he had seen the birds first. Romulus argued that he was the favorite of the gods because he had seen twelve instead of six vultures. Neither would give in, so Romulus took a sacred plow and plowed a furrow around his town on the Palatine. Along the furrow he built a wall, and the inclosed settlement he called Rome after his own name. Remus, mocking his brother, leaped over the wall and was killed by Romulus. 

And tradition credits him with saying: "So shall every one die who dares to leap over these walls." 

Vergil's Aeneid. Many years later the greatest of Roman poets, Vergil (70-19 B.C.), wrote his great epic, the Aeneid. The poem deals with the life and adventures of the legendary hero, Aeneas. It is one of the world's literary masterpieces and has influenced the literature of modern times. Vergil worked on this poem for ten years and on his death bed wanted it destroyed because he said "it lacked perfection."

A Symbol of Rome. The story of Romulus and Remus and the founding of Rome is oral tradition. But the she-wolf suckling the babes became a symbol in Roman history and was renowned throughout the Roman world both in sculpture and painting.

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

Statues of the scene adorned the public squares of many cities, and it was painted on the sails of the flagship of all Roman fleets. 

The Sabine Maidens. - Livy, the historian, continues the story. Romulus wanted more inhabitants for Rome. He built an asylum, or place of refuge, on Capitoline Hill. He invited 

The Romans erected bronze statues in honor of the she-wolf. This one is at the 

Capitol, Rome. 

dissatisfied people, outlaws, and runaway slaves from neighboring towns to come and settle in Rome. Over all Romulus reigned as king.

As yet, however, there were very few women in the new city. If Rome was to continue, wives had to be found for the new citizens.

The surrounding communities refused to allow their daughters to marry the Romans. This angered Romulus, and he proclaimed a great festival in honor of one of the gods, to which he invited every one to attend. The Sabines from near by flocked to the city with their sons and daughters. They were curious to see the games and refuge of the lawless. While the unarmed Sabines were watching the sports in the arena, Romulus gave the word to the Romans, who, well armed, rushed in, captured the maidens, and drove the men from the city. Soon the Sabine women became accustomed to their fate and married their captors.

The Sabine War: Tarpeia. The Sabine men returned home, determined to rescue their women. Later, their army marched to the gates of the Roman fortress. There they met 

Tarpeia (tär-pe'yȧ), the daughter of the warden of Rome, as she went to draw water from the spring outside the walls of the city. 

They asked Tarpeia what reward she wanted to unlock the gates and let them in. "Give me," she said, "what you wear on your

left arm," meaning their heavy gold bracelets. This was agreed, and, late at night, Tarpeia opened the gates and the Sabine army passed into the city. As they passed, they threw not only their bracelets but their shields at the girl, and she was crushed to death. The rock on Capitoline Hill where Tarpeia died was known as the Tarpeian Rock. Thereafter all Roman traitors were taken to this rock and thrown over its side to die. 

Latium United. The two armies waged a fierce battle within the city. The result was in doubt until the Sabine women rushed in between the warring ranks. The husbands on the one side and the fathers and brothers on the other ceased fighting. 

Peace was declared, and the two towns were united under Romulus. The Sabines occupied the Quirinal (kwir'i-năl) Hill. 

Later Latium was conquered, and, as we have seen, these three tribes were organized as the Latin kingdom. 

The Tarpeian Rock. 

It was from this rock that Tarpeia was thrown to her death.

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

The Passing of Romulus. It was Romulus who, according to legend, organized the government into tribes and curiae, established the senate and the assembly, and divided the people into patricians, clients, slaves, and plebeians. He reigned for thirtyseven years. Then one day, the myth runs, he was reviewing his troops. A heavy storm arose, and when the skies cleared, Romulus had disappeared. He had been taken to heaven in a fiery chariot by his father, Mars. 

Numa Pompilius. After the passing of Romulus (about 

753-716 B.C.), he was followed by six other legendary kings. The first of these was a Sabine named Numa Pompilius. Numa organized the religious institutions of Rome. He appointed a pontifex maximus, or high priest, whose duty it was to supervise the religious customs and institutions of Rome. This is a good place to learn something of the religion of the people. 

Roman Religion; Vesta. The Romans believed that the spirits of their dead ancestors guarded the household. Food was placed on the family hearth, which was believed to be the home An imaginative painting of a group of vestal virgins teaching others the duties of a vestal. Their duties and training lasted for thirty years. many privileges. 

They had

of these spirits. The goddess of the hearth and home was Vesta (věsta), to whom a large circular temple was erected. Six maidens of patrician or noble blood were chosen to serve the goddess for thirty years and to take care of the sacred fire. 

During their term of service the "vestal virgins" could not marry. They were, however, the most privileged persons in 

Rome. They had the power of life and death in certain cases and could interview the rulers at any time. Their duties were exacting because the Romans believed that should the sacred fire go out calamity would befall the city. For this reason if a vestal broke her vows she was buried alive. 

Roman Deities. While ancestor worship played an important part in the life of the Romans, their religion was greatly influenced by the Greeks who had settled in southern Italy. There was a great deal of similarity between the Greek and Roman gods. 

Like Zeus, the Roman god Jupiter ruled in the sky; he was the god of gods. One of his brothers, Neptune, ruled the seas, and another brother, Pluto, was the god of Hades (the underworld). Juno, the wife of Jupiter, helped the affairs of earth.

Roman goddess of beauty, corresponded to the 

Greek goddess Aphrodite. him manage 

Venus, the 

A bronze coin used by the Romans about 200 B.C. It was worth about two cents. On it was the head of Janus.

The favorite god of the Latins was Mars. 

Great feasts were held in his honor, and the first month of the Roman year, March, was named after him. Another favorite was Janus (ja'nus), the double-faced god, who protected the home. The first month of our present calendar received its name from him. Other deities were Vulcan, the god of fire and forge, Saturn, the god of agriculture, Ceres (se'rēz), the goddess of grain, Mercury, the fleet-footed messenger of the gods and the god of commerce, and Minerva, the goddess of wisdom. Mythology. -The early traditions or myths which relate to the religion, origin, and adventures of the gods of ancient people

This imaginative drawing shows Tarquinius Superbus purchasing the Sibylline Books, which the Romans believed gave advice and told about the future. They were closely guarded in a stone vault under one of the temples and were written in Greek. are called "mythology." Numerous and fascinating are the stories to be found in Roman mythology. Our own literature contains many references to those tales, and many words in our language such as "vulcanize," "cereal," and "martial" owe their origin to them.

Temple of Janus. - Numa erected a temple at the entrance to the Forum in honor of the god Janus. In time of war its doors were opened and in time of peace they were closed. Only twice from the days of Numa to the time of the Emperor Augustus (31 B.C.) were the doors of the temple closed. This gives us some idea of the warlike nature of the Romans. 

The College of the Augurs. The religious institutions of 

Rome played an important part in the actions and destiny of the nation. The most important of these was the College of the

Augurs (ô'gurz). Its members were sixteen persons appointed from the patrician class. The augurs, as its members were called, lived in a large temple in Rome. They pretended to predict the future by studying the habits of birds and animals. No law could be passed, no war could be declared, or any public act begun until the augurs had discovered the will of the gods. 

They became very influential and powerful and could veto the acts of the king or the senate and prevent expeditions, marriages, and business transactions. 

The Sibylline Books. One of the later kings (Tarquinius 

Superbus) purchased from a strange woman three mysterious books which were said to contain prophecies of the city's future. 

The Sibylline Books were consulted in times of grave danger. 

The Pontiffs and Heralds. The College of Pontifices was headed by the pontifex maximus and his assistants, called pontiffs. 

They regulated the time, the form, and the proper procedure for

In Rome there were many drains built of stone. These carried away the city's refuse.

All emptied into the Cloaca Maxima, the central drain which led to the Tiber River. This famous sewer is composed of three layers of arched stone, thirteen feet high. The floor is paved with stones and is thirteen feet wide.

all Roman worship and festivals. The members of the College of Heralds acted as messengers throughout Rome and to foreign countries.

The Reforms of Numa. Numa, it is said, reformed the calendar, divided the land among the people, and encouraged the Romans to do their own farming. He organized the workers of different trades into guilds. The men of the guilds met to improve their workmanship, care for their people, and take an active part in the social life of the community. 

The Cloaca Maxima. There is little to tell of the next two kings. Under them Rome fought with her neighbors a great deal of the time and accomplished little. The last three of the legendary kings are known as the Tarquins. With them Roman history becomes more reliable; written records were begun and some of their public works still remain. The most famous of these is the 

Cloaca Maxima, a large sewer. It drained the damp, unhealthy, and marshy valley between the Palatine and Capitoline hills. It passed through the city into the Tiber, ridding Rome of its refuse and waste. Its tunnels were of stone. This sewerage system, which is still in use, was more complete than any system used in Europe until the nineteenth century.

Tarquinius Priscus. - The first of the Tarquins, Tarquinius 

Priscus, began the massive wall that surrounded the city. He erected a magnificent temple on Capitoline Hill, which covered more than eight acres of ground. Its marble walls, solid brass doors, and statuary were for centuries an object of wonder to the Romans. 

Circus Maximus. - Another achievement of Priscus was the

Circus Maximus (the great circus). This was a huge athletic

The Roman Army. - Under the Tarquins the army was reorganized. All the freemen were divided into two groups. The first was organized as a reserve corps of older men. The second, 66 comprising the younger men, was formed into a corps for active service. A company, consisting of one hundred men, was called a century," and the individual soldiers, "centurions." Each of the two corps consisted of eighty-five centuries or 8500 men. 

Since a legion was made up of 4200 soldiers, there were four legions in the two corps. The wealthiest men were required to use their horses and to form the cavalry. The middle class had to purchase full suits of armor: helmets, shields, and coats of mail. 

The poorer people were not very well armed. 

The Census. It was during this period that the people were gathered together every five years and a census (Latin word for "count") was taken. All citizens had to give a record of themselves, their children, and their property. 

The End of the Kingdom. The last of the Tarquins, Tarquin ius Superbus, was a tyrant. Against him the Romans rose in revolt. 

The king who was away at war was deposed and sentenced to exile. 

Three times the Tarquin king tried to regain his throne. The first time his armies were defeated on the Roman frontier. The next year Lars Porsena, prince of Clusium, captured Janiculum and besieged the city. But Horatius and his two comrades held the 

Etruscan army at bay, while the Romans cut down the bridge leading into the city. Lars Porsena so admired the bravery of the defenders that he concluded a peace treaty and withdrew. third conflict was fought along the shores of Lake Regillus. 

Romans were again victorious. Tarquinius fled and soon died.

CHAPTER XI THE EARLY ROMAN REPUBLIC 

(509 B.C. to the Carthaginian Wars, 264 B.C.)

When the Republic Was Young. — When the Roman republic was established (509 B.C.), Darius I was king of Persia. During the twenty years before that event the Egyptian and Babylonian empires had fallen before the armies of Cyrus the Great. And the twenty years following witnessed the Persian invasions in Greece. 

Therefore the republic was young when Miltiades at Marathon and Leonidas at Thermopylae gained immortal fame.

Forming the Republic: the Consuls. After the overthrow of 

Tarquinius Superbus the people reorganized the government. 

The assembly, which consisted of patrician and plebeian citizens, elected two patrician magistrates, called consuls, to serve for a term of one year. The consul, with the exception of a few priestly duties, enjoyed the same powers as the former kings. When either consul appeared in public, twelve lictors marched in front of him. These lictors were soldiers, and each one carried a bundle of rods (called fasces) bound together, in the middle of which was an ax. The fasces had signified the power of the king to flog and put to death. This power was now given to the consuls.

The Dictator. There were two things about the powers and the election of the consuls that proved unsatisfactory at the beginning. In the first place each consul had the right to veto or prohibit the orders of the other. In times of grave public danger such as war, this division of power greatly weakened the government. To correct this situation, a dictator was appointed. He was elected by the senate for a term of six months. His authority while in office was absolute, and he was preceded by twenty-four lictors. 175

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

The Plebeians Object to a Sham Democracy. The second cause for dissatisfaction came from the plebeians. As members of the assembly they were allowed to vote for consuls and laws. 

But the candidates had to be patricians, and all other officers as well as the laws had to be approved first by a patrician senate. 

The plebeians saw through this trickery, and so they complained. 

They represented the large part of the population the great middle class, the merchants, the skilled and unskilled workers, and the tillers of the soil. The plebeian complaint was heard and resulted in the Valerian laws.

These laws received their name from 

Valerius, the first dictator, and were passed in the first year of the republic. 

Lictors. Notice the fasces. 

The Valerian Laws. The first of the 

Valerian laws stated that any Roman citizen ordered to be flogged (corporal punishment) or put to death (capital punishment) had the right to appeal the case to the assembly of the people. This limited the power of the consuls, so that within the limits of Rome the lictors carried the fasces without the ax and lowered them in the presence of the people. 

It

The Writ of Habeas Corpus. This first law of Valerius is sometimes called the Habeas Corpus Act of the Romans. gave to the people the right of appeal to justice. In the United 

States today no person can be kept in prison unless the court decides that the prisoner be held for trial. The courts try the case as soon as possible. The Constitution of our government (section 9, paragraph 2) reads: "The privilege of the writ of habeas corpus shall not be suspended unless when in cases of rebellion or invasion the public safety may require it." 

Only once in our history has this privilege been taken from the people, and then by President Lincoln during the Civil War.

THE EARLY ROMAN REPUBLIC 

177 

Other Valerian Laws. Another law of Valerius limited the amount of money which could be placed on the people as fines. 

This checked a great deal of graft, that is, the securing of money by unjust means. The plebeians, before this act was passed, were often overfined by the officials, who kept the surplus. A third law provided that officers, called “quaestors,” be appointed to care for the finances of the state. 

While the Va 

The First Secession of the Common People. 

lerian laws marked a progressive step in democracy, or government by the people, they did not touch the real grievance of the common people. The formation of the republic was accompanied by unrest and many border wars. The plebeians were forced to serve in the army without pay. Very often they had to leave their herds uncared for and their fields untilled. When they returned, many of them found their buildings and crops destroyed. Naturally they wanted to get a new start in life. 

To do this, they had to borrow money from the patricians, who charged them high interest rates. If the plebeians failed to meet their interest or pay their debts, their lands were taken from them and given to the patricians. They, themselves, were either thrown into prison or sold as slaves. 

Such were the conditions when they were again called to join their legions and repel an enemy. They refused and marched to a neighboring hill, later called the Sacred Mount. There they decided to build a city of their own. The patricians became alarmed. They well realized that these common people were the backbone of the army and the government. Without the plebeians the republic would fall. So peace envoys were sent to the Sacred Mount.

A Parable on Coöperation and Peace on the Sacred Mount. 

The plebeians at first could not be persuaded to return. Then, it is said, one of the patrician senators told the following story of the Body and the Members.

Once upon a time the members of the body resolved that they would no longer support the stomach, which did nothing at all

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

but lay at ease while they toiled. "We will not carry it," said the feet. "We will do no more work for it," cried the hands. "And we will not chew a morsel for it, even if the food is placed between us," declared the teeth. They kept their word, and the stomach suffered; but they suffered with it, and soon they, too, began to waste away. From this story the parties realized that it was necessary to work together and to coöperate. 

The plebeians decided to return if their wrongs were righted, and to this the patricians agreed. 

The Sacred Covenant. A treaty known as the Sacred Cov enant was made between the two classes. All old debts were canceled, and the plebeians who had been imprisoned or enslaved were set free. Two and later ten plebeian officers, called 'tribunes," were elected, one from each tribe.

The duty of the tribunes was to guard and protect the plebeians against any injustice. They had the right to annul the acts of any patrician magistrate. Their lives were declared to be sacred. 

If any one injured a tribune, he received the "curse of the gods" and could be killed. The tribunes were not allowed to go more than a mile beyond the city walls. Their homes were open day and night as a refuge for plebeians who had complaints to make. 

In time their power increased until they vetoed measures even of consuls whose acts were against the interests of the people. 

Once, according to legend, a patrician was exiled by the tribunes. During a famine in Rome he suggested that no corn be distributed among the "plebs" (plebeians) until they gave up their tribunes. As the years went by, other victories were won by the lower order. They were permitted to marry within the patrician class, and eventually they gained the office of consul. 

The duties of the tribunes became so heavy that officials called "aediles" were appointed to help them. 

The Decemvirs and the Twelve Tables. — Today all the laws of our cities, states, and nation are published. No one can evade punishment by claiming ignorance of the law. But in the early days of the Roman republic many people suffered because

THE EARLY ROMAN REPUBLIC 

179 they had no way of knowing what was lawful or unlawful. In 

451 B.C. the masses demanded that the laws be written down. 

A committee of ten men, called the First Decemvirate (decem is the Latin for "ten" and vir, for "man"), was appointed for that purpose. The decemvirs were to have complete control of the government until the work was complete. 

This first committee was unable to finish the task at the end of the first year. A new decemvirate was elected, and the laws were gathered into a code and written down on twelve bronze tablets. The tablets were placed in the Forum where every one could read them. After this no Roman could complain of ignorance of the law, and every Roman school boy had to learn the contents of the Twelve Tables. 

The Story of Virginia. - The Second Decemvirate under the leadership of Appius Claudius ruled like tyrants and refused to give up their power. The tyranny of Claudius and his attempt to enslave the plebeian maid, Virginia, infuriated the people. The army rebelled, overthrew the decemvirate, and restored the consuls and tribunes. The story of Virginia inspired Lord Macaulay to write another of his famous Lays of Ancient Rome, which reads in part: 

Of all the wicked Ten still the names are held accursed, And of all the wicked Ten Appius Claudius was the worst. 

He stalked along the Forum like King Tarquin in his pride; 

Twelve axes waited on him, six marching on a side. 

The townsmen shrank to right and left, and eyed askance with fear

His lowering brow, his curling mouth, which always seemed to sneer. And then the following which explains something of the everyday life of the Romans.

Over the Alban mountains the light of morning broke; 

From all the roofs of the Seven Hills curled the thin wreaths of smoke;

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

The city gates were opened; the Forum, all alive 

With buyers and with sellers, was humming like a hive; 

Blithely on brass and timber the craftsman's stroke was ringing, And blithely o'er her panniers (baskets) the market girl was singing, And blithely young Virginia came smiling from her home; 

Ah! woe for young Virginia, the sweetest maid in Rome!...

Roman Roads. The Romans made progress in other fields besides government. Over a hundred years passed, and another 

Appius Claudius in 312 B.C. began the great system of road building which helped to make Rome famous. The Romans were in the midst of conquests that were eventually to make them masters of Italy and the Mediterranean world. Appius had a paved road constructed from Rome to Capua (kǎp'û-à) in order to move his legions quickly from the capital to the conquered province of Campania. The road was extended later to Brundisium (brun-dĭzh'ĭ-um) in southern Italy - a port where vessels left for Greece and Egypt. The road was called the Appian 

Way after Appius.

The construction of military highways was carried on by the successors of Claudius until the common saying was: "All roads lead to Rome." The roads were wide and made of concrete. 

Gutters along the sides carried off the dirt and water. Paths were constructed along these highways for the convenience of people who had to walk. This scheme avoided danger and traffic congestion when the legions were on the march or when groups of caravans and pleasure seekers were traveling to and from Rome. Along the roads were watering troughs, resting places, and milestones which indicated the distance from one place to another.

Aqueducts: the Roman Waterworks. A year later, 313 B.C., Appius began the construction of aqueducts. The drinking water for Rome until this time had been secured either from

Paved roads led from Rome to various parts of the Roman Empire. They aided in reaching and governing distant provinces.. Some of these roads are used today. Modern roads of concrete and macadam bind the great American nation more closely. Good roads make the city and the country districts closer neighbors. 181

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

wells or from the Tiber. The increased population and unsanitary condition of the water led Roman engineers to seek new and better methods of gaining their water supply. 

The first aqueduct brought water from the Sabine hills, eleven miles from the city, and was constructed underground. In time eleven aqueducts were built at enormous expense and a great amount of labor. In some instances the water was brought from a distance of sixty miles. The conduits were built of masonry (brick and stone) and lined with Roman cement. The water flowed by gravitation, and so the aqueducts, which wound around hills, across plains, through tunnels, and underground, had to be graded.

Large ponds were constructed at convenient places for the water to pass through and deposit any impurities which had been collected. The cleansed water emptied into large reservoirs within the city, from which it was carried by lead or earthen pipes to smaller reservoirs in different parts of Rome. A Roman aqueduct at Segovia (sā-gō'vyä) in Spain, built more than 2000 years ago, is still in use today. 

The Principle of the Siphon. It is sometimes said that the old Roman engineers, because they graded or sloped their aqueducts, did not understand the principle of the siphon. By that principle a pipe or tube can be bent in such a way as to permit water to be transferred from a lower to a higher level. 

While there is some doubt concerning the Romans' knowledge of the siphon, it is certain that they did not have the advantage of the great pumping engines used by modern engineers. Therefore we must marvel with Pliny, the Roman historian, who wrote several hundred years after Appius Claudius began the first aqueduct: "If any one will carefully measure the quantity of the public supply of water for baths, reservoirs, houses, trenches, gardens, and suburban villas; and, along the distance which it traverses, the arches built, the mountains perforated, the valleys leveled, he will confess that there never was anything more wonderful in the whole world."

Aqueducts like that above supplied ancient Rome with a water supply. In contrast notice our modern system by which water is carried through valley and over mountain to the distant city. 183

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

Sack of Rome, 390 B.C. While Rome was working out reforms in government, building roads, temples, and aqueducts, and increasing her trade and commerce, she was waging relentless warfare with her neighbors. Her armies were not always successful. In 390 B.C. the Gauls, a barbarian people living in what is now France, left their homes in the northland and marched toward Rome. They defeated the Roman legions, broke through the gates, burned, pillaged, and plundered homes, shops, and temples. The citizens fled and left the city to the conquerors.

From the Roman historian Livy the following story has come : 

Brennus, king of the Gauls, received word that his country was being attacked by other barbarian tribes. He offered to leave 

Rome if he were given 1000 pounds of gold. The Romans agreed. While they were weighing it out, Brennus threw his sword on the scale and cried, "Woe to the vanquished." At that moment Camillus, who had just been made dictator, arrived with his troops and cried, "Rome is not ransomed with 

HARREN 

An imaginative drawing of the attack on Rome by the barbarians. Find a battering ram and a machine for hurling rocks.

THE EARLY ROMAN REPUBLIC 

185 gold, but with steel!" The Gauls were driven out of Italy, and the Romans returned and rebuilt their city. 

Rome, Master of Italy. - One hundred years after the sack of Rome by the Gauls, all of Italy except a few of the Greek colonies in the south had been conquered by the Romans. The 

Latin language, religion, and customs were adopted by the conquered people, and Roman citizenship was conferred on these new allies. Rome was now master of Italy and so turned her attention to world affairs.

CHAPTER XII  (264 B.C.-31 B.C.) 

Carthage. Carthage, which had been founded as a Phoenician colony about 800 B.C. (Chapter I), was located in northern 

Africa, near the present city of Tunis. By 200 B.C. Carthage and its numerous colonies were known as the Carthaginian Empire. 

Her territory included much of northern Africa, all of Spain, and the islands of Corsica, Sardinia, and western Sicily. She was the great rival of Rome for supreme power in the Mediterranean world. Three terrible wars, beginning in 264 B.C. and ending with the destruction of Carthage in 146 B.C., were fought between the two countries. 

First Punic War (264-241 B.C.). - The Carthaginians like the founders of their city, the Phoenicians, were wonderful sailors.

The Roman fleets at first were no match for those of 

Carthage. But on land there were no better fighters in the world than t the men who comprised the legions of Rome. Toward the end of the first war the Romans hit upon the following scheme. 

They built wooden

Bh a l 4502 jadian upom ads Roman galley. These were manned by slaves, criminals, and prisoners of war. bridges which were fastened to the masts by a rope in the front part of the ship. When a Carthaginian vessel drew near, the rope was loosened, and the bridge, which had huge spikes on the 187

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

end, fell and fastened itself to the deck. The Romans then rushed over the bridge and fought in legion formation the same as they did on land. By this method Rome won a decisive naval battle. Carthage asked for peace, and the first Punic war was ended after twenty-three years of warfare. 

Hannibal. But it proved only a truce. About twenty years later the struggle began anew. During this war the 

Romans had to face the greatest of Carthaginian generals Hannibal, the son of Hamilcar Barca. The Barca family was one of the wealthiest and most influential families in Carthage. 

Hannibal at the age of nine was taken by his father to one of the temples, and there with his hands on the altar the boy swore eternal hatred to Rome. At the age of twenty-six he was placed in command of the army. 

The Second Punic War (218-201 B.C.). Hannibal crossed the 

Alps with an army estimated at 100,000 men, military supplies, camp followers, and about forty war elephants. Town after town fell before the invaders; the inhabitants of many others surrendered and joined his victorious army. Snow, ice, mountains, rapidly flowing rivers, and Roman legions could not stop his advance. 

At one time he was trapped in a valley surrounded by Romans. 

During the night, it is said, Hannibal fastened burning torches to the horns of several thousand oxen, and had them driven up the mountain side. The Romans rushed after the oxen, which they had mistaken for fleeing Carthaginians, while Hannibal and his army marched to safety. He

At last the dreaded invader was near the gates of Rome. sent hurriedly to his brother, Hasdrubal (hăs'droo-bal) for reenforcements, but at Metaurus the Roman legions rallied and defeated Hasdrubal before he could join Hannibal. Hannibal was now forced to retreat.

A Roman general, Scipio, suggested that an army be sent to Carthage. The Carthaginians, he said, would become alarmed and send for Hannibal. The Romans agreed, and Scipio was sent to Africa. Hannibal was recalled. And in the year 202 B.C.

the army of Scipio defeated the army of Hannibal at Zama. 

The second Punic war was over, and Scipio was given the title of 

Africanus a great honor. Han- 

nibal was driven into exile by the Romans and later committed suicide. 

The Events Leading to the Third Punic War. The second 

Punic war broke the power of 

Carthage. One of the terms of the treaty that closed the conflict was that Carthage should never attack an ally of Rome. Taking advantage of this, the king of Numidia, a Roman ally, made several attacks on Carthage, destroying and plundering homes and farms. The Carthaginians Hannibal. 

appealed to Rome. The Roman ambassadors sent to judge the case decided in favor of Numidia. 

The attacks continued, and Carthage in desperation made war on the Numidians. Their armies were defeated and sent "beneath the yoke," which was the sign of defeat. Besides, they had broken the treaty with Rome. 

(National Museum, Naples.)

The Carthaginians sent an embassy to the Roman senate, who commanded that three hundred children of the noblest families be given up as hostages if the Carthaginians wished to maintain their independence. The Carthaginians did this. A 

Roman army then appeared before their city and demanded. further conditions, namely, that they give up their weapons and all military equipment within the city. This, also, was agreed to by Carthage. Then the final decree of the Roman senate, jealous of the growing commerce of their rivals, was read: "Carthage must be destroyed, but the inhabitants might build a new city provided it was located ten miles from the

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

coast." The Carthaginians were furious and decided to defend their city. 

Roman Conquests: Carthage Destroyed. Hasdrubal, who had been exiled with Hannibal by the Romans, was recalled and given command of the army. The temples were turned into workshops. Statues, vases, cooking utensils, and everything that could be used for war purposes were collected and recast for the defense of Carthage. The women cut their long hair and braided it to make strings for bows. No sacrifice was too great, and they held out for nearly four years. Then the city was taken; 50,000 men, women, and children were sold into slavery. Carthage was burned.

Cato, the Roman censor (an officer who took the census and managed the finances and public works), had for years ended all his speeches with the words "Carthago delenda est" (Carthage must be destroyed). There was no need now for him to repeat these words. Carthage was destroyed. North Africa,

Spain, Sardinia, Corsica, and Sicily were Roman. At the same An artist's conception of Roman gladiators. These men were trained to figh man or beast for the pleasure of the Roman crowds.

time all the Greek colonies in Italy as well as Macedonia, Corinth, and Asia Minor were conquered and became subjects of the one-time little trading post on the banks of the Tiber. 

Post-War Conditions in 

Rome. 

Roman supremacy was bought at a great sacrifice. 

Thousands of Romans had been killed, and there were thousands of widows and orphans.

The returned soldiers and common people were again forced to sell or give up their lands. 

Captive slaves poured into 

Rome and the average Roman, unable to compete with slave labor, was thrown out of work. 

He wandered from place to place and finally drifted into Cornelia and "her jewels," Tiberius the larger cities. and Caius.

Social wars, slave revolts, and mob risings became common. 

Government officials became more arrogant and corrupt. About 

2000 wealthy Romans owned all the land, but they began to fear the great army of unemployed, and so they arranged public entertainments free of charge. Chariot races, gladiatorial combats (usually slaves, called gladiators, fought until one was killed), and free bread lines became common. 

But the great mass of Roman citizens wanted work and the right of a happy healthy family life. The times provided a champion for the people. 

Cornelia, Mother of the Gracchi. There was living in Rome during this period a wonderful woman - Cornelia, the daughter of Scipio Africanus. She had two sons, Tiberius and Caius

Gracchus (grǎk'us). Cornelia, until the boys were seven years of age, was their only teacher. She taught them honesty,

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

obedience, self-reliance, and reverence for the gods. She probably pictured the conditions in Rome and told them how much the Roman world needed the right sort of men. "I am called the daughter of Scipio. I wish to be known as the mother of the Gracchi," she is reported as saying. 

Tiberius Gracchus. Tiberius grew to manhood, joined the army, fought in the third Punic war, returned home, took up the cause of the people, and was elected tribune. "You are, as Romans, called 'lords of the earth,' without possessing a single clod to call your own. The poor have fought to maintain the luxury of the rich." This was a part of his speech to the 

Roman people. 

The Agrarian Law. As tribune he ordered all the land holders who held more than three hundred acres of land illegally to give up the excess. The government lands were then divided into eight-acre plots and given to the poor. The new owners and their descendants were forbidden to sell their property. This was done to keep the land in the hands of the poor. A small rent was charged, but conditions in Rome became much better. This law was called the Agrarian law from the Latin word ager meaning "land."

The wealthy opposed the law and were bitter towards Tiberius. At the next election this friend of the people was assassinated by men hired by the senate. 

Caius Gracchus. - Tiberius was dead, but his influence lived. 

The wealthy were more careful in the future, and the poor realized their strength. A few years later when the senate attempted to undo some of the reforms of Tiberius, the people arose and elected his brother Caius as tribune. The reforms of Tiberius were restored, and others made. But Caius met the fate of his brother; he too was assassinated. History has fulfilled the wish of Cornelia, for she is known as the "mother of the Gracchi." 

Caius Julius Caesar (100-44 B.C.). — After the Gracchi civil wars occurred, barbarian invaders threatened Rome, and political upheavals took place. The patrician and plebeian fought for power, ambitious men sought to gain control of the government, 

---

and finally a strong man was able to do this. He brought order out of chaos, extended the boundaries of the republic, made improvements, and added to "the grandeur that was 

Rome." His name was Julius Caesar (sēʼzar). 

His Mother's Influence. Julius Caesar was born July 12, 100 B.C., and was descended from one of the great patrician families of Rome. At an early age his father died, and his education and care were left to his mother, Aurelia. Caesar owed much to the influence of his mother, and his love and respect for her never wavered throughout his life. Tacitus, a Roman historian, compared her with Cornelia, mother of the Gracchi. 

Caesar's Marriage and Exile. At the age of seventeen Caesar married Cornelia, daughter of Cinna, one of the great leaders of the democratic party or the party of the people, which at that time was fighting the aristocratic party or the party of the patricians. Sulla, the leader of the latter party, commanded Caesar to divorce his wife, but Caesar refused. Thus he took his stand with the common people and was exiled. 

Starts for the University of Rhodes. - After he was pardoned, Caesar returned to Rome and continued his education, studying Greek philosophy and rhetoric. In Rome as in Greece oratory played an important part in politics, and Caesar was ambitious to win political honors. So he set sail for the island of Rhodes to study oratory at its university, for he was intent upon mastering the art.

Captured by Pirates. As Caesar's ship neared Miletus, it was captured by pirates, and Caesar was taken prisoner and held for ransom. While he waited for his friends to gather money, he told his captors that he would hang them as soon as he secured his freedom. The ransom was paid, and Caesar was freed. He equipped two ships, gathered several crews, returned to Miletus, captured the pirates, hanged them, and took back his ransom money.

His Various Offices. He went on to Rhodes, completed his course in oratory, returned to Rome, and was elected quaestor,

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

A painting of a chariot race by Ulpiano Chéca. Great crowds came to enjoy these races. 

Later he became an aedile. As aedile he had charge of the public games. In order to please the crowds and add to his own popularity, Caesar borrowed over a million dollars and gave entertainments that thrilled and pleased the voters. They looked upon him as the coming leader of the democratic party which had been crushed by Sulla. He was made pontifex maximus, and as such his power and influence increased. A little while later he became a praetor, but Caesar was heavily in debt. 

Provincial Governor, or Propraetor of Spain. He secured the office of governor or propraetor of Spain. As provincial governor he had to collect the taxes in his province and turn a stated amount over to the home government. Everything that he could collect above that amount was considered legally his own. In a little over a year he had taxed the people of Spain so highly and had received so much money in excess taxes, that he was able to pay his debts and return to Rome. 

The First Triumvirate (60 B.C.). Caesar now desired to be consul. He got in touch with Pompey, a great military leader, and with Crassus, the wealthiest man in Rome. Caesar's

1 popularity, Pompey's reputation, and Crassus's wealth made these three men the real rulers of Rome. They are known as the First Triumvirate (tri, meaning "three," and vir, "man").

Government positions and favors could be secured only through one of these three men. They were the real "political bosses" of 

Rome and had developed a modern political machine. But each was jealous of the other; each desired to be sole ruler. Caesar now did a strange thing. He asked to be appointed governor of all Gaul. Pompey and Crassus were surprised but granted his request. They were both glad to have him leave. He was already too popular and powerful. 

Caesar in Gaul. - Caesar waged eight campaigns against the 

Gauls, conquered three hundred tribes, took thousands of captives, and extended the Roman boundary to the Rhine River. 

He crossed the English Channel into Britain. In all the lands of his conquests the Romans left Roman law, customs, ideals, art, architecture, and science. Caesar wrote the history of his campaigns, of the people he conquered, and of their life and Caesar meets Vercingetorix, a Gallic chief. This painting is by Lionel Royer.

leaders. These pamphlets were sent to Rome, and the story of 

Caesar's Gallic wars was the talk of the city. Throughout western Europe and America they are read today by high-school boys and girls who study Latin. At the time they added to the author's popularity. Cicero, the famous orator of that day, in a public speech cried, "Let the Alps now sink; the gods raised them to shelter Italy from the barbarians, but they are no longer needed." What did he mean?

While Caesar was in Gaul 

Pompey, the Consul of Rome. 

serving his second five-year term, Crassus, who had been made governor of Syria, was killed. The senate, fearing Caesar, sent for Pompey, made him a member of the aristocratic 30 

Cleopatra. From a painting on slate found at Tivoli, Italy. party, and elected him sole consul and dictator of Rome. "The Die Is Cast!" 

Caesar sent word to the senate that he would return and offer himself to the people as a candidate for consul. He was ordered to disband his legions or be declared a public enemy.

Roman law stated that no proconsul could enter Italy with an army. The Rubicon 

River marked the forbidden boundary in the north. 

Caesar knew that if he obeyed the law and went to Rome without troops, he would be captured and killed. Therefore, when Pompey refused to disband his army, Caesar with one legion crossed the Rubicon and is said to have exclaimed: "The die is cast!"

Made Dictator for Life. The people flocked to his standard. 

Pompey and the aristocratic party fled. Caesar followed and

defeated them in Thessaly. Caesar followed Pompey into 

Egypt, and there the former consul was assassinated. Caesar fell in love with Cleopatra, the Egyptian queen. This celebrated beauty was to play an important part in his life. After quelling several revolts for Cleopatra, he returned in triumph to Rome, crushed rebellions in his own provinces, and in 46 B.C. was made dictator for life. 

The Power of Caesar. The power of Caesar in the next few years was absolute. He was a king in everything but name. 

He dressed in purple robes, which were a symbol of royalty. 

Statues were erected in his honor, and he was worshiped as a god. of the Romans.

The Reforms of Caesar. Caesar strengthened the government by extending the rights of Roman citizenship to people in the provinces. He increased the number of senators and permitted Gauls to sit in that body. He reduced the taxes in the conquered countries and allowed their peoples to decide how their taxes were to be raised. In Italy he divided the public lands among the poor and compelled slave holders to employ one free man for every two slaves. Landless people were given lands in Roman colonies. He provided work for the jobless by constructing public buildings, roads, and public baths. He reformed the calendar by making the year consist of three hundred sixtyfive days and adding one day every fourth year. "Thou too, Brutus!" - Death of Caesar, 44 B.C. In the midst of his reforms and improvements he was killed. He was accused by many of desiring to destroy the republic and of making himself king. Others claimed Cleopatra was prompting him to remove the capital to Alexandria in Egypt. 

He was attacked in the senate chamber by some seventy senators. He defended himself until he recognized his friend 

Brutus, a staunch republican, among the assailants. Then he is said to have exclaimed, "Et tu, Brute!" ("You too,

Brutus"), and covering his head received the knife stabs until he fell dead.

ROMAN CIVILIZATION The Roman Empire. 

Caesar was gone, but with his death passed even the form of the Roman republic. His immediate successors threw away all pretense of democracy, and the Roman Empire was established.

CHAPTER XIII  A Young Legionnaire Hastens to Rome. Caesar's grand nephew and sole heir was Gaius Octavius. At the time of his uncle's death Octavius, then eighteen years of age, was serving with the Roman legions in Greece. He hastened to Rome to claim his rights. His action led to a quarrel with Mark Antony, the close friend of Caesar. Antony claimed that most of Caesar's money was public money and should be returned to the treasury. 

Octavius was not discouraged. He catered to the people, gave magnificent shows, and became a popular idol. Soon the two men settled their differences and became friends. They with another influential citizen, Marcus Lepidus, formed the Second 

Triumvirate. 

Friends of the Republic: Cicero and Brutus. The three men agreed among themselves to rule the Roman world for the next five years. Cicero, the renowned Roman orator and patriot, objected to this. He placed himself at the head of the senate, led the cause of freedom, and tried to save the republic. His efforts were in vain. Civil wars followed, and Cicero was murdered. Brutus and Cassius, who had collected an army after the death of Caesar, were defeated at Philippi in Macedonia, 42 B.C. Both men committed suicide by falling on their swords.

Death of Antony and Cleopatra. Lepidus was accused of being a traitor and was expelled from the triumvirate. Like 

Caesar and Pompey before them, Octavius and Mark Antony waged war against one another for supreme power. Antony went to Egypt, where he became infatuated with Cleopatra. 

He divorced his wife Octavia, sister of Octavius. It was rumored in Rome that he intended to overthrow his fellow dictator and move the capital to Alexandria. Octavius declared war, and in RA-14 201

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

Painting of Antony and Cleopatra by Alma-Tadema. It shows Cleopatra meeting Antony at Tarsus, in Cilicia. 1 a naval battle off the western coast of Greece, near Actium, the fleets of Antony and Cleopatra were defeated, 31 B.C. They killed themselves. Egypt became a Roman province, and Octavius returned home in triumph. 

Caesar Augustus: Roman Emperor. He was hailed as the savior of Rome. The senate gave him the title of Augustus (august) and Princeps Civitatis, the first of all citizens. They named one of their calendar months, August, after him. He was also known as Imperator (commander of the army), from which we get the word "emperor." Augustus, as he was now called, retained many of the forms of the republic. But this was mere show; the senate and all other government officials were under his absolute rule. The Roman republic was gone forever; a 

Roman empire had taken its place. 

Caesar Augustus was not a tyrant. He encouraged commerce, art, and literature. He established peace throughout the empire, and, for the second time since the days of Numa Pompilius, the

temple of Janus was closed. This does not mean, however, that his long reign of forty-four years (31 B.C.-14 A.D.) was one of continual peace.

The Battle of Teutoburg Wood (9 A.D.).

Roman general who conducted himself in a very despotic manner. He issued harsh orders and imposed heavy taxes upon the 

Germans, who under their brave leader Hermann (Arminius) revolted. In the dark, thickly grown forestland of Teutoburg Wood they routed and destroyed a Roman army of three legions (about 18,000 men).

When the news of the disaster reached Rome, the people were greatly alarmed. Augustus, wearied and tired with the problems of state, exclaimed: "Varus! Varus! give me back my legions." 

Importance of Teutoburg Wood. The battle of Teuto berg Wood was to play an important part in future events.

The Germanic tribes, unlike the Celts and Britons (Chapter XII), retained their indepen- Augustus Caesar. Under him the Rodence. They did not become man Empire was established. 

Roman. Their descendants within a few centuries were to overthrow Rome. They were to lay the foundations for several countries in modern Europe, especially England. 

The Golden Age of Latin Literature. The reign of Augustus marked the conclusion of the "Golden Age" of Latin literature.

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

It was in this age that Cicero lived, wrote, and delivered his famous orations. The poet Vergil produced his Aeneid (see Chapter X). In this great epic Vergil tried to add to the glory of Rome by connecting its history with that of the old Trojan heroes. His ideas were taken from the Iliad and Odyssey of Homer. 

The historian Livy wrote his history of Rome at this time (see Chapter X). It consisted of one hundred forty-two books, but only thirty-five of these have been preserved. The history, called the Annals of the Roman People, began with the founding of Rome and ended with the events of 9 B.C. Livy had a delightful way of telling his stories, but they are not always reliable. The two other famous writers of this age were the poets 

Horace and Ovid. 

The Roman Baths. Augustus had many beautiful buildings erected. “I found Rome a city of brick and left it a city of marble," he said. Large public baths were built. They contained swimming pools, hot and cold water, gymnasiums, and reading rooms. The citizens gathered at these places to discuss business affairs and politics. Several famous Roman baths still exist in parts of the world which at one time were under Roman control. The wealthy citizens had their own private baths, where they entertained lavishly. These bathing palaces were called "thermae" by the Romans.

The Colosseum. The two most famous structures of the 

Roman Empire were the Colosseum (kõl-o-sēʼum) and the Pantheon (păn-the'ŏn). The Colosseum, a vast amphitheater, was begun in the reign of Vespasian (69-79 A.D.) and was completed by his son, Titus (79-81 A.D.). It seated over 40,000 people, covered six acres of ground, and its walls were one hundred fifty feet high. The floors of the Colosseum were to run red with the blood of wild animals, gladiators, and Christian martyrs within the next few centuries. 

The Pantheon. - The Pantheon was completed under Hadrian (117–138 A.D.). This temple, dedicated to Mars, Venus, and the

Julian emperors, is still standing. The dome of the building is

Ruins of the Colosseum. 

The Pantheon of Agrippa, Rome. 

205

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

one hundred forty feet from the ground and is made of concrete. 

Within the building were many altars dedicated to the gods. 

The tiled floor, gilded dome, and numerous statues made this a place of awe and beauty. The home of Thomas Jefferson at 

Monticello and the main building of the University of Virginia, which he founded, are both patterned after the Pantheon. 

Money Changers and Banks. - Business was brisk during the days of the empire. Sailors and merchants from the wide world visited Rome. The people came from strange lands and carried with them strange money. This they could not use in Rome, so money changers reaped a profit by exchanging foreign currency for Roman coin. The money changers were also bankers. They had in their office buildings large brass-bound boxes. The 

Romans deposited their money in these banks and used a system of checks very much as we do today. People in need of money could borrow from the bankers at the legal rate of interest, which was twelve per cent.

The Roman Home. In the Roman home was an inner hall. 

Over its doorway there was generally inscribed the word "Salve" (Welcome). This inner hall led into a large oblong room called the "atrium." An opening in the ceiling of the atrium let in light, fresh air, and rain. Beneath this opening was a tank of beautifully colored marble. The rain water dripped from the roof into the tank through terra-cotta pipes. The floors were laid in mosaics, and the walls were painted and frescoed. The household gods and the altars dedicated to them were located around the tank.

Small, stuffy bedrooms surrounded the atrium. At one end of this main room was a row of pillars heavily curtained which led into a small but well-furnished room where the family records were kept. It contained elegant chairs and costly tables and adjoining it was the master's library. A small lobby led from this room into an open court. There could be found beautiful fountains and graceful statues. Round the court was a broad porch where the family could sit and entertain visitors. Beyond the court lay

the large dining room, and in the rear of the bedrooms was the kitchen. At the far end of the court stately pillars marked the entrance to the famous gardens of these Roman homes. 

The Public Dole. There was, in the days of Augustus, a bad side to Rome. The city had its slum districts with their crowded tenement houses, poorly built and poorly ventilated. 

There were thousands of poor people dependent upon public charity for food and pleasure. The government bought corn and helped to feed more than 200,000 of them. Storehouses for this purpose were located throughout the city. an added expense to the taxpayer and a drain on the public treasury.

This dole was 

The Masses Entertained. - Augustus himself says: "Three times in my own name, and five times in that of my sons and grandsons, I have given gladiatorial exhibitions. In these exhibitions about 10,000 men have fought. . . . I have given hunts to African wild beasts in the circus, the Forum, and the amphitheater and about 35,000 beasts have been killed. Today people are entertained in modern stadiums like this. What sports do you suppose they may see here?

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

"I gave the people the spectacle of a naval battle beyond the Tiber. . . . In this contest thirty beaked ships, triremes, and biremes were engaged, besides more of smaller size. About 3000 men fought in these vessels besides the rowers." What the emperor was doing to appease the masses is what other wealthy men of Rome were doing also. 

Jesus: His Childhood. But by far the most important event in the reign of Augustus occurred in the distant province of Syria. This event was the birth of Jesus of Nazareth, the founder of Christianity. The exact date of his birth is not known, but it was sometime between 6 and 4 B.C. The place was Bethlehem of Judea, a little village near Jerusalem. Jesus's early life was spent in Galilee, where he followed the carpenter's trade. 

His Ministry. After thirty years of obscure life Jesus entered on his public ministry, which lasted only three years. He taught those basic things of life which never change — love, truth, and honor. He brought a message of "Peace on Earth." He preached the brotherhood of man and the dignity of labor. Although he drew many to his following, he antagonized the leading men by criticizing their morals. Moreover he disappointed the hopes of many because they expected him to lead a rebellion against Rome and declare himself king. By false accusations his enemies brought him before the Roman administrator of Judea, Pontius Pilate. Pilate unwillingly consented to his death. In the most tragic scene in history he gave up his life, nailed to a cross on Calvary's hill. 

Christian Missionaries: Paul. The teachings of Jesus were carried on by his disciples and missionaries. They carried his message to all parts of the world to Africa, Asia Minor, Greece, and Rome. They traveled far to the north and converted thousands among the barbarian tribes. Wherever they went, they suffered hardships and persecutions, but they carried on and seldom faltered. The most ardent Christian missionary was 

Paul. He was a great scholar, a fearless leader, and, although a 

Jew, he was a Roman citizen with rights as such. He determined

to go to Rome, to the very heart of the empire, and preach the gospel of the Nazarene. 

There were many

The Romans Persecute the Christians. 

religious sects in Rome, but the government did not interfere with any of them. The Christians, however, refused to send their children to the Roman temples or to the Roman schools. They criticized the gladiatorial com- bats in which human lives were cruelly sacrificed. They refused to join the Roman legions because they were opposed to war. In fact they taught many things that the Romans felt would cause the breakdown of their empire. 

For that reason the government ordered them to cease their teaching or suffer. The 

Christians preferred the latter.

But the Christians could not escape the long arm of Roman law. Thousands of them were taken captive and sent to the arena, where they were thrown to the wild beasts. The first great persecution took place during the reign of Emperor Nero. 

It is believed that Paul met his death at that time. 

Gaudentis, Architect of the Colosseum. - Gaudentis (gô'dentis) was the architect who built the Colosseum upon the request of Vespasian. The poem, Death of Gaudentis, on pages 210 and 

211 gives a vivid description of the Colosseum and the methods used in a vain attempt to stamp out Christianity.

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

Christianity, the State Religion of Rome. Scenes such as those which have been described did not stop the progress of Christianity. It gained many converts, and churches were established throughout the empire. In 311 A.D. the Roman emperor of the East (the empire had been divided) ordered all persecutions to cease. The following year the emperor of the West, Constantine the Great, accepted Christianity. A little more than a century later it was adopted as the state religion. 

The Empire for Sale (193 A.D.). — A long line of emperors followed Augustus. Some of them were excellent rulers, but many of them were unfit to rule. The government became corrupt, its citizens indifferent, and the army unable to defend its borders. In 193 A.D. the praetorians, or soldiers who guarded the capital, assassinated the ruler and offered the empire to the highest bidder. It was purchased by a wealthy senator named Didius 

Julianus. He gave 25,000 sesterces (about $1,000) to each of the 12,000 praetorians or about $12,000,000 in all. Septimius 

Severus, leader of the troops along the Danube River, hastened to Rome. He defeated the praetorian guards and had them banished from the country. He overthrew the corrupt Julianus and was proclaimed emperor by the senate. 

The Walls of Hadrian. The barbarian tribes in western 

Europe were a continual threat during the days of the Caesars. 

About seventy years before the sale of the empire the Emperor Hadrian built his two famous walls. They were erected to protect the Romans from the feared invasions of those wild, northern tribes. The first of these fortifications was located in Britain. 

It was seventy miles long, and its strong ramparts and high watchtowers were guarded by Roman legions. For three hundred years they held in check the warlike Picts and Scots in the north. 

Parts of this wall still remain to remind travelers of those bygone days. Hadrian next constructed on the continent a stone wall and chain of forts three hundred miles long. This fortification strengthened the Rhine-Danube frontier. Its defense, however, was to sap the main power of Rome in later years.

Alaric at the city of Athens. This is a painting by Thiersch.

The Sack of Rome by Alaric the Goth, 410 A.D. But the 

Roman walls, forts, legions, and methods of warfare could not stop the march of the barbarians. In 410 A.D. Alaric (ǎl'a-rik), chief of a Germanic tribe called the Goths, invaded Rome. 

They pillaged and plundered the city. They loaded their wagons with gold, silver, tapestries, and furniture.

Alaric issued orders that no Christian church was to be touched and that no person who had sought refuge within its doors was to be killed. After a week of terror he left Rome and started south. He intended to cross over into northern Africa and found a kingdom. He was doomed to disappointment, for on the way he was taken sick with a fever and died. His soldiers buried him.

The Roman captives who aided in this work were killed so that no one would ever know the burial place of Alaric. 

The Vandals. In time the Visigoths (West Goths) were given lands in Spain and southern France. They intermarried with the Romans and established a kingdom of their own. These lands passed from the Roman dominions forever. The Goths were followed by the Vandals. These people marched into north 

Africa and conquered the Roman province located there. Their
