destruction of life and property was great. They took to their ships and became the most notorious pirates in the Mediterranean. Their actions were so unnecessary that even today we use the word "vandalism" to mean the willful destruction of property.

Attila the Hun and Leo the Great. The most feared and warlike of the barbarian tribes were the Huns. Their leader, Attila (ǎt'ĭ-là), was called the "Scourge of God." He boasted that the grass never grew where once his horse had trod. He sacked and destroyed town after town in Italy. He marched to the gates of Rome. The city was defenseless, and the emperor had fled. There was no brave Horatius to save the people. 

But there was a champion the fearless and unarmed Pope Leo the Great. He met Attila at one of the city gates. A long conversation was held, after which the Hun withdrew and marched north. 

The Battle of the Marne: Chalons 451 A.D. The Goths, the Franks, and the Burgundians (the last two were Germanic tribes) united with the Romans. The combined armies met 

Attila at Chalons on the Marne River. And there, in 451 A.D., Attila was defeated. Two years later he died.

The End of the Roman Empire of the West. In 476 A.D. the 

German tribes united, elected one of their chiefs as leader, and marched to Rome. They deposed the Roman emperor and divided the land. They took one third of all the farms or produce, just as they preferred. The Romans accepted their leader, Odoacer (ō-do-a'ser), as emperor. The next century was one of confusion - Rome was invaded by first one tribe and then another. 

What was the Roman Empire of the West dwindled down to the confines of Italy. The Roman Empire of the East, however, remained intact for a thousand years more. It fell before the Turks in 1453 A.D.

Another Background. — The civilization of Rome, like that of 

Greece and the ancient Orient, had to make way for a new people and new events. For the next five hundred years there was to

ROMAN CIVILIZATION 

be chaos and disorder in western Europe. The torch of civilization was to burn low during those years, but the foundations of modern Europe were being laid. Again we face another background and catch a glimpse of world events. The new scene is that of the Feudal Age among the Europeans of the west.

BACKGROUND FIVE  

We leave the Italian peninsula in the midst of the barbarian invasions. We have witnessed the events of more than a thousand years of Roman history. We saw the little trading post on the banks of the Tiber grow and organize first a kingdom, then a republic, and finally an empire. We watched the Roman legions proudly march throughout the far-flung boundaries of imperial Rome. We noted their government, religion, sports, amusements, literature, and scientific achievements. We saw the coming of the Christians, and learned about their worship, their martyrdom, and their influence. But all of this is crumbling as our airship passes over the Alps to bring us into new lands to meet new peoples and a new age.

Here we shall see disorder and confusion at first. There will be hundreds of petty rulers unable to establish order. Then several powerful leaders will point the way to better things. 

Later a new form of government, called feudalism, will be adopted. Castles will be built and guarded by knights in armor. 

The church, the school, the farm, and labor will all be drawn into this feudal system. And in the end we shall see order gradually taking place of chaos. This age will furnish the background for many of our modern laws, customs, languages, operas, court practices, governments, literary works, and ideals. 

We shall witness the beginnings of such countries as modern France, Spain, Germany, and England. We shall fight and worship with the Franks in Frankland and with such other Teutonic tribes as the Angles, Saxons, and Jutes. We shall see these tribes under Charles Martel stop the Mohammedan invasion, and later we shall see them united under Charlemagne (shär’lē-mān). 

In England a great nation will be welded under the guiding genius of an intrepid and brave leader, Alfred the Great.

RA-15 219

CHAPTER XIV  

Western Europe after the Fall of Rome. For three hundred years after the overthrow of the Roman Empire in the west, there was disorder and confusion in western Europe. During those years no one ruler was able to establish order. The untutored barbarians with their chiefs remained a law unto themselves. 

Finally in the year 800 A.D. the king of the Franks, Charlemagne (Charles the Great), became powerful enough to be acknowledged emperor of the Romans. He established a stable government in what is now Italy, France, Germany, and part of Belgium. But upon his death in 814 A.D. the government fell to pieces. His successors were unable to hold so vast a territory occupied by unruly people. Petty kings and nobles arose to dispute their claims. And soon western Europe was torn by warfare and strife. The years between the fall of the western 

Roman Empire in 476 A.D. and the discovery of our country in 

1492 are called the "Middle Ages," or 

Middle Ages," or "Medieval Times.'

Civilization did not make many strides forward in these years, although, as you read, you will find that many things of importance came about.

Feudalism and Its Needs. 

Those years from about 800 A.D. to 1300 A.D. were years of insecurity. Because there was no strong government, the life and property of the people were con stantly in danger. Robber bands roamed the countrysides, and pirate crews sailed the seas. Princes, farmers, merchants, and artisans became desperate. They sought ways and means o protecting their lands, their farms, their merchandise, and thei families. The system they adopted is known as “feudalism.” 

Castles, Walls, and Moats. - Towns under independent ruler were strongly fortified. Stone castles were built by princes and nobles. These castles were located on the tops of high hills 220

mountain cliffs, or other points of vantage. They were surrounded by high walls or deep moats (ditches) and protected by soldiers in mail armor. 

The farmers, merchants, and workmen sought protection on the land of these princes and nobles.

Influence of the Feudal 

Age. For five hundred. years every form of human activity was drawn into the feudal system. It influenced the government of the people, their lands, farms, and homes, their commerce and trades, their education, and their religious institutions. To understand the Feudal

Age, it is necessary to study the feudal system and learn how it affected the everyday life of the people. Then we shall 

ALUEEN 

A restoration of a medieval castle. A feudal lord built his castle like a fort. Notice the thick walls which made him feel secure. 

The moat, or body of water, around the castle was another protection. How did the drawbridge help? discuss the men and the events which made possible a few of our modern nations. 

Fiefs, Lords, and Vassals. The basic foundation of feudalism was the "fief." The fief, as a rule, consisted of land, although it might be a privilege of any kind granted by a ruler to a subject. 

In the case of the former the king would give to his strongest and most loyal subjects or to some powerful rival whom he feared a large tract of land. The men who received these territories would, in turn, parcel out the land to their followers, and so the process of division continued. Those who gave the land were known as lords, lieges, or suzerains; the persons who received it

The vassal is performing the ceremony of homage to his lord upon receiving his fief. Taken from a ninth-century manuscript. were called vassals or liegemen. Thus a man might be a lord and, at the same time, might be a vassal to some one from whom he had received a large fief. 

Homage and Investiture. 

When a lord conferred a fief upon a vassal, the procedure was marked by a very important and solemn ceremony.

With bared head the vassal knelt and placed his hands between those of his lord. He took an oath of allegiance (fealty), swearing to serve his suzerain in all things, even though his life were at stake. Because the vassal became the lord's man (Latin homo), this process was termed doing "homage." The ceremony was ended by the act of "investiture," which represented the lord's part of the contract. Usually some of the soil or a small branch of a tree from the fief was placed in the vassal's hand to symbolize his right and title to the land. The fief then became hereditary (handed down from father to son) in the family of the vassal.

Obligation of Lord and Vassal. - The chief obligation of the vassal in an age when warfare occupied so much attention was military service. The duty of the lord in these troubled times was to protect his vassal. The vassal had to be ever ready to

answer the call to arms. In times of peace the vassal would serve about one month of the year in preparation and drill, very much as our National Guards do today in America. The lord also called his vassals to serve on juries or to act as judges in his courts.

When a son fell heir to his father's fief, he was forced to pay a large sum of money to his lord, usually all the income from his lands for one year. This was called a "relief." If a vassal died without an heir, the land was returned to the lord. This was known as escheat," and we use the word today in connection with persons who die without leaving a will. Under these circumstances a certain percentage of their property is returned to the state. 66 Disloyalty on the part of the vassal was punished by taking his fief from him. The knighting of a lord's son or the marriage of his daughter was an occasion for great celebration and festivities. 

In order to pay these expenses, the lords exacted money or farm products from their vassals, known as "aids." 

Knight, Serf, Free Tenant. We have seen how one vassal after another divided his lands and exacted homage from some sub-vassal. The lowest vassal was usually called a knight, and his holding was known as a "knight's fee."

Living upon the soil and working it was a class of people known as "serfs." The status of the serfs differed in various countries and in different times. As a rule they were little better than slaves. In some cases they were given a home and a small plot of ground. They could not leave the land, and they could not be driven from it. If the fief changed hands, the serfs were included in the deal along with the buildings and cattles. Serfs were also called "villeins."

Besides the serfs, there were "free tenants" who tilled the soil. Free artisans made shoes, clothing, and other necessities. 

Carpenters, bricklayers, and merchants carried on their various occupations. It was a busy, workaday world with but little leisure for the lower classes.

The Manor House. The lord occupied a large tract of land called the "demesne." This was his own private territory. 

Here was situated the castle or manor house, the home of the lord and his family. Because of the many wars the lords had to live in castles for protection. These castles were usually hard 

EST MIDDLE FIELD EAST 

to reach and were often surrounded by moats, which could be crossed only by a drawbridge.

The main room of the castle or manor house was the long, dark hall in which was an open open fireplace. 

Here on a winter's night the family sat around a cheerful blaze. Often traveling minstrels sang while the lord entertained the owner of his fief or bother worthies. Here, too, Strip system. From a plan of the common field of Burton-Agnes, Yorkshire, England. were held the trials, councils of war, and banquets. The bakery, the brewery, the kitchen, the laundry, and the chapel were all a part of the manor. The lord's castle was far more comfortable than the poor homes of the villeins. They lived in tiny huts made of mud and sticks and thatched with straw. Often the farm animals spent a night in the hut with the villein and his family. 

The Common or Open Fields. Outside the demesne were the common or open fields, which were cultivated by the serfs and freemen. Two or three days of each week the serfs had to

devote to working on the lord's demesne. All the grain that the serf raised had to be taken to the lord's mill to be ground, and for this privilege he had to give a portion of his produce to the lord. In like manner he had to make his wine in the lord's press and bake his bread in the lord's oven, leaving a portion of each as payment for the service.

The Strip System in England. — In England there developed the "three-field" system. It was given this name because the farm land was divided into three parts. Each field was again divided into many strips. The farmers or serfs cultivated their respective strips. There was a certain time appointed for all to plow, sow, and harvest. The crops were rotated, so that one group of strips would raise a heavy crop one year, a lighter crop the second year, and lie fallow (untilled) the third year.

During this third year the soil was given a rest and a chance to regain its fertility. Cattle, sheep, and swine were allowed to pasture on the "waste" (unused woodland or marshes) or in the uncultivated meadows under the care of the lowly swineherd. 

Little John makes Robin Hood, king of the greenwood, fall heels over head into 

Harsh Laws of the Feudal 

Lords. Towns and villages grew up around the manors.

They, as well as the old Roman cities, were drawn into the feudal system. All as a rule were governed by a feudal lord who owned the land. Harsh laws were passed which permitted only the noble and his friends to fish in the streams or hunt on any of the meadows or open spaces. 

Severe penalties were inflicted on the serf and people of the the stream.

middle class who trespassed on these special domains in any way. 

The common people smarted under these injustices. Often their heroes of legend and literature were persons who rebelled against these conditions. These heroes were pictured as outlaws who fought the nobles and aided the common people. Such are the tales of Robin Hood in England, Otto of the Silver Hand in 

Germany, Rob Roy in Scotland, and William Tell in Switzerland.

In time the people and towns were to become free of the nobles' tyranny. 

Robin Hood. - Robin Hood had killed one of the king's deer and later had killed a king's forester who had threatened his life. 

He was outlawed and sought refuge in Sherwood Forest in the center of England. He was joined by other bold men and gradually the "merry band" of Robin Hood was formed. One of his followers was Allan-a-Dale, of whom Sir Walter Scott (17711832 A.D.), the Scotch poet, wrote: Allan-a-Dale (Taken from "Rokeby ")

Allan-a-Dale has no fagot for burning, Allan-a-Dale has no furrow for turning, Allan-a-Dale has no fleece for the spinning, Yet Allan-a-Dale has red gold for the winning. 

Come read me my riddle! come, hearken my tale! 

And tell me the craft of bold Allan-a-Dale.

II The Baron of Ravensworth prances in pride, And he views his domains upon Arkindale side. 

The mere [sea] for his net, and the land for his game, The chase for the wild, and the park for the tame; 

Yet fish for the lake, and the deer for the vale, Are less free to Lord Dacre than Allan-a-Dale!

III 

Allan-a-Dale was ne'er belted a knight, Though his spur be as sharp, and his blade be as bright; 

Allan-a-Dale is no baron or lord, Yet twenty tall yeomen will draw at his word;

And the best of our nobles his bonnet will vail, Who at Rere-cross on Stanmore meets Allan-a-Dale!

IV 

Allan-a-Dale to his wooing is come; 

The mother, she asked of his household and home: "Though the castle of Richmond stand fair on the hill, My hall," quoth bold Allan, " shows gallanter still; 'Tis the blue vault of heaven, with its crescent so pale, And with all its bright spangles!" said Allan-a-Dale.

V The father was steel, and the mother was stone; 

They lifted the latch, and they bade him begone; 

But loud, on the morrow, their wail and their cry:

He had laughed on the lass with his bonny black eye, And she fled to the forest to hear a love-tale,

And the youth it was told by was Allan-a-Dale! 

The Bishop of Rome: His Importance. Like the govern ment, the land, and the laboring classes the Christian Church was drawn into the feudal system. After its recognition by the state it was organized into provinces similar to the Roman Empire. A bishop, assisted by priests and deacons, governed each province. The most important bishop in the Western Empire lived at Rome. With the breakdown of the empire and its invasion by the barbarians, the bishops of Rome, who were known as the popes, became the real leaders of both church and state. 

Christian Missionaries: Their Work. Christian missionaries went among the Goths and the Franks (northern Gaul).

They

An abbey of the thirteenth century. Find the church and the cloisters. Do you see the gardens and the wall? traveled to Briton and Ireland, carrying the gospel of Jesus among the Britons, the Scots, the Celts, the Angles, the Saxons, and the Jutes. They converted many of the people among these barbarian tribes and established churches and monasteries. 

The Monasteries. Around the monasteries was an inner court called a cloister, which was surrounded by many buildings such as the bakery, the dining room, the storehouse, the dormitories, and the chapel. Some of these monasteries became very wealthy through gifts of money and land. The monks, however, if they were the followers of St. Benedict (480-533 A.D.), took vows of poverty, obedience, piety, and labor. Most monasteries had similar rules. Since men from all walks of life could be used in these institutions the farmer, the artisan, and the scholar many classes of people took the vows and became monks. One rule of the monasteries was that all pilgrims, strangers, and persons in trouble who sought refuge had to be kindly received. 

Within the walls a refugee felt safe, for the monasteries were held sacred even by the barbarians. 

---

During the Feudal Age monks became soldiers and guarded these orders. Like the castles of nobles and the feudal towns, high walls and moats surrounded the monasteries. During this period of disorder much of the literature of the old Greek and Roman civilizations was preserved by the monks. When order succeeded chaos, this fact was to serve mankind in many ways. 

Romanesque and Gothic Architecture. Much time and labor were spent in the building of beautiful cathedrals from the time of Charlemagne to the fifteenth century. A cathedral is the church of a bishop. These Middle Ages, or Medieval Times, produced two types of architecture. The first was the Romanesque (rō-măn-ěsk'), which was built with rounded Roman arches and heavy outside buttresses to reënforce the buildings. The windows were small, and large stone pillars were erected on the 

3230 V903 SDDERS 2 Brown Bros. 

Cathedral of Notre Dame. Notice its high spires and majestic towers.

inside to hold up the ceiling. The second type of architecture was known as Gothic (goth'ik). It consisted of high, pointed arches and flying buttresses. The new Gothic architecture made possible the wide, lofty windows, which during the Middle Ages were made of brightly colored and beautifully designed stained glass. 

The cathedral of Notre Dame in Paris has the flying buttresses, high spires, and majestic towers characteristic of the Gothic architecture. 

The Building of Cathedrals. The beautiful medieval cathedrals were not built in a day. People from one generation to the next aided in the work. Rich merchants and nobles contributed money and material. The poor peasants donated their pennies and their labor. There was but one church, the Catholic 

Church, and all Christians belonged to it. Rich and poor, young and old, all assisted in the work. People from surrounding villages and towns came from miles around to help in any way they could free of charge.

The Merchant Guild. 

Another important institution of this period was the guilds. They were part of the towns which had grown with the trade fostered by the desire of the people for things, such as silks and jewelry, which they could not produce themselves. There were two classes of guilds: the merchant guilds and the craft guilds. The first were organized to protect the merchants in their home towns. They protected and regulated trade. Only the members of a merchant guild could buy and sell within the town without being taxed. Merchants from other towns were forced to pay a tax or toll before entering the town gate. The guild was very strict with its own members. "Cornering the Market" Forbidden. - No one was permitted to buy more goods than he could sell within a limited time. This was to prevent what we today call "cornering the market."

Before this rule was passed, a person could buy up all of one kind of goods, hold the entire stock, and then force people to pay him a high price for the article they desired. Such an action under the watchful eye of the merchant guild was a serious offense.

Offenders were severely disciplined by very cruel punishments. 

The guild also inspected the weights and measures of the merchants, and it went hard with any one found cheating the public by these methods. 

Certain days during the week were called "market days." On these days local goods were sold. Frequently fairs, lasting for several days, were held. At the fair not only local goods were sold, but also goods of all kinds brought from foreign countries. Jugglers, traveling players, and minstrels were always present at the fairs to amuse the crowds. 

The Craft Guilds. The craft guilds consisted of workmen who were interested in the trades. The workers in different trades separated and formed their own organizations. There were the butcher guilds, the carpenter guilds, the shoemaker guilds, and the guilds of bakers, tanners, and tailors. The craft guilds forbade their members to go from town to town or from house to house to ply their trade.

No one was allowed to work at any craft unless he was a member of his particular craft guild. Instead the members had to work in their own shops, which were located on certain streets or in a certain section of the city. Just as in ancient Babylon so also did the people of the Middle Ages know where to go to purchase a particular article. The guilds regulated the hours of labor, the price of goods, and inspected the material and work of its members.

Model of a medieval guild hall.

The Apprenticeship of Boys. If a boy wanted to be a baker, he and his father would go to the officers of the bakers' guild. 

There they would inquire if there was any room for new members. If there was and the boy had the proper qualifications, he became an apprentice. This meant that he was hired out to some master workman for a period of from three to seven years. 

While learning his trade, the boy lived in the master's home and received his board and clothing. When his period of apprenticeship was over, he was required to make a sample baking of some kind. He then took it before the guild meeting, and if it passed inspection, he was made a journeyman. As a journeyman he was paid regular wages and was admitted to membership in the guild. 

Social Life of the Guilds. The guilds cared for the sick, the widows, and the orphans of their organization. Each guild had its own pew in the churches and recognized some patron saint, who was supposed to guard its welfare. Each year a day was set aside to honor the memory of this saint. At this time the guild gave either a mystery play or a miracle play. 

Mystery Plays. The mystery plays represented stories from the Bible. The miracle plays dealt with the lives of the saints. 

The mystery plays were started by the Catholic Church, and at first they merely represented some Biblical scene. 

There was no acting or talking; the chief function of the play was to present a living picture of the Biblical characters. Later a group of scenes was presented and acting was added, but there was no talking. This dumb acting was called "pantomime." The plays grew very popular and were acted at first in the church yards and later on the town streets. One reason for the popularity of the mystery plays was the fact that so many people were unable to read. 

The Motion Pictures of the Middle Ages. The guilds added songs and dialogues to the plays. They built large wagons and decorated them with beautiful scenery. Each guild tried to outdo all the other guilds in the presentation of its scene. The people crowded the streets and watched the different scenes as

other 

A mystery play. Redrawn from an old print. the floats went by and unfolded some Old Testament story. 

These were the motion pictures of the Middle Ages. - - 

The Passion Play at Oberammergau. In Oberammergau 

(ō'ber-äm'er-gou), Bavaria, the greatest of all the mystery plays is still acted. Every ten years the inhabitants of this little town portray the life of Christ. The actors are mostly skilled craftsmen who spend their lives preparing for this "Passion Play." In 1930 people from all over the world visited the little Bavarian village to witness this play, which has come down from medieval times. 

Education during the Feudal Period. - Feudalism played an important part in the education of the youth. The guilds, as we have seen, took care of the boys who wanted to learn a trade. 

The church school provided an education for those who wished to prepare for the clergy. There the pupils were taught to read and write Latin, the universal language of the church. They were given a little arithmetic, geometry, and astronomy. Music was taught because of its importance in conducting church 

KEKE

services. Besides the educational opportunities afforded by the guilds and the church, there was the training of the nobility for knighthood.

The Page: His Lessons in Chivalry. The aim of the last mentioned school was to train the young noble for a military life. 

The system of discipline was known as "chivalry." Like the Spartan and Athenian youth the boy remained with his mother until he was seven years old. Then he was sent to the castle of some noble, and his training for knighthood began. Until he was fourteen the boy served as a page to the lord and lady of the castle. He ran errands, waited upon the table, and learned minstrel songs and music. He was taught to respect his elders and superiors, to be kind to the poor, to help the weak, to be courteous to all women, and above all to be brave. The ladies at the court gave him lessons in courtesy and good manners. The knights taught him how to ride a horse, throw a javelin, and to use a sword, shield, and lance.

The Squire. When the page reached the age of fourteen, he became the attendant of some knight. He was now called a 'squire." He cared for the knight's horse and armor and went with him to the hunt, the tournament, and the wars. When he was twenty-one, the squire, if he had proved worthy in all things was ready for the ceremony of knighthood. 

The Ceremony of Knighthood.—The squire gathered togethe his sword, shield, and armor. Then the evening before the grea event of his life he went to a near-by church. He laid his equip ment on the altar and spent the night in prayer. Early in th morning he went to confession and attended mass. After thi he was instructed in the duties of knighthood. He next dresse himself in full armor and prepared for the final ceremony. H appeared before his lord and knelt upon one knee. 

In the presence of all the court attendants he took the vow of knighthood. The lord then struck him on the shoulder wit the flat of a sword. This blow was called the "accolade." The the youth was commanded to rise as "sir knight." His lon

years of training were over; he was now ready to take his place among men as a man. Sometimes, because of exceptional bravery or for some other worthy cause, the squire might be knighted on the field of battle or wherever the occasion warranted such action. 

The The Tournaments. 

tournament was the chief sport and amusement of the

Feudal Age. This was often presided over by some beautiful lady known as the queen of the tournament. Knights in full armor, either on foot or horseback, fought for the glory and honor of proving themselves a champion. The knight often wore the colors of his lady love and fought for her hand in marriage.

The large arena in which the SEGEN knights fought was called the An imaginative drawing of a tournament. "lists." People came from It was in these sham battles that knights miles around to witness these were allowed to show their courage and skill. What were their weapons? affairs and cheer on their favorites. It was to the tournament that Allan-a-Dale was referring when he says in the poem : "And the best of our nobles his bonnet will vail, Who at Rere-cross on Stanmore meets Allan-a-Dale!"

Such in brief was the Feudal Age that slowly developed in western Europe after the downfall of the Roman Empire in the west. 

We have seen how the nobility, the land, the laboring and merchant classes, education, and the church were affected by this RA-16

system. It was an age which attempted to bring order out of chaos. It was an age which presented men and events which were to lay the foundations upon which strong nations were to be built. To those men and those events we shall now turn our attention.

CHAPTER XV  

The Franks in Gaul. - The Franks became the most powerful and the most important of the Germanic or Teutonic tribes in western Europe. They laid the foundations for what are now modern France and Germany. When Alaric sacked Rome in 

410 A.D., they were living in the territory located near the mouth of the Rhine River. They were among the allies who helped defeat Attila and his Huns at the battle of Chalons in 451 A.D. 

Their chief at that time was Merovius. His fame spread after the repulse of Attila, and he became the founder of the Merovingian line of Frankish kings in Roman Gaul. 

Gaul in the Days of Merovius. Like their fellow Teutons the Franks were tall, fair-complexioned, blond, and blue-eyed. 

When they first wandered into Gaul, they found the Romans in full control. The Gauls had given up their weird religion of 

Druidism (Chapter III) and had accepted Christianity. Monasteries and churches had taken the place of the Druid temples. 

At Carnac in Brittany the remains of the great stone altars of the Druid priests can be seen today. The Franks were to be greatly influenced by Roman civilization and the Christian faith. 

But in the days of Merovius they still held to their Germanic religion, customs, and laws. 

The City of Asgard; Woden and Freya. - The Teutonic tribes believed in many gods, who were believed to live in a beautiful and shining city called Asgard (ǎs'gärd). At Asgard there were many palaces, in one of which lived Woden or Odin, the king of the gods. He sat on a golden throne and had charge of the winds, the rains, and the snows. He wore a golden helmet and a pale, blue cape trimmed with stars, for he was also the goo of the heavens. Each morning two ravens flew to earth and returned in the evening. Perched on the shoulders of Woden 238

they gave him the news of earthly men and events. 

The wife of Woden was Freya (fra'a), the goddess of sunshine, of flowers, of love, and of everything that was beautiful. 

Thor, Tiew, and Loki.

There was another palace for Thor, a son of Woden. Thor rode in a chariot drawn by goats, and when he was angered he threw his mighty hammer across the heavens, which caused thunder. In a third palace lived Tiew, the 

Teutonic god of war. It was the sword of Tiew, which Attila claimed he possessed and wielded in all of his battles. Loki was the god of fire and the trouble-maker among the gods. 

From the names of four of these gods we get the names of four days of our week: Tuesday or 

Tiew's day, Wednesday or Thor in his chariot drawn by goats. Notice his hammer. 

Woden's day, Thursday or Thor's day, and Friday or Freya's day. 

Valhalla: the Abode of Heroes. At Asgard was located the great hall of Valhalla. This was the home of all Teutonic warriors who had died bravely in battle. The Franks and other 

Germanic tribes were fearless fighters. The soldier believed that if he died heroically in battle, one of the beautiful Valkyries (vǎl-kir'iz) or war-maidens took care of him. With her flying steed she carried him swiftly through the heavens to Valhalla. 

There, guarded and ruled by Woden, he associated with heroes. 

There he fought or hunted to his heart's content, and in the evening, refreshed by medicine from the gods, he sat around the large banquet table and listened to the songs of mighty deeds.

The Eddas and the Nibelungenlied. - Long after the Ger manic tribes ceased to believe in their many gods, the stories were still told. About the twelfth century they were collected and written down in the literature we know today as the Icelandic Eddas. For centuries, too, the stories of their legendary heroes had been handed down from father to son. Then in the thirteenth century some unknown poet wrote the Nibelungenlied (nē bē-loong-en-lēt), or the song of the Nibelungs. For years it had been the favorite story about the gods and fabled heroes of old. It now became the great German epic and the background of much in our modern literature and music. 

The Rhinegold. Briefly, this is the story of the Nibelungenlied. Once upon a time a wonderful treasure of gold lay hidden at the bottom of the Rhine River. This wealth was guarded by three mermaids called the Rhine-daughters. It was said that any one who secured the gold and fashioned a ring from it could be master and lord of the earth. The first to do this was Alberich (äl'ber-ik), an ugly dwarf who belonged to the race of the Nibelungs.

Sometime after this Woden ordered the two giant brothers, Fasolt and Fafnir, to build for him over night the palace of Valhalla. As a reward for their work he promised them Freya. 

This fair goddess, in addition to her other duties, had charge of the gardens in Asgard. It was her apples, which when eaten by the gods gave them their eternal youth. When the time came for her to depart with the brothers, she became afraid and wept. 

The other gods complained to Woden, and he was forced to seek some other reward for his builders. At

Woden and Loki went to the home of Alberich, seized him, his gold, his ring, and his Tarnhelm. The last was a wishing cap, which enabled its wearer to appear invisible, travel through space, assume any form, and to increase his strength many times. 

Valhalla the gold, the ring, and the Tarnhelm were given to the giants, and they released Freya. Alberich pronounced a terrible curse upon the ring. It was to bring misfortune and death to its

owner until it was returned to the Nibelungs. The brothers quarreled over the treasure, and one of them was killed. The other sought refuge in a cave, and there in the form of a dragon he guarded his wealth. 

Siegfried and Brünnehilde. Then one day young Siegfried (sēg'fred) came seeking adventure. He was the son of a Volsung king and hero (some- times he is described as a Netherlander). 

With his magic sword, Balmung, he entered the cave, killed the dragon, and bathed quickly in its blood. The dragon's blood touched all parts of his body, except one little spot between his shoulders where a linden leaf had fallen.

Hence this was the only place where it was possible for any one to wound him.

He had accidentally A Valkyrie, one of the maidens of Woden. tasted the blood of the dragon, and this he discovered made it possible for him to understand the language of birds and beasts. "Ring, Hoard, and 

Tarnhelm for Siegfried, now Lord of the Gold," a wood bird sang. 

And then later from this same songster came the words: "A glorious bride for the Volsung have I. On the rock of the 

Valkyries she sleeps, and guarded by fire is her home. Who fights the flames for love wakes Brünnehilde (brün-e-hil'dē), and Brünnehilde he wins for his own." In this adventure' 

Siegfried was again successful, and he won the love of the beautiful Valkyrie. But the curse of Alberich was on Siegfried, who still possessed the ring. He and his bride were not to enjoy their happiness very long.

One day Siegfried went to the court of Gunther, king of the Burgundians. There he met Hagen, the wicked son of Alberich. 

Hagan gave Siegfried a magic drink which caused him to forget all about Brünnehilde. Through the efforts of Siegfried, Brünnehilde was compelled to marry Gunther. This led to anger, jealousy, and hate. Siegfried was killed, then Gunther, then

Hagen, and finally Brünnehilde committed suicide. Before dying, however, she threw the ring back to the Rhine-daughters, and the terrible curse of Alberich was removed.

Another version of this epic states that Siegfried, after winning the treasure, married the beautiful Kriemhild (krēmʼhilt), sister of Gunther. Then he went to Iceland to help Gunther win the hand of the fair Brünnehilde, queen of the northland. Kriemhild and Brünnehilde quarreled. Siegfried was killed by Hagen, and to avenge her husband's death Kriemhild married Attila the Hun. With the aid of the Hun's warriors all the main characters were slain, and the curse of the Nibelung was fulfilled.

Minnesingers and Troubadours. Such was the Nibelungen lied as it was sung by the minstrels in the castle halls of the Feudal Age. At the same time the minnesingers of Germany and the troubadours of southern France went from court to court. 

They sang their songs of romantic love and deeds of chivalry accompanied by musical instruments. In northern France there developed about the eleventh century the great epics which centered around the life of Charlemagne. The finest of these is the 

Song of Roland. The stories of Roland are delightful reading, and, while legendary, give a vivid picture of the life of the Franks in those days.

Operas of Richard Wagner. - Richard Wagner (18131883 A.D.), the renowned German musician, based his most famous operas on the Nibelungen tales. They include Das 

Rheingold, Die Walkure (the Valkyries), Siegfried, and the Dusk of the Gods.

The Laws of Frankland. The laws of the Franks were personal. If a person committed a crime, the penalty very often

depended upon his social standing. A noble accused by a serf needed only a few witnesses to swear that he was telling the truth to be declared innocent. On the other hand a serf accused by a noble often needed as many as forty witnesses to bring about the same result.

Ordeal by Fire. — Very often an accused person who could secure no witnesses was forced to submit to a test or ordeal. The 

Franks believed that if a man was guilty, he would rather confess than anger his gods by telling a lie. There were three very common ordeals - the ordeal by fire, the ordeal by water, and the wager of battle. In the first case the accused was compelled to carry hot irons or to thrust his hand into a fire. If his burns. healed quickly, he was declared innocent, if not, he was judged guilty. Then sometimes he was blindfolded and ordered to walk over red-hot plowshares placed along a road. If he was fortunate and missed the plowshares, he was permitted to go free. 

The Ordeal by Water. If the ordeal was to be by water, the victim was sometimes bound hand and foot and thrown into a river or creek. If he floated, he was guilty; if he sank, he was innocent. In the latter instance he often drowned before the judges could bring his body to the surface. Another water test was to have the accused put his arm in boiling water. After three days if there was little or no sign of burns or scalds, he was declared innocent. 

The Wager of Battle. The wager of battle was based upon the belief that the gods would give victory to the innocent. 

Accused persons who were unable to secure witnesses could fight their accusers in combat or hire others to fight for them. The victor was always declared innocent of crime.

Clovis Accepts Christianity. These were the religion, the legends, and the laws of the Franks in the days of Merovius. 

His successors were unable to make much headway in Gaul until the time of Clovis (klō'vis). At the yearly assembly of the 

Franks, called the Field of March, the Frankish warriors raised Clovis on a shield and declared him their king. He was but

Clovis was a born warrior and a good statesman. His soldiers, as brave as their leader, helped Clovis become master of a great kingdom. fifteen years of age at the time and the son of a former king who had been very much disliked. Clovis married a Christian princess, and according to legend he and 3000 of his warriors accepted Christianity. He conquered the greater part of Gaul, made Paris his capital, and there he died in 511 A.D. 

The "Do-Nothing Kings." The kings who followed Clovis were so weak and powerless that they were called the "do-nothing kings." They still received ambassadors and opened the great national festivals. On such occasions they were elegantly dressed. They came to the scenes, according to ancient custom, in an open cart drawn by oxen and driven by a swineherd. But the real rulers were the "mayors of the palace." 

They were chosen by the assembly to govern in times of peace and to lead the armies in times of war. 

Charles Martel. The most famous of these mayors was 

Charles Martel, a great statesman and soldier. He was a king in everything but name. He conquered the Burgundians and

united the Franks of Gaul and Germany. To conduct these wars, Martel (the Hammer) took money from the monasteries. 

He appointed his own nobles as abbots to govern these institutions. In some cases he built new monasteries and chose their officials. The wealth he secured enabled him to support his army and conduct the government. But the feat for which 

Charles Martel is best known is his defeat of the Mohammedans or Saracens (sǎr'a-senz) at the Battle of Tours (toor) in 732 A.D. 

Before discussing this event, we must discover who these 

Mohammedans were. 

Mohammed: the Hegira. The founder of the Mohammedan religion or Islam was Mohammed. He was born in the city of 

Mecca, Arabia, about 570 A.D. When a young man, he claimed that the archangel Gabriel appeared to him in a dream and said: "There is but one God, and Mohammed is his prophet."

His wife and a few friends believed in him, and he soon had many followers. But the idol-makers of Mecca were convinced that his belief would ruin their business. Therefore he and his converts were driven from the city. They fled to Medina (ma-de'nä). This was in the year 622 A.D. and is called "the 

Hegira" (hěj'ĭ-ra; flight). This year is used by the Mohammedans in reckoning their time in the same way as the Christians use the birth of Christ. 

The Moslem Creed: the Koran. Mohammed forbade his followers to make images, to gamble, to eat pork, or to drink wine.

The Moslems, as his disciples were called, were permitted four wives. Every Moslem had to recite, once in his lifetime at least, the Moslem creed: "There is no God but Allah, and Mohammed is the prophet of Allah." Five times daily the Moslem must face toward Mecca and pray. From a high tower, called a minaret, a person known as a muezzin calls the faithful to prayers. 

The ninth month of the Mohammedan year is observed as the fast of the Ramadan. During that period trade, war, and even conversation between male and female are forbidden. Islam recognizes as prophets, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus, but it considers

An artist's conception of a fierce attack by the Mohammedans, who were finally defeated at Tours. Study their clothes, their weapons, and their fine horses.

Mohammed the last and greatest of all. The sacred book of the 

Mohammedans is the Koran (kō-rän'). 

Mohammedan Conquests. The followers of Jesus were taught "peace on earth." The Moslem was taught to wage war on all unbelievers, and if he died in these Holy Wars, he entered paradise. Within a century after the death of Mohammed his followers had conquered Persia, Syria, Egypt, North Africa, and Spain. Everywhere they went, the Arabs absorbed and often improved the civilizations with which they came in contact. 

The City of Cordova, Public Lamps, and Paved Streets. - 

When the Moslems, who were known as Saracens in Spain, entered the country, many of the Goths fled into France. A few remained, however, and kept up a ceaseless warfare with the invaders for seven centuries. Among these was the legendary hero of Spanish literature, the Cid.

The Saracens settled down and organized a government. 

They built beautiful temples (mosques), established schools, laid out well-planned cities, and developed agriculture. The city of Cordova (kôr'dô-va) had more than 200,000 houses and more than 1,000,000 inhabitants. At night the city and streets were made safe for a distance of ten miles by the light of public lamps. Seven hundred years later there was not a single public lamp in the city of London. The streets of Cordova were paved, as was the case in Granada, Seville (sev'il), and Toledo. 

But Paris in the sixteenth century could boast of nothing better than gravel sidewalks.

The Saracens The Advanced Civilization of the Saracens. 

introduced into Spain the cultivation of rice, sugar, cotton, spinach, saffron, and the culture of silk. They brought with them the Egyptian system of irrigation by floodgates, wheels, and pumps. They knew how to work earthenware, iron, and steel. 

The Toledo sword blades were famous throughout the world, as A mosque and obelisk at Constantinople. The mosque is the most original type of Mohammedan architecture.

66 was also their Morocco leather. They were familiar with the use of gunpowder and the mariner's compass. Their smallest weight was a grain of barley, four of which they called a carat." 

We still use the grain as a unit of weight and measure the fineness of gold by so many carats. They established schools and universities. While the people in western Europe were insisting that the earth was flat and depending upon magic to cure disease, the Saracens were far more advanced. They studied geography by the use of globes, and their doctors performed the most delicate operation with the knife. These were the people who now prepared to conquer the Franks and invade western Europe. 

But Charles Martel stood ready to face the Saracens' battle lines. 

The Battle of Tours, 732 A.D. - Early one October morning in 

732 A.D. the opposing forces met at Tours. The ever-conquering 

Moslems went into action shouting their battle cry of victory.

Under their great leader, Martel, the Franks, wearing armor and carrying spears, met the attack. All day long the battle waged until thousands of men lay dead on both sides. During the night the Saracens retreated toward southern Gaul and Spain. 

Thus, said one historian, the whole course of European history was changed. It advanced more slowly in civilization, and it remained Christian rather than Islam.

Pepin, King of the Franks. — In 752 A.D. Pepin (pěp'în), the son of Charles Martel, sent ambassadors to Pope Zacharias at 

Rome. They asked the pope this question: "Who should be king of the Franks, he who is called king but exercises no power, or he who exercises the power but does not hold the title?" The pope replied that the one who had the power should be king. He then ordered Archbishop Boniface (missionary to the Germans) to anoint Pepin king of the Franks. This was done, and the Frankish custom of choosing their own rulers passed from their hands. 

Charlemagne, 771-814 A.D. Three years after the death of

Pepin in 771 A.D. his son Charlemagne became king. He ruled for more than forty years. During that time he conquered the

Coronation of Charlemagne, the sixty-eighth Roman emperor.

Painting by 

Lévy. 

Saracens in southern Gaul and drove them beyond the Pyrenees. 

He subdued the Saxons, the Slavs, and the Bavarians and made them a part of his kingdom. But Charlemagne's chief interest 

Iwas not war. He divided his territories into counties or marks and over each he placed an officer called a count or marquis. 

The counts were to maintain peace and act as judges in their districts. The work of the counts was watched carefully by 

Charlemagne's messengers, one of whom was a bishop and the other a noble. They usually traveled together. 

Charlemagne's Interest in Education. Charlemagne, who spoke Latin and understood Greek, was interested in education. 

He entertained scholars who came from all parts of Europe at his court. He was a friend of Harun-al-Rashid, the Mohammedan ruler of Baghdad and the hero of the Arabian Nights. 

He wanted his subjects to be enlightened and wished to revive the old Greek and Roman art and science. He issued an order which read: "Let every monastery and every abbey have its school, where boys may be taught the psalms, the system of

Charlemagne made many visits to the schools. On one occasion, it is related, the children from the poorer families did much better work than the sons of the noble. He praised the sons of the poor and then turned upon the others and said: "You pretty and dainty little gentlemen who count upon your birth and your wealth, you have disregarded my orders and your own reputations and neglected your studies. Let me promise you this: if you do not make haste to make good your former negligence, never think to get any favors from Karl." But the efforts of 

Charlemagne were to bear little fruit. This was not his fault, but it was due to the fact that no ruler for centuries after Charlemagne's time was able to keep order in western Europe. 

Charlemagne, Emperor of the Romans. — The pope at Rome was having a difficult time. His enemies disputed his election and even waylaid and beat him. Then Pope Leo III, for this was his name, appealed to Charlemagne for aid. The king of the Franks marched into Italy and settled the dispute. Pope 

Leo was very grateful, and on Christmas Day in 800 A.D. Charlemagne attended St. Peter's Church in Rome. He was dressed in the princely robes of the old Roman patricians. As he knelt in prayer, Pope Leo placed a golden crown upon his head and proclaimed him "Charles Augustus, Emperor of the Romans."

The people of Rome rejoiced at what they thought was the return of the glory that had been imperial Rome. Charlemagne now demanded an oath of allegiance from every man in the empire. 

The Death of Charlemagne. Fourteen years later the long reign of Charlemagne was ended by his death. At his own request he was buried in the vault of a cathedral in the city of Aix-la-Chapelle (ěks-lå-shȧ-pěl'). Clad in his imperial purple robes, he was seated on a throne. On his head was a crown,

in his hand a scepter, at his side a sword, and on his knees lay an open Bible.

The Beginning of Modern Europe. After Charlemagne's death his empire was divided. The eastern part of the Frankish kingdom, occupied mostly by Roman Gauls, became modern France. The western part of the kingdom, whose inhabitants were chiefly Germanic, became modern Germany. The remaining parts of the empire finally developed into modern Belgium, Holland, and Switzerland. Italy became a country of many city-states until these were finally united in 1871 A.D. In 

1492 A.D. the Saracens, or Moors, were driven from Spain. The country was then united under Ferdinand of Aragon and Isabella of Castile.

Pilgrimages. It was the custom during the Feudal Age for 

Christians to make journeys to the graves of their saints. Such a journey was called a "pilgrimage." This was one way for a person to atone for his sins, and it was encouraged by the church. 

Some pilgrimages were short and of local origin. But the greatest pilgrimage of all was to the Holy Land in Palestine. There one could worship at the shrine made sacred by the founder of Christianity.

The Holy Land. After the fall of the Roman Empire in the west, the Holy Land came under the control of the Turks. They were Mohammedans, but they allowed the Christians to make their pilgrimages unmolested. They profited by the trade of these visitors and welcomed them. About the middle of the eleventh century a more ferocious group of Turks gained possession of the Holy Land. They began to abuse the Christians. 

The returning pilgrims complained to the pope. As time passed, conditions became worse. Robbery, murder, and torture were practiced by the Turks. Finally, in 1095 A.D., Pope Urban II preached a holy Crusade against them. 

Pope Urban's Call. - Pope Urban's plea started a movement the following year which was to last for two centuries. The call to wage a holy war against the Turks was answered by kings,

feudal lords, artisans, serfs, and even little children. The reasons for undertaking a Crusade, the name given to the attempts to drive the Turks from the Holy Land, were many. Many of the 

Crusaders were devoutly religious and willing to give their life for the cause. Some loved adventure and used this means of gaining excitement. In some instances criminals were re-e leased from prison and permitted to enlist in the Crusades; kings and princes hoped to carve out a state for themselves in the East when the Turk was overthrown. 

The Crusades. With the cry "God Wills It" and a red cross on the breast of its members, Crusade after Crusade marched towards the 

East. These Crusades have been called the most romantic expeditions in history. A knight on horseback enlisted in a holy cause. He traveled?

over land and sea. He saw A Crusader.

From a thirteenth-century 

new people, new places, and learned many interesting things. On the other hand suffering and tragedy followed many expeditions. 

King Richard of England, known as the Lion-Hearted, ransomed his kingdom to go on a Crusade. He was bold, fearless, and a wonderful leader. He fought hard in Syria and won the respect of the Turkish sultan, Saladin. Upon his return home he was captured and imprisoned by the French and German king. England raised a large sum of money and RA-17

purchased his freedom. Historians usually number eight of these 

Crusades. Some were more important than others. The most pitiful were the children's Crusades. Thousands of French and 

German children enlisted in the cause. Thousands traveled by water, and many of these were sold into slavery. Thousands marched overland, where many of them died and were buried in unmarked graves.

A Few Results of the Crusades. These struggles ended as they began — with the Turk in possession of the Holy Land. 

The returning Crusader, however, had gained a broader viewpoint on life. The art, literature, science, food, clothing, and other commercial products of the East were superior to those which he had known in his homeland. A brisk trade sprang up between the East and the West. Pepper, cinnamon, nutmeg, cloves, ginger, camphor, and indigo were imported into western Europe.

Gold and silver cups, laces, ivory, and silk from Arabia were now seen in the homes of feudal merchants and princes in the West. 

There was a great growth in trade and consequently in the towns. 

Such were the people and the events of the Feudal Age on the continent. We now turn our attention to the British Isles across the English Channel.

The Crusade to the Holy Land.

CHAPTER XVI  

The Roman Occupation of Britain. - Roman legions had been stationed in Britain since the days of Julius Caesar, 54 B.C. 

Four hundred years later the troops then on duty were recalled. 

This was because of the barbarian invasions of Rome under Alaric. During that period the Britons had been conquered and many of them enslaved. The power of the Druid priests had been crushed and the spirit of the natives broken. A few years after the legions departed, the government and social life of the Romans disappeared. A few Latin words, towns, walls, forts, baths, and roads remained. The Britons regained their native land but were able to hold it for a short time only. 

The Beginning of Angle-land or England. - A new danger now faced them. They were attacked on their northern frontiers by the Picts and Scots and were invaded from the east by the Angles, Saxons, and Jutes. These last three were Germanic tribes from the peninsula of Jutland or Denmark and the land south of it. Instead of marching south into Gaul and Italy, they crossed the English Channel into Britain. 

Hard pressed, the British chief of Kent made an alliance with the Jutes to repel the invasion of the Picts and Scots. The allies were successful, but a few years later the Jutes and their kinsmen turned on the Britons, drove them from Kent, and occupied the land themselves. The Saxons then conquered the lands in the south and west. These were called Sussex, Wessex, Essex, and Middlesex. Finally the Angles took possession of all the unoccupied land in the east. This became Northumbria and Mercia (mûr'shid). The whole country, consisting of these seven kingdoms, took its name from the Angles. It became 

Angle-land or England, and its language and people were called 

Anglo-Saxon. 

256

According to legend Arthur proved his kingship by pulling forth the sword from the stone at Canterbury. 

It took more 

King Arthur and His Order of the Round Table. 

than a century for these various tribes to conquer the Britons. 

In the meantime Christianity had gained a foothold on the island. 

This is also the period of the famous British chief and leader, King Arthur. Legend is not certain whether he fought the Scots or the Saxons. He was finally defeated, and his followers fled to the hills of Wales and to Brittany in France. 

Strange and romantic stories were told about King Arthur (520 A.D.). He was pictured as a brave, courteous Christian knight. Writers, minstrels, and poets of a later day told the tales of Lancelot, Tristram, Sir Galahad, and other members of King

Arthur's Order of the Knights of the Round Table. Alfred 

Lord Tennyson (1809-1892), the English poet, tells of King Arthur and his knights in the Idylls of the King, twelve narrative poems which begin with "The Coming of Arthur" and end with 

"The Passing of Arthur." Previous legends of Arthur set an example for the knights of the Middle Ages.

Only the best and the bravest knights were permitted a seat at the Round Table. No knight who would stoop to lie, cheat,

steal, or harm the poor in any way was permitted in the order. 

Each of the knights had his own chair, on which his name was carved, around the table. There was one seat, known as "the Seat 

Perilous," which had no name upon it. This was reserved for the knight who would find the Holy Grail. This was believed to be the cup from which Christ drank at the Last Supper. According to the Arthurian legends Sir Galahad, the perfect knight, succeeded in finding the Grail. Three others had visions of it. 

The Vision of Sir Launfal. James Russell Lowell (18191890 A.D.), the American poet, wrote The Vision of Sir Launfal (län'fŏl). It tells of the experiences of that knight in his search for the Holy Grail. Sir Launfal is introduced as a young man starting on his quest.

Part First, III 

The drawbridge dropped with a surly clang, And through the dark arch a charger sprang, Bearing Sir Launfal, the maiden knight,

In his gilded mail, that flamed so bright It seemed the dark castle had gathered all 

Those shafts the fierce sun had shot over its wall

In his siege of three hundred summers long, And binding them all in one blazing sheaf, Had cast them forth: so, young and strong, And lightsome as a locust-leaf,

Sir Launfal flashed forth in his unscarred mail, To seek in all climes for the Holy Grail. V As Sir Launfal made morn through the darksome gate, He was 'ware of a leper, crouched by the same, Who begged with his hand and moaned as he sate; 

And a loathing over Sir Launfal came;

The sunshine went out of his soul with a thrill, The flesh 'neath his armor did shrink and crawl,

And midway its leap his heart stood still 

Like a frozen waterfall;

For this man, so foul and bent of stature, Rasped harshly against his dainty nature, And seemed the one blot on the summer morn So he tossed him a piece of gold in scorn. 

Years passed while Sir Launfal traveled throughout the world in search of the Holy Grail. He grew old and broken in health. 

He was keenly disappointed in his failure to obtain the object of his search. He decided to return home. As he reached the old castle and the same gate, he had the following experience: 

Part Second, II 

Then the soul of the leper stood up in his eyes 

And looked at Sir Launfal, and straightway he 

Remembered in what a haughtier guise

He had flung an alms to leprosie, When he caged his young life up in gilded mail And set forth in search of the Holy Grail. 

The heart within him was ashes and dust; 

He parted in twain his single crust, He broke the ice on the streamlet's brink. 

And gave the leper to eat and drink, 'Twas a moldy crust of coarse brown bread, 'Twas water out of a wooden bowl, 

Yet with fine wheaten bread was the leper fed, And 'twas red wine he drank with his thirsty soul. 

Then a voice said: .'Lo, it is I, be not afraid!

In many climes, without avail, Thou hast spent thy life for the Holy Grail; 

Behold, it is here, this cup which thou 

Didst fill at the streamlet for me but now; 

This crust is my body broken for thee,

This water his blood that died on the tree;

The Holy Supper is kept, indeed, In whatso we share with another's need: 

Not what we give, but what we share, For the gift without the giver is bare; 

Who gives himself with his alms feeds three 

Himself, his hungering neighbor, and me.' 

Augustine, First Archbishop of Canterbury. In 597 A.D., after the death of Arthur and the defeat of the Britons, Augustine, a Christian missionary, arrived in England. He brought with him by order of the pope a band of forty monks. Ethelbert was now king of Kent. His wife was a Christian from Frankland. 

This royal family invited Augustine to Canterbury, the capital of Kent. In time the king and 10,000 of his followers were converted. Augustine became the first archbishop of Canterbury and erected a monastery to train missionaries. Soon missionaries, monasteries, and churches were seen throughout England, Scotland, and Ireland. 

Egbert, King of the English, 828 A.D. The Witenagemot (wĭt'ě-na-gě-mōt) (meeting of the Wise Men) of Wessex elected Egbert king in the year 801 A.D. At the time of the election 

Egbert was at the court of Charlemagne where he had been a refugee for thirteen years. He was an ardent admirer of the emperor and returned to his native land determined to follow the example of Charlemagne. He conquered the kingdoms which had been established by the Angles, Saxons, and Jutes. Thei rulers became his vassals, and in 828 A.D. he was able to refer to himself as "Egbert, by the Grace of God, king of the English.' 

The royal line of England today traces its descent from this firs sovereign.

The Danish Vikings in England. The young nation ha scarcely been united before its existence was threatened by new invaders. These were the Danes, Northmen from the Scandina vian peninsula and the fiercest of the Teutonic tribes. Thei kinsmen overran and occupied land in Italy and Spain. Unde

The pirate Northmen plundered the coast of Europe.

Rollo they founded Normandy in France, and under Rurik they laid the foundation for modern Russia. These old Vikings, unable to secure a living in their own country, took to the sea and became pirates. Some reached the coast of our own country. 

The Danes in England, however, met their equal in one of the greatest and most beloved of English kings- Alfred the Great. 

Alfred the Great (849-901 A.D.). He was the youngest of

Egbert's four grandsons. As a youth he had spent several years at Rome with his father. His stepmother, who was a greatgranddaughter of Charlemagne, was very much interested in Alfred's education. At an early age he had learned to read and to write and was a favorite of every one with whom he came in contact. Each of his three brothers had ruled before him. But in 871 A.D., when the last of these had died, Alfred, at the age of twenty-two, was crowned king. 

The Defeat of Alfred: His Flight. It was a difficult task that faced the young monarch. The Danes were invading the

An artist's conception of the story of King Alfred and the peasant's cakes. 

What does this story tell you about Alfred? country. They robbed and set fire to homes, farms, churches, and monasteries. They tortured and killed women and children. 

With their dreams of Valhalla and wild cries to Odin, they attacked and plundered settlement after settlement. 

There was no time to celebrate the coronation of Alfred. The 

Danes were being reënforced by invading Vikings. Alfred with his army met the Danish invaders, was defeated, and compelled to retreat. His troops were scattered, and with a few faithful followers he fled to the wilds of Somersetshire. It was at this time, legend says, that he sought refuge in a peasant's hut. The owner's wife did not know that her visitor in his tattered garb was King Alfred. She ordered him to sit by the fire and see that her cakes did not burn. Another story relates how he went into the Danish camp disguised as a minstrel. And there while

the Danes applauded his singing, Alfred gained a knowledge of their numbers and plans. 

The English Rally and Alfred Is Victorious. In the spring of the year 878 A.D. the invaders were camped at Edington. 

Alfred rallied his forces around him, marched towards their stronghold, and surrounded it. The Danes were unable to secure any food, their supply gave out, and they surrendered. 

The Danish leader accepted Christianity, acknowledged Alfred as his overlord, and signed the Treaty of Wedmore. 

The Treaty of Wedmore the Danelaw. - By the terms of this treaty the Danes agreed to remain in the eastern part of the country along the coast. This territory was called the Danelaw and was much larger than Alfred's kingdom. But since they had agreed to be his vassals, he ruled the entire country and issued his orders from his capital at Winchester. 

Alfred, the Lawgiver: "Judge Thou Not One Doom to the

Rich, Another to the Poor." - King Alfred was great in war, but he was even greater in time of peace. He collected the laws of his people and had them written down. His code began with the 

Ten Commandments and ended with the Golden Rule, "Whatsoever ye would that men should do to you, do ye even so to them, for this is the law and the prophets." He stated that any subject who obeyed these sections of his code would need no other law book. His laws were far more just than the old personal laws of the Teutonic tribes. Some idea of this can be gained from his command: "Judge thou very evenly; judge thou not one doom to the rich, another to the poor." 

The Beginning of the English Navy. To maintain peace within his country, Alfred built a navy. He designed the war vessels himself. These fast-sailing ships proved more than a match for even the Vikings. He is considered as the founder of the English navy. To further check any uprising in the Danelaw, he constructed a line of forts along his frontiers. 

Alfred, His Place in History. This busy king established schools, rebuilt many of the monasteries, and personally aided
