in translating several books from the Latin to the English. He devised a method for measuring time. He made six candles, each of which took four hours to burn. Each candle was marked by twelve notches, and when it had burned down to one of these, he knew that twenty minutes had passed. He surrounded these candles with plates of horn to keep out the draft. 

Alfred died in 901 A.D. As a military leader, lawgiver, promoter of civic pride, and ruler of a nation, he ranks among the leaders in world history and well deserved the title - Great. 

Canute (1017-1035 A.D.). - Within a century after the death of Alfred, the Danes gained the upper hand. The 

English king placed a tax on all the property owners of his kingdom. The money thus raised was given to the Danes in return for their promise not to invade England. This Danegeld, or Dane-money as An imaginative drawing of Canute on it was called, did not satisfy the beach. the Danish leaders very long. A nation weak enough to pay tribute was weak enough to be conquered. So in 1013 A.D. this was done, and a Dane was acknowledged king. This ruler was succeeded by his son, Canute (ka-nut'). 

The Rebuke of Flatterers. Canute's rule was harsh, but he finally brought peace to the land. Like most men who enjoy power and influence, Canute was surrounded by many flatterers. 

It is said that they even went so far as to tell him that he was lord of the land and sea and that even the waves would obey him.

To teach them a lesson, he had them place his throne on the beach, where he commanded the waves not to touch the border of his robe. When one of the waves wet his garment, he turned to the courtiers and said: "Do not forget that the power of kings is a small matter. He who is King of kings and Lord of lords, he is the one whom the earth and the sea and the heavens obey." 

Edward the Confessor - Westminster Abbey. There were but four Danish kings who ruled in England. The English ruler before the invasion had married a princess from 

Normandy. During the

Danish occupation she returned home with her small son, Prince Edward.

He remained at the Norman court for more than thirty years. Among his many friends was his 

Westminster Abbey. cousin, William, the Duke of Normandy. This was to have a far-reaching consequence upon the history of England, as we shall see later.

The 

In 1042 the Danish kings were expelled from the country, and Edward returned to his native land and was crowned king. 

Because of his piety he was called Edward the Confessor. most notable achievement of his reign was the building of Westminster Abbey, so called because it was an abbey located at the west end of London. Here most of England's kings have been crowned and are buried. Burial in Westminster Abbey even today is considered the greatest honor that England can bestow upon her famous citizens. 

William of Normandy. Upon the death of Edward in 

1066 A.D., Harold, a Saxon noble, became king. He was the choice of both Edward and the Witan. When the news of this event reached Normandy, Duke William prepared for war. He claimed that both his cousin and Harold had promised him the

throne. But his claim was not well founded, since only the 

Witan had the power to name England's king. William was the grandson of Rollo, the old Viking who had invaded France and settled in Normandy. The duke worked all that summer building a fleet to transport his troops to England. He received the sanction of the pope for his expedition. 

During the fall of this same year the Danes again invaded England. Harold marched north and defeated them at the battle at Stamford Bridge. In the meantime William of Normandy had landed with his troops on the southern shore. The 

Saxon period in English history was about to end. 

The Battle of Hastings, 1066 A.D. - Harold hurried south, and on the morning of October 14, 1066, met the enemy at Senlac, or

Hastings. There, five miles from the coast, the battle raged all day. The Saxons fought on foot with battle axes and javelins; the Normans had archers and cavalrymen. The tide of victory swung back and forth until dusk. Then Harold was struck in the eye with an arrow and killed. His bodyguard was slain to the last man, and the Norman conquest of England was assured. 

The Land System of the Conqueror. -William marched northward to London. The city surrendered on Christmas Day, 1066 A.D., and the conqueror was crowned king in Westminster

Abbey. He gave the people of London a charter which permitted them all the privileges enjoyed under the Saxon kings. The 

Saxon nobles who swore fealty to him were permitted to retain their lands. Those who refused or who had fought against him at 

Hastings were deprived of their fiefs. These were divided among his Norman followers. In order to weaken the power of his vassals, he gave them small portions of land in different parts of the kingdom. This made it difficult for them to rally their forces in an attack on the king. 

The Results of Hastings. The battle of Hastings is con sidered one of the fifteen decisive battles in world history. It meant for England a strong united government. The English were brought into contact with the country. The Anglo-Saxon

language was softened and enriched by its mixture with Norman- French. 

The Bayeux Tapestry. French and English accounts of this battle were written at the time. But the most famous description is that which is supposed to be the work of William's queen, Matilda. It is a piece of tapestry, two hundred fourteen feet 000 long and twenty inches wide.

It is woven in beautiful colors which picture seventy-two scenes of the conquest. This is preserved in the Bayeux (ba-yû') Cathedral in France, and is known as the Bayeux Tapestry. From it we gain an idea of the dress and weapons of the period. 

William Takes the Census and Builds the Tower of Lon don. Twenty years after his coronation William took a census of his kingdom. His census-takers took account of the land, sheep, cattle, serfs, The White Tower. Keep of the and income of all the inhabit- 

Tower of London. 

ants. This information was set down in what was called the Domesday Book. He constructed a prison, which was to become famous in the annals of English history as the Tower of London. 

Hereward the Wake. William the Conqueror was kept busy quelling one revolt after another. The fire of Saxon patriotism was not altogether dead. In the swamp lands of Ely the great 

Saxon chief, Hereward, with his brave little band of followers defied the Normans. It was said of Hereward, called the Wake "Had there been three more like him in the island, the Nor mans never would have entered it." But there was only one

Hereward, and the Anglo-Saxon rulers gave way to a new line of royalty -the Norman kings.

The Plantagenets. - Henry II, the great-grandson of William the Conqueror, ascended the English throne in 1154 A.D. Besides being king of England Henry through inheritance and marriage controlled more than half of France. His father, the Count of 

Anjou (än-zhōo'), had the habit of wearing a sprig of golden blossomed broom plant in his helmet. The French word for this plant was plantagenet. Because of this Henry and his successors for the next two hundred years were known as the Plantagenet kings.

Our Modern Jury System Begins: Petit and Grand Juries. — 

The Normans had reintroduced into England the old and unfair method of dealing out justice through trial by combat. Upon the general complaint of his subjects Henry abolished this. He ordered that a body of twelve knights familiar with the case should decide the fate of the accused. These men were called jurors because they swore to tell the truth. He sent judges throughout the kingdom to hold court in the different counties. 

To aid these judges when they visited the district, a grand (large) jury, composed of not less than sixteen men, reported all criminals. 

Later the people accused by the grand jury were allowed to go before the petit (small) jury of twelve knights. The petit jury then decided whether they were guilty or not guilty. Thus began our modern system of petit and grand juries still used in American courts.

Magna Carta, 1215 A.D.: Its Importance Today. — Nearly a century later King John broke faith with his English subjects. 

He made them pay heavy and unjust taxes. Much of the money he used for his own personal pleasure. He refused innocent men a trial by jury and imprisoned them upon his own will and whim. 

His people finally rebelled and demanded justice. King John became alarmed, and, on June 15, 1215 A.D., he met several thousand armed knights on the banks of the Thames (těmz) River at 

Runnymede. This was a memorable day, for he sealed on that

RA-18

date the document known to history as Magna Carta or the Great Charter. Among its many provisions were: 

1. No taxes were to be levied except by a National Council. 

2. No freeman was to be imprisoned except by the law of the land. 

3. Justice was to be neither sold, denied, nor forbidden. Thus began our present system of taxation by representation, and our privilege of the Writ of Habeas Corpus. The latter makes it unlawful to hold a person in prison without reason. In Latin it means to have the body. 

Parliament: the Model for Modern Law-Making Bodies. - 

The next important event in the political history of England during this period was the beginning of the English Parliament. 

The charter provided for a meeting of the chief nobles and the king, and at first it was merely a discussion between them. Later, during the reign of Edward III, there was trouble over taxes and a meeting of nobles and common people was held. In 1265, merchants, landowners, barons, bishops, and representatives from the towns met. In 1295, during the reign of Edward I, the people were permitted to elect representatives from their own class to sit in what was called the House of Commons. The nobles were represented in the House of Lords. The former house shortly after this gained the right to help in the making of laws. This bicameral (two-house) arrangement became the model for most law-making bodies today. Land, Wool, and War. As we know, the English kings owned a great deal of territory in France. This caused jealousy between the two nations. Another cause of hard feeling was the commercial rivalry. The wool industry had become popular in England. 

Large stone houses or factories, where the wool was combed and carded by handworkers, were built. Many of the skilled workmen from Flanders were invited to settle among the English. This they were glad to do because the French kings were taxing them very heavily. Wool soon became the chief source of England's wealth. The bitterness between England and France increased.

In 1338 A.D. war was declared, and because the English were not driven from France until 1453 A.D. the conflict is known in history as the "Hundred Years' War." Of course it was not a continuous affair, but it kept the common people on edge and conditions in general unsettled. It was during one of the engagements of this war that gunpowder was used for the first time in western Europe. This was at the battle of Crécy (kra-se') in 

France. Cannon were used by the English to annoy the French horses. From that day at Crécy says Green in his History of 

England: "Feudalism tottered slowly but surely to its grave."

Joan of Arc. The last days of the war were made famous by the French peasant girl, Joan of Arc - the maid of Orleans. She was born in the little village of Domremy and during her girlhood longed to do something for France. After many adventures she finally reached the king and gained his confidence. Clad in a white armor and carrying a white banner, she led an inspired French army in battle after battle. She was finally captured by the English, tortured, and put to death. But she was more 

Joan of Arc entering France. Painting by Lenepveu.

dangerous dead than alive; her spirit lived on, and within a generation England's power in France was broken. 

By this time the chaos and disorder of the Middle Ages were over. Strong governments had been established in France, Spain, England, and elsewhere. The barbarian invasions had ended, and feudalism had received its death blow at Crécy. A new day for the civilization of western Europe was already beginning. We now turn to this, our next background, in the march of world events.

As you know, the growth of trade and more peaceful conditions permitted the development of towns. In these towns, you will find, progress was to be made in many fields. 274

BACKGROUND SIX  

We have seen how the feudal system gripped all classes of people during the Feudal Age; how the inhabitants of Frankland and Angle-land gradually formed their nations; how the days when "knighthood was in flower" slowly appeared and then slowly disappeared. We have seen the development of the monastic system, the guilds, and the mystery and miracle plays; we have heard folklore and legends set to music by wandering minstrels; and have seen the growth of representive government and trial by jury, and the effect upon commerce of the Crusades and inventions.

Remaining in the same lands, with an occasional trip across the 

Alps, we watch the dawn of "A New Day for Civilization." We shall see order established and scholars, artists, and scientists at work. We shall see how national languages were formed; how paper and the printing press came to western Europe and how they affected mankind. We shall see nations torn by religious, political, and commercial wars. We shall see how strong national governments emerged and what events made possible the dawn of American history. 

CHAPTER XVII THE RENAISSANCE 

The Renaissance. A new day dawned for the civilization of western Europe with the movement known as the Renaissance (ren-e-säns'). The word is derived from the French and means "revival" or "being born anew." It is used by historians to describe that period in history which marks the transition from feudal or medieval times to modern times. 275

As we have already seen, conditions became more settled about the thirteenth century. People had a better opportunity to think and study. Ignorance and superstition began to give way before a spirit of research. A thriving trade was being carried on with the East. Scholars in universities and monasteries began to study the old Greek and Roman civilizations, and their own world gained a new meaning. The science, art, literature, and learning of the past were born anew. The dates generally given for the Renaissance are from the fourteenth to the seventeenth century. But the tap roots of the movement began before that time, and in reality the movement has not ended yet. 

We shall see in this background how we of today have profited from the works of men who lived and wrought during that age. 

The Early Universities. About the year 1000 A.D. univer sities were established in western Europe. Students in those days collected in a certain town or city because of the presence there of some famous teacher. These students came from every nation in Europe. Very often they wore caps showing from what state or country they came. Many quarrels arose among the students because of the differences in their homelands. Taverns, boarding houses, and cottages were crowded, however, and the university community prospered. Towns and cities for this reason competed with one another for a university. Pupils and teachers were given special privileges; for example, they were free from taxation. 

Classes and Pupils. Most of the early universities were quartered in the old cathedral schools or in buildings near by. 

Classes began at five or six o'clock in the morning. The teacher occupied a seat in front of the room. Fresh straw covered the floor, and on this the pupils sat because no desks or chairs were provided. There they sat for six or seven hours a day, writing notes from the master's lecture on waxed tablets. 

The University of Bologna: Medicine and Internes. Many famous universities were organized during the next two centuries. 

The University of Bologna in Italy opened its doors in 1200 A.D.,

THE RENAISSANCE 

277 It although the city had been a center of learning since 1088. became famous as a school of law and medicine. There the health laws of Hippocrates (Chapter IX) were included in the course of study. It became the custom, too, for advanced students to spend part of their time in visiting and aiding the sick within the city. 

Thus our present method of requiring medical students to have hospital practice (interneship) is not a modern idea. At one time there were more than 10,000 students enrolled at the University of Bologna.

University of Paris: Peter Abelard (1079-1143 A.D.).—The beginnings of the University of Paris were laid during that same year, 1088 A.D., but it was not until 1150 that the university was actually established. It became noted for the study of theology (science of religion). Some historians say that this was a mere school until the arrival of Peter Abelard (ab'ě-lärd). He attended the university as a student and at the early age of twenty-two was permitted to teach. Thousands flocked to 

Paris to hear this young master teach the revolutionary doctrine: "Through doubting we come to think and reason." It was the custom in the medieval universities for the majority of the students to believe everything that was taught. 

They never doubted or investigated what Aristotle had written or what the clergy taught. In the latter case to doubt was often considered a crime. Abelard's many pupils began to think for themselves. The mental shackles which had bound them to the narrow ideas of science and culture were loosened. 

Thinkers and Fagots. 

Peter Abelard, famous scholar and teacher. 

Abelard, as a churchman, had taken a vow not to marry. He broke this vow by a secret marriage to one of his former students. This brought upon him the disfavor

of the clergy. The controversy with the church and the responsibility of leadership among his pupils broke his health. At his death he was still far ahead of his time. Many brilliant men were burned at the stake before the day arrived when thinkers were to receive pensions and medals instead of fagots. 

Besides Bologna and Paris there were many other universities at this time. Oxford and Cambridge, established later, were the oldest and most famous in England.

Roger Bacon (1214-1294 A.D.). —- Roger Bacon was an English man who lived about one hundred years after Abelard.

He, too, Merton College, the first of the Oxford colleges. 

Although educated in logic, he later made a

He became a teacher was educated at the University of Paris. 

philosophy (see Plato, Chapter VIII) and special study of science and mathematics. at Oxford, and in his scientific studies he predicted the airplane and the submarine. He taught that nature should be studied by actual observation and experiment. This remarkable man read the books of the famous scientists of Greece in the original Greek language. He understood the composition of gunpowder, which he and his friends used for fireworks. He believed that the earth was round, and invented a telescope to study the heavenly bodies. 

Bacon was severely criticized by some of the church orders for his beliefs. The pope, however, was his friend and encouraged

THE RENAISSANCE 

279 him in his work. Later he was imprisoned for teaching and believing many things which have come true since his day. 

New National Literature. The Crusades had their effect on the Renaissance, for through them the people of western Europe came in contact with the science, the culture, and the ideas of the East. The travelers saw new lands and new commercial opportunities. The universities were influenced by these new ideas; the merchants by the new possibilities for trade. The first influence led to inventions, which in turn aided traders to seek and discover new trade routes to the East. Seeking these new routes led to the discovery and settlement of India, Africa, and America. 

But before this occurred, the spirit of nationalism developed. 

Men coming home began to realize more than ever that they were Italian, English, French, or Spanish. New national literature arose. Groups of writers in each country, instead of writing in 

Greek and Latin, began to write in the language of the common people in their own language. 

The Greatest Poem of the Middle Ages. The most noted piece of literary work during this dawn period of modern civilization was the Divine Comedy. It was written by Dante (dǎn'tė), a writer of Florence, Italy. It has been called the greatest poem of the Middle Ages and the greatest poem of any age written in Italian. Dante deserted the Latin of which he was a master and wrote in the language of his people. 

This great poet was born at Florence in the year 1264 A.D.

There were many wealthy families in the city, and it was the center of art and literature in Italy. Dante's father was a lawyer and a member of the lower nobility. Many of Dante's poems were inspired by his love for a young girl, Beatrice.

Dante took sides in a civil war and was forced to leave Florence. 

He never returned to his native city again. His family remained and managed the best they could. As Dante wandered from city to city, he lived by writing a few poems. Occasionally his wife would send him some of his manuscripts which he had left at home. Among them was the half-finished Divine Comedy. He

JON 

A NEW DAY FOR CIVILIZAT 

Brown Bros.

The meeting of Dante and Beatrice. 

A photograph of Henry Holiday's painting. completed the poem, which met with instant approval. The city of Florence forgave him and invited him to return home. But he scorned this offer, and when he died was buried at Ravenna. 

The Divine Comedy. In the Divine Comedy, Dante and the 

Roman poet, Vergil, take a trip. They traveled over a great winding road and entered a deep pit shaped like a cone. The cone was divided into three parts. At the bottom was Hades, where Dante met many people who had been great sinners on earth. As they journeyed upward to the middle section called Purgatory, they met other people who had worked their way out of Hades. There 

Dante and Vergil found persons undergoing a lighter form of punishment in preparation for Paradise. As the two passed through Purgatory, they came to a woods. In the distance they saw a broad flame of fire which hid everything from view. 

Vergil told Dante that beyond the flame were Paradise and his beloved Beatrice. At first Dante hesitated to jump through the 1

THE RENAISSANCE 

281 flame, but the encouraging words of Vergil banished all fear. He leaped, and before him stood Beatrice, more beautiful than ever.

The poem ends with Dante and Beatrice overjoyed with the thought that they were to be together forever. 

English and French Literature. Shortly after Dante's Divine 

Comedy was written, John Wycliff (wik'lif) translated the Bible into English. Copies were made, and the work was circulated among the common people. Later Geoffrey Chaucer (chô'ser) (1348 A.D.) wrote his Canterbury Tales. This is the story of thirtytwo pilgrims on their way to the tomb of Thomas à Becket in Canterbury. Each pilgrim tells his or her own story. There is a monk, a lawyer, a doctor, a nun, a merchant, a tailor, a weaver, and others. This work was also written in English and gives a very good description of the everyday life of the people of the time. 

Like Dante in Italy and Chaucer in England Froissart (frwä-sär') wrote the famous Chronicle in his native language, the French. 

The Chronicle describes chivalry, knighthood, and the events of the Hundred Years' War during the days of the author. Thus it was that during the Renaissance national literatures were slowly being formed. 

Petrarch, 1304-1374 A.D. - Petrarch (pē'trärk), an Italian, has been called the "Morning Star of the Renaissance." He became famous for his love sonnets to Laura. Disappointed in this 

Chaucer's pilgrims leaving Tabard Inn. From Urry's Chaucer, 1721.

affair of the heart, he buried his grief in a search for old manuscripts written in Greek and Latin. He and his followers looked in dusty garrets and deserted monasteries for these works. 

Petrarch loved this classical literature. He tried to pass his enthusiasm on to others and was very successful. 

Thousands of scholars were eventually searching every nook and corner of Europe for Greco-Roman literature. The writings of Vergil, Ovid, Livy, and Cicero were discovered and copied. 

Students learned to know these old authors. Petrarch made them seem real and human. The early Greeks, also, came in for their share of glory and praise. Homer, Plato, and Sophocles were read, discussed, and remembered. Many of the things that we know today about Greek and Roman learning were brought to light by the patient scholars of this time, and many of our great writers have studied the Greek and Roman classics. 

Paper Mills in Western Europe. The copying of these old manuscripts was an expensive and tedious proposition. Until the fourteenth century all of this work was written by hand on parchment and vellum, a very fine parchment made of calfskin. 

The first paper was probably made by the Chinese. The 

Arabs learned the process from them, and later the Saracens carried the art into Spain. From there it was introduced into southern France. But when Charles Martel defeated the Moors at Tours, paper making was taken back to Spain. There paper mills flourished while the Christians were yet unaware of how paper was made. About 1250 A.D. many Greeks fled from the 

Turks and came to western Europe. They introduced paper making into Italy about 1275 A.D. Two hundred years later there were paper mills in all of the European countries. 

The Printing Press. A more rapid method of writing was necessary to keep pace with the large supply of paper now being turned out. In 1438 John Gutenberg (goo'těn-běrk) of Mainz, Germany, invented movable wooden type. Eighteen years later he printed the Bible in Latin. This was the first complete book produced by a printing press.

It is strange to think that less than five hundred years ago books in Europe were written by hand. When the printing press was invented, the supply of books greatly increased and many more people were able to own them. 

Compare the printing press of long ago with the printing press of today. 283

The Beginning of Copyrights. - The copyists complained about the printers, saying that they were ruining their business. 

As a result people who used printing presses were compelled to secure a license. Printed books were taxed very heavily by the clergy, and the proceeds turned over to universities. The need of securing a license to print a book from the church authorities was the beginning of our modern copyright law. 

Results of Paper and Printing. With the introduction of paper and printing, books became cheap and more accurate. 

Poor people could enjoy them now as well as the rich people. 

Teachers in schools and universities could order enough books to supply a class. More schools were established, and more subjects were studied. Paper and printing scattered learning throughout western Europe. The Renaissance had gained new allies. 

Leonardo da Vinci, 1452-1519 A.D. - In the realm of art, painting, and sculpture during this age, Leonardo da Vinci (vēn'chē) was one of the leaders. He was a musician, engineer, and scientist as well as a sculptor, architect, and painter. It has been said of him that he was master of more subjects than any man in history. 

At one time he applied to the Duke of Milan for a position. Part of his letter read: "I believe I am equaled by no one in architecture in constructing public and private buildings. I can execute sculpture, whether in marble, bronze, or terra cotta. In drawing and painting, I believe I can do as much as any other man. I am ready at any time or any place to prove to you my powers." 99 

Da Vinci, the Scientist. This letter was not a mere boast.

It was the words of a man who could twist horseshoes between his fingers and bend iron bars across his knees; a man who was an excellent harpist, able to play and sing his own compositions; a man who studied science, believed that the earth was round, and understood the planets and orbits. He knew the power of steam, how to dredge, how to use levers, screws, and cranes, and how to raise and lower immense weights. He raised the massive structure of a stone church several feet and placed underneath it a solid foundation.

THE RENAISSANCE 

285 The Last Supper. In a convent at Milan. 

Da Vinci's Painting "The Last Supper." - In order to please the Duke of Milan's wife, Leonardo painted "The Last Supper" on the dining-room walls of a monastery. This room was under the special care of the duchess. The famous picture was painted in oils. Despite the passing of time, the picture still creates a profound impression. The moment which Leonardo chose to represent was not the beginning of the supper. It was when the statement "One of you will betray me" was uttered. How those words affected each disciple is easily seen from the remarkable portrayal of their feelings on each disciple's face. 66 Mona Lisa."-- Until the year 1911 A.D. there hung upon the walls of the Louvre in Paris a renowned picture called the "Mona Lisa" (mō'nä lē'zä). It, too, was the work of da Vinci. He was fifty years of age when he began the work, and it took him four years to complete it. Yet Leonardo's dying regret was that he had not finished this picture. It is a half-length portrait of a female figure. A mixed landscape forms the background. The artist revealed himself in this picture by showing what an ideal face should express: love, beauty, joy, sorrow, and health. Yet the very serious smile that plays around her lips shows that life is good regardless of its many hardships and temptations. RA-19

Michelangelo, 1476-1564 A.D.- Michelangelo (mī-kěl-ǎn'jê-lō) was another great Italian artist of this period.

He was born in a 

Mona Lisa. 

small village a few miles from Florence. At fourteen he became an artist and worked at his profession until he was eightynine. 

Michelangelo's father, who wanted him to secure an easy government position, scoffed at the idea of his son becoming a painter, but the boy's mother secretly encouraged him. 

She was much interested in the charcoal drawings the little fellow made on the walls of the house. Michel- This famous picture is in angelo was one of a hundred the Louvre, Paris. students educated by Lorenzo the Magnificent, ruler of Florence. He soon became famous as a sculptor. His statues of David and of Moses are world famous.

The Sistine Chapel: "The Story of Creation." - Finally he was called to Rome to begin the most gigantic task of his whole career. This was the painting on the ceiling of the Sistine (sis'ten) 

Chapel, seventy feet from the floor. The artist had to lie on his back upon a high scaffold in order to work. He built his own scaffolds, drew his own designs, and employed the best color artists in Italy to lay the base for the series of pictures which he had in mind. The artists failed because they could not grasp 

Michelangelo's ideas. He discharged the whole group and destroyed what little work they had done. He shut off the ceiling from view and worked alone.

THE RENAISSANCE 

287 For days Michelangelo would not leave the building because he feared some harm would be done to his paintings. He drew his food up on a string and slept on the scaffold. A year passed, and no one had seen his work. The pope became impatient, and a part of the scaffold was torn down to permit him to view the painting. He was overjoyed with what he saw. But three more years elapsed before it was shown to the public. This took place on "All Souls' Day" in 1512. Michelangelo had painted the Biblical story of the Creation. There are more than three hundred figures larger than life in the vast painting. This was necessary, because they were to be viewed from such a great distance. He was thirty-nine years of age when this famous piece of art was completed. "The Last Judgment."- Twenty years elapsed before he began painting "The Last Judgment" on the wall at the upper end of the chapel. The redeemer is shown as a powerful figure in judgment. The "condemned" are arranged on one side waiting to be judged, while the joyful "saved" are grouped together on the opposite side. 

Michelangelo was 

The Dome of St. Peter's Cathedral. eventually made chief architect of St. Peter's Cathedral by Pope 

Paul III. This famous sculptor, architect, and painter worked under seven popes during his lifetime. As an architect he directed the construction of the dome of the Church of St. Peter. 

This marvelous monument alone would have made its architect famous. America has recognized the genius of Michelangelo by using his dome as a model for the crowning glory of the Capitol at Washington. 

Condemned in One Age Honored in Another. There were many other famous scholars, scientists, writers, painters, and sculptors during this age. Copernicus (ko-pûr'nĭ-kus) (14731543 A.D.) was born in Poland. He advanced the theory that the earth was a globe and revolved with other planets around the sun.

The universities and the clergy taught that all the other planets revolved around the earth. Copernicus, knowing

Helen Lentut ont 

R. Rafius

St. Peter's Cathedral. Notice the dome. At the right is the Vatican, the palace of the pope. that he would be condemned as a heretic, published his theory. 

He received a copy of his book while on his death bed, and within a few hours he was beyond the reach of earthly persecution. 

Besides Copernicus there was Galileo (găl-i-lē'ō) (1564-1642 A.D.), who proved by actual experiment that many scientific beliefs previously held were false. He was brought to trial, threatened with physical torture, and forced to deny his theories. Condemned by inferior men of his own day, both he and his ideas have been honored by the people of modern times.

The Reformation. For many years the Roman Catholic 

Church had been the one and only church in western Europe. 

Its head, the pope at Rome, was very powerful and could, if he wished, force kings to do as he commanded. Numerous assistants, from archbishops down to the priests, kept the pope

THE RENAISSANCE 

289 supplied with knowledge of what was going on and carried out his orders.

Gradually a movement fostered by the knowledge gained from reading the Greek and Roman classics and the development of "thinking for one's self" began. People began to doubt some of the church's teachings and to speak against some of the church practices, especially the collection of taxes. This movement, which was an attempt to correct certain abuses and unjust practices, was led by a German monk, Martin Luther. only spoke against church practices, but also began to write against them. It was easy for him to reach a great many people because of the new printing press. Later he was to translate the Bible into the German lan- guage.

In time Martin Luther, who was forced to leave the church, and his followers were called Protestants, because they "protested" against church practices. The religious excitement spread throughout 

Europe, and the Netherlands, Denmark, Norway, Sweden, parts of Switzerland, and later England became Protestant countries. 

In Switzerland John Calvin, who had fled from France, led the movement. During the next two hundred years Catholics were to persecute Protestants in Catholic countries, while Protestants were to persecute 

Catholics in Protestant countries. There 

Martin Luther. was to be much suffering and bloodshed before mankind learned the folly of such persecutions.

The Catholic Church also had its reformation led by leaders who wished to improve its organization. One of these leaders was Pope Paul III, who was aided by the converted soldier of fortune, Ignatius Loyola (lo-yō'lä), who at one time had made a pilgrimage to Palestine.

The Jesuits.-Loyola organ ized a group of seven men, of whom only one was a priest, into the Society of Jesus. They took vows of poverty and chastity and vowed to fight heresy and to spread Christianity throughout the world. Loyola offered the services of his organization to the pope, but the society was not recognized until 1540.

These Jesuits, as the members of the Society of Jesus came to be called, founded Ignatius Loyola. From a painting by schools and carried the gospel Rubens. everywhere. They faced danger, hardship, and death fearlessly, and played an important part in the exploration and colonization of Spanish and French 

America.

CHAPTER XVIII  The Rise of the Tudors: Henry VII (1485-1509 A.D.). — The 

Hundred Years' War between England and France ended in 1453 A.D. (Chapter XVI). Two years later the families of Lancaster and York both claimed the English throne. For thirty years the claimants and their followers were torn by civil strife. 

This war is known as the War of the Roses, because the followers of the Duke of York wore as an emblem a white rose, while those of the Duke of Lancaster wore a red one. Finally, in 1485, the struggle ended in a victory for Lancaster. At that time Henry 

Tudor was crowned king of England on the battlefield. 

A Strong Ruler Welcomed. The rise of the Tudors marked the beginning of a strong national government in England. 

Many of the nobles had been killed during the war, and their property had been taken by the king. The common people were tired of warfare and of the misery that followed in its wake. 

They longed for peace, and to secure this they were willing to welcome and to aid a strong ruler.

Henry Tudor was a wise and shrewd king. He crushed one rebellion after another and broke the power of ambitious nobles. 

He established order throughout the land, promoted art and literature, and saved money for the government. He took advantage of his subjects' desire for peace and gained almost absolute power, which they willingly allowed him to usurp. 

The Royal Tax. The wealthy nobles could afford to maintain large bands of followers. Their strength was a menace to the king's power, so Henry decided to tax them heavily. This affected both his friends and his enemies. The following story was told about the Earl of Oxford, a stanch supporter of the king. 

The earl wanted both to honor and to make an impression upon his sovereign. He invited Henry to his castle. His retainers in 292

costly dress held a parade for the king. Henry was amazed at the splendor of the entire reception. Upon leaving he said: "My lord, I thank you, but my attorney must speak to you.' The earl was fined 15,000 pounds, about $60,000. 

Morton's Fork. - Another method which Henry used to gain revenue (income) for the state was by means of gifts or benevolences. Rich people were asked for a gift of so many pounds in English money. Archbishop Morton had charge of these collections. He devised a scheme which became known as "Morton's Fork." The archbishop's agents spied on wealthy people to see in what fashion they lived. If a noble was frugal and spent little money, he was told that he could afford to give because he saved so much. If a noble lived in splendor, the agents said he could afford to give to his king since he spent so freely upon himself. No matter what way a wealthy person lived, he was sure to be caught on one of the prongs of Morton's Fork. 

The Court of the Star Chamber. In order to enforce his laws against the nobles, Henry induced Parliament to create the "Star Chamber." This was a court which consisted of seven members and met in secret session. Fines and prison terms were imposed upon persons without a trial by jury. While it was meant to weaken the power of nobles, it was nevertheless a symbol of tyranny. It violated the principles of the Great Charter and later was considered a menace to the growing democracy of England. 

The Renaissance Begins in England. - Henry was very careful to avoid wars with European nations. He realized their enormous cost and the drain on England's man power. This was fortunate for the English people. It permitted them to benefit by the Renaissance. In England, during the reign of Henry VII and his successor Henry VIII, that movement centered around three names - Colet, Erasmus, and More. 

Colet: Train the Youth and Improve Society. — These three men were known as the Oxford reformers. This was because the revival started with John Colet at the University of Oxford.

Colet became famous as a student of the Bible. He was appointed dean of St. Paul's Cathedral in London. He became convinced that the only way to improve society was to begin with the young.

With this in mind he founded the St. Paul's School for boys. 

Colet used his time and energy to improve both the methods of teaching and the studies at St. Paul's. Pure Latin and 

Greek were taught, and the best authors were substituted for the daily floggings. The school became famous throughout England. Many noted clergy and learned scholars received their early training on the hard benches which filled the halls of this renowned institution. It became the model for hundreds of grammar schools built throughout England at a later date. 

Erasmus (1465-1536 A.D.). — Erasmus was a Dutchman who taught Greek at the University of Cambridge. He wielded a great influence upon the learned men of Europe, and interested them in Greek culture, the subject in which he himself was very interested. In a letter to a friend he said: "I have given my whole soul to Greek learning, and as soon as I get some money I shall buy Greek books, and then I shall buy some clothes." 

Sir Thomas More (1480-1535 A.D.). — Sir Thomas More was a very close friend of Erasmus. He was raised in the household of 

Cardinal Morton. More attended Oxford, studied law, and was eventually appointed Lord Chancellor by the king. This was the highest civil office in England.

Utopia. In 1515 More published a book called Utopia. The word is derived from the Greek and means "Nowhere." The 

Utopia of Sir Thomas More was an imaginary country probably located far out on the ocean. In Utopia peace and happiness prevailed. There were no rich and no poor everybody worked. Laws were based on justice and reason, and men could worship God as they saw fit without being persecuted. Every one was educated. The streets and houses had excellent drainage systems, and because of these and other sanitary conditions many diseases were avoided.

The book was an attempt to show the English people how they 

Most of the reforms advocated 

But in More's day they seemed could improve their conditions. in Utopia have since come true. like the talk of an idle dreamer. 

Today, however, we still speak of ideal situations as being utopian or fanciful. He

Henry VIII (1509-1547 A.D.). -The work of the three Oxford reformers was encouraged by Henry's son, Henry VIII. ascended the throne in 1509 A.D. at the age of eighteen. During

E the first year of his reign he married his brother's widow, Catherine of Aragon. She was a daughter of Ferdinand and 

Isabella, rulers of Spain, and an aunt of the emperor of the Holy Roman Empire, Charles V. After twenty years of married life 

Henry decided to secure a divorce from Catherine. They had several children, all of whom, except one daughter, had died in infancy. The king was very anxious to have a son to succeed him, for there was some doubt at that time whether it was wise or even legal for a woman to reign in her own right. Henry VIII. From the frontispiece to Coverdale's Bible, 1535, designed by 

Holbein. What is the king doing?

Henry about this time fell in love with Anne Boleyn (bool'in), one of Catherine's maids in waiting. He sent his powerful minister, Cardinal Wolsey (wool'zi), to the pope at Rome to plead for a divorce. Wolsey argued that Henry's marriage to his brother's widow was illegal. The pope was very hesitant about gaining the ill will of Charles V, who was now the most powerful figure in Europe. Therefore he declared the marriage legal and refused the divorce. Upon that decision resulted the separation of England from the Catholic Church. With this step began the Reformation in English history. 

Death of Cardinal Wolsey. — During Martin Luther's quarrel with the church, Henry had defended the pope. As a reward for his stand he had been given the title of "Defender of the Faith." (This title is still used by English kings.) But now all was changed, the king was bitter toward the pope. He even accused 

Wolsey of being false, stripped him of all his wealth, and ordered him to London to stand trial. However, the old and faithful cardinal, broken in spirit and health, died in a monastery before reaching London. His last words to the abbot were: "If I had served my God as diligently as I have done the king, he would not have given me over in my gray hairs."

The Act of Supremacy, 1534 A.D.- Shortly after this (1535 

A.D.) Henry married Anne Boleyn. The pope threatened to excommunicate him. (If a ruler was excommunicated, all of his subjects renounced him and paid allegiance to a new monarch.) 

The English Parliament stood by their king and passed the Act of Supremacy. By that act Henry VIII was declared head of the 

English church. It also stated that any person in England who opposed this move was guilty of treason. In this manner the separation from the papacy took place, and the Church of England, or the Anglican Church, was established. 

The Execution of Sir Thomas More. The Anglican Church abolished some of the forms of Catholicism and added a few Protestant ideas. But in reality it was neither Catholic nor Protestant. There was much dissatisfaction and opposition on the

part of both sects. Sir Thomas More, the author of Utopia, was executed because he would not acknowledge Henry as head of the Church of England. 

Monasteries Destroyed; Farmers Thrown Out of Work. — 

The king took possession of the monasteries. He took all their wealth and used it to further his own needs. This was done at a most unfavorable time, for wool growing was becoming very profitable in England. For this reason many farmers had turned their farms into sheep pastures. Because they inclosed their farm lands, the system was known as "Inclosures." A shepherd and dog now replaced many workers who had formerly been needed to raise grain. Many poor people were thrown out of work. The monasteries, which had cared for charity cases, were no longer able to help. Because of this one historian has said: "The whole realm was overgrown with beggars and thieves." 

The Bill of Attainder. In order to crush all opposition against the new church, a Bill of Attainder was passed. By this act an accused person was not permitted to defend himself in court trials. 

This procedure was most hateful to the English people. More than two hundred years later it was such a fearful instrument that the people of the United States in their Constitution said: “No bill of attainder shall ever be passed."

The Marriages of Henry VIII. - There was one daughter born to Henry and Anne Boleyn. Her name was Elizabeth, and she was later to reign as queen of England. After three years of married life the king imprisoned Anne in the Tower of London, and had her beheaded. The next morning he married Jane 

Seymour, who had been the former queen's maid of honor.

This third wife died the next year leaving a son, Edward. 

The Heirs of Henry. - Henry VIII died in 1547. An act of 

Parliament had been passed which permitted him to name his successor. By the terms of his will he was to be succeeded by his son, Edward VI, who was to rule under the guidance of his uncle. 

If there were no male heirs after Edward or his son, the crown was to pass to his daughters. The first of these, as we have seen, was

The second was 

Edward reigned only a 

She tried to bring Eng Mary, whose mother was Catherine of Aragon. 

Elizabeth, daughter of Anne Boleyn. few years and was succeeded by Mary. land back to the Catholic Church, and her reign was marked by persecution and bloodshed. Her death in 1558 paved the way for one of England's most renowned rulers - Queen Elizabeth. "Good Queen Bess." Elizabeth was twenty-five years old at the time of her coronation. For forty-five years she governed the realm. The new queen was loyal to her country and considerate of her subjects. The English people in turn were loyal to her. They credited "Good Queen Bess" with England's greatness and prosperity during this period. 

Queen Elizabeth enjoyed sports of all kinds and possessed excellent health. It was said that she could hunt all day, dance all night, and work hard and well on state affairs the next day. 

She spoke French and Italian and read Greek. She loved music but cared little for poetry and literature. This partly explains why she failed to appreciate and generously aid that famous group of men headed by William Shakespeare. However, they helped to make her reign compare with that of Pericles of Athens. 

Shakespeare at the court of Queen Elizabeth.

The new queen was vain and loved flattery. She believed the nice things her courtiers said about her. Sometimes her silly actions would amuse the whole court. Very often she was rude to her ministers, and when she became angry her language was crude, rough, and unladylike. She had her virtues and her faults, but she proved a wise, courageous, and good ruler.

The Privy Council. — Elizabeth grew up at a time in English history when she had to be very cautious. A hasty word or a false move on her part would have led either to her imprisonment or to death. In later life her decision on important matters was never made hastily. She knew the value of good advisers and was fortunate in knowing how to choose the wisest and best. 

She appointed as her chief adviser Sir William Cecil, later called 

Lord Burleigh. Upon his appointment Elizabeth told him: "This judgment I have of you, that you will not be corrupted with any gifts, that you will be faithful to the State, and without respect to my private will, you will give me that counsel which you think best." This he did faithfully until his death forty years later. Cecil was ably assisted by a small group of men called the Privy Council. The queen always took this council into her confidence before making any decision which affected the government.

Queen Elizabeth and the Church of England. During the reign of Elizabeth the Church of England was firmly established. 

The queen was declared the supreme head of the church. She was given the power to appoint bishops and archbishops. A creed of thirty-nine articles of faith was written, and every one was compelled to abide by them. All were required to attend the established church. If they refused, they could be fined or imprisoned. It was made a crime for any person to attend a Catholic mass. This state of affairs caused much discontent during the early part of Elizabeth's reign. But she exercised her power very shrewdly. When she ascended the throne, she was strongly and bitterly opposed by the Catholics.

She was a Elizabeth

The Enemies of Elizabeth. This was because they considered her mother's marriage to Henry VIII illegal. They favored her cousin, Mary Stuart, Queen of Scotland. Mary was the granddaughter of Margaret, sister of Henry VII.

Catholic and married to the dauphin of France. found France, Scotland, the pope, and the English Catholics arrayed against her. Philip II, king of Spain, wanted to marry 

Elizabeth. He was a powerful monarch and as long as there was any possibility of this union, Scotland and France were on the defensive. Elizabeth put Philip off year after year. All this time England's shrewd ruler was gaining the love and loyalty of her subjects. She had no intention of marrying the Spanish king. He finally realized this and sought for some excuse to attack England. 

The Spanish Armada, 1588 A.D. The excuse was soon found. 

Elizabeth made peace with France and sent aid to the Dutch in their war against Spain. The so-called Elizabethan "Sea Dogs,” under Sir Francis Drake, attacked and captured the Spanish treasure ships off the coast of South America. Mary Queen of 

Scots was beheaded by order of Elizabeth and her advisers. 

The execution of Mary brought the long-smoldering feud between Elizabeth and Philip to a head. In the year 1588 he collected a large fleet called the Armada. FO The Spanish Armada. From a tapestry in the House of Lords.

It went

The fleet sailed from Spain in a glorious manner. first to the Netherlands where the ships were loaded with Spanish soldiers on service there against the Protestants. The approach of the Spanish fleet aroused English patriotism. Catholics and 

Protestants joined in a common cause against the invaders. The 

English navy was placed in command of Lord Howard, a Catholic, and Sir Francis Drake, a Protestant, was second in command. 

The English sailors proved better seamen than the Spaniards. 

After an eight-day sea fight the Spanish Armada was forced to retire for repairs. As the fleet lay off the coast of Calais (kăl'ā) in France, English fire ships attacked and scattered their vessels. 

They were an easy prey for the English vessels lying in wait. 

The Channel was blocked, so the disappointed Spanish leaders sailed north. From there they hoped to return home by way of 

Scotland. But fate favored the English. Fierce storms arose. 

The remnants of the Spanish fleet found the waves and rocks more cruel and disastrous than English cannon and firebrands. 

Few survivors returned home to relate the tragic end of the once proud and mighty Armada. The Spanish sea power was broken.

England became "mistress of the seas." The door was opened for English colonization of America and other parts of the world. 

The Literature of Elizabethan England. English literature flourished during the time of Elizabeth. During the last ten years of her life Shakespeare produced his best tragedies and comedies. Francis Bacon wrote his famous essays. The first plays of Ben Jonson appeared, and Spenser wrote his Faërie Queene. During the Tudor period Homer's Iliad, Plutarch's work, and Vergil's Aeneid were translated into English. The writers of 

Elizabeth's time used many of these stories in their dramas. 

It is difficult to say exactly what caused so much literary genius to appear in the space of those few years. There were, however, several causes that aided the movement. The Italian Renaissance had made its way across the English Channel. Exploration and discovery with its broad outlook stirred the imagination of literary people. Then, too, Elizabeth in the latter part of her RA-20

reign had adopted a moderate religious policy. The country was not kept in constant turmoil because of religion, and writers directed their attention to other things. 

The Elizabethan Theater. The theater was changed during the days of Queen Elizabeth. This was mostly due to the efforts of William Shakespeare and his followers. Before his time plays pork/cus at orchestia ingressus mimorum ades. profcanium.

Swan Theater, Eankside, London. 

From a sketch made in 1596. were given in church yards or on floats drawn through the streets. They were now acted in a building erected for that purpose. The theater of those days was a plain wooden structure with eight sides. 

The sides facing the stage supported rows of seats. These seats were protected by a roof and were occupied by the wellto-do people, who were per- 

Imitted to walk around on the stage while the play was going on. The poorer classes shoved one another around for standing room in the center of the theater, which was called the pit. This section of the build- ing was not covered. The light through the opening, however, had to serve for audience and players. Many times the actors had to hurry through their parts to avoid the early darkness of winter afternoons.

All plays were acted by men. Female parts were taken by youths dressed in costly gowns. At best the scenery was simple but more often it was entirely lacking. There was no curtain to indicate the start or the end of an act or to permit stage hands to change a scene. This was accomplished by a large written sign 

For example, if a scene was to take place on an island, a large

placard read "This is an island." Since the stage settings failed to give the audience any background, the playwriters had to create beautiful descriptions. These early English theaters, like those of Athens, took the place of magazines and newspapers. 

The Tudor Influence on England. - Queen Elizabeth never married. She died in 1603 without an heir, and the House of 

Tudor came to an end. The Tudor period was one of progress in 

English history. The government was strengthened. The two 

Henrys and Elizabeth had wielded almost absolute power. 

Under Henry VII literature and science were encouraged. He also financed the explorations of John and Sebastian Cabot, who laid the foundation for England's claim to America. 

The Quarantine. Because of the unsanitary living conditions the plague was a frequent visitor to the land. of Henry VIII a quarantine system was begun. The city of 

London passed a law which stated that homes infected by the plague were to be marked with "wisps of straw." A person who had been exposed carried a white rod in his hand. Houses and even sections of the city affected were isolated or closed. No one was permitted to leave or enter the area. If any person broke the quarantine, he was fined or imprisoned. 

In the reign

The Elizabethan Era. - The Elizabethan era was marked by many changes. Beautiful stone houses with glass windows and chimneys were now built for the first time. Beds with soft pillows became common. Pewter plates and spoons replaced the wooden ones of former days, and forks first appeared in 1608.

Many people said that these were an "insult to Providence who has given us fingers." In the homes of the rich the walls were hung with costly tapestries and glass mirrors. Painting decorated the ceilings. The household furniture was beginning to be highly carved and much more artistic. Steel needles were used for the first time. Potatoes, tobacco, and corn were introduced from America. Newspapers and the modern postal system were launched. Many other events took place, a few of which will be described because of their influence on American history.

The Bourbons in France: Henry IV (1589-1610 A.D.).- 

While the central government in England under the Tudors was growing strong, the same thing was taking place in France under the Bourbons. The first of these rulers was Henry, Prince of Navarre. For years previous to this France had been torn by religious strife. The last of the old line of kings had been murdered in 1589. The nearest heir was the youthful Henry of Navarre, a Protestant. Some time later he became a Catholic. 

The Edict of Nantes, 1598 A.D. This first Bourbon king did not forget the Protestants. In the year 1598 A.D. he issued in the city of Nantes (nănts) a general order or edict, known as the Edict of Nantes. By the terms of this proclamation the Protestants, or Huguenots as they were called, were given certain privileges. They were allowed the same political rights as the Catholics. They were permitted to hold government positions and to occupy certain fortified towns. In those towns they were permitted to worship in their own churches. 

Henry Given Absolute Powers. Henry IV inherited a bank rupt nation and a discontented people. The nobility and clergy were represented in the French Estates-General, a body which corresponded to the English Parliament. Since these two classes had plotted against his succession, Henry abolished the system. He organized the Assembly of the Notables, in which were represented the clergy, called the First Estate, the nobility, called the Second Estate, and the great middle class, or Third Estate. Henry called the assembly together and asked them to suggest a way to solve the financial difficulties of the country. 

They were unable to do this and asked Henry to assume full responsibility. The French, like the English, aided their ruler in the march toward absolute government. 

Henry IV was undaunted by the task thrust upon him. He placed a tax on land and on salt. He required men to purchase their positions if they desired to hold public office. In this manner he placed France on a firm financial basis. Furthermore he encouraged agriculture, promoted the silk industry, and aided

th ​LOL ​A reconstruction of the château of Cardinal Richelieu. Its rooms were decorated with rich tapestries, rare statuary, and pictures. 

commerce by protecting merchant vessels from pirates. He established peace throughout the land and upon his death a contented people had learned to call him "Good King Henry." 

Louis XIII and Cardinal Richelieu. Henry IV died in 

1610 A.D. At that time his son and successor, Louis XIII, was only nine years old. The boy's mother attempted to rule for him, but in a short time French affairs were in a turmoil and the country was on the verge of civil war. The situation was saved by the appearance of a brilliant statesman, Cardinal Richelieu (re-she-lyû'). In the year 1624 Louis XIII began to rule in his own right. The young king was incapable of handling the numerous problems of the government. But his appointment of Richelieu as chief minister in the council saved the situation. 

The Power of the Huguenots Broken. The iron-willed minister decided to accomplish two things which would make France a strong national government. The first of these was to overthrow the power of the Huguenots; the second was to humble the nobility. The Huguenots had already planned to set up an independent Protestant government; La Rochelle, a city on the 

---

western coast, was to be the capital. Richelieu laid siege to this stronghold. The city held out for more than a year, but faced with famine and disease and cut off from all outside aid, the defenders were forced to surrender in 1628. One after another the remaining Protestant cities were defeated, and the political hopes of the Huguenots were crushed. They were, however, permitted freedom of worship. crown. 

The Nobility Subject to the King. The nobility were also a menace to the king's power. To offset this, Richelieu organized an extensive spy system. Men accused of conspiracy against the government were tried by a commission appointed by the Local taxes were collected by special agents of the king, called intendants. These men also supervised local affairs and reported to Richelieu. Gradually the power of the nobility was broken, and all government agencies were controlled in Paris. 

Under Richelieu absolute government was firmly established. 

The Thirty Years' War (1618-1648 A.D.). Richelieu had a third problem. This was to humble the mighty house of Hapsburg in Austria. At home there was peace, but outside of France there was a great religious war in progress. It is known as the 

Thirty Years' War, and was a struggle between the Protestant German princes and the Catholic countries of Spain and Austria. 

Richelieu saw an advantage for France if he aided the German

Protestants. He himself was an official of the Catholic Church. 

Why then did he wish to aid the Protestants in a foreign land while he fought them at home? His purpose was to extend the boundaries of France to the Rhine River and to make his country the most powerful in Europe.

When the French entered the war, both sides were nearly exhausted. For nearly twenty years the German territory had served as a battlefield. Cities were plundered and burned. 

Trades and industries were neglected, and commerce had almost ceased. Farms were laid waste, and most of the people were living in poverty. In the end the combined forces of Sweden and 

France triumphed. Although Richelieu was not to see the results,

for he died in 1642, he gained his ends. The Hapsburgs of 

Austria and Spain sued for peace, and the power of the French king was recognized in Europe.

Treaty of Westphalia, 1648 A.D. — By the terms of the treaty 

France received Metz, Verdun, Toul, and the province of Alsace, west of the Rhine. The independence of Switzerland and the 

Dutch Republic was recognized. There were many other clauses in this treaty which greatly affected the map of Europe, but for our present story they are of little importance. 

Philip II and the Netherlands. In the year 1555 A.D. Philip

II became king of Spain. He inherited the Spanish possessions in America and the Netherlands. The Netherlands included the countries we know today as Belgium and Holland. The word means "lowland." The Rhine, the Meuse (müz), and the Scheldt (skelt) rivers flow through this country. These rivers frequently overflow their banks. The sturdy inhabitants in those days, even as now, had to meet this danger by digging a system of canals to carry off the overflow. Then, too, part of the land is below the sea level and dikes had to be built for protection against the sea. 

The network of canals, dikes, and windmills with the rich green meadows made the Netherlands a place of artistic beauty. 

The Dutch were a liberty-loving people, prosperous and happy until the Reformation disturbed their peace. The Protestant teachings spread across the German border into the Netherlands. 

Philip II determined to crush the movement. 

Spanish Persecutions in the Lowlands. He remained in the country and established the Inquisition. This was a secret court which adopted cruel tortures to compel heretics or those accused of heresy to confess. People were burned at the stake, strangled, and buried alive. But Protestantism in the Netherlands increased despite these persecutions. Finally Philip returned to Spain, but a Spanish army under the Duke of Alva remained. This harsh general executed so many people that thousands fled to England and to the German states for safety.

William of Orange: Champion of Holland. But a champion appeared for the Netherlands in the person of William of Orange. 

He was a former Catholic, who during the persecutions accepted the Protestant faith. He sold most of his property and, with the money, raised and equipped an army. Fearlessly he declared war on Philip II, the most powerful monarch in Europe. William had courage, and he instilled the same spirit in the hearts of the lowland people.

However, the hired soldiers of William were no match for the trained veterans of the Duke of Alva. It began to look like 

William of Orange. From an old print. a lost cause. Finally the northern provinces under the Dutch took to the sea. Many of the seacoast towns were captured, and the Spanish forces driven out.

The seven provinces of the north, known as Holland, declared their independence. William of Orange was elected governor. Philip II recalled Alva and placed the armies of Spain under a new commander. 

Siege of Leyden, 1574. The most heroic event of this war was the siege of Leyden. The Spaniards surrounded the city and demanded its surrender. For four months the inhabitants defied their enemy and defended their city. They were faced with starvation but still maintained their faith in William of Orange. Looking toward the sea, they could distinguish the 

Dutch fleet loaded with supplies. As a last resort the dikes were cut, and the ships sailed up to the walls of the city. Leyden was saved. William and the cities of Holland built a university at Leyden as a memorial for the brave stand its people had taken against Spanish tyranny.

The Dutch Republic. - By the Treaty of Utrecht in 1579 the ten southern provinces acknowledged the authority of Philip.

Their country, which was mainly Catholic and French, became Belgium. The Dutch continued the struggle, and Queen Elizabeth of England openly aided them. The destruction of the 

Spanish Armada weakened Spain, and the Dutch hopes were raised. Then, in 1648 A.D., by the terms of the Treaty of Westphalia the independence of the Dutch Republic was assured. 

Russia. For almost three hundred years after 1480 A.D. the 

Russian princes were forced to pay tribute to their conquerors, the Mongols, barbarians from Asia. Because of this fact a strong national government developed late in Russia. In 1613 the 

Russians were able to free themselves from this Oriental tyranny and to conquer northern Asia. The new ruling family was known as the House of Romanov (rô-mä'nof). 

The Romanovs were to govern this vast territory until the Revolution of 1917. 

PIERRE I Peter the Great (16891724). Peter became. ruler of Russia in 1689. At that time his people were following the habits and customs of Eastern countries. The young czar, or king, grew up in Moscow, where he met people from France, England, Holland, Italy, and Germany. Peter realized that these countries had a civilization far greater than Russia. He determined to make his half-barbarian country more modern. The best students in 

Russia were sent throughout Europe to learn western ways of 

Peter the Great. Painting by L. Caravaque.

work and manners of living. Peter traveled everywhere to learn new things, and even worked as a laborer in the shipyards of 

England and Holland. 

It was very difficult to get the Russian people to change to the new ideas which Peter and the students brought to them, but the young king was strong-willed as well as enthusiastic. He forced the men to shave off their long beards. Their long robes were replaced by European trousers. Women were compelled to travel without veils and were encouraged to attend dances. He found it very difficult, however, to change the customs of the people in Moscow.

Peter then decided to build a new capital for his new civilization. At this time Russia did not have a seaport in the west, a fact which worried Peter greatly. He fought a war with Sweden and gained a strip of land along the Baltic Sea, for, he said, a window was needed on the Baltic Sea to let in the light of European civilization. Thousands of Russians worked for months to fill in the low marshy seacoast, many of whom lost their lives from exposure before the great undertaking was finished. The new capital was called St. Petersburg after its founder. It is now called Leningrad. The Russians called their king Peter the 

Great because he had given them a strong government and had introduced them to western civilization. 

Catherine the Great. — Almost fifty years later a remarkable ruler came to the Russian throne who continued the work which Peter had begun. Catherine, also called "the Great,” did much to make Russia a great nation. She extended its boundaries in both Europe and Asia.

The Dawn of American History. -England and France had developed strong national governments. The power of the 

Spanish king was still absolute. The Dutch had taken their place among the family of nations. While these political events were taking place, those same nations were interested in other things. One of their chief interests was the colonization and settlement of America. Many of the events so far related in the

two chapters of this background were causes for the dawn of American history. This will be the subject of our next chapter.

CHAPTER XIX  

World Commerce Begins. - Genoa and Venice were the two most important of the many city-states which had been organized in Italy after the fall of Rome. During the Feudal Age they became the ports of embarkation for the crusading expeditions. Their citizens were kept busy supplying ships, food, clothing, and money to the Crusaders. The Crusades, as we have seen (Chapter XV), stimulated commerce between the people of western Europe and the Orient. The Genoese and 

Venetian merchants continued to encourage this trade. They journeyed to the Black Sea region, where they met the caravan trains from Syria, Arabia, India, and far-away China. From these traders they purchased spices, silks, linen, ivory, and costly gems. These products were then transported to points along the Mediterranean Sea and western Europe. Thus a commercial interest which was world-wide began to grip the people. 

Marco Polo Visits the Far East and a Commercial War Follows. The Italian sailors returned home with marvelous stories which they had heard from the traders of the Orient. 

They told about the treasures of wonderful lands in the Far East. 

These countries were called Cathay and Cipango (China and Japan). There were two brothers by the name of Polo who lived in Venice. They became very much interested in these strange tales and determined to visit this treasure land of the Orient. This they did. The brothers, when they returned, gave such a remarkable description of Chinese civilization that few people would believe them. 

The Polos had been so delighted with their first visit that they prepared for a second. On this trip they were accompanied by young Marco, a son of one of the brothers. The courageous travelers spent three years journeying overland from the Black 313

Sea to distant China. They passed through many strange countries, following caravan routes where they could and making new trails where none existed. Often their march was blocked by robber bands, but finally they reached their destination. 

The emperor of China was Kublai Khan (1259-1294 A.D.), who became very fond of Marco and gave him many positions of Marco studying his maps and charts. trust in the government. 

He sent him on special missions throughout the empire and into Japan. While 

Marco was serving the Great

Khan in distant parts of 

China, his father and uncle were collecting costly gems.

After twenty years the three Polos returned to 

Venice. Their old friends and associates refused to believe their accounts concerning the wealth of China. 

Their doubts vanished when they saw the costly collection of diamonds, rubies, and sapphires. The wealth of the Polos helped to create a deadly rivalry between Venice and Genoa. Each wanted to gain control of the trade routes to the Far East, where this fabulous wealth was found. 

In the war which followed, Marco Polo was placed in command of the Venetian fleet. Many naval battles between the two city-states were fought in the Mediterranean Sea. Finally Polo and several thousand of his seamen were captured. The commander was imprisoned in the city of Genoa. While there he had plenty of time to recall his past adventures in Asia. These he told to a fellow-captive, who wrote them down.

Not long after this The Book of Ser Marco Polo appeared. 

This book was widely read in Europe and created much interest in the Orient. It may be that two hundred years later Columbus based his dreams about the East on a book written by this prisoner in Genoa.

The struggle begun by Venice and Genoa for the control of these trade routes was to be continued by European nations for the next two centuries. This struggle was to result also in the accidental discovery of America. Since the fifteenth-century explorers were so interested in China, let us take a look at that wonderful land. 

Chinese Myths and Legends: The Divided Dragon. The 

Chinese had a civilization almost as old as that of Egypt or the Fertile Crescent. They too had their myths to explain the creation of the world and other things which they could not understand. They had their religion, legends, and hero stories. One of the most famous of the last is the tale of 

The Divided Dragon. 

Every year, so the story runs, the ancient Chinese sacrificed a little child to their water god. This was done to calm his anger and to avoid a national calamity. The lot fell one year upon the royal family. Sadly they prepared a basket and padded it with the finest silk. The royal infant was placed in the basket and set afloat on the river, where he would finally be seized

HOID Badal 

A reconstruction of the ancient Lunho-ta pagoda at Hang-chau. by the water god. A rice-grower and his wife coming home from work spied the basket at the water's edge. They were filled with

pity for the little infant and, defying the ancient custom, took him home with them. These humble peasants had a little son of their own, and the two boys grew up together. Every one thought that the boys were twins, for the rice-grower had stamped half of a dragon on each of their chests. This was a common Chinese practice when twins were born in a family. 

When the boys grew older, they were sent to school. The young prince learned very rapidly and was soon far ahead of his foster brother. The Chinese government agents were attracted by this brilliant lad, and he was educated free of charge. 

Years later a great famine occurred in the land. Thousands of hungry people revolted against their ruler. The peasant army was at first successful. The commander of the royal army was the kind rice-grower's adopted son. 

In the midst of one of the battles the general was unhorsed. The peasant brother sprang at him with raised dagger and then lowered his weapon. 

He had spied through the open tunic the divided dragon on his old playmate's breast.

The tide of battle changed; many peasants were captured and ordered to be killed. Among them the general saw the man who had spared his life. He recognized his old friend and foster brother. He was touched by his act of love earlier in the day and ordered him released. For centuries the Chinese pasted a divided dragon on all gifts to their dearest friends.

The Chinese Develop a Civilization. — The Chinese at a very early date learned how to irrigate their land and renew its richness by fertilization. They developed a written language and a great literature. They established schools throughout the land, and until very recently pupils were compelled to memorize most of their lessons.

A Wise Man. Confucius (kon-fu'shi-us), who lived from 

551-478 B.C., was a wise Chinese teacher and philosopher. He taught reverence for one's parents, and his teaching became a tradition and practice in China down through the ages. Many of his sayings were published, the most widely known being,

"What you do not wish done to yourself, do not do to others."

The followers of Buddha (bood'a) entered the country about the ninth century. Buddha had lived and taught in India during the time of Confucius. He had taught his people to forget their selfishness and to serve their fellow men. Like the Egyptians (Chapter IV) the Buddhists believed in the transmigration of souls. Soon this religious faith took a firm hold on the people of 

China, and the land was dotted with beautiful temples or pagodas. 

Long before the Polos visited China, the Chinese had learned to print with movable wooden blocks. Their first newspaper had appeared before the birth of 

Christ. They knew how to paint and to make silk, porcelain, and gunpowder. They used a system of water wheels to raise water from a lower to a higher level, and they had invented a mariner's compass. 

The idea of this delicate instrument was probably brought to Italy by the Polos on their return from the Far East. 

The Compass. One of the ear liest compasses found in western Europe had the names of the winds written on it in Italian. This faithful little machine gave the
