Before its introduction into the west, seamen hugged the coast. They were afraid to venture very far out at sea on cloudy or stormy nights. Now all was changed.

The North Star could hide behind the storm clouds, but ships. were able to hold to their true course. 

A Search Begins for New Routes to Old Lands. - Aided by the compass, brave and courageous men spent years on the ocean seeking new routes to old lands. By 1450 A.D. the mariners of 

RA 21

Tales of cities like that of Mien with its gold and silver towers were told by Marco Polo on his return from the Far East. western Europe had also learned to sail against the wind, how to use fairly accurate sea charts, and how to read the astrolabe. 

This was an instrument used for the taking of altitudes of heavenly bodies. From it the sailor was able to determine time and latitude.

In 1453 A.D. the Turks captured Constantinople. They exacted a high tribute from caravans journeying to and from the East. Their government was not strong enough to protect these overland merchants from robbers. Traders could not afford either to pay the tribute or to run the risks of the old routes. 

So they sought another way out of the difficulty. 

Spain and Portugal took the lead in searching for new trade routes to India and China. Many attempts were made to reach those lands by sailing south around the coast of Africa. 

It was not known then how large that continent really was. 

But there was a general belief that as one sailed south, the climate became hotter and hotter, just as it grew colder and colder towards the north. The Atlantic Ocean was called the sea of

darkness" and was considered a barrier instead of the great highway it was to become. Prince

Prince Henry Establishes a School for Navigators. 

Henry of Portugal took the lead in seeking a new route to the east by way of Africa. He started a school for the preparation of navigators. He secured the best teachers he could find. 

They taught geography, map-making, information about the shape and size of the earth, and navigation. Many expeditions were sent south in attempts to round the southern part of Africa. One exploring party after another went farther and farther, but all returned convinced that there was no end to the continent.

Da Gama Finds a New Route to the Orient. In 1486 Bar tholomew Diaz (dē'äsh) rounded the point of Africa. He called it the Cape of Good Hope, a name which indicated a hope of reaching the Orient. Then in the summer of 1497 Vasco da 

Gama (gä’mä) with a fleet of four small vessels set sail from Lisbon, Portugal. He rounded the cape, sailed north along the 

African coast, and then eastward across the Indian Ocean. 

May of the following year he reached Calicut on the southwest coast of India. A new route to the East had been found. Another chapter in the Renaissance movement was completed. 

Another new day for civilization had dawned. Prince Henry was dead, but his work moved on. In

The Influence of Toscanelli. - There was living in Florence, Italy, at this time a man by the name of Toscanelli. An astronomer and librarian, he became interested in geography. His fame as a scholar spread. He studied the old Greek manuscripts and became convinced that the world was round. He wrote and prepared maps explaining his ideas. He believed that a route east was possible by sailing west across the Atlantic Ocean. 

Toscanelli died in 1482, but ten years later a bold sea captain by the name of Christopher Columbus was ready to test that idea. 

The Dawn of American History - Columbus. Christopher 

Columbus was born in Genoa, Italy, about 1446 A.D.

As a boy 

---

he played around the docks, went to school, helped his father who was a wool comber, and was little different from other boys. 

From the wharves of Genoa he saw the ships sailing in and out of that port. He heard the sailors talk about their voyages, their fights with Mohammedan and Christian pirates, and their battles with or against the other city-states of Italy. In school 

Columbus was very much interested in mathematics, geography, history, and science. He attended the University of Pavia and was considered well educated. 

At the age of fourteen he went to sea. He became familiar, as he said, "with every place to which men sail." He sailed down the coast of Africa, and it is thought that on another voyage he went as far north as Iceland. There he probably heard of 

Vinland and the voyages of the Northmen under Leif Ericson.

In 1470 Columbus married the daughter of an Italian sea captain and settled in Lisbon. He made his living as a map-maker. His father-in-law had been one of Prince Henry's navigators, and his charts were given to Columbus, who studied and pondered over them whenever he could find any leisure. He talked to sailors, he read all the scientific articles he could secure, and he corresponded with the scientists of his day.

All of these things he did because he had the idea that if the earth was round, as some people believed, then the East could be reached by sailing due west. He submitted his plan to Paolo 

Toscanelli. Toscanelli agreed with him and sent him one of his own maps on which he represented the earth as being much smaller than it really is. The eastern coast of Asia and Marco 

Polo's island of Cipango (Japan) were shown as only a moderate distance from the west coast of Africa and Europe. The chart showed no intervening lands between the two continents. 

Columbus became convinced that this idea of the earth's shape was correct, and he determined to prove it. 

He was poor, and to carry out his plans he needed ships and money. His only chance of getting these was to gain the interest of some wealthy person or ruler. The story of how Columbus

OCE AN SPICE IS The world as it was known in 1490. appealed in vain to the merchants of Genoa, to King John of Portugal, and then waited in Spain for seven long years until Queen Isabella finally came to his aid, is too well known to need repeating here.

On August 3, 1492, he set sail from the harbor of Palos with three small ships. He had a crew of ninety men, some of them convicts whom the queen had commanded to accompany him. 

He headed west out on the unknown waters of the Atlantic. 

It was one of the most daring voyages of all history. It was made at a time when most people believed that the earth was flat; at a time when people were very superstitious and believed in witches, ghosts, and terrible monsters who inhabited both land and sea. This was a time when water had to be carried in barrels, with no process or chemicals to keep it pure; a time when there were no canned fruits or vegetables or any system of cold storage. A long voyage other than along the coast

would often thin the ranks of able-bodied seamen. Columbus, however, hoped to secure enough wealth to finance another Crusade into the Holy Land. So with faith in his ideas, with a stout heart, and with his compass, this last of the Crusaders sailed on and on.

Finally, on October 14, he sighted land and dropped anchor in the harbor of a small island. This he called San Salvador. octubre demil cuatrocientos noventa poos r reinando en Esp

An old medal showing the joy of Columbus at seeing land.

He called the natives Indians, because he thought he had reached some part of India. He returned to 

Spain, where he was hailed as a hero by King Ferdinand, Queen Isabella, and the people. It was a great year for the royal pair.

They had just succeeded in expelling the Moors from their land, and their newly made admiral, they thought, had just found a new route to the Far East. 

Columbus made four voyages across the Atlantic between 1492 and 1502. On his third voyage he touched the mainland of South America. He never realized that he had found a new world. The great explorer died thinking that he had reached the land of Marco Polo. His courage, however, was to make this possible, and in addition it brought about the dawn of American history. 

The Old World Claims the New. In 1493 Pope Alexander VI 

at the request of Spain and Portugal divided the undiscovered world between the two countries. He drew an imaginary line through the center of the earth, about two hundred fifty miles west of the Azores. All territory discovered west of this line was to belong to Spain, all east of it to Portugal. This was known

as the Papal Line of Demarcation. The following year by the 

Treaty of Tordesillas the line was extended westward about 1000 miles from the Cape Verde Islands. This gave all of the new world except Brazil, which was east of the line, to Spain. 

Henry VII of England did not intend to be outdone by Spain and Portugal. He ignored the Treaty of Tordesillas and empowered the Italian navigator, John Cabot, to explore, settle, and claim all heathen lands for England. In 1497 Cabot set sail in the little ship Matthew with a crew of eighteen men. That same year he touched the northern coast of the new continent. 

He claimed the "New-found-land" (Newfoundland) for the 

English king.

Another Italian sea captain, Verrazzano (věr-rät-sä'nō), was employed by Francis I of France. Like Henry VII the French king ignored the claims of Spain and Portugal. Verrazzano sailed along the coast of what are now the Carolinas and plundered Spanish treasure ships. He was finally captured and slain by the Spaniards. Ten years later Jacques Cartier (kär-tya'), a Frenchman, landed in Newfoundland. He discovered and sailed up the St. Lawrence River, and founded "Mont Réal or Montreal. He also built a fort on the site of 

Quebec. As his progress was blocked by swift rapids, Cartier was compelled to turn back. Like all other explorers he believed that there was a water passage through to the Far East. The falls that checked his advance were therefore called the La 

Chine or "China" Rapids. 

Much later, in 1682, La Salle (lå sål') sailed from the Great Lakes to the mouth of the Mississippi River. Consequently he claimed for France all the land drained by the Mississippi and its tributaries. Thus Spain, Portugal, England, and France all claimed either the whole or the same parts of the country. For the next four hundred years there was to be a struggle for the western hemisphere before it was to be settled as it is today. 

Martin Waldseemüller Names the New Land "America."

In 1501 Amerigo Vespucci (věs-poot'chē), a citizen of Venice,

set out on an exploring expedition for the king of Portugal. He sailed south along the mainland discovered by Columbus. One day he made a startling discovery. His astrolabe showed that he was south of the equator. He was certain now that this land was neither India nor China, because those countries, as all navigators knew, were above the equator. He wrote glowing accounts of the new land which he had discovered and said: "We found what may be called a new world, since most of the ancients said there was no continent below the equator.' 

In 1507 the scholars at the college of St. Die in France were preparing a new edition of Ptolemy's geography. All of the latest discoveries were to be included in the book. One of the professors, Martin Waldseemüller, suggested the name of Amerigo for what we today know as South America. The new map appeared with this country labeled "The Land of Amerigo." 

It was later changed to America, the feminine of Amerigo, to conform with Asia, Africa, and Europa. 

Spain Explores and Settles America. By 1519 A.D. Spain had established colonies in Haiti, Porto Rico, Jamaica, and Cuba. 

During that year Ferdinand Magellan (må-jěl'ăn), sailing for Charles V of Spain, left Seville in command of five small vessels. 

He crossed the Atlantic, sailed south along the South American coast, discovered and passed through the strait now called the Strait of Magellan, and headed westward across the Pacific Ocean. The little fleet sailed for more than three months. The men suffered terribly. They ran short of food and water, and it is said that they chewed pieces of sail and ate sawdust. Many of them died of scurvy, a disease resulting from the lack of fresh vegetables. Finally they arrived at a group of islands, which were later called the Philippines in honor of Philip, king of Spain. 

This was the same Philip of our last chapter, who wanted to marry Queen Elizabeth, who sent his Armada against England, and who tyrannized the Netherlands.

The World is Round. - Magellan was killed in the Philippines while fighting the natives. In July, 1522, the Vittoria, in com-

mand of Sebastian del Cano, arrived in the port of Seville with but a few survivors of Magellan's original crew. They had left the Philippines, continued westward, crossed the Indian Ocean, sailed around Africa, and reached home. This voyage proved two things: first, that the earth was round, and second, that 

America was not a part of China, but a continent separated from the Far East by a broad ocean. 140° 120° 100°

Cortes Conquers Mexico. While Magellan was making this memorable voyage and while Martin Luther was leading the revolt against the papacy, Hernando Cortes (kôr'těz) conquered Mexico. That country was governed by a powerful emperor called Montezuma (mon-tê-zōō'ma). Mexico possessed much gold and silver, which above all else the Spaniards wanted. 

The Aztecs, too, had developed a very high type of civilization, and were much more advanced than most of the original inhabitants of America.

The Aztec Civilization. The Aztecs had a sufficiently simple system of notation in their arithmetic. For example, they expressed the first twenty numbers by a corresponding number of dots, and twenty was represented by a flag. Larger numbers were counted by twenties and in writing by repeating the number of flags. Fractions of a sum were shown by drawing only a part of the object. With this system the Aztecs worked out a calendar, a chronological record of events, transacted their business, and made calculations in astrology.

Many of their temples were burned and a great deal of their picture-writing was lost or destroyed by the Spanish conquerors. 

Yet enough of it remained to describe much of their life and work. 

It pictures their government, agriculture, commerce, and education. The latter stressed particularly their schools of music. 

The Aztecs had a cruel pagan religion and were not averse to eating human flesh in their religious festivals. 

De Soto's Journey. In 1539 De Soto, a Spaniard, and six hundred men sailed from Cuba, where he was governor, to search for another "land of gold."

They landed in Florida. For three and a half years they traveled through forests, across swamps and rivers, often fighting with the Indians whom they met. Finally they reached a broad river the Mississippi. But De Soto was not to profit by his great discovery, for he died soon after. He was buried in the 

Mississippi's waters because his followers did not wish to have the Indians learn of his death.

De Soto, the noted Spanish explorer, was the discoverer of the Mississippi River. From one of the members of his expedition the world of the time learned much about the geography of what is now the states of Alabama, Arkansas, Florida, Georgia, Louisiana, Mississippi, South Carolina, and

Tennessee. 

327

About the time De Soto landed in Florida, Coronado set out This he did not find, although he discovered to look for gold. 

the Great Plains. 

Drake Circumnavigates the Globe. Twelve years after the conquest of Mexico (1531) Pizarro (pi-zär'rō) conquered Peru. 

This land on the west coast of South America was occupied by the Incas, Indians with a civilization as high as that of the Aztecs. The wealth of Peru was greater even than that of 

Mexico. Within a few years the Spanish treasure ships were on their way to Spain. Soon, however, their lookouts became very nervous. The Elizabethan Sea Dogs headed by Francis Drake were lying in wait to plunder and capture their vessels. On one of these expeditions Drake captured a treasure ship worth a million dollars that was bound for the Philippines. This bold seaman did not dare return by way of the Atlantic, because the Spanish fleet was all set to capture him. He crossed the Pacific, reached Africa, rounded the Cape of Good Hope, and returned home in 1580the first Englishman to circumnavigate the globe. Later he was knighted by Queen Elizabeth, and, as we have seen, was placed second in command of the English fleet against the Spanish Armada.

Raleigh Receives a Monopoly and Tries to Colonize Virginia. - At the beginning of the seventeenth century Spain had established many colonies in South America. England, France, and

Holland at that time had not founded one permanent settlement in the New World. A few Englishmen had made several attempts but had failed. One of these was Sir Walter Raleigh (rô'li), a favorite of Queen Elizabeth. She had granted him a monopoly on wine. This meant that he was the only man in England who could sell wine. Of course he became very wealthy. He tried to plant three colonies in what is called Virginia, but the expense proved too heavy for any single man. 

English Stock Companies Settle America. The business men of England profited by the mistakes of Raleigh. They believed that colonies could be established along the Atlantic

One of the first stockades at Jamestown. Redrawn from an old print. What means did the colonists adopt to protect themselves? coast somewhere between Maine and Florida (this was the terri tory explored by John Cabot and claimed by England). But they were convinced that this could be done only through the medium of groups of people working together. 

Stocks and Dividends. In 1606 two commercial companies composed of English merchants and traders were formed to colonize America. To raise enough money to buy ships and supplies and to otherwise finance a colony, the leaders admitted new members. They permitted these people for a certain sum of money to purchase a share or shares in the company. This share was called stock, and the buyers were known as stockholders. The profits which the company made in America were to be divided among those people who held stock. The money so divided was called (as it is today) "dividends." The first two companies thus founded were the London Company and the Plymouth Company. 

Motives for English Colonization. The following year the 

London Company sent one hundred colonists to Virginia. They settled along the James River and called their colony Jamestown. 

This was the first permanent English settlement in America -

our colonial history had begun. The history of the American colonies is familiar to all. A few years later the Pilgrim Fathers settled in Plymouth. The time was ripe for European colonization. The Reformation, as we have seen (Chapter XVIII), had resulted in religious persecutions. Catholics and Protestants alike sought refuge in this country. Many people sought adventure, and many others had visions of getting rich by securing quickly and easily gold and precious stones. Then, too, there were hard times in England. You will recall that farmers had been thrown out of work because of the sheep inclosures and that charity work had ceased when Henry VIII took over the monasteries. 

This had caused much misery and hardship. These economic conditions led people to seek a new opportunity in America. 

Pointing the Way to a New Era: Penn's Holy Experiment. 

In 1681 William Penn pointed the way to a new era. In his 

Pennsylvania colony he set up his "holy experiment." This was his belief that men of all religious faiths could live together, worshiping God as they saw fit. He made murder and treason the only crimes punishable by death. The prison dungeons so common in Europe became sanitary workhouses in the land of Penn. He invited many of the people from the German states, who had been made homeless by the Thirty Years' War, to settle in his colony. Because of his good work the "holy experiment proved a success. A view of New Amsterdam, which was settled by the Dutch and which later became English. This picture is taken from a mural by Louis Jambor.

The last English colony, settled by James Oglethorpe in Georgia, was a refuge for debtor prisoners. These unfortunate persons were imprisoned in England because they were unable to pay their debts. Even in many parts of America this practice did not cease until after the nineteenth century. 

Holland and France Settle America. While England was gaining a foothold in the New World, so were the Dutch and the French. The former settled on

Manhattan 

Island and called their colony New Amsterdam. 

It was captured by the 

English in 1664 and became New York. 

Blockhouse at Quebec.

The French established their first permanent settlement at Quebec (kwêběk') in 1607. Intrepid explorers and Jesuit missionaries within fifty years had established a French empire, known as New France (Canada), in the north. This remained in their possession until 

1763 when by the Treaty of Paris, which closed the French and English (Indian) War, it was ceded to England. 

We have now traced the revival of ancient art, literature, and science the development of strong national governments and the dawn of American history. Contrasted with the Feudal 

Age, these events ushered in a "New Day for Civilization." 

This was followed by other problems which had to be faced by the people of western Europe. Chief among these was the tyranny of kings. The struggle against that tyranny will be the subject of our next background.

RA 22

The desire for religious, political, and economic freedom caused many people to leave the Old World and search for new homes where they would be free from tyranny. 334

BACKGROUND SEVEN  

We have seen how the scholars, writers, painters, and scientists have made possible a new day for civilization, how strong national governments were organized in western Europe, how world commerce began, and how it in turn brought about the dawn of American history as men searched for new routes to 

India and China. 

Con- Our dirigible is nearing the end of its long journey as we witness the events of the next three hundred years. We shall see the rulers of England and France establish absolute governments. 

We shall see kings deprive their subjects of religious and political freedom. We shall see them persecute and overtax their countrymen until the people can stand it no longer and rebel. stitutional governments and republics will be established. We shall see Napoleon Bonaparte at the head of his armies move across the stage and turn Europe upside down. We shall see him pass and new leaders arise. Among these will be men who will attempt once more to bring the common people under the tyranny of absolute monarchs. Their attempts will fail, because of the courage of other men - leaders of the common people in the struggle against tyranny.

CHAPTER XX  

The Trend toward Democratic Government. - There was a decided trend toward more democratic government in Europe and in America from the seventeenth to the end of the nineteenth century. This struggle occurred first in England. Then followed the American and French revolutions during the eighteenth 335

century. The early years of the nineteenth century witnessed the independence and formation of the Latin American republics and the beginning of modern Greece. In the later years the unorganized states of Italy and Germany fought successfully against foreign oppression and formed united nations. These and other events of this whole period will serve to explain much of our modern life. We shall begin this background, then, with the first of those struggles the English Revolution. 

King James I Succeeds Queen Elizabeth (1603). · - James VI 

of Scotland became King James I of England after the death of 

Queen Elizabeth in 1603. He was the son of Mary Stuart,

Queen of Scots (Chapter XVIII), and the grandson of Margaret, sister of Henry VII, the first of the Tudor kings. The thrones of England and Scotland were united now for the first time, but because of the petty jealousies of both countries each nation retained its own separate Parliament. 

The Union Jack (1707). — They were finally united in 1707.

At that time it was agreed by both governments that Scotland should have representatives in the English House of Commons and the House of Lords. Great Britain upon this occasion adopted a new flag the Union Jack. It bore the red cross of 

St. George and the white cross of St. Andrew, the patron saints of England and Scotland. 

James Claims to Rule by "Divine Right."— Parliament had considered many claimants to the English throne before it had finally accepted James. He was favored because he was a 

Protestant. But the new king gained the ill will of his subjects immediately. He boldly stated that he did not rule by the consent of Parliament but by the will of God. He claimed to be 

God's lieutenant on earth. He informed the people that he and his successors would rule in a manner pleasing to God, regardless of what their subjects wanted or thought. This theory of the "divine right of kings, held by the rulers of bygone ages, did not please the English people. It violated certain principles of the Magna Carta (Chapter XVI) and 

---

Hampton Court Palace, the largest in Great Britain, on the bank of the 

Thames River. threatened the freedom of a nation. It gained many enemies for the House of Stuart and was to end in armed rebellion. 

The Hampton Court Conference and the Puritan Petition. — 

The first serious problem that confronted James was a religious controversy. A group of people had remained in the Anglican 

Church with the hope that eventually it would be purified of many Catholic forms. These people were called Puritans. They presented the king with a petition at Hampton Court. It contained a request to simplify the religious services of the church. 

James lost his temper, scolded the Puritans, and called them his secret enemies. He dismissed them and refused to consider their wishes. 

The Persecution of Catholic and Protestant Groups: the 

Pilgrim Fathers. Many of the Puritans now withdrew from the Church of England and established their own forms of worship. When the king learned of this, he said: "I will make them conform (agree) or I will harry them out of the land or worse." To escape persecution many of them fled first to Holland and then to America. Because they separated from the

Anglican Church and refused to conform with its rules, these people were called Separatists or Non-Conformists. Because of their wanderings we know them as the Pilgrim Fathers who A reconstruction of the old court room in the Guild Hall where the Pilgrims were tried, Boston, England. Is it like a modern court room in any way? settled Plymouth in 1620.

Under the rule of James I both the Catholics and these Protestant groups were persecuted. 

The King Quarrels with Parliament. 

The next quarrel of the king was with Parliament. He came before that body and asked for money to carry on the government. The members hesitated to grant his request at once because of his previous statement that he did not rule by their consent. James lost his temper and took matters into his own hands.

He began to place and collect taxes on imports and exports without the permission of Parliament. Many people refused to pay these taxes. They claimed that they were illegal because in the Magna 

Carta it was stated that "no taxes should be levied with- 

Despite this the king's out the consent of the National Council." judges either forced the English to pay or sentenced them to jail.

This plan seemed to succeed, and James grew more bold. 

He dismissed Parliament and ordered the election of a new one. 

The newly elected members were more stubborn than those who had been recently dismissed. The king had lost the respect of

Parliament. Gradually a bitter feeling developed between the followers of each. Sooner or later the question Who controls the taxing power of the country, the king or Parliament? — had to be answered. 

The Impeachment of Lord Bacon. In 1621 the quarrel resulted in the impeachment of Sir Francis Bacon. This man was the highest judge in all England and as such was the Lord Chancellor. Bacon was a famous writer, philosopher, and scientist. But his friendship with the king embittered Parliament. The House of Commons impeached (accused) him for taking bribes in lawsuits which were tried before him. He confessed his crime before the House of Lords and was convicted by that body. He was ordered to pay a heavy fine and was sentenced to prison. After Bacon had spent a few days in the 

Tower of London, James secured his release. 

Bacon Points the Way to Our Modern System of "Cold Stor age." Lord Bacon would never have been impeached had he not been the tool of King James. The House of Commons was determined to offset the tyranny of their ruler by removing his servants. We remember Sir Francis Bacon as the first modern scientist. His method was to observe things in nature, collect all the facts, then study and experiment with them. This plan has been very generally followed ever since. Bacon defended himself before Parliament with the plea that all his wealth had been used to equip laboratories for the new science. 

After he had been removed from office, he continued his experiments. One of these caused his death. Bacon became convinced that decay could be prevented by the application of cold. 

He killed and cleaned a chicken, then packed it in snow. He proved his point, and our modern system of "cold storage" began. 

While performing this experiment, Sir Francis Bacon contracted a cold and died. So this friend of King James became a martyr to modern science, and his final work was to be a blessing to all. 

Charles I (1625-1649): the Settlement of Massachusetts. 

James I died in 1625 and was succeeded by his son, Charles I.

Ewing Galloway

This meat-packing plant in South Carolina shows one of the modern uses of cold storage.

The new monarch, like his father, believed in the "divine right" of kings. He not only inherited but continued the discontent of the Puritans and Parliament. He bitterly opposed those Puritans who had remained within the Anglican Church but who were continually seeking reforms. These people became so dissatisfied that they received from the Council of New England a land patent, or the right to hold land in America. Then they secured a royal charter from the king which permitted them to settle in the land north, south, and west of the Charles River. 

In 1630 a thousand people, most of whom were Puritans, left England to settle in Massachusetts. Before the reign of King 

Charles ended, 15,000 English emigrants had found homes in that part of the New World. The foundations of the New World had been laid.

The Founding of Maryland, 1632. — Charles I had married a 

French Catholic princess, Henrietta Maria. The laws against the Catholics which had been passed during the reign of James I were still in force. So, in 1632, Charles I granted a tract of land in America to his Catholic friend, Lord Baltimore. This colony became a haven of refuge for persecuted Catholics and was named 

Maryland after Queen Henrietta Maria.

The King Dismisses Parliament and Plays the Tyrant. In the meantime Charles was having trouble with Parliament. He needed money to conduct several petty wars against Spain and France. At first he was voted money for this purpose, but his expeditions against those countries were failures, and the English became dissatisfied. Parliament refused to grant him any more funds. The king then dismissed it, called another, and when the second one refused his request he dismissed it also. Charles now began to play the tyrant in earnest. He imprisoned the 

The founding of Maryland. Why do you think they have the cross? Notice the priest.

leaders of the House of Commons. He collected taxes without the consent of Parliament and forced his subjects to make him "voluntary" loans. If any person refused to hand over these gifts, he was thrown into prison and deprived of a trial by jury. 

Troops were quartered in the people's homes in order to intimidate and compel them to do the king's will. 

The Petition of Right Is Signed (1628). — When the Parlia ment of 1628 met, it was determined to secure justice. The 

House of Commons informed Charles I that they would grant him no more money until he respected their rights as Englishmen. 

To be certain of this they compelled him to sign the Petition of 

Right. The terms of this document stated in part: (1) Forced loans, gifts, or taxes should not be collected without the consent of Parliament; (2) No troops should be quartered in private homes during times of peace; and (3) No one should be imprisoned without a trial by jury. This petition really renewed the provisions of the Magna Carta. The king agreed to its terms (1628), but within a short time he ignored them and broke faith with his subjects. 

The King Revives the Monopolies. Charles revived the monopolies, which had proved so unpopular in the days of Queen Elizabeth. The king gave his friends the sole privilege of buying and selling many articles of food, drink, clothing, and shelter. The leaders of Parliament denounced this action, and one member said concerning the "monopolists": "They sip in our cup, they sup in our dish, they sit by our fire."

The House of Commons under the leadership of Sir John Eliot drew up a remonstrance (objection) to these new acts of tyranny. The speaker of the House was a puppet of the king, and he refused to put the question to a vote. Then a few members rushed forward and held him in his chair while objections to the king's actions were voted upon. Charles had Eliot arrested and sent to the Tower, where he died after three years' imprisonment. Just as Bacon was a martyr to the cause of science, so 

Eliot was a martyr to the cause of liberty.

Charles I Rules without Parliament (1629-1640). 

For the next eleven years (1629-1640) the king refused to call Parliament, and he ruled alone. During that time he violated all the principles of the Magna Carta and the Petition of Right. He continued to collect taxes illegally and imprison his subjects without jury trial. One of his favorites, Archbishop Laud, so persecuted the Puritans that thousands of them fled to America. 

The War with Scotland: Parliament Convenes. Then Laud tried to force the people of Scotland to accept the English Book of Common Prayer and adopt the forms of the established church. The Scotch Presbyterians drew up a covenant (agreement) which bound its signers to resist these despotic measures. 

Charles ordered an army into Scotland, and the Scotch Covenanters prepared to resist.

Because his war with the Scots would require both men and money, Charles I felt compelled to call Parliament. This he did, and in 1640 that body convened. The people of England had been outraged for a long time, and their representatives were in no trifling mood. Archbishop Laud and another stanch friend of the king were impeached by the House of Commons for their despotism. They were found guilty and put to death. Parliament then passed an act which declared that it could not be dissolved without its own consent. The king, alarmed by the growing opposition throughout the land, signed the act. He had also revived the hated Court of the Star Chamber which Henry

VII had established. This was now abolished along with many other grievances. 

Cavaliers. Notice their clothes. 

Civil War the Cavaliers and the "Roundheads."- England at this time had no standing army. Each county and large

town had a militia. The militia was composed of citizens from the ordinary walks of life, who devoted part of their time to military training. These troops were under the king's control, but 

Parliament now demanded that this authority be given to them. 

Charles refused and rallied the nobility, members of the Anglican Church, and the poorer people of northern and western England around his standard. Parliament organized an army. The more prosperous towns and the middle classes favored its cause, and in 1642 civil war began. 

The Royalists were called Cavaliers, and the men in the army of Parliament were known as "Roundheads," because they cut their hair short. This was quite a contrast to the prevailing style and the long-flowing locks of the Cavaliers. 

Oliver Cromwell Defeats the Royalists. At first the Royalists were successful. But later the Puritans, under the leadership of Oliver Cromwell, gained control of both Parliament and the army. When the war began, Cromwell had organized and trained a company of men from his own community. The following year he was placed in command of a regiment of Puritans, who went into battle praying and singing psalms. They were known as Cromwell's "Ironsides" and gained the reputation of being the best fighting regiment in the land. In 1644 Cromwell was made commander of the parliamentary army. He defeated Prince Rupert, cousin of the king, at the battle of Marston Moor in the north of England. In that battle Cromwell's Ironsides led the attack, and their fame spread. The following year the king's army was scattered at Naseby (nāz′bi), and Charles fled to Scotland. He surrendered to the Scots, who handed him over to the English Parliament. 

The Trial and Execution of Charles I. — Parliament appointed one hundred thirty-five men as a high court of justice. On 

January 20, 1649, the king was brought to trial before this court.

He was condemned as a "tyrant, traitor, murderer, and public enemy," and was sentenced to die. Ten days later he was beheaded in front of the royal palace of Whitehall, in London.

The Commonwealth or Republic (1649-1653). - Parliament, shortly after the execution of Charles I, abolished the office of king and the House of Lords. A commonwealth, or republic, was proclaimed, and the nation was to be governed by a council of state. A president of the council was appointed, and the famous poet, John Milton, was chosen secretary for foreign affairs. The real ruler was Oliver 

Cromwell, who was in command of the army.

Enemies of the New Republic. - 

The new government had enemies on all sides. It was opposed by the 

Royalists, the Anglicans, and the 

Presbyterians. The last named had tried to gain control of Parliament, but the Puritans had defeated their plans. Besides these enemies, there were enemies within. republicans known as levelers adqoobr 

Oliver Cromwell.

From a 

One group of painting by Van der Faes. 

wished to reduce all classes to the same level with respect to political power. Another group wanted to abolish private property and arrange for an equal division of economic goods. 

Such an arrangement is called "communism." The present 

Russian government was founded on this principle (Chapters 

XXIV and XXV).

Many people in Ireland and Scotland remained loyal to the ex-king's son, Prince Charles. Armies were raised in both countries with the hope of regaining the government for him. 

But Cromwell at the head of his army marched into Ireland. 

Homes were burned to the ground; men, women, and children were killed; and many of the survivors were compelled to leave the country or be sold into slavery. Later these same troops crushed all opposition in Scotland, and Prince Charles fled to the continent. He was to remain in exile until 1660. In that year he was recalled by the people.

Barebone's Parliament. Because of so much discontent 

Cromwell feared a general election. He was afraid that the enemies of the republic would gain control of a new Parliament. 

To offset this, he with the aid of his army officers nominated one hundred thirty-five "God-fearing" men, the majority of whom were from the country districts. Since the voters were permitted to vote for no one else, they were of course elected. The Royalists ridiculed the whole affair and in particular the country members. 

They nicknamed it "Barebone's Parliament," because one of the members, a London leather-merchant, was named Praise- 

God Barebone.

The Protectorate (1653-1660). -A constitution, called the 

"Instrument of Government," was now drawn up. A protectorate was established, and Cromwell was appointed Lord Protector for life. Later he was offered the crown and would have accepted, but the army objected. For the next seven years 

Cromwell was the sole ruler of England. He dismissed Parliament in the same manner as had the Stuarts. He divided the realm into military districts, placing army officers in charge of those places. Sports and amusements, such as fox hunting, horse racing, and dancing, were considered sins. People who took part in any of these things were severely punished by the stern Puritan leaders.

The Navigation Acts, 1651: Their Effect on the Colonies. 

Under Cromwell England's supremacy of the sea was more firmly established. He defeated both the Dutch and the Spanish navies. In 1651 the first Navigation Act was passed. This law forbade any English merchant to bring into the country (import) or send out of the country (export) any goods in foreign vessels. This applied to the colonies also. These acts proved very expensive to the English colonies in America. Previous to this they could import and export their goods in ships that offered the lowest rates. The Navigation Acts became so hateful to the Americans during the next century, that they were one of the causes which led to the American Revolution.

The Restoration, 1660.- Cromwell died in 1658, and for the next two years his son Richard tried to govern the country but failed. The people, too, had grown tired of Puritan intolerance and military rule. A Parliament was elected, and Prince Charles was invited to return to England. He swore to abide by the 

Magna Carta and the Petition of Right. All persons who had revolted against Charles I were pardoned except the members of the court who had condemned him to death. Some of these 餐​、 The surrender of Fort Amsterdam. Who is the man in the foreground? regicides (men who had killed the king) were put to death, and others escaped to foreign lands. A few fled to Africa and to 

America. If you read Twice Told Tales by Hawthorne, a nine teenth-century American writer, you will recognize one of these regicides in the story of the "Gray Champion." The coronation of Charles II is known in English history as the "Restoration,' because the Stuart dynasty was restored to the throne. 

New Amsterdam Captured: the Writ of Habeas Corpus Act. 

It was during the reign of Charles II that the war begun by

Cromwell against the Dutch was renewed. In 1664 an English fleet, as we have seen, captured New Amsterdam and renamed it 

New York after the brother of Charles II, the Duke of York.

The cause of democracy was further aided in 1679 by the passage of the Habeas Corpus Act. This assured any person who had been arrested of the right to secure an order from a judge permitting him to come before the court to learn whether his imprisonment was legal. 

The Glorious Revolution, 1688. — Charles II died in 1685, and he was succeeded by his brother, James. The reign of 

James II was short-lived. By his actions he appeared to have learned very little from the English Revolution. He tried to make 

Catholicism the state religion of England. He imprisoned his subjects without a trial by jury and ruled without the consent of Parliament. He sent harsh governors to America and endeavored to deprive the colonists of their charters and rights. In 

1688 the English people rebelled for a second time and drove 

James II from England.

The Bill of Rights. - The older daughter of James II was a 

Protestant and married to William, Prince of Orange and the president of the Dutch Republic. Parliament now invited 

William and Mary to accept the throne of England. The invitation was accepted, but the new rulers had to sign the Bill of 

Rights. 

This was the third great document in English constitutional history. It forbade any king to lay taxes, maintain a standing army in time of peace, or to suspend any law of Parliament. All subjects were to enjoy the right of petition and trial by jury, and all future kings were to accept the Protestant faith. 

This marked the second struggle of the English people against the tyranny of the Stuarts and is known as the "Glorious Revolution." The first ten amendments to the Constitution of the 

United States are called the Bill of Rights. If you read these amendments, you will understand why they have been given this name.

John Milton and His Great Epic Poems. The two out- - standing literary figures of these times were John Milton and John Bunyan (bůn'yăn). John Milton was born in London in the year 1608. He was educated at the famous St. Paul's School founded by Colet (see Chapter XVIII) and at Cambridge University.

Milton became involved in the English Revolution and sided with the Puritans. After the execution of Charles I he was appointed secretary of foreign affairs. Because of his position 

Milton found it necessary to defend the new government, and he wrote many pamphlets in its favor. From overwork at this time he later lost his eyesight. After Cromwell's death and the overthrow of the commonwealth, Milton went into retirement. 

He had hoped to escape the persecution that followed the Restoration. But he was arrested and imprisoned for several years. 

After his release he moved to the outskirts of London, where he lived in poverty with his three daughters. It was there that he wrote and dedicated to these girls his great epic poems, Paradise - Lost and Paradise Regained. 

Milton and his daughters. Painting by Munkacsy.

RA-23

Paradise Lost. In writing these epics, Milton copied the form and style used by Homer and Vergil. Instead of writing about famous heroes, he used the Holy Spirit as his subject. 

In the first epic, Paradise Lost, Milton describes the creation of the world, then the great war in heaven. The poem ends with 

Adam and Eve being driven from the Garden of Eden. It reflected the Puritan ideals in the life of the poet: the conflict between good and evil, between love and beauty. 

Paradise Regained. - Thomas Elwood, a Quaker, visited Milton in his small, simple home and during a conversation said: "Thou hast given us Paradise Lost, but what of Paradise 

Regained? Four years later Milton dictated to his faithful daughters the story of Paradise Regained. The blind poet took as the theme for his second great epic, the temptation of Christ in the wilderness. The first poem explained man's downfall through temptation, that is, the success of the evil side of life. 

In the second epic man was redeemed through Jesus Christ, and the good eventually conquered evil. 

John Bunyan Writes Pilgrim's Progress. - John Bunyan was born in 1628, the year in which Charles I signed the Petition of Right. He died in 1688, the year in which the Glorious Revolution occurred. John Bunyan sprang from the common people. 

He was a tinker by trade and possessed little education. Bunyan 10001

sided with the Puritans and fought in the army of Cromwell. 

After the war he became a Baptist preacher. Later he was sent to prison because he refused to conform to the English Church. 

While in jail Bunyan planned and probably finished his renowned story Pilgrim's Progress. 

The story takes a Christian spirit on a journey, during which many perils and temptations are overcome. Christian starts from the City of Destruction and journeys to the New Jerusalem. 

He passes through the entire life of joy and sorrow, victory and defeat. The book became one of the most popular ever written. 

It is enjoyed today by old and young alike. The former see in it the experiences of their past life. The latter enjoy the adventures of the Christian Pilgrim. Bunyan wrote in a plain style that was understood by the common people of his day. 

The work of both Milton and Bunyan explains the Puritan thought of those revolutionary days.

Dr. William Harvey and the Theory of Blood Circulation. 

The year in which John Bunyan was born and the Petition of 

Right was signed witnessed another great event. Dr. William

Harvey made a great discovery about the human body. Since the days of Aristotle people had believed that there were two blood streams running through a person's system. Each was supposed to circulate independently of the other. It was also thought that there were two kinds of blood, the dark and the red. The dark-colored blood was believed to come from the liver, the red blood from the heart. Harvey's teacher had discovered the valves in the blood veins, but the pupil carried the master's work still further. Dr. Harvey proved to a doubting world that there is only one true circulation of the blood stream. He made his tests and experiments with living animals. He has been called the founder of human physiology. 

Sir Isaac Newton and the Law of Gravitation. It was also during the reign of Charles I that Isaac Newton was born (1642). 

As a small boy Newton loved to take things apart and put them together again. While a student in college he became interested

in mathematics and science. He maintained this interest throughout his entire life. One day, while walking in the garden of his estate, he noticed an apple as it fell from a tree. He wondered why the apple fell down and not up. He argued with himself that the same thing would happen if the apple fell from a tall building or a high mountain. Would this force, which pulled all objects toward the earth, continue no matter how high up you went or would the force grow less and how much? 

Then Newton asked himself a startling question. Would the same force that pulled the apple to the ground keep the moon in its orbit? It took Isaac Newton twenty years to prove that the same force, which he called gravitation, acted upon both moon and apple in the same way. The early Greeks had marveled at the powerful attraction that the earth had for objects near its surface. One of those early philosophers had made the statement that probably the same force that attracted bodies to the earth held the planets in their orbits. 

Newton's conclusion was that all objects in space attract each other with a force depending on their weight and distance from each other. For example, the earth, which is a large object, attracts smaller objects toward its center. The moon attracts small objects near its surface to its center. Therefore, the earth, being larger than the moon, attracts the latter toward the earth. 

The moon forms an orbit around the earth instead of shooting off in a straight line. Similarly the earth is attracted to the sun, a larger body, and forms an orbit around the sun. 

The First Law of Motion. The above principle is summed up in Newton's first law of motion. Every body continues in its state of rest or uniform motion in a straight line unless acted upon by an outside force. Since Newton's day his theory of the construction of the universe has been accepted. 

Lately, however, a group of present-day scientists headed by Einstein have changed it a little by adding some new ideas. 

But as a whole Newton's laws of gravitation have stood the severe test of critics.

Thus we see that the struggle against tyranny in England made it possible to enjoy a more democratic government. At the same time the work of such men as Bacon, Milton, Bunyan, Harvey, and Newton enriched the life of mankind in other : fields.
