CHAPTER XXI  The Tyranny and Absolute Rule of the Bourbons. - The 

French Revolution began in 1789.

Since the days of Cardinal 

Richelieu and Louis XIII (Chapter XVIII) the kings of France had enjoyed absolute power. Louis XIV (1643-1715), like 

James I of England, was a firm believer in the theory of the divine right of kings. He is credited with saying, "I am the State."

That statement certainly expressed his idea of government. He levied all taxes without the consent of his subjects. He imprisoned people without a trial. He made laws, declared war, and appointed or removed public officials. He forbade freedom of religion, freedom of the press, and freedom of speech. 

The Estates-General of France had not met since 1614, and the people for more than a century had no voice in local or national affairs. Louis XIV called the representative government of the 

English Parliament an "intolerable evil." But the injustice and tyranny of absolute rule in France were to cause much discontent and misery. It was to end in a revolution far more drastic than that which had occurred in England. 

A Hard-Working King "Whose Vanity Was without Limit."— 

Louis XIV was a hard-working king. He attended to many small details of the government and read numerous letters from his governors in the far-off possessions of America and India. He answered this mail personally and issued orders that affected every phase of the colonists' lives. He loved display and flattery. 

One historian, Saint Simon, said of him: "Louis XIV's vanity was without limit or restraint; it colored everything and convinced him that no one even approached him in military talents, in plans and enterprises, or in government.” 

Louis XIV Builds Palaces with the Taxpayers' Money. — One of his plans, and one which he realized, was the construction of 355

3 G A beautiful room in the palace at Versailles.

Notice the walls and the furnishings. 

an enormous and magnificent palace at Versailles (vĕr-sä'y'), on the outskirts of Paris. It contained many rooms filled with works of art, paintings, and expensive furniture. Near by, smaller palaces were erected, among which was the Trianon (see Chapter XXV). The buildings were surrounded by beautiful lawns, parks, groves, and musical fountains. Thousands of poor French peasants were compelled to work at Versailles without pay. Despite this the cost was very great. Some historians estimate it at $100,000,000. This sum had to be paid by the people in the form of taxes. 

Amid the

The Social Life at the Grand Monarch's Court. 

grandeur of Versailles, Louis, whom his flatterers called "Grand Monarch" and "Sun-King," held his court. Thousands of his friends among the nobility were invited to live at the palace of Versailles. These courtiers were a privileged class. They were exempt from taxation and received fancy salaries called pensions.

They had little or nothing to do but to take part in the gay social life of the court.

Louis XIV Louis XIV as a Patron of Art and Literature. 

wished to be known as a patron of art and science. He invited the great French dramatists to his court. Among these was 

Molière (mô-lyâr'), a playwright and an actor. His comedies, which concerned the follies, the vanities, and the weaknesses of his countrymen, amused the king and his courtiers. One of his immortal characters is "Tartuffe." The plays of Molière are as popular today as they were over two hundred years ago. 

French drama in the days of Louis was recognized as the best in 

Europe. Louis XIV encouraged artists, scientists, and writers by granting them pensions. During his reign the Royal Library of Science was founded, and he aided the Royal Library of France. The latter is one of the most famous libraries of the world and contains more than two and a half million books. 

The Dark Side of the "Sun-King's " Reign. From all outward appearances the "Grand Monarch" seemed to be getting along famously. He became the model for other kings in western 

Europe, and his court was imitated by Peter the Great in Russia and later by Frederick the Great in Prussia. The French language became the court language of Europe. It was the language spoken by diplomats and the language in which treaties were written. But Louis XIV was not satisfied. He persecuted his subjects, waged costly wars, and added to the misery of the masses. This is the dark side of his reign, the side which at a future date was to turn not only France but Europe upside down. 

The Edict of Nantes Revoked 1685: Its Effect. The Protes tants had been granted freedom of worship by the Edict of Nantes (Chapter XVIII). In 1685 the king revoked this law. He tried to force the Huguenots to accept the Catholic faith, and he punished all who refused. The dragoons (soldiers) were quartered in the homes of the Huguenots with orders to annoy and persecute the occupants until they renounced their religious beliefs. The majority surrendered to the king's wishes, but thousands remained

true to Protestantism and fled from the country. Orders were then issued that no heretic should leave France alive. Despite this, it is estimated that 300,000 made their escape. They went to Holland, Prussia, England, and America, and those countries gained by their coming.

The Huguenots represented the great middle class of France. 

They were the bankers, the manufacturers, and the merchants. "As rich as a Huguenot" was a common saying in France at that time. Many of the Huguenots who came to America settled in the Carolinas. A few found homes in Virginia, in the middle colonies, and in New England. John Jay and Paul Revere are but two famous Americans who descended from those settlers. 

Wherever they went, the Hugue 

Louis XIV at a masquerade. What nots carried their industry and skill. 

Their expulsion was a serious blow to the prosperity of France. 

The Wars of Louis XIV. — Louis 

XIV felt that a great monarch must be a great conqueror. In 

1667 he attacked the Spanish 

Netherlands (Belgium), because he hoped to annex that country to France. His advance was so rapid and his army so imposing that all 

Europe was alarmed. He was soon at Holland's frontier. The Dutch persuaded England and Sweden to enter an alliance against France. 

This was done, and the plans of 

Louis were defeated. The next does he symbolize? (Redrawn year he made peace with Spain. from a print in the Bibliothèque Nationale.)

The Dutch Put Their Faith in 

William of Orange. But Louis was bitter toward Holland. During the next few years he broke up the alliance against him and prepared for war. The Dutch

had medals made which represented "Joshua arresting the sun in its course." Holland of course was Joshua, and the sun stopped in its course was none other than the French king. 

Louis, as he himself said, was "cut to the quick" by this action. 

He crossed the Dutch frontier with an army of 100,000 men. 

The little republic put their faith in William of Orange, who later with his wife Mary was to rule England (Chapter XX). “To Die on the Last Dike." The friends of William tried to persuade him that it was hopeless to resist the French; that his country would be lost. To these people he replied, "There is a way never to see it lost, that way is to die on the last dike." He cut the dikes, and the waters of the North Sea drove the proud army of Louis XIV from the country. 

The War of the Palatinate, 1689-1697. Undaunted, Louis in 

1689 set out to conquer and annex the Palatinate (på-lătʼĬ-nât), a 

Protestant country along the Rhine River. William of Orange, now king of England, organized another alliance against France. 

The war which followed lasted for eight years and spread to America. There the French and English colonists fought against each other in what has been called King William's War. 

How It Affected American History. -The armies of France overran the Palatinate and laid waste the country. Thousands of German peasants were made homeless and were faced with poverty. William Penn (Chapter XIX) invited them to his colony in Pennsylvania. There they found a haven of refuge. 

They enjoyed religious freedom, and soon the eastern part of the province was dotted with the prosperous farms of these people, who were misnamed "Pennsylvania Dutch." The War of the 

Palatinate ended in 1697 with little gain or loss of territory for either side. 

Four years

The War of the Spanish Succession, 1701-1713. 

later the grandson of Louis was made king of Spain. This close union of France and Spain was dangerous to the peace of Europe. 

England, Holland, and Austria declared war against the two nations. The conflict dragged on for thirteen years. Again in

America the subjects of France and England were drawn into the struggle. There it was known as Queen Anne's War, because 

Anne was the reigning queen of England during that time. In 

Europe it was called the War of the Spanish Succession, and it ended in 1713.

By the terms of the treaty France and Spain were never to unite. Austria received the Spanish Netherlands, known now as the Austrian Netherlands. England was given the Rock of 

Gibraltar, which commanded the entrance to the Mediterranean Sea. In addition she compelled France to give her Nova Scotia, Newfoundland, and the Hudson Bay region. Louis saw his mistake too late. He died in 1715 after a long reign of seventy-two years. 

Colbert Tries to Balance the Budget. The vast building program, the upkeep of the court, and the many years of warfare drained the French treasury. During the early years of his reign Louis XIV was fortunate in having an excellent minister of finance a man named Colbert. He introduced a budget system. From year to year he attempted to figure out what the expenses of the government would be, and, after estimating this, he would see that enough taxes were collected to meet those expenses. This is called "balancing the budget." 

As the years passed and expenses increased, Colbert was forced to tax the people more and more. To cut down expenses, he suggested the dismissal of the numerous favorites who were drawing pensions. In his attempt to balance the budget he gained the ill will of all classes. Finally the task became too great. He died the most hated man in France and was buried in the dead of night to prevent a riot. 

Louis XV (1715-1774): "After Us the Deluge." - The Grand 

Monarch was succeeded by his great-grandson, Louis XV, who ruled for the next sixty years. He waged wars and continued the merry-go-round of pleasure and extravagance at Versailles. 

During his reign France lost her possessions in both America and India (Chapter XVIII). The cost of government increased.

The French were taxed more and more, and finally the nation was heavily in debt. The lower classes had no rights, because Louis 

XV, like a true Bourbon, ruled as an absolute monarch. Most of the people in the upper classes were satisfied. These favorites of the king replied to any complaint: "After us the deluge." 

Little did they dream how true that remark was to be. 

The American Revolution. It was during the reign of Louis 

XV that events were taking place which led to the American Revolution. In order to help pay the costs of the French and 

Indian War and to help support the British army and navy, England decided to tax her colonies in America. Many of the colonists, particularly the merchant class, were already embittered because English laws were hurting their trade. 

Acts of Trade and Navigation Acts. One group of these laws was known as the Acts of Trade. These acts forbade the 

English colonists in America to ship certain named goods to any country but England. It did not matter if the Americans could get a better price from another country. These same acts compelled the colonists to buy certain specified goods from the mother country, so that if the colonists were able to purchase the same kind of goods cheaper from another country, they were unable to do so. Another set of laws was known as the Navigation Acts. These acts made it unlawful for the colonists to ship their goods in any vessels except those which belonged to England. 

In addition three fourths of the crew had to be English subjects. 

These were followed by such distasteful acts as that which permitted the use of "writs of assistance" or search warrants in looking for smuggled goods in the colonists' homes, and the Stamp Act, which required the colonists to use stamped paper purchased from the British government for such things as contracts, bills, wills, and newspapers. This act brought about the Stamp Act 

Congress, which framed petitions against the act and sent them to the king and Parliament. 

The Townshend Acts. When, in 1767, the British Parliament passed the Townshend Acts, there was even more trouble in

Samuel Adams addressing a town meeting. 

America. By these acts a tax was laid upon lead, glass, paper, and tea shipped into the colonies. With this money colonial governors and judges were to be paid by the British Parliament. 

This gave the Americans cause for alarm, for they feared that this arrangement would compel these officials to favor the mother country in their decisions in order to hold their positions. 

Boston Tea Party. -Finally the opposition of the colonies caused England to repeal all the Townshend Acts except a small tax on tea. This tax was continued not only for revenue income, but also to show the colonists that England had the right to tax them. Thousands of pounds of tea were shipped to America by the British East India Company, which had been granted a monopoly on tea. The company offered their tea to the colonists at a lower rate, although it included the tax, than it could be smuggled in from other countries. Finally, three shiploads of tea from the company sailed into Boston harbor. The government

officials could not decide what should be done with it: one thing was certain, the people would not buy it because of the hated tax. A group of young patriots disguised as Indians soon answered the question. In the middle of the night, they boarded one of the ships and dumped its costly cargo into the harbor. 

The citizens of Philadelphia and Charleston also refused the imported tea.

The Result. Up to this time England had overlooked the violations of her laws on the part of Massachusetts. Now the time had come to teach the colonies a lesson. Massachusetts was to be made an example. Parliament closed Boston harbor and would not allow the people to hold town meetings. Massachusetts resented these actions of Parliament, and the other colonies showed their sympathy by sending food and supplies. Then 

England sent General Gage and 5000 British soldiers to be quartered in Boston. 

First Continental Congress. - Feeling ran high throughout the colonies. Delegates were elected by all the colonies except 

Georgia to meet in Philadelphia and discuss their wrongs. On 

September 5, 1774, fifty-five famous colonial leaders, among whom were George Washington, John Adams, and Patrick Henry, met in Carpenter's Hall to discuss ways and means of recovering their rights. They summed up their grievances since the time when 

Parliament began to enforce the tax laws. A petition was sent to the king, and letters of friendship and pleas for help were sent to the people of Canada and England. The colonists also resolved to close their ports to British trade. Before adjournment they agreed to meet the following May to receive the answers to their petitions. 

The Battles of Lexington and Concord. - Many events took place before the colonial leaders came together for their second meeting. Patriot groups clashed with British regulars in and about Boston. General Gage planned to seize the American arms and ammunition which were located at Concord. On the night of April 18, 1775, Paul Revere warned the countryside

The Second Continental Congress. 

that the British were coming. The next morning on the village green at Lexington the British regulars faced the minutemen, or patriot militia. A volley of British musketry caused the death of eight patriots. Then the English marched on to Concord. 

At Concord Bridge the British were halted by the colonial troops, and they retreated toward Boston. The news of Lexington spread rapidly. The aroused patriots fired from behind stones and fences as the British columns marched home. The losses for the British that day were three times as great as those of the colonists. 

Bunker Hill. Sixteen thousand enraged militia surrounded 

Boston and took charge of the hills overlooking the city. Captain William Prescott, their leader, fortified Breed's Hill. General Gage was joined by Generals Howe, Clinton, and Burgoyne, all of whom came to assist in routing the patriots from the surrounding hills. On June 17, 1775, the British, with drums playing and flags flying, charged up Bunker Hill in solid formation. CADE

Twice they were repulsed by the heavy fire of the colonial forces. 

On the third charge the patriots had to withdraw, for their ammunition had given out. The British took the hill, but the 

American forces had won a great moral victory. It showed beyond a doubt that the Americans could meet the seasoned British regulars on even terms. Little hope was now left for peace between the mother country and her colonies. 

Second Continental Congress and Its Work. The Second 

Continental Congress had met May 10, 1775, at Philadelphia.

The battle at Bunker Hill had been fought while it was still in session. This Congress, which had met without power to make laws, soon assumed the right to set up a kind of government. On 

June 14, Washington was chosen commander-in-chief of the American armies. Plans were made for raising an army and for borrowing money to support it. Trade agreements were made with foreign countries, and arrangements were made to exchange ambassadors.

Declaration of Independence (July 4, 1776). -The Congress took a far-reaching step when it adopted the Declaration of Independence, July 4, 1776. This famous document, which was written by Thomas Jefferson, stated that all men are created equal with the right to life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness, and summed up the tyrannical acts of George III against the American people. It also declared the colonies free of the British crown.

The American Revolution. There were eight years of warfare on land and sea before England recognized the colonies as free and independent. During this period Washington marched his armies from Boston to Yorktown, always fighting against superior forces; always great even in defeat. On sea John 

Paul Jones gained undying fame for his daring exploits against real British men-of-war. Men such as Daniel Morgan and Nathanael Greene won decisive victories for the Americans.

Many men of foreign birth joined the colonies in their fight for liberty. Lafayette, a French nobleman, became a trusted RA-24

general on Washington's staff. Baron Von Steuben, a Prussian army officer, became the drill master for the patriot army. After the American forces had won the brilliant victory at Saratoga, the French government formed an alliance with the colonies in February, 1778. Benjamin Franklin, the American ambassador, aided greatly in forming this alliance. The war was sure to end in favor of the Americans when Cornwallis, the British general, was surrounded at Yorktown. Washington blocked his way on land, and a French fleet prevented his escape by sea. Cornwallis surrendered his army of 7000 soldiers October 19, 1781. 

A Glorious Peace. In the treaty of peace which followed, England recognized the independence of the colonies. The 

Mississippi River was recognized as the western boundary. Both nations were allowed to use the river for shipping. Americans were allowed to use the Newfoundland fisheries on equal terms with the British. The American representatives at the meeting which met in Paris were Benjamin Franklin, John Adams, and 

John Jay.

Valley Forge. - Valley Forge is a sacred shrine for every true 

American patriot. Washington kept his small army here during the cold winter of 1777-1778. It took all the courage and ability that he possessed to keep his army together, for the soldiers lacked food, clothing, and the proper shelter. Desertions increased daily, and the surrounding country was filled with spies. 

But the spirit of Washington won, and many loyal soldiers were willing to suffer through that cruel winter with him. Spring brought new hope through the French alliance. Washington's army was reorganized and gained many new recruits. The tide had turned in favor of the Americans. 

Articles of Confederation. There had been several attempts at colonial union before 1776. In most instances these had resulted in failure. When the Declaration of Independence was signed, most people recognized the need for some form of permanent government. John Dickinson of Pennsylvania was chosen chairman of a committee to draw up bonds of perpetual union

among the colonies. Their work was called the Articles of 

Confederation. Congress approved the Articles of Confederation in November, 1777. They became the law of the land on 

March 1, 1781, when ratification had been completed by all the colonies. The Articles of Confederation remained in force only eight years. They failed to do the things that they set out to do, for Congress had been granted power without authority. Commerce and taxation were left to the states. Instead of peace and harmony among the colonies intense rivalry resulted on account of trade. Congress could not compel the states to pay taxes. 

The Critical Period in American History. The period from 

1783 to 1789 has been called the "Critical Period in American History." Since Congress under the Articles of Confederation had no real powers, the states began to quarrel among themselves. 

Conditions kept getting worse until many people felt that the new government would surely fail. Washington and other leaders became alarmed. 

The Constitution. A convention of all the states was called to meet in Philadelphia in 1787. Its purpose was to amend and strengthen the Articles of Confederation. Its members, however, drafted a new instrument of government - the Constitution. Among other things it provided for a chief executive -a President - to enforce the laws. It gave Congress the power to lay taxes and to coin money. It established a Supreme Court to interpret the laws. The Constitution corrected many of the weak points in government that had not been cared for by the Articles of Confederation. It was ratified by the states after a long and bitter struggle. Washington was elected the first President of the United States under the provisions of this new document and took office in April, 1789. 

Louis XVI. — In 1774, on the eve of the American Revolution, Louis XVI was crowned king of France. This unfortunate monarch inherited a bankrupt treasury and a discontented people. 

The long years of tyranny since the days of Louis XIV were gradually arousing a downtrodden nation. Certain influences

were at work, and a change was drawing near. To understand these coming events, we must go to the people. 

The Clergy and the Nobility. For years France had recognized three general classes of people. This class distinction caused much discontent and bitterness. The clergy and the nobility comprised about five per cent of the population. These were the privileged orders. The higher clergy included the bishops and the abbots, who were appointed by the king with little consideration of their fitness for office. They were, for the most part, sons of the nobility and received handsome salaries. 

The church owned about one fifth of all the land in France. It was free from taxation, but the clergy cared for the sick and poor and controlled education. This work was carried on by the poor parish priests, who received very little for their labors and were not numbered among the privileged classes. They toiled among the common people and suffered and sympathized with them. When the Revolution broke out, many took sides with the Third Estate.

The nobles possessed large estates. They were given the important offices in the government and in the army and received favors and pensions from the king. They were exempt from the most burdensome taxes that were crushing the nation. 

The Third Estate. Ninety-five per cent of the population belonged to the Third Estate. This class consisted of the peasants, tradesmen, and professional groups, such as the doctors, lawyers, and writers. They enjoyed no privileges, and the bulk of the taxes fell on them.

The Peasants. The peasants, many of whom were still serfs, were not allowed to protect their crops from wild game. To do so would have ruined the sport and amusement of the nobles. 

They were still forced to grind their grain in the lord's mill, to use his wine press, and to bake their bread in his oven. They had to leave a certain amount of each with him as payment for this privilege. They were compelled to work on the roads and public buildings without pay.

The "Farming Out" of Taxes: the Gabelle. It was the unjust system of taxation, however, which caused most of the hardships. The salt tax, called the gabelle, was “farmed out” to collectors. These collectors promised the government a certain amount, but all the money that they could secure above that amount they were permitted to keep. This paved the way for enormous profits and injustice. 

As a rule people were compelled to buy seven pounds of salt for each member of the family over seven years of age. They paid whatever price was demanded by the tax collector. Consequently the provinces which were exempt from the gabelle paid nothing. Others were taxed five to ten times as much as a neighboring province. Only one fifth of the money thus collected ever reached the government. People who refused to pay were imprisoned and persecuted in other ways. Numerous other articles which the people absolutely needed were farmed out in the same way as salt.

Tariffs and Tolls. Tariffs and tolls were collected on goods when they crossed the national frontier and when they passed from province to province. In addition merchants had to pay a toll as they crossed each of the numerous bridges throughout the country. By the time food and clothing reached an inland town, the price was far beyond what the common people could pay. Fish shipped from the coast to Paris cost nearly fifteen times the original price. Besides these taxes the peasant had to pay church tithes and feudal dues in the form of products and labor. 

These combined taxes amounted to four fifths of his income. "Lettres de Cachet" and the Muzzling of the Press. - Another source of income for the government and one which was feared and hated by all classes was the "lettres de cachet." 

These were sealed letters of arrest containing the signature of the king. They were sold to wealthy nobles who merely filled in the name of some personal enemy and had him imprisoned. 

This endangered the liberties of all. There was no such thing as a writ of habeas corpus or trial by jury.

Editors and authors who criticized the government were sent to prison or exiled. France at this time refused to recognize either freedom of speech or freedom of the press. 

Events That Were Spelling "Doom" for French Tyranny. 

As time moved on, conditions grew worse. Then a group of fearless writers began to stir the people. France began to feel that what England had accomplished for representative government it could do also. The French people grew tense as they heard of the heroic struggle of Washington leading his ragged armies against Great Britain. Their forces crossed the sea to help those English colonists create a nation that believed in a government by, of, and for the people. Those same troops returned home to a land which tolerated a government by, of, and for the king and a favored few. our All of these things were Conths Voltaire. to spell doom for French istulos tyranny. relay lay, basa

The Literature of France Stirs the People. - One of the first writers of France to defy imprisonment was 

Montesquieu (mon-těs-kū'), who lived from 1689-1755.

He wrote a book called Spirit of Laws. It was widely read and went through twenty-two editions. The author opposed the idea of divine right. He suggested that the French government be modeled after that of England. 

Voltaire, 1694–1778. 

The next writer to champion the cause of the people was Voltaire (vol-târ'), the most gifted literary figure of the age. He attacked the injustices of

both the Church and the State. He was imprisoned time and again, but it did the authorities no good. After his release he would write a stinging article about imprisonment without trial by jury. The oppressed people of France read the works of Voltaire with delight, and the government was unable to still his pen. 

Diderot and His Encyclopedia. - Diderot (dēd-ro') was a friend of Voltaire. He and a number of scientists, writers, and reformers began the publication of an encyclopedia. Their definitions of government, law, and taxation were far different from the meaning of those words as they actually existed in France. The Church officials condemned the first two volumes when they appeared, and the king forbade any more to be published. But the work was continued secretly and after twenty years was completed.

The Debt of France and the American Revolution. This literature did much to enlighten and educate the lower classes. 

The nobility and higher clergy, however, could not see the "writing on the wall" (Chapter IV). Rumors, scandals, plots, merrymaking, and extravagance continued, while the Third Estate was overtaxed and browbeaten. In 1778, when Louis XVI decided to aid America and crush England, France was $500,000,000 in debt. 

To finance that war the king was compelled to borrow vast sums.

During the next ten years conditions became alarming. Louis 

XVI felt that something must be done. 

The Failure of Turgot. -- He sent for Turgot (tür-gō'), one of the ablest financiers in France, and appointed him minister of finance. The king promised to back his new official in any reforms that would remedy the burden of taxation. Turgot began his work in earnest. He abolished the forced labor and many of the useless pensions of the king's favorites. He had also planned to do away with the old feudal dues and establish a more just system of taxation. But before he could accomplish these last measures, he was dismissed. The poor, weak king listened to the storm of protest from the court drones. He listened to the pleadings of his gay wife, Queen Marie Antoinette,

an Austrian. She hated Turgot, who had risen to his high position from the lowly Third Estate. She put her spite above the interests of France.

Other ministers were called in, but they accomplished nothing. 

Then he consulted the Assembly of the Notables, composed of the nobility and higher clergy. They refused to accept any reforms that would compel them to share the burden of taxation. The calling of the Notables occurred in 1778. That year was a dreadful one in France, for there was a famine throughout the land and thousands died and tens of thousands were hungry. 

The people of France were starving and penniless, while the king spent $4,000,000 of their money for the upkeep of his horses and carriages.

The Calling of the Estates-General. Something had to be done and done quickly. Louis XVI was advised to call the 

Estates-General, the old representative body composed of the clergy, the nobility, and the Third Estate. One hundred seventyfive years had passed since it had last met. Orders were issued for a general election, and in May, 1789, the Estates-General of France assembled. Burdened with the grievances of almost two centuries, the Third Estate stood united on three things. They wanted representative government, equal taxation for all classes, and the abolition of feudal dues. 

The Third We Are Here by the Command of the People. 

Estate, representing ninety-five per cent of the people, had been allowed six hundred representatives. The two higher orders were permitted three hundred members each. It had been the custom of the Estates-General during feudal times to vote by classes, that is, each estate voted separately. The Third Estate objected to this. They argued that if this was done, the nobility and the clergy could outvote them two to one. They insisted that the three orders meet in one house and vote as individuals. 

Their request was refused, and the king ordered the Three Estates to adjourn to their separate houses. The Third Estate defied the order of Louis XVI. The master of ceremonies turned upon

The Third Estate 

The Three Estates as shown in a contemporary cartoon. is welcoming the nobles and clergy to the National Assembly. them and said: "You heard the king's command." Then Count

Honoré Mirabeau (mē-rȧ-bō'), a noble who had been elected to the Third Estate, thundered his famous reply: "Go tell those who sent you that we are here by the command of the people, and here we shall stay until driven out by the point of the bayonet." 

The National Assembly and the Storming of the Bastille. 

Stay they did. They declared themselves the "National 

Assembly" and began the preparation of a constitution. The king directed the nobility and clergy to join them. The French 

Revolution was under way. But still Louis XVI did not understand the temper of his people. He heeded the ill advice of his courtiers who suggested that he gather the hired Swiss and 

German troops that formed the royal bodyguard and prepare to drive the deputies home. 

The people were furious. Rumors spread there was to be a massacre — the king was determined to regain his absolute powers. 

A mob gathered and began to parade, riot, and steal. On the morning of July 14, 1789, they stormed the Bastille, a notorious

prison of Paris. It was a symbol of tyranny. There many men had been imprisoned without trial, there Voltaire and Mirabeau had been persecuted. Within a few hours the prison was captured, its seven prisoners freed, and the building destroyed. An imaginative drawing of the storming of the Bastille. 

Lafayette, a nobleman who had fought under Washington, was now put in command of the National Guards. He sent the key of the Bastille to Washington at Mount Vernon. Today the fourteenth of July is celebrated as a national holiday and is known as Bastille Day.

The Reforms of the Assembly. -The Assembly now started to work in earnest. Within a few months it abolished the old feudal dues and other privileges. It drew up the Declaration of the Rights of Man, which stated that "men are born free and re-

main equal." It established trial by jury, freedom of speech, freedom of religion, and freedom of the press. It took over all the church property and placed it under the control of the state. 

The National Assembly then formed a constitution. The king, who was now held a prisoner in Paris, was compelled to sign it. 

France was now a limited monarchy with a constitution. The 

Revolution had been accomplished. 

The Flight and Capture of the Royal Family. Many of the dissatisfied nobles fled from the country. They sought refuge in foreign courts, where their tales alarmed the royal families, who feared that this idea of constitutional government might spread. The émigrés, as these French nobles were called, schemed and plotted. Finally they raised an army and appeared on the frontiers of France. The king and queen with their children tried to join them. They left the city in the dead of night, but were captured and brought back to Paris as prisoners. 

France at War: the "Marseillaise" by Rouget de l'Isle. - 

In 1791 the emperor of Austria and the king of Prussia issued the 

Rouget de l'Isle singing the "Marseillaise." Painting by Pils.

Declaration of Pillnitz. This was a proclamation calling on all the European countries to unite and force France to accept the old arbitrary powers of Louis XVI. The French nation was aroused, and the following year the newly elected legislative assembly declared war on Austria. The common people raised troops and rallied around the flag of the Revolution - the tricolor of France. Enthusiasm ran high. The soldiers from Marseilles marched northward, singing their stirring marching song just written by the young army officer, Rouget de l'Isle. "Ye sons of France, awake to glory. 

Hark, hark what myriads bid you rise!

Your children, wives, and grandsires hoary, Behold their tears and hear their cries. 

Shall hateful tyrants, mischief breeding, With hireling hosts, a ruffian band,

Affright and desolate the land, While peace and liberty lie bleeding? 

To arms, to arms, ye brave! 

The avenging sword unsheathe!

March on, march on, all hearts resolved 

To victory or death."

This song was taken up by all and became first the battle hymn of the Revolution and then the French national anthem. 

The Reign of Terror and Execution of the King and Queen. — 

The armies of France were victorious and invaded Germany and the Austrian Netherlands. But at home civil war had broken out. The peaceful revolution was over. For the next three years the government was in the hands of first one and then another of the many radical groups. Tens of thousands of people were executed, both among the royalists and the common people. 

All was confusion and bloodshed—so much so that this period has been called the Reign of Terror in French history. 

Louis XVI was sent to the guillotine (a new invention for beheading people) on January 21, 1793, and Marie Antoinette met

the same fate ten months later. Executions became daily occurrences in Paris and throughout the other cities of France. 

The Republic of France: the Directory. Finally in 1795 the more level-headed groups secured control of the government and a Directory was established. The executive power of the government was placed in the hands of five directors. The legislative or law-making power was vested in two houses, the members of which were elected by all property holders. France was now a republic, but the country was to have little peace for the next twenty years. 

This was

The Remarkable Career of Napoleon Bonaparte. 

due to the remarkable rise and career of Napoleon Bonaparte. 

At the beginning of the Revolution Napoleon was a young lieutenant in command of an artillery company. In 1795 he was made commander of the army of Italy. He defeated the Austrian பர 

Removal of the bronze horses from Venice by Bonaparte. From a drawing by Carle Vernet.

and Sardinian troops and occupied Milan. He next invaded 

Egypt in an attempt to cripple the trade of England in India. 

In Paris he was hailed as a hero. Upon his return he overthrew the Directory and established a Consulate. Napoleon became 

First Consul or chief executive. Later he was given that position for life. But his ambition was not satisfied. His armies overthrew one European country after another, and his popularity and power increased.

In 1804 Bonaparte sent for the pope, and in the presence of an imposing assemblage he crowned himself Napoleon I, Emperor of France. For the next ten years the whole continent of Europe was torn by war. England and the United States (War of 1812) were drawn into the conflict. Old men and young boys were drafted into the French armies to fight for the glory of the emperor. There was hardly a home among the European nations that was not saddened by the ambitions of this man. 

Franz Gruber Writes "Silent Night." It was during this period of war, disaster, and misery that the most famous and

At Christmas time in 1808, the children in his little school were the first to sing: "Silent Night! Holy Night! 

All is calm, all is bright

Round yon Virgin 

Mother and Child, Holy Infant, so tender and mild, Sleep in heavenly peace, Sleep in heavenly peace." "Silent Night" has since been sung by all people and translated into all languages of the Christian world. 

The Defeat of Napoleon. But so long as Napoleon Bonaparte ruled as emperor, there seemed little hope of things being calm, bright, or peaceful. He conquered nations, overthrew kingdoms, and placed his brothers and sisters on the thrones of other nations. 

Finally England formed a coalition against him, and he was defeated at the battle of Waterloo, in Belgium. His star had set, and Napoleon Bonaparte was sent to the island of St. Helena, off the coast of Africa. There he remained until his death in 1821. 

The Attempt to Curb Democratic Government. - Bourbon absolute government was ended in France. The French struggle against tyranny had turned Europe upside down. The old order was about to change, but the advocates of special privilege in government affairs were to meet in Vienna at a great congress of the nations. A final attempt was to be made to curb democratic government. How it succeeded or failed is to be told in the next chapter.

CHAPTER XXII  The Congress of Vienna (1815) Reshapes the Map of Europe. 

Many of the royal rulers whose armies had brought about the downfall of Napoleon met at Vienna; the capital of Austria. 

This meeting, which was held from 1814 to 1815, is known as the Congress of Vienna. The purpose of its delegates was to tear down the governments formed by the exiled emperor, restore the old monarchies, and reshape the map of Europe. When the work was finished, no one was satisfied except the larger powers. Even among those countries it was the crowned heads and not the average man and woman who received any consideration.

The people of Belgium, Poland, Italy, and Germany were greatly disappointed. Belgium, or the Austrian Netherlands, was annexed to the Dutch Republic. The Belgians, who differed from the Dutch in nationality, language, and religion, had hoped to become an independent nation. But this was not realized until 1829, when a kingdom was established. 

Poland, whose entire territory had been divided among Austria, Prussia, and Russia twenty years before, had asked to be restored to the family of nations. But her request was denied 

(see Chapter XXV). 

Austria, through the influence of her master diplomat, Prince Clemens Metternich (mět-er-nik), did not want to see a united Italy to her south or a united Germany to her north. So Italy was divided into several small kingdoms with keen rivalry existing between the kings and the pope, and two of them, Lombardy and Venetia, were given to Austria. Germany was organized as a loose confederation of states without any strong central government. Other countries, including Great Britain, Sweden, and Switzerland, were given new territory. 382

The Holy Alliance, 1815: Lofty Ideal. Another result of the 

Congress was the formation of the Holy Alliance. The leader of that movement was Czar Alexander I of Russia. He had become very religious and was sincere in his desire to establish international peace. The Alliance stated that its members 

I would be guided by the teachings of "the eternal religion of = God our Savior." They declared that justice, charity, and = peace would be the rule in their = own governments and in their relations with other governments. This was a lofty ideal, but few of the signing nations except the czar and the king of 

Prussia considered it seriously. 

One diplomat called it a "piece of sublime nonsense." 

Metternich Forms the Quadruple Alliance to Destroy De mocracy. Prince Metternich 

Metternich. 

From a painting by 

T. Lawrence. had agreed to the Holy Alliance, but he felt that he had a better method of securing world peace. 

He hated all forms of democracy and believed that the common people should have no share in government. Within a few months he organized the Quadruple Alliance, composed of Austria, Russia, Prussia, and Great Britain. In 1818 France, which had restored a Bourbon king to the throne, joined the Alliance, but Great Britain withdrew in 1822. 

The Duke of Wellington, the victorious English general against

Napoleon at the battle of Waterloo, bluntly told the French that 66 it was constitutions that made kings." Metternich had determined to uphold the old idea of absolute monarchies. He decided with the aid of the Quadruple Alliance to crush all European

movements to secure representative governments. He was the most powerful figure in Europe from 1815 to 1848. But during those years there was a widespread revolt against him and the so-called Metternich system. It first began in those countries along the western Mediterranean.

The Revolt in the Western Mediterranean. Napoleon had made his brother, Joseph, king of Spain. The Spanish people resented this and remained faithful to their former Bourbon ruler. In 1812, after the victories of Wellington in their country, they organized a representative assembly called the Cortes. 

They also adopted a constitution which limited the power of the king. The Congress of Vienna restored Ferdinand VII to his throne. He immediately proved himself ungrateful to his loyal subjects by abolishing the Cortes and the constitution. He forbade freedom of the press and freedom of speech. He established again the old feudal privileges and persecuted thousands of persons who expressed democratic ideas. But the patriots of Spain revolted against this despotism, and civil war resulted. 

While Spain was in turmoil, a revolution developed in Naples. 

That province was also ruled by a Bourbon king, who played the part of a tyrant. His armies were defeated, and the people forced him to sign a constitution modeled after the one which Spain had adopted in 1812. 

During the wars of Napoleon the royal family of Portugal had fled to Brazil, in South America. The Portuguese then established a constitutional government. 

In 1821 the people of Piedmont, a part of the kingdom of Sardinia, revolted. They, too, demanded a voice in their local and national affairs.

Metternich Overthrows Constitutional Government in the Western Mediterranean. These struggles against tyranny in the western Mediterranean alarmed Metternich. He called a meeting of his allies - Austria, Russia, and Prussia — and resolved to crush those liberal movements by armed force. An 

Austrian army marched into Naples. The constitutional - -

government was abolished, and the king was permitted to exercise the absolute authority he had previously enjoyed. Metternich ordered another army of 80,000 men into Piedmont, and the rebellion there was cruelly crushed. 

Simon Bolivar, the "Washington of South America."- Ferdinand VII was elated with the success of these ventures. He now called on Metternich to aid him against his revolting colonies in Latin America. Those colonies, under the leadership of Simon 

Bolivar (bol'i-var), called the "Washington of South America," had rebelled against Napoleon. Several of them had established republics. When, therefore, Ferdinand after his restoration decided to restrict their commerce with other nations, they determined to retain their independence. In this new struggle 

Bolivar helped to free other colonies from Spanish control. 

The Monroe Doctrine: a Challenge to Metternich. Such was the condition in South America when the king of Spain asked help from Metternich. The Quadruple Alliance prepared to answer his request by sending an army to the New World. But at this point the United States interfered. President James 

Bolivar at the battle of Carabobo.

Monroe, in his message to Congress in 1823, issued a protest which was to be known in history as the Monroe Doctrine. In simple words it declared: (1) that there was to be no further 

European colonization in the Western Hemisphere; and (2) that the United States would not meddle with any European system of government. It also stated that "we should con- 

Although Charles X was crowned amid scenes of great splendor, his reign was short. The people would not tolerate tyranny. From an old print. sider any attempt on their part to extend their system (Metternich's) to any portion of this hemisphere as dangerous to our peace and safety." The 

United States stood ready with her armies to defend this protest, and Great Britain stood with her. This was the reply that our country flung back across the Atlantic to the enemies of representative government. And Prince Clemens

Metternich heeded that reply. 

Another French Revolution, 

1830. As we have seen, Louis

XVIII became king of France in 1814. The people had not forgotten the principles for which they had fought during the French Revolution. And neither had this restored Bourbon. He signed a constitution which gave his subjects a share in the government. In 1824 Louis died and was succeeded by his brother, who ruled as Charles X. 

Charles was an old man nearly seventy years of age when he ascended the throne. He had spent a great part of his life in exile and during those years he had grown bitter toward all forms of democracy. He was encouraged now by the apparent success of Metternich's policy. He dismissed the Chamber of Deputies, deprived 75,000 people of their right to vote, and muzzled the

press. He was voted almost a billion francs to repay the nobility who had lost their property during the Revolution. 

The people were furious. On the morning of July 28, 1831, the crowds in Paris took up the cry, "Down with the Bourbons."

The royal troops tried to restore order, but they were attacked by the revolutionists, who had erected temporary barricades. 

For three days hundreds of pitched battles were fought in the streets. On the third day the Revolution was over, the tricolor of France replaced the white flag of the Bourbons and waved triumphantly over the city. Charles X fled to England, and 

Lafayette took charge of affairs until a new government was organized. 

Louis Philippe- the Citizen King. A great many people desired a republic, but they were overruled by the wealthy middle class known as the bourgeoisie. A constitution was adopted, and 

France became a limited monarchy once more. Louis Philippe (fē-lēp'), a cousin of Charles X, became king. He was disliked by the nobility because he had fought in the republican armies. 

The people called him their "Citizen King," but Metternich referred to him sneeringly as the "Barricade King."

The Second French Republic. -Louis Philippe had a troubled reign, for France was divided by many political parties. There was the monarchist party which desired the return of Charles X and his heirs. Another group called the Bonapartists clamored for the restoration of Napoleon's family. A third party wanted a republic, and several radical groups hoped to abolish the private ownership of property and any existing form of government. All of these parties opposed Louis Philippe, and as time went on it grew more difficult for him to satisfy any one. In 

1848 he abdicated, and for the second time in French history a republic was established. 

The Second French Empire: Louis Napoleon. - The new con stitution provided for the election of a president who was to serve for only one term of four years. In December, 1848, the voters of France elected Louis Bonaparte to that high office.

JARDIN ROYAL 

des.

Champs Elysees 

This drawing, which is taken from an old print, shows you some of the French people fighting in the city streets.

He was a nephew of Napoleon and was very ambitious to follow in the footsteps of his famous uncle. To do this, he began, secretly at first, to break faith with the people. He imprisoned his enemies and gained control of the army. He violated the constitution by standing for election a second time. He was elected for a term of ten years.

There was one more step to take before his political ambition was to be realized. And in 1852 he took that step. He succeeded in establishing the second French empire by popular vote, and he was crowned Emperor Napoleon III. (The son of the first Napoleon had died.) He now longed for military glory and the prestige that the first emperor had enjoyed among the nations of Europe. He soon began to take a great interest in 

European affairs. We shall meet him again before this story ends. In the meantime important events were taking place in 

Austria, in Italy, and in Germany. 

- The Revo- 

Louis Kossuth and the Downfall of Metternich. lution of 1848 in France and the formation of the second French

republic encouraged the downtrodden people of other countries. 

More than a dozen revolutions occurred in Europe during that year, but the most serious uprising took place in Austria. This was due to a speech delivered by a Hungarian patriot named Louis Kossuth (kosh'oot). He denounced the despotic rule in his own country and the tyranny of Austria. This speech was read by the common people in Vienna. In the middle of March, 1848, they rose in rebellion. The crowds gathered and marched through the streets yelling, "Down with Metternich." Like the people in Paris they too erected barricades and demanded a constitution. Metternich became frightened, fled from the city, and sought refuge in England. 

The Czar of Russia Alarmed. 

- The 

Austrian emperor granted his subjects a constitution and the right to vote.

Hungary was declared a republic, and Louis Kossuth was elected president. The czar of Russia, Nicholas I, became alarmed, for he feared the influence of this movement on his own subjects. A 

Russian army was sent to aid Austria. The Hungarian troops were defeated and forced to surrender. Kossuth escaped and was forced to flee. 

Hungary again became an 

Louis Kossuth.

Austrian province. In 1867, however, it became a part of the 

Austro-Hungarian Empire with its own Parliament. This dual monarchy, as it was called, lasted until the end of the World War 

(see Chapter XXV).

Vienna refused to create a united Italy. Two provinces, including the famous cities of Milan and Venice, became a part of the Austrian Empire. Petty Austrian princes governed Parma, Modena, and historic old Tuscany (Chapter IX). Rome and the territory cutting diagonally across central Italy were known as the Papal States and were under the rule of the pope. Naples and the island of Sicily were governed by a despotic Bourbon and were known as the Kingdom of the Two Sicilies. In northern 

Italy, however, there was one independent state. That state was 

Sardinia, and its king, Victor Emmanuel II, was a true Italian patriot. In this country and around this king those people rallied who desired a united Italy. 

Joseph Mazzini Joins the Carbonari. — The story of that struggle begins with Joseph Mazzini (mät-sē'nē). He was born in the renowned city of Genoa in 1805. As a small boy he was familiar with the misery and unhappiness of his countrymen, caused to a large extent by foreign oppression. When a young man, he joined the Carbonari, or charcoal burners. This was a secret organization which planned to secure Italian liberty by violent methods. He became very active, was arrested several times, and spent many years as an exile. 

He Organizes "Young Italy." — While in prison Mazzini began to realize that Italy would never become united by using terror. 

He planned a new secret organization, which he called "Young Italy." He appealed to those Italians who were under forty years of age, for, he declared, the youth must be educated for leadership and a life of self-sacrifice. Mazzini proved to be an excellent leader. He was a poet by nature and had a vivid imagination. His speeches were filled with the zeal of a Crusader. 

Past experiences, he said, had taught Italy that she could not depend on foreign nations for aid. The Italian people alone had to unite their country. 

Count Cavour: Italian Statesman. Another man who was to play a leading part in making Italy a nation was Count Cavour (kå-voor'), prime minister of Sardinia under King

France appeared to be 

So Cavour sought the

Emmanuel II. Like Mazzini he hated Austria, but he disagreed with the leader of Young Italy in his methods of securing unity. 

Cavour, the statesman, argued that the Italians were not strong enough to expel Austria. He believed that Italy should secure the aid of some strong military power.

Sardinia's Part in the Crimean War. 

the ideal nation for such an alliance. favor of Napoleon III by sending an army to his aid in the Crimean War. This war was fought on the peninsula of Crimea in the Black Sea. England and France fought against Russia. In the peace treaty signed at Paris (1856), Sardinia was recognized as an equal by the nations of Europe, and Lombardy was ceded to it. 

The Austro-Sardinian War: Treachery of Napoleon III. Two years later Napoleon III made a treaty with Cavour against Austria. By its terms all Italy was to be a free and united nation.

In the Austro-Sardinian War which followed (1859), the French forces poured over the Alps. Giuseppe Garibaldi (gä-rē-bäl'dē), a fearless general and beloved Italian patriot, hastened from his country home. He gathered his thousand volunteers, famed in history as the "Red Shirts," and sailed for Sicily. An Italian victory seemed certain when Napoleon, to the disgust of all Italy, withdrew his support.

He offered the excuse that his own country was threatened by Prussia. But at heart he did not wish to see a strongly united Italian nation. 

Garibaldi and His "Red Shirts " Conquer Sicily and Naples.

The treaty which France made with Austria did not suit the people of Sardinia, Cavour, or Victor Emmanuel. Garibaldi was ordered to push on with his conquest of Sicily. Fighting desperately and against heavy odds, he and his brave little band within a few weeks were masters of the island. The brave leader then landed in southern Italy, where the Bourbon king, Francis I, was encamped with 100,000 men. The "Red Shirt " army was cheered by the people as it marched toward Naples. 

Thousands deserted their king and joined the ranks of Garibaldi.

The royal troops also deserted, and Francis I fled from the country. The patriot leader entered Naples, where he was Garibaldi. hailed as "liberator." 

Victor Emmanuel, at the head of an army, marched south and annexed the 

Papal States. Garibaldi hastened to Rome and prepared to attack the

French, who were defending the city. All Italy considered Rome its natural capital, but Cavour did not think it wise to gain the ill will of France. at this time. He issued orders for Garibaldi to wait for the time being.

The Dream of Italian 

Patriots Realized. In 

1861 Italy was united with the exception of Rome and Venetia. A Parliament representing the people was called, and modern Italy was born. Cavour died shortly after this, but in 1870 his dream, as well as that of all Italian patriots, was realized. At that time Austria and France were defeated by Prussia. Venetia and Rome were then annexed to Italy. Again the "Eternal City" on the banks of the Tiber became the capital of a united Italy. 

The Confederation of the Rhine, 1806. - The Prussian victory also made possible a united Germany. Since the Congress of 

Vienna the German states, like those of Italy, had waged war against the Metternich system and Austria. Before the French 

Revolution Germany had consisted of several hundred independent petty states. In 1806 Napoleon reduced this number and formed the Confederation of the Rhine.

The leaders at Vienna determined that these German states should remain a weak and loose league without any strong central government. Both Austria and France feared a united Germany as they feared a united Italy.

William I and Bismarck. — In 1861 William I became king of 

Prussia. He and his prime minister, Otto von Bismarck (biz'märk), championed the cause of German national unity. However, both men believed in the divine right of kings. They planned to make a German nation in which the people would have but little share in its government. 

The Policy of "Blood and Iron." The king wanted to organize a large army, based upon compulsory military training. But large armies cost money, and the liberals in the Prussian Parliament refused to grant him the necessary funds. William I became disgusted, but Bismarck was undaunted. He taxed the people illegally and built an efficient and powerful military organization. When the Parliament objected, he replied: "German unity must be accomplished not by speeches, not by votes, but by blood and iron."

With this policy of "blood and iron," Bismarck hoped to accomplish two things. The first was to humble Austria and make Prussia the leader in German affairs. His second plan was to unite, and that by force if need be, all the German states.

The Seven Weeks' War, 1866. In 1864 Austria and Prussia waged war against Denmark. This country owned land which 

Bismarck wanted. They won the war and were given the two 

German provinces of Schleswig and Holstein. Bismarck demanded that the two states be annexed to Prussia only, but Austria refused. The man of "blood and iron" declared war. 

Within seven weeks the Prussian armies were victorious. Bismarck dictated the terms of the peace treaty signed at 

Prague. Prussia received Schleswig, Holstein and other territory, and united the twenty-two states north of the Main River into the North German Confederation. Those states south

of the Main had sided with Austria. Italy, you will recall, had aided Prussia in this war and received Venetia. 

France and Prussia Prepare for War. - Napoleon III was very jealous of Bismarck's success. Germany was rapidly becoming a nation, and its people had caught the spirit of patriotism. The French emperor desired a war. He wished to play the part of a hero. The Prussian prime minister wanted a war. He hoped that the national pride of south German states would prompt them to join Prussia. In fact he had already made a secret alliance with them to that effect. 

The Causes of the Franco-Prussian War, 1870. — A cause had to be found for war. The shrewd and wily Bismarck wanted to arrange things so that France would start the struggle. About this time the Spanish throne became vacant, and it was offered to a relative of the Prussian king. This alarmed the French, because they would be placed between two Hohenzollern (hō′ĕn-tsôl-ērn) (name of the Prussian royal family) rulers. France protested, and the German prince withdrew his claims. But the excited 

French officials wanted the Germans to promise that at no time would they accept the crown of Spain. In an exchange of telegrams between high government officials in both countries, messages were secretly changed. When they were given to the press in Paris, it appeared that the Prussians had insulted France. 

In Berlin, on the other hand, it appeared that the French had insulted the Prussians. Mobs in both capitals were aroused and demanded war. To Bismarck's delight Napoleon III issued the challenge against Prussia.

The Defeat and Flight of Napoleon III. - The efficient Ger man armies were on the march immediately to invade France. 

In this war which began in 1870 and lasted ten months, the French were completely defeated. In the battle of Sedan Napoleon III and his army of over 100,000 men were captured. The emperor was released and fled to England.

Siege of Paris: Gambetta's Escape in a Balloon.-The German armies closed in on Paris and laid siege to the city.

The second

empire had come to an end with the flight of Napoleon III, and a republic was proclaimed for the third time. Leon Gambetta (găm-bět'ȧ), a young republican lawyer, was chosen head of the temporary government. While the people of Paris were starving because of the German siege, Gambetta escaped from the capital in a balloon. He traveled throughout France and inspired the 

French to raise new armies. These troops caused the German armies a great deal of trouble. In the end the French were compelled to accept the harsh terms laid down by the war lords of Prussia. 

The Harsh Terms of the Frankfort Treaty. The conflict was closed by the Treaty of Frankfort. France was to pay to Germany an indemnity of one billion dollars, an immense sum. 

But to make the conditions more severe, the money had to be paid within three years. A German army was to occupy French soil until the payment was made. In addition to this France was to cede Alsace and a part of Lorraine to Germany. This territory remained a part of the German Empire until the end of the World War in 1918. 

The Third French Republic. By heroic efforts the French people raised the money demanded of them within eighteen months. The French government is still a continuation of the third French republic established at this time. It now has a president elected by the assembly for a term of seven years. The legislative body consists of two houses: a Chamber of Deputies elected by manhood suffrage for four years, and a Senate elected by electoral colleges for nine years. 

The German Empire. It was during the Franco-Prussian war that the German Empire was established. King William of 

Prussia was made emperor. The people were represented in a lower house called the Reichstag (rīks'täk). But its members could make no constitutional amendments or lower taxes if fourteen members in the upper house objected. That body was the 

Bundesrat (boon'dĕs-rät), and the representatives were appointed by the rulers of the different German states. Seventeen members

Reichstag Building, Berlin. of the Bundesrat were chosen by the king of Prussia, who was also the German emperor. It is plain to be seen that the united German people had little to say in their government. But within fifty years the absolute rule of the German emperor crumbled, and representative government triumphed. 

The Machine Age. The struggle against tyranny, as told in this background, brings us to the year 1870. Side by side with these political revolutions in Europe and America there was another revolution taking place. It was a revolution in agriculture, in industry, in communication, and in transportation. This movement is known as the Industrial Revolution. It was to change the everyday, workaday life of man. It made possible the dawn of a new era in the history of mankind an era which we of the present day have called the Machine Age. This then will form our next and our last background.
