​The Industrial Revolution brought with it many changes which greatly affect the everyday life of man. The people of any country may enjoy the products of any other land - products which are brought to them by the many ships which sail the seas. In trains and in automobiles man travels inland. In factories is done the work once carried on in the home. 400

BACKGROUND EIGHT  

We leave England and the countries of western Europe. The figures of Charles I, Cromwell, Bunyan, Newton, Dr. Harvey, the kings of France, Napoleon, Garibaldi, and Bismarck fade in the distance. The principle of democratic government, for which the common people fought, and the work of the scholars will loom brighter and brighter.

We have ended our journey to the "land of time." Our giant airship is ready to land in the midst of the greatest changes in the history of mankind. Side by side with the political revolutions was another revolution. It was a revolution in industry, agriculture, and commerce. Historians have called this the Industrial 

Revolution. We shall see how it altered the everyday, workaday life of man. We shall see many machines invented to do the work that man had done for ages. We shall witness the good and the evil results of those inventions. In this Machine Age of wheels, levers, belts, and dynamos, production of goods, methods of travel, trade, communication, and transportation will be revolutionized and changed.

Nations will seek raw material and new markets. In order to secure these raw materials, brave men from these nations will explore, settle, and develop new lands. Large navies will be built, and large armies will be raised to protect their foreign possessions. Rivalries, secret alliances, and injustices will result in the greatest and most destructive war in the annals of history. 

Many lives will be lost, and millions of dollars will be spent. 

After that a reaction will set in, monarchies will be overthrown, and the world will attempt to establish a better code of morals and a better understanding among men and among nations through man-made agencies and methods. RA-26 401

CHAPTER XXIII  We The Economic Life of Eighteenth-Century Europe. 

have followed the progress of mankind from the earliest times to the eighteenth century. During those years man's methods of making a living (called his economic life) had changed but little. If an ancient Egyptian farmer had been brought to life and sent to western Europe in 1700 A.D., he would have found nothing about farm work to baffle him. He would have been familiar with both the methods and the tools for plowing, sowing, reaping, threshing, and irrigating. The same thing would have held true for any skilled handworker of old Babylon. The weaver, the potter, the smith, or any other artisan could have taken his place in industry (with permission from the guilds) without any difficulty. Furthermore these ancient visitors would have found nothing new in the methods of transportation or communication to surprise them. 

The Industrial Revolution and the Machine Age. The year 

1776 marks the beginning of a great change in all of these things. 

Man learned to utilize steam, gas, and electric power. He built machines for his farm work and for his industries. He applied these new powers to transportation and communication. He constructed railroad trains, steamboats, automobiles, and airplanes. These carried him from one end of the earth to the other speedily, quickly, and safely. At the same time he learned to communicate with his neighbors the world over within a few moments. He invented gigantic machines which made use of wheels, levers, pistons, hammers, and dynamos. These did the work that formerly required thousands of men. 

This rapid change, known as the Industrial Revolution, would have amazed and confused not only the ancients but the workers of Napoleon's and Washington's day. One invention followed 402

another in rapid succession. By the twentieth century men realized that they were living in a Machine Age. It is an age that has brought many comforts and many benefits to mankind, but it is also an age that has brought many problems. The chief concern of world leaders today is this: How can we best adjust ourselves to these new conditions? This Machine Age will form our last background in world history, and the Industrial Revolution will be the subject of our present chapter. 

A Change Takes Place in the Methods of Farming. The scientific research let loose during the Renaissance has continued down to the present day. About the beginning of the eighteenth century men began to apply this science to their everyday needs. 

The first steps were made in the methods of farming. You will recall that during the Feudal Age (Chapter XIV), the crops were rotated, that is, one piece of land would raise a heavy crop one year, a lighter crop the second year, and lie fallow (untilled) the third year. During the third year the soil was given a rest and a chance to regain its fertility. It was now discovered in 

England that beets and turnips could be raised on this untilled land without weakening the soil. This gave the English farmer an additional crop and one which enabled him to feed more cattle. 

At the same time men were experimenting and discovering better grades of fertilizer. This enriched the soil and made it possible to raise more and better farm products on the same amount of ground. 

The first threshing machine was invented by a Scotch mechanic in 1786. It was a crude affair, but it enabled the farmer to separate his grain more rapidly than ever before. Forty years later our modern thresher came into existence. This machine made it possible for a few men to thresh 2000 bushels of grain in a single day.

About this time the first cast-iron plows appeared. (Cast iron is iron which has been melted and poured into a cast or a mold.) This enabled the farmer to plow a deeper furrow and to do it much more rapidly. Thomas Jefferson, author of our

Declaration of Independence and the third President of the 

United States, improved the shape of this plow so that it made a still better trench or furrow. At first many American farmers complained that the iron poisoned the soil. But this was no more reasonable than the complaint against the use of forks at an earlier date.

The abolition of crop rotations in order to make use of all the land at all times, better fertilizers, and the invention of threshers and cast-iron plows increased farm production. The land holders who could afford these improvements grew prosperous. Old and new harvesting methods. 

But the poor tenant farmers (renters) could not compete with their more fortunate neighbors. They gave up the struggle and either hired out as farm hands or moved to the cities. Strange things were happening there. The age-old industries of man were being changed. The Industrial Revolution was on. 

The Old Method of Spinning: the Distaff and Spindle. The spinners and the weavers of cloth were the first workers to be affected by the invention of power-driven machinery. Before this took place, cloth was manufactured by hand. In their raw state cotton, wool, and flax consist of a twisted mass of fiber or threads. At first this fiber was carded or combed into loose bunches on the end of a stick called a distaff. The worker then twisted or spun the material into a yarn, which was then wound on a spindle.

About the fourteenth century the spinning wheel had been introduced from India. It was a common household utensil from the sixteenth to the eighteenth century. By this method the spindle was placed upon a frame and connected by a belt to a large wheel. The wheel was set in motion by pressing a foottreadle, which turned the spindle and twisted the fiber into threads. But even with the spinning wheel only one thread could be drawn out at a time. The work of the spinner was a slow and tiresome process. 

The Old Method of Weaving Cloth. The weavers took the yarn after it had been spun and placed it upon a wooden frame, or loom. Threads were attached to the loom, running from the top to the bottom of the frame. These threads were called the "warp." Crosswise threads, called the "woof," were then woven over and under the lengthwise threads by means of a large needle called a shuttle. This, too, was a slow process, but it took nearly ten spinners to supply enough thread to keep one weaver busy. 

Kay Invents the "Flying Shuttle," and the First Victims of the Machine Age Complain. - To make matters worse, in 1733 

John Kay invented the "flying shuttle." This enabled the weavers to work twice as fast as before. The manufacturers installed the new machines in their factories, and it was now impossible for the spinners to keep all of the weavers busy. 

Many of the latter were thrown out of work. They were the first victims of the dawning Machine Age. They became desperate and blamed their misfortune on the inventor. A mob broke into Kay's home, destroyed his machine, and threatened his life. He fled to Paris, where he lived a lonely and sad life. He never received any money for his invention, and died a few years later in poverty. 

The "flying shuttle" had come to stay. It was a laborsaving machine, and certain workers had to suffer. This has been the sad story of all inventions that have revolutionized industry. But men in this first instance saw that the wise thing

to do was to adjust themselves to the new conditions. So in 

1761 the English Royal Society for the Encouragement of Manufactures offered a prize to the ing. person who would invent a quicker method for spinning, one that would enable the spinners to keep pace with the weavers. 

A Spinning Wheel Falls, and an

Idea Is Born. James Hargreaves 

(här'grēvz) was a poor weaver, who lived in Lancashire, England. Like his fellow workers he was continually faced with the problem of unemployment. One day as he sat in his humble cottage his wife's spinning wheel fell over on the floor. The wheel continued to turn, and the

Old and new methods of weav- spindle stood in an upright position. 

This gave Hargreaves an idea. He built a machine with a number of upright spindles which were made to revolve by means of one wheel, and eight threads could be spun at one time. Later it spun sixteen and then eighty threads. It was so simple that a child could operate it very easily. Hargreaves called his invention the "jenny" in honor of his wife. Neither James nor Jenny Hargreaves received any money for the invention. But, like Kay, they were persecuted by the workers. 

Arkwright Invents a Water Frame for Spinning. Just as the American Revolution was beginning in 1775, Richard Arkwright took out a patent for the "water frame," which he had invented some years before. This machine was run by water power, and consisted of rollers through which the fibers were drawn out and twisted into strong threads. Arkwright, a poor barber, bought several factories and installed his new machinery in them. He was an excellent business man and soon grew

wealthy. King George III honored his achievements by making him a knight.

In 1779 Samuel Crompton invented the "spinning mule." He took the most practical parts of both the "spinning jenny" and the "water frame," and combined them. With this machine one spinner was able to spin two hundred threads at one time. 

The manufacturers gave Crompton $300, but in 1820 Parliament voted him $25,000. He spent the bulk of this money trying to improve his invention, with the result that seven years later he died a poor man. 

What the Discharged Laborers Did Not See. The tables were now turned, for the weavers had too much thread. Another adjustment was necessary. In the meantime many spinners lost their jobs. But Edmund Cartwright, an 

English minister, in 1785 invented a loom with an automatic (self-running) shuttle. Like Arkwright's "water frame" it was operated by water power. This was done by means of a water wheel kept in motion by the running water of a brook or stream. The power loom made it possible to weave more cloth than the spinners could supply. 

Because of these inventions it was now possible for one person to accomplish and do what had required more than one hundred persons previously. 

Old and new methods of spinning.

Again the weavers found themselves without work, and again they took vengeance on the inventor. You will recall that both Kay and Hargreaves had been treated in this same unfortunate manner. Cartwright's home and machinery were destroyed, and he was ruined financially. The

discharged weavers saw only the evils of this labor-saving machine. They could not foresee that more men would be needed to smelt iron, make machinery, and care for the increased production. They could not foresee that the discovery of America and new routes to the Orient were already creating a world market. And even so the busy shipbuilders, shippers, sailors, and machinists were not helping them in their trouble at the time. They were weavers and workers without any means of support or care for their families. Eventually this unequal production of first the spinner and then the weaver was overcome by improvements in both industries. 

Whitney Invents the Cotton Gin and Increases the Supply of Raw Material. But a new problem now faced the textile workers. They were able to manufacture cloth so rapidly that it was impossible to keep them supplied with the raw material. 

At regular intervals the factories were shut down for the want of cotton, wool, or flax. This problem was solved in the case of cotton by Eli Whitney, the son of a Massachusetts farmer and a graduate of Yale University. In 1794 he invented the "cotton gin." This made it possible to separate the seeds from the cotton more quickly in a given time than fifty slaves could do it by hand. Cotton-raising now became the chief industry of our southern states. Negro slavery, which had been decreasing in America, now increased. More slaves were needed to supply the English mills with raw cotton. 

Muscle, Wind, and Water Power Give Way to Steam Power. 

- In the early stages of civilization man depended upon muscle power to do all of his work. Later he built windmills to lighten his burden. And still later, as we have just seen, he built his factories along creeks and rivers. There the running water turned large wheels, which by means of belts propelled his machinery. But human muscle tires; the winds are not always favorable, and during a dry spell the streams are too low to accomplish their purpose. Something more reliable was needed.

The ancient Greeks and the scientists of the Renaissance period

had experimented with steam. And during this Industrial 

Revolution man discovered the secret of steam power. 

Thomas Newcomen and James Watt. The demand for machinery made it necessary to seek new methods of melting or smelting iron. Coal began to be used instead of wood, and large blast furnaces replaced the old hand bellows. The coal mines were often flooded with water. In 1706 Thomas Newcomen (nů-kŭm'en), a mechanic, invented a steam engine to pump water from one of these mines. Sixty years later this same engine was sent to Glasgow, Scotland, for repairs. There it was turned over to James Watt, another mechanic. Watt became interested and made several improvements in Newcomen's engine. One of these was a device to make the engine turn the wheels of a machine attached to it. In 1785 steam power was applied to the textile industry, and production was further increased. The great need of manufacturers now was better and quicker transportation. They did not have long to wait.

The Steamboat Is Invented: Fitch and Rumsey. John 

Fitch of Connecticut applied the steam engine to navigation.

He built a steamboat and made a successful trip on both the Delaware and the Susquehanna rivers in 1787. He tried to interest his countrymen in using this mode of travel to the west. 

But his neighbors made fun of him. They knew him only as a poor carpenter with no schooling and had no confidence in his "crazy" idea. Fitch became discouraged and killed himself.

About the same time James Rumsey of Virginia had a steamboat operating on the Potomac River. In 1802 this method of travel was being used on the Firth of Clyde Canal in Scotland. 

One of the first steamboats. 

A modern ocean liner.

The Work of Robert Fulton. The man who received the most credit for this invention, however, is another American, Robert Fulton. Fulton was a portrait painter and a native of 

Pennsylvania. He had invented a submarine and a vessel propelled by steam power. He offered both inventions to Napoleon and tried to convince the emperor that with these he could easily defeat the English navy. But Napoleon had no faith in Fulton's plan. The inventor returned home and organized a steamboat company. In 1807 he built the Clermont, which made regular trips on the Hudson River between New York and Albany.

Thirty years later the Savannah crossed the ocean from Savannah, Georgia, to Liverpool, England, in twenty-five days. The following year another vessel made the same trip in fifteen days. 

Within a century our modern ocean liners were covering the same distance in four, five, and six days.

The First Locomotives. The first locomotive was invented by Richard Trevithick (trěv'i-thik), an Englishman. In 1814 

George Stephenson, a Scotchman, constructed a similar engine to haul coal from a mine pit to a smelting oven. 

Sixteen years later he built the first railroad, and passenger service was started between Manchester and Liverpool. His first locomotive traveled at the rate of twenty miles an hour. Today our crack express trains travel a mile a minute. 

The Improvement of Communication. Men had been experimenting with electricity since the days of Queen Elizabeth. 

Many names, beginning with William Gilbert, the queen's private physician, added to mankind's knowledge of the electric spark and electric power. Among these were Benjamin Franklin, Volta, Ampere, Galvani, Day, Faraday, and many other renowned men. 

Profiting by their discoveries, Samuel F. B. Morse, an American, constructed the first telegraph line between Baltimore and Washington. On May 24, 1844, he clicked off the message "What hath God wrought." In 1876 Alexander Graham Bell invented the telephone, which enabled the human voice to carry thousands

of miles. The world had hardly stopped marveling at this achievement, when Marconi (mär-kō'nē), an Italian, sent his first wireless message. Within a few years Cyrus W. Field laid the Atlantic cable. And less than ten years ago the radio, which is so common today, was made practical for world-wide use. 

The Everyday Life of Mankind Is Changed. - Steam power soon gave way to electric power, and iron was in many cases replaced by steel. The automobile and the airplane were invented and the everyday, workaday life of mankind was changed. 

The Factory System Begins: Capital and Labor. Previous to the Industrial Revolution most of the industries were carried on within the home. The tools of the weaver, the shoemaker, the tailor, and other workmen were simple and cost very little. The new machinery was expensive, and only the wealthy could afford to buy it. So men called capitalists (because they used their money or machines to further production and make more money) built large factories and installed the latest machines. 

They located their factories near rivers, railroad centers, and coal mines. 

Ewing Galloway 

Goods were now made and transported quickly and 

The handworkers cheaply. were unable to compete with the factory owners. Tradesmen had to give up their little gardens and cottages in the country and move to the city. There they rented a cheap home or a few rooms in a large tenement house 

A slum street. The Federal government is to help build model low-price apartments to take the place of such tenements.

located near their work. This was necessary because the capitalists insisted upon working their help for long hours very often from six o'clock in the morning until seven or eight o'clock at night.

The Division of Labor and Mass Production. Under the old guild system journeymen workers made an entire article, such as a shoe, a watch, or suit of clothes. But under the factory system this was changed. Machines were invented to do some particular detail of the work. For example in the shoe factories one machine cut the soles, another the uppers, and still another cared for the stitching. This "division of labor," as economists call it, was so simple that women and children could care for the machines as it turned out the separate work. Shoemakers of the old type were not even needed. Wages were reduced, men were thrown out of work, and woman and child labor in factories began. The factories could now turn out a great quantity or mass of articles in the same time that it would have taken the old guild workers to produce one. The day of mass production was born with the Machine Age. 

Labor Unions Are Organized. The social and economic history of the nineteenth century is filled with many bitter quarrels between capital and labor. The old guild system broke down with the division of labor, and the workers then organized labor unions. These unions were legalized in England in 1825, and in the United States about the same time. On the continent of Europe the government forbade such organizations during the Napoleonic wars and the struggle against Metternich. 

Labor unions in those countries were considered dangerous to government. By the middle of the century France, Germany, and Austria permitted working men to organize. 

The “Laissez-faire" Policy.—The economists (men who study finance, commerce, and labor) at this time held the theory that there should be no interference with the capitalistic class or the industrial system. In France Turgot preached the doctrine of "let things alone." This idea is known in economics as the "laissez-faire" or "let-alone" policy. But in all countries the

83213 

Fairchild Aerial Survey 

A modern watch factory at Waltham, Massachusetts. labor unions became powerful. With the growth of democratic governments, they began to wield a great influence in politics. 

They advocated laws that would abolish child labor. Before these laws went into effect, little children from seven to eleven years of age were placed in factories where, for a very small salary, they worked ten and twelve hours a day. The unions fought and won the battle against unsanitary factory conditions, the use of machinery which endangered the lives of workers, and the long working hours so injurious to the health of men and women.

Government Regulation of Industry. 

Governments began 

to realize that it was their duty to regulate industry and protect all citizens. Social workers helped to rid many cities of their slums. They waged a war against the owners of the crowded tenement houses, which were breeding places for both disease and crime. The fight for better living conditions is still going on. 

The Literature of Adam Smith and Karl Marx. The litera ture of this period dealt with these new conditions. In 1776 

Adam Smith, a Scotch professor, wrote a book called the Wealth of Nations. It was the first important book which ushered in the science of economics. Smith did not believe in government regulation of industry. Many of his theories are still considered sound. 

About ninety years later Karl Marx, a German, wrote a book called Capital. He believed that the workers who produced

the goods should share more of the profits. Too large a share of these, he claimed, went to the capitalists. In 1878 he organ ized the International Working Men's Organization. His fol lowers adopted the slogan: "Workers of the world unite! You have nothing to lose but your chains and all the world to gain." 

Marx, the father of Socialism, was driven out of one country after another and died in England. But, as we shall see later, his influence is still being felt in the world today. 

The Home Life of Yesterday. Thus we see that within the short space of one hundred years, the home and industrial life of mankind was changed. Less than forty years ago in the 

United States our grandparents were living under far different conditions than today. Our population was rural, that is, most people lived on farms or in small country towns. The home was the center of industry. The father and sons built the house and the barns; they raised the grains, vegetables, and fruits; they raised cattle, hogs, and chickens, and supplied the meat for the family table. The mother and daughters spun and wove cloth and made much of the clothing. They baked their own bread, made their own butter, preserved their own fruits, and made their own jellies during canning season. They had no modern conveniences; they used oil lamps and burned wood or coal in their kitchen, sitting room, and parlor stoves. They did their own washing, ironing, and sweeping with the crudest household implements.

There were no automobiles, and the dirt roads were often impassable. People traveled by horse and wagon, and when twenty miles away, they were a day's journey from home. A trip to town was a treat, a visit to the city was an event. 

The Home Life of Today. But our grandparents have wit nessed many changes since their childhood days. The dirt roads have been replaced by miles and miles of cement highways. The horse and wagon is rarely seen now; the automobile has taken its place. The blacksmith shop and the livery stable (where horses were kept and hired), so common in their day, are gone.

United States Bureau of Roads 

A road before and after improvement. The dirt road is now a modern concrete highway. What other improvements were made? rence. 

Instead there are the garage and the gas station. A ride into town is a mere incident a trip to the city an ordinary occur- 

Electric sweepers and washers, canned fruits, vegetables, and meats from the corner store, and the cheapness of readymade clothes have lightened the home burdens of both man and woman. Electric lights, radios, steam heaters, telephones, and daily newspapers add to the comfort and the convenience of the modern home. Of course there are many outside attractions today that our grandparents did not have. These have brought a problem which must be solved even as man has faced and solved many other problems.

World Colonies and War. The Industrial Revolution and the Machine Age created a desire on the part of all nations to seek new lands for raw material and new markets for their manufactured products. An era of world-wide expansion or colonization went hand in hand with mass production. This movement led to keen competition, jealousies, and military conflicts. The story of how the nations of the world fought for colonies and the wars that resulted will be told in the next chapter.

CHAPTER XXIV  

The Machine Age and the New Industries. Many new industries developed as a result of the Machine Age. Men found employment as coal miners, iron and steel workers, and oil and gas drillers. Coal, oil, steel, and iron were a few of the products needed to make machines. These machines in turn were to be used on farms, in industries, and for transportation and communication purposes. Machinists, engineers, and electricians found work in a thousand or more different fields. Chemists were needed to secure other products from minerals and vegetables. 

These other products are called by-products. For example, a few of the by-products of coal are tar, oils, paints, and illuminating gas. Other scientists, who understood the laws and mathematics of machinery, were necessary for the success of the age. Economists were employed to study the best and cheapest business methods. This is only a brief list of the new industries, trades, and professions ushered in by the Industrial Revolution. But it is large enough to have mystified any one who could have returned from the eighteenth-century world. 

Trade Rivalry and Search for Lands. — Trade and commerce became more and more important. A keen rivalry developed among nations. They sought raw materials for their manufacturing plants and new markets for their manufactured goods. 

They needed oil, coal, rubber, metals, and minerals. They sought both the products and the markets in unexplored and undeveloped lands like Africa and the islands of the sea, or in the weak countries of Asia. This search for and conquest or purchase of lands and colonies for commercial reasons is called "imperialism." How this led to jealousies, secret alliances, large armies and navies to protect those possessions, and war will be the story of this chapter. 419

Africa Is Called the "Dark Continent."- Let us begin with

Africa. In the year 1800 A.D. so little was known about the vast interior of Africa that it was called the "Dark Continent." 

It remained so until the middle of the century when it was explored by that fearless Christian missionary and explorer, David Livingstone.

David Livingstone Prepares for His Life Work. David 

Livingstone was born in Blantyre, Scotland, March 19, 1813.

His family was poor, and at the age of ten he went to work in a cotton factory. He worked fourteen hours a day from six o'clock in the morning until eight o'clock at night. With his first week's wages he bought a Latin grammar. This he placed upon his loom, and whenever he had a few spare moments he studied a word or a sentence. For the next nine years young 

Livingstone toiled during the day and studied late into the night. 

At the end of that time he entered the University of Glasgow. His great ambition was to become a medical missionary in far-off China. 

After his graduation war between England and China broke out, and the ports of China were closed to Englishmen. About this time he met the famous missionary, Dr. Robert Moffat, who had just returned from South Africa. Dr. Moffat told him of the vast plain in the north where he had sometimes seen in the morning sun the smoke of a thousand villages where no missionary had ever been. Livingstone decided to go to Africa at once. So in 1841 David Livingstone arrived in Cape Town,

South Africa. He found the Dutch and British settlers engaged in a political quarrel.

The British and Dutch Occupy South Africa. The Dutch had settled around the Cape of Good Hope in the seventeenth century. They called their settlement Cape Colony. The 

English seized it in 1806 during the Napoleonic wars. A few years later the conquerors paid Holland about $30,000,000 for the territory. At that time there were about 10,000 Dutch settlers, called Boers (bōōrz; farmers), a few thousand Germans and French Huguenots, 17,000 Hottentots or natives, and

30,000 slaves. The Boers disliked the British rule. They resented the fact that English was made the court language, and they became indignant when England freed all the slaves, although the former slaveholders were paid $15,000,000 for their loss. The Boers decided to migrate to the north. 

Between the years 1836 and 1840 the "Great Trek," as it was called, began. Thousands of these Dutch farmers crossed the 

Orange River and organized the Orange Free State. A few years later others moved across the Vaal (väl) River and founded the Transvaal Republic. Natal, northwest of Cape Colony, was annexed by the British. Thus, about the middle of the nineteenth century, South Africa consisted of the English colonies of Cape Colony and Natal and the independent republics of 

Transvaal and the Orange Free State. 

Livingstone and Mary Moffat among the Natives. It was during this general disturbance that Livingstone established his first mission station in South Africa. He had visions of those many villages where no missionary had ever been. Fearlessly he traveled northward from one native tribe to another. 

Wherever he went, he started churches and schools. He taught the black men how to build better homes, how to farm by the latest methods, and how to irrigate their lands. He ministered to their sick and helped them in every way possible. He was aided and encouraged in this work by his faithful and devoted wife, Mary Moffat, a daughter of his old missionary friend. 

This dauntless Christian woman faced the unknown and suffered the hardships of African life until 1862, when she died. 

The Explorations of Livingstone. With a sad heart David

Livingstone pushed on. His work in Africa covered a period of thirty years. During that time he carried the gospel and explored the territory almost to the equator. He preached and wrote against the terrible slave trade. This gained for him the ill will of the Boers, the Portuguese, and the Arabs, all of whom made profits from this inhuman traffic. But he gained the love and esteem of the black men.

He Is Lost to the Outside World. - Livingstone crossed Africa from the Atlantic to the Indian Ocean. He followed the courses of the Zambezi and Upper Congo rivers and discovered the famous Victoria Falls of the Zambezi. He wrote several very interesting books describing the land, the people, the animal life, and his thrilling adventures. His books were read and eagerly awaited by Americans and Europeans. Four years after he had An African village typical of those visited by Livingstone. buried his wife, he set out to discover the source of the Nile River. The Nile, the cradle of ancient Egyptian civilization, had held this secret down through the ages. For five years 

David Livingstone was lost to the world. People everywhere began to believe that he was dead. 

Stanley Ordered to "Go Find Livingstone." -But James

Gordon Bennett, editor of a New York newspaper, held to the belief that the famous missionary explorer still lived. He called

in one of his reporters, Henry M. Stanley, and told him to 

Go find Livingstone." 66 

The real name of Henry M. Stanley was John Rowlands. 

He was born in Wales in 1841, the year Livingstone arrived in Africa. John was a very poor boy and at an early age ran away to sea. He landed one day in New Orleans. A little while later he was adopted by Henry M. Stanley of that city and took the name of his adopted father. When the Civil War in our country broke out, he joined first the Confederate Army and then the Union Navy. After the war he became a reporter. 

The Finding of Livingstone: His Death and Burial in West minster Abbey. — In the fall of 1871 Stanley, with his American relief party, found Livingstone. The great explorer and missionary was at Ujiji (ōō-jē’jē), a native village on Lake Tanganyika (tăn-găn-yē'kä). He was sick with fever and was suffering intense pain. His food supply was nearly gone, and, added to all this, Livingstone was keenly disappointed. He had failed to find the source of the Nile. But he refused to turn back, and after he had gained a little strength he continued westward, determined to succeed.

But he was too weak to stand the strain, and in the spring of 1873 he died. His faithful friends, the black men, for whom he had labored so long, carried his body halfway across Africa to Zanzibar, on the coast of the Indian Ocean. From there it was taken to England. At home a nation mourned for this one-time boy of the cotton mill. To show their appreciation of his achievements, they buried him in Westminster Abbey among 

England's immortal dead. 

Leopold of Belgium Seizes the Congo Free State. But it was Stanley who interested Europeans in the conquest of Africa. 

He returned and continued the exploration begun by Livingstone. 

He discovered the Congo, one of the largest rivers in the world. 

This vast region of central Africa was rich in rubber, ivory, and palm oil. Stanley interested Leopold II, king of Belgium, in the wealth of this land. The king organized the International

African Association to explore and civilize the country. In 1878 

Leopold formed and financed the Belgium company. Stanley was made the agent for this organization, and he traveled from tribe to tribe, establishing trading posts and making treaties with the native chiefs. In 1885 Leopold II seized the entire Congo region as his personal property. He became the richest monarch of Europe as the wealth of Africa poured into his treasury. 

European and American people became indignant when they learned how the natives were treated. Those innocent victims were enslaved. They were cruelly punished, and very often their villages were burned to the ground. In 1908 Leopold gave his property to the Belgian nation. The territory is known as the Congo Free State and is about eighty times larger than little Belgium, of which it is a part. 

The Rush of European Nations into Africa. - Africa was now ripe for the rush of European nations to seize her lands, her people, and her raw materials. Germany took as her share more than a million square miles of this land. She established 

German Southwest Africa and German East Africa. Portugal occupied Angola in West Africa, Mozambique (mō-zăm-bēk') or East Africa, and Portuguese Guinea. The French seized 

Cameroons, Upper Senegal, Niger, the Sahara Desert, and the island of Madagascar off the southeast coast. 

Spain and Italy also received a share of the continent. In 

1847 the independent republic of Liberia was founded and settled by former slaves from the United States. Since 1912 our country has controlled the collection of customs duties (tariffs) for the little republic. So the great interior of Africa, unknown to 

Europeans in 1840, was almost entirely occupied by them in 1900. Its map looked like a crazy quilt because so many colors were needed to show the national possessions. 

The Boer War (1899-1902). — In the meantime diamonds and gold had been discovered in South Africa. This led to a conflict, known as the Boer War, between the British and Dutch. 

The Kimberley diamond fields were discovered near the Orange

Free State in 1869, and the richest gold mines in the world were opened in the Transvaal in 1894. Of course thousands of people started a "rush" to the gold and diamond fields. Most of the new settlers (and they numbered many thousands) were Englishmen. The Boers, as we know, had no love for the Britons, and, since they were now outnumbered, the Dutch refused the British any voice in their government. The latter complained that they were taxpayers and that the Boers were unjust. The 

Boers replied that they had made the "Great Trek" to rid themselves of British rule and that they intended to control their own affairs.

English raiders began to invade the two little republics. The 

Dutch united and declared war on the British. The struggle lasted for several years (1899-1902). The Boers fought bravely and had the sympathy of the world. They were no match, however, for the large and well-equipped armies of Great Britain. 

The gallant and stubborn defense of the Dutch farmers weakened, and they were finally defeated. But the English, very wisely, granted them self-government. In 1909, Cape Colony, Natal, the Transvaal, and the Orange Free State organized a representative government called the Union of South Africa. 

China Since the Days of Marco Polo. The search for raw materials and markets was not confined to Africa alone. While many of those same nations were establishing dominions in that country, they were also seeking a foothold in Asia. The Chinese had held aloof from the rest of the world since the days of Marco Polo. Their civilization since those days until the nineteenth century had advanced but little. The people were living very much the same life as had their ancestors in the days of Kublai Khan. Their country had been conquered in 1644 by invading 

Tartar tribes, called Manchus (măn-chooz'), who had set up an absolute government and had ruled ever since. These rulers avoided as much as possible any trade or contact with foreigners. 

They adopted what is known as a policy of isolation. But the weakness of the country and its vast undeveloped resources

L. Green, from Publishers Photo Service 

A modern section of Shanghai. In what ways does it resemble an American city? Notice the automobiles. (so essential for the Machine Age) became the prey and the prize for which other nations fought.

The "Grab" for Chinese Territory Begins: the Opium War. 

-The British were the first to gain an advantage. They had secured India just south of China by the treaty that had closed the Seven Years' War in Europe, 1763. That same war had been fought in America, where it was known as the French and Indian War.

The use of opium had become such a curse in China that it was prohibited by strict government laws. The Chinese tried to stop the importation of opium from British India. China had a lawful right to do this, but the English demanded the repeal of the Opium Laws. When China refused, Great Britain declared war. This is the conflict known as the Opium War. It is that same struggle that detained Livingstone in England and as a result directed his attention to Africa.

The war lasted two years (1840-1842). The Chinese army and navy were helpless before the British. The English battleships

were equipped with the latest engines and cannon.

The new age had developed machines for war as well as machines for industry. China, representing the old handicraft system of work and war, was forced to surrender. 

An agreement was signed by the two nations known as the Treaty of Nanking. By its terms China was forced to pay England a large sum of money for the opium destroyed. (Such a payment is called an indemnity.) She was compelled to give 

Great Britain the island of Hongkong and to open four seaports to English commerce. It was further agreed that British subjects who committed crimes on Chinese soil were to be tried and punished by English law and English officials. No country listed among the family of nations at that time would have agreed to this last clause. But the shameful part of the whole treaty was the one which forced China to legalize the opium trade. The "grab" for Chinese territory and trade had begun. 

The United States, France, and Germany Enter China. 

Two years after the Opium War ended, Caleb Cushing, representing the United States, secured a commercial treaty with China. 

Five ports were opened to the trade of our country, and we were given extraterritorial rights in all of them. The French, allied with the English, declared war and defeated the Chinese in 1860. 

More ports were opened, and later France seized Indo-China. In 

1897 two German missionaries were killed in the Province of 

Shantung. A German fleet was sent to Kiaochow (kyou'chō); troops, which took possession of the province, were landed. Again 

China was forced to surrender. Germany received a ninety-nineyear lease on the town of Kiaochow and was given certain commercial privileges (called a sphere of influence) in all of Shantung. 

This did not end imperialism in China. In the meantime Commodore Matthew C. Perry of the United States Navy had opened the ports of Japan. This led to other events of world-wide importance.

The Japanese, like the Chinese, also had adopted a policy of isolation. The work of Perry in 1853 marked the dawn of a new

era for Japan. Great Britain, France, Russia, and Holland demanded commercial treaties from Japan and received them. 

The Remarkable Rise of Japan. But the Japanese decided to learn from these Europeans. They abolished their old feudal system, did away with serfdom, and empowered their Mikado or emperor to form a strong centralized government. They were fortunate in having a great statesman and ruler at this time, Mutsuhito (moot'soo-he'to). In his famous Charter Oath he promised to work for reforms of all kind. 

Japanese students were sent to America and Europe to study commerce, medicine, engineering, farming, and government. 

They returned home and built factories installed with modern machinery. They established up-to-date methods in farming, transportation, and communication. The Mikado reorganized the government, making it an absolute monarchy copied after and very similar to the German Empire. Within forty years 

Japan had created a modern army and navy. No country in all 

Publishers' Photo Service 

Tokyo, the capital of Japan, has many buildings which are as modern as those in our own land. Notice the river barges.

history had made such rapid progress. By 1894 she was a serious rival of European nations both in commerce and in war. 

Japan Declares War on China. At that time Japan desired to colonize the peninsula of Korea (kō-rē'à). The king of Korea owed allegiance to China. Both he and the Chinese government objected to any Japanese settlement of that country. The little island kingdom declared war on China. Soon the Japanese with their modern Europeanized army were at the gates of Peiping, and China surrendered. She gave up her rights in 

Korea and acknowledged its independence. The island of 

Formosa was ceded to Japan. The Chinese had agreed also to cede southern Manchuria and Port Arthur to the Japanese, but at this point Germany, Russia, and France interfered. They refused to permit this, but their motive was not a noble one. 

Russia Takes Advantage of China. Russia, a newcomer, desired a lease on that territory herself. She immediately demanded and received it. The Russians were permitted to build railroads in southern Manchuria. They fortified Port Arthur and connected it by rail with the Trans-Siberian railroad. A few years later the czar of Russia ignored the claims of Japan and sent his Cossack troops to occupy Korea. 

A Secret Alliance Is Formed, and Another War Is Fought for

Chinese Territory. In 1902 England and Japan had formed a secret alliance, in which they both agreed that if one was at war with another nation and a third party interfered, they would come to each other's aid. With this assurance and a strong army and navy Japan in 1904 declared war on Russia. To the astonishment of the world the Russian armies were quickly defeated and the Russian Baltic fleet completely disabled. The treaty of peace, at the invitation of President Theodore Roosevelt, was signed. at Portsmouth, New Hampshire. Russia agreed to leave Manchuria and cede Port Arthur and the Liaotung (lé-ou-toong') peninsula to Japan. The independence of Korea was acknowledged, but in 1910 it was annexed to Japan. The Japanese were adopting European methods rapidly.

The Boxer Rebellion, 1900.- If China had any rights in this war that had been waged for her lands, she was totally ignored. 

Beginning with the twentieth century and continuing down to the present time, one phase of China's history has been that of more seizures, more foreign wars, and many internal revolts. 

Another side of her history pictures the gradual awakening of a powerful nation of more than 300,000,000 souls.

In 1900 a secret society was formed in China known as the "Order of the Patriotic and Harmonious Fists." Its members, called Boxers, objected to all the modern inventions introduced by the Europeans. They resented the invasion of their country and determined if possible to expel all foreigners. They destroyed European property and massacred Christian missionaries. In the summer of 1900 they assassinated the German ambassador and threatened the lives of all foreigners within the city of Peiping. American, Japanese, and European troops were rushed to the scene and saved the situation. 

Chinese Students in America. The Chinese government was forced to pay an indemnity of $320,000,000 for the destruction of foreign property. The United States received as its share $25,000,000. Half of this, however, was returned to China to be used for the education of Chinese students in America. 

The Chinese Form a Republic, 1912. — The return of the 

Chinese students had its effect upon China. They felt that the salvation of their country lay in the adoption of western civilization. They advocated and established one change after another. In 1909 a revolution began, and three years later 

China became a republic.

The Growing Importance of Island Possessions. To guard their possessions in Africa and Asia, nations were forced to support large armies and navies. The islands of the seas, therefore, became important for reasons other than their raw materials and possibilities for markets. They were needed as coaling stations and bases of supplies for ships. The island possessions of the larger countries increased as the Machine Age developed.

So many

The Desire for Peace: The Hague Conferences. 

wars had been fought because of this commercial rivalry, that serious people in all lands became much concerned. They sought means and methods of securing peace. In 1899 Czar Nicholas II was responsible for a conference of nations which met at The Hague (hāg) in Holland. Twenty-six nations agreed to submit any controversies which did not involve their honor or personal interests to arbitration, that is, they would allow a third party to decide the merits of their case rather than go to war. 

In 1907 a second conference was held at the same place. At this meeting the nations made rules for warfare, and these rules became a part of international law. The use of poisonous gases, the bombardment of hospitals and defenseless towns, and the laying of mines on land or sea were forbidden. 

Secret Diplomacy: the Triple Alliance and the Triple Entente. 

Despite these moves for peace the distrust and rivalry among European nations continued to grow. Secret alliances, pledging the aid of the signers in case of war, were made behind closed doors. The common man, who would have to fight and suffer in those wars, went about his work calmly. He did not know that his rulers were offering his life as a pledge in these secret schemes. But in a world that was soon to be torn by the most dreadful conflict in history, he was to learn about these things. 

He was to become a wiser and more serious citizen. 

In 1882 Italy joined Germany and Austria-Hungary in the Triple Alliance. These three countries agreed to aid each other if any one of them were attacked by another nation. England, France, and Russia united secretly to combat the power of the Triple Alliance. They formed what was called the Triple Entente. 

Trouble in the Balkan States. The Balkan Peninsula in southeastern Europe had been dominated by the Turks for centuries. Greece had gained its independence in 1831. The other states were Bulgaria, Rumania, Albania, Montenegro, Bosnia, Herzegovina, and Serbia. In 1877 Russia defeated

Turkey, and in the following year the European diplomats met at the Congress of Berlin to decide the fate of the Balkans. 

Bosnia and Herzegovina were placed under the control of Austria, and the remaining states were declared independent. In 1908 there was a revolution in Turkey which greatly weakened the power of that country.

Within the next five years the Balkan states were in con- 

BALKAN TROUBLES 

TRAVE stant trouble, and one war followed another. Austria decided to annex Bosnia and 

Herzegovina. Serbia objected.

This was contrary to the agreement at Berlin, and, too, Serbia had hopes of uniting with those two Slavic states. They were of the same nationality, the same religious faith, and spoke the same language. This union would give Serbia an outlet to the sea, so she called her Parliament and talked of war with 

A cartoon called "The Boiling Point" Austria. from Punch, 1912.

The Triple Alliance swung into action, and Germany insisted that Austria should have her wish. Russia sympathized with the Slavs. England was worried about the construction of the Berlin to Baghdad railroad by Germany, for it would enable the Germans to secure much of India's trade. But for the time being the Triple Entente held back. Germany grew anxious about the weakened power of 

Turkey. She depended upon the strength of that country for the success of her railroad, through whose territory it had to pass. 

The growing hatred of Serbia was dangerous to her schemes. 

The Archduke of Austria Is Assassinated. This was the situation in the Balkans with the dawn of that tragic year, 1914. 

The European nations were uneasy, and they were preparing 

---

for a struggle. Millions of dollars were voted in Germany for the preparation of war. Laws were passed in France and in Belgium making the military training of youth compulsory. Armies were being equipped with more deadly engines of war in all countries. 

Finally the excuse for war occurred. The Archduke of Austria, Francis Ferdinand, and his wife were assassinated on June 28, 1914, at Sarajevo (så-rä'ya-võ), the capital of Bosnia. The murder had been planned in Serbia, and the assassin, a Bosnian youth, had been encouraged by the minister of education. 

The World War Begins, 1914. One month later Austria declared war on Serbia. The stage was all set. Germany mobilized her armies and came to the aid of Austria. On 

August 1, the German government declared war on Russia. 

Within a few weeks France and England had joined their ally Russia, and the World War began. 

Before it ended (1918) twenty-four nations had joined England, France, and Russia against Germany, Austria-Hungary, Turkey, and Bulgaria. The former were called the Allies, the latter the Central Powers. Italy broke away from the Triple

Alliance and in 1915 joined the Allies. 

The Germans Violate the Neutrality of Belgium. In 1839 the European countries, including Prussia, had agreed to the neutrality of Belgium. This meant that it was not to be attacked or invaded by any foreign country. The German chancellor now referred to this solemn agreement as a mere scrap of paper." He demanded permission from King Albert of Belgium to allow the troops of Germany to pass through that country. 

This would enable them to attack France from the north. The 

Belgian king not only refused to grant the request, but stated that he would defend his nation's right of neutrality.

The Invasion of Belgium. - The German commanders ordered their armies into Belgium. The invaders laid waste the farmlands and destroyed forests and industries. They searched, looted, and burned homes and towns. Thousands of people were made homeless, and those who were not taken prisoners RA-28

fled before the invading Germans. English and French troops were rushed to the aid of Belgium. The Allies were forced to retreat day after day. And most of Belgium was occupied before they were able to check the advance of the enemy. 

The First Battle of the Marne. By September the kaiser's troops, which were advancing from another direction, were within twenty-five miles of Paris. All hope seemed lost. Marshal 

A French front line in the Somme district. 

Joffre (zho'fr') ordered his soldiers to stand and die, if need be, to the last man. The 

British sent reënforcements, and Joffre sent for every ablebodied man in Paris. Then from September 5th to the 

10th the Allies fought stubbornly and furiously. The 

German offense weakened, then broke, and finally their armies were in full retreat.

The brunt of this battle was fought along the Marne 

River and for that reason has been called the first battle of the Marne. The Battle Lines. From now on this war became a stalemate. 

Both sides settled down to trench warfare. These trenches, ranging in depth from three to fifteen feet, zigzagged along the road sides and through fields and villages. Between the firstline trenches of the opposing forces was "no man's land." This ground was strung with barbed wire to check the advance of either side. The trenches extended for a distance of four hundred miles on the western front and 1400 miles on the eastern front. The former extended from the channel ports in Belgium to the Vosges (vōzh) Mountains near Switzerland; the latter from the Gulf of Riga in Russia to the borders of Rumania.

Rules of Warfare Are Ignored. All of the rules of international warfare were violated first by Germany and then in retaliation by the Allies. Airplanes bombed defenseless towns and hospitals. Women and children were killed. Thousands of soldiers died in agony from the effects of poisonous gas. , Slinking submarines and hidden mines killed many on the high seas. Most of the victims were innocent neutrals. It was the inhuman submarine policy adopted by Germany that helped to bring the United States into the conflict on the side of the Allies. 

The World Becomes War Weary: the Russian Revolution. — 

For three years the war had continued. Millions of people, mostly men and boys, had been killed. The combatants were warsick and weary. In 1917 the Russian soldiers began to desert by the thousands. They were scantily clothed and poorly fed. They had been betrayed by officials who seemed to have little regard for human life. The absolute monarchs of the 

Russian Empire had played the part of tyrants for generations. 

One hundred thousand men quit their jobs in February and refused to return to work until their wrongs were righted. A few months later a revolution occurred. The czar was forced to resign. Alexander Kerensky, a socialist, took over the government. But when he attempted to continue the war, he was turned out of office, and a more radical group, called Bolsheviki, assumed control of affairs. Their leader was Nicholas Lenin, who was supported by soldiers, laborers, and peasants. In the early part of March the new government signed a treaty of peace with Germany.

The United States Declares War, 1917. The doom of the 

Allies during the Russian collapse seemed certain. A million 

German soldiers could be spared from duty on the eastern front and deal a final blow to the Allies on the west. But on April 6, 1917, the United States declared war on Germany. The German submarines, despite our many protests, had been sinking our vessels without warning. German agents in our country were plotting against the government, and finally British secret

service men heard, caught, and deciphered a code message as it was being sent from Berlin to Mexico. This message told of the unrestricted submarine warfare which would begin in February, 1918. In case the United States entered the war, Mexico was asked to join the war against our country. President Wilson made it plain that we had no quarrel with the common people of Germany. The war, he said, was to be fought against an autocratic government based upon military despotism. 

The The Race to the Western Front: Château-Thierry. 

war now became a race between the United States and Germany for the western front. The German troops had to be rushed across the continent of Europe to the front lines in France and Belgium. The American troops had to be trained and carried across the 3000 miles of the submarine-infested waters of the 

Atlantic Ocean. Who would win?

As the German armies, during March and April, 1918, advanced closer and closer to Paris and the channel ports, defeat for the Allies seemed assured. But in May General John J. 

Pershing was ready with 2,000,000 American soldiers. In June the advance guard of these troops won immortal fame by stopping the German drive at Château-Thierry (shä-to-tyě-re') on the Marne River. 

Brown Bros. 

Tanks in France in 1918.

The Defeat of the Central Powers. The German morale broke down, the hope of the Allies returned. Marshal Foch (fōsh) of France was now placed in command of the Allied armies. 

He started a series of attacks which drove the Germans back day by day. Late in the summer and early fall they were in full retreat in Italy, Belgium, France, and the Balkans. Bulgaria surrendered, then Turkey and Austria-Hungary. In November 

German sailors mutinied and seized several warships as a protest against the wars. People in many of the German states revolted and overthrew their governments. The kaiser gave up his throne and fled to Holland, where he has remained ever since. 

The Armistice Is Declared: Its Terms. At eleven o'clock on November 11th an armistice (hostilities ceased) was signed. 

The war was over, and throughout the world there was rejoicing. 

Bells were rung, whistles were blown, and parades were held.

By the terms of the armistice Germany was forced to evacuate all invaded territory. She was to surrender most of her war vessels and all of her submarine and torpedo boats. The Allied armies were to occupy the country along the Rhine River. 

These were but a few of the conditions agreed to by Germany. 

The Peace Treaty. The final terms of the peace were signed at Versailles near Paris the following year. Germany was forced to pay an enormous amount of indemnity money. She gave up all of her colonies and returned Alsace-Lorraine to France. 

She agreed to abolish universal military training and reduce her army and navy. The German representatives complained bitterly, but the Allies remained firm. 

Woodrow Wilson and the League of Nations. President

Woodrow Wilson was the outstanding figure at the Peace Conference. He attempted to correct many of the evils that had caused this war. He sought justice for the smaller nations and for China. He hoped to abolish all secret alliances and future possibilities for war. To accomplish these things, President 

Wilson succeeded in placing in Part I of the treaty his plan for a League of Nations. It was accepted and signed by all the

Brown Bros.

The Council of Four in Paris. These men decided the main terms of the 

Name them. treaty. principal powers of the world except the United States, Mexico, and Russia. 

The United States Senate refused to ratify the treaty on the ground that the League of Nations would entangle us in European affairs. In 1920 we signed a separate treaty with Germany. "Does It Pay?" The war was over but not its effects. It has been called correctly a "World War." The people of Europe, Asia, Africa, America, and the islands of the sea were involved. 

More than 60,000,000 men and women had been mobilized. 

About 10,000,000 or one out of every six were killed, 20,000,ooo or one out of every three were wounded. This terrible toll affected millions more. Men and women the world over were asking the question, "Does it pay?" The answer lies with the citizens of the world in this and future generations. 

Robert Southey (1774-1843), a famous English poet, gave expression to the same thought when he wrote the poem The 

Battle of Blenheim. In part he says:

"They say it was a shocking sight

After the field was won;" 

For many thousand bodies here 

Lay rotting in the sun : 66 But things like that, you know, must be 

After a famous victory. 'And everybody praised the Duke, Who this great fight did win." "But what good came of it at last?" 

Quoth little Peterkin. "Why, that I cannot tell," said he; "But 'twas a famous victory."

CHAPTER XXV  

The World of Today. As a result of the World War many changes occurred which affected the life of mankind. By the terms of the several peace treaties new nations were formed. 

Revolutions took place in many countries, and monarchies were overturned. Attempts were made to reduce the size of armies and navies and to create a better understanding among nations. 

The conflict had cost billions of dollars, and the problem of debts and how to pay them faced the governments. The burden of heavy taxation and unemployment confronted the people of all countries.

But the world today has much for which to be thankful. The 

Industrial Revolution was accompanied by scientific progress that brought many comforts and many pleasures. Some of these were discussed in Chapter XXIII. Medical science has done much to stamp out disease and diminish pain. Other sciences are helping to abolish the fears and superstitions held by man in bygone days. Music, art, literature, and sports furnish opportunities for leisure time. Universal education is producing a more intelligent citizen. Government and social reforms are giving the common people a new deal. 

The above description is but a general view of world conditions today. What caused the more important of these will be the subject of this last chapter. 

The Restoration of Poland. Not far from the White House in the city of Washington stands the statue of Thaddeus Kosciusko, the brave Polish patriot. Kosciusko came to our country during the American Revolution and became one of Washington's most trusted generals. He built the first fortifications at West Point, which later became the site of the United 

States Military Academy.
