When Kosciusko returned to his homeland, he found the country in desperate straits. Russia, Austria, and Prussia were marching armies into Poland and stealing and annexing portions of its territory at will. In 1795, by their third and last partition, they annexed the entire kingdom and Poland ceased to exist as an independent nation.

Kosciusko rallied around his standard a small band of peasant patriots. For a time they fought successfully against fearful odds. They defeated both the Prussian and Russian armies.

But in the end the little army, poorly equipped and far outnumbered, was defeated. Kosciusko was carried from the field badly wounded. The hopes of Polish freedom were shattered.

There was nothing on the map to show that the country ever existed. 

Then in 1918 President Wilson in one of his "fourteen points" insisted upon the restoration of an independent Polish state inhabited by a Polish population. By the terms of the Versailles 

Treaty this was done, and Poland became a republic. Its first leader was Jan Ignace Paderewski (på-dě-ref'skė), one of the world's great musicians. After a few years, conditions, as in all of Europe, became unsettled. In 1926 the government came under the control of a dictator, but it still retained its republican forms. As we honor the name of Thaddeus Kosciusko, Poland in turn honors the name of Woodrow Wilson. 

The Treaty of St. Germain, Austria. - Between September, 1919, and July, 1923, the Allies made separate treaties with Austria, Bulgaria, Hungary, and Turkey. By the first of these, known as the Treaty of St. Germain (a suburb of Paris), Austria was separated from Hungary and compelled to cede territory to Rumania, Poland, Italy, Hungary, and several new states already in the making.

Austria was made a small inland state with a population of only 7,000,000 people. It adopted a republican form of government, and the former emperor, Charles, resigned from public life for a few years. The little republic was compelled to surrender

its merchant ships and make heavy payments in goods (reparations) to the Allies. The burden was too heavy, and the penalty too severe. The people were overburdened with taxation, and the masses reduced to poverty. In 1922 Austria decided to give up and gave notice to the other countries that she could no longer maintain her existence as a national state. The League of Nations came to her rescue, and conditions were improving until the great world panic of 1929. 

The Treaty of Trianon: the Plight of Hungary. The treaty with Hungary was signed in June, 1920, in the palace of the Trianon at Versailles. After the war a republic had been proclaimed in that country, but a few months later the Communists gained the upper hand. The new rulers organized a government similar to Russia, but within a year it was overturned and a kingdom established with a regent at the head of its affairs. 

The next event in the changing political fortunes of the state was the unsuccessful attempt of Charles, the former emperor, to regain the throne. The plight of the Hungarian people in 

1923 was no better than that of Austria. 

The New States of Czechoslovakia and Yugoslavia. — In another of his "fourteen points" President Wilson had stated that the different peoples of Austria-Hungary, with the same national customs and languages, should have the right to organize their own governments. In several instances this was done. Much of the territory taken from Austria and Hungary became a part of two newly formed states. One of these was the republic of 

Czechoslovakia (chěk-ô-slô-vä’ki-a), which comprised the union of Czechs and Slovaks. The other was the kingdom of Yugoslavia (yōo'gō-slä'vi-ȧ), which consisted of those Slavic peoples in the Balkans who had long desired to unite. This was Serbia's dream come true (Chapter XXIV).

Old Nations Emerge in New Forms. In the course of the next few years, Mesopotamia became a kingdom with the old city of Baghdad as its capital. was reorganized as a kingdom. 

About the same time Egypt Thus, after many centuries,

these two countries, which comprised our story of ancient Oriental civilization, emerged again among the family of nations. 

Turkey, which had been torn by revolutions before 1914, suffered still more during the World War. The political conditions after the war were very unsettled. In 1923, under Mustapha 

Kemal Pasha, a republic was established and order was secured. 

The British Commonwealth of Nations. Great Britain, too, was affected by the signs of the times. The far-flung dominions of the British Empire became restless and discontented. They felt that England was assuming too much power in regulating the affairs of the entire empire. In 1926, at a conference in 

London, it was decided that: "Every self-governing member of the empire is master of its own destiny — and it is subject to no compulsion whatever." The Dominions are now practically independent of England except for a slight bond of union. The 

British Empire became the British Commonwealth of Nations. 

It included Canada, South Africa, Australia, New Zealand, and the Irish Free State. 

The Formation of the Irish Free State. The Irish Free State had been established in 1921. The formation of this republic was the outgrowth of many years of strife between England and Ireland. In the early part of the nineteenth century influential groups of Irish people began to advocate "home rule" for Ireland. 

They wanted the privilege of self-government. The bitterness between the two countries increased as time passed. Then during the war an organization was formed called the Sinn Fein (shin fan). They demanded the complete independence of 

Ireland, and to gain this end they rebelled against the mother country in 1916. The British army was hurried into Ireland. 

Fifteen leaders of the Sinn Fein party were executed, and hundreds were thrown into prison. This action did not quell the rebellion but only added fuel to the fire. Thousands of people now joined the movement for complete independence. 

Ireland itself was divided on this question. Northern Ireland or Ulster had been settled by immigrants from Scotland and

England. These Ulsterites, as they were called, had never been in sympathy with either home rule or independence. They united with the English against the Sinn Feiners of the south. 

For three years the country was torn by civil war, and it suffered all the horrors that accompany such a conflict. Finally in 1921 the factions came to an agreement. The people of southern 

Ireland formed the Irish Free State. It was agreed that the new government should have its own parliament (law-making body), its own army, and its own chief executive.

The new republic was not to be completely independent.

It was to be a part of the 

British Commonwealth of Nations. In 1932 Eamon de 

Valera was elected president.

During this entire struggle he had been one of the outstanding leaders for Irish independence. One of his first acts was to notify England that the oath of allegiance to that country would be stricken

Wide World Photo 

Eamon de Valera. from the constitution of the Irish Free State. During the latter part of this same year the Prince of Wales passed through the Irish Free State on a visit to Ulster. He was accompanied by a heavy guard because of the unfriendly attitude of the people in southern Ireland. The events were too near, as yet, to have erased the bitter feelings that developed because of this conflict. 

India Becomes Dissatisfied with British Rule: Mahatma 

Gandhi, the Leader of India. - In India, also, the people were becoming dissatisfied with British rule. They, too, demanded self-government. Their leader was Mahatma Gandhi (gånd'hė),

one of the most remarkable men of modern times. Gandhi wielded a powerful influence over both the Hindu and the Mohammedan people. He and his followers refused to coöperate with the British until their demand was fulfilled. They refused to buy or sell English-made goods, to serve in the British army, or to send their children to the English schools. As a protest against the Machine Age introduced into India by Great Britain, Gandhi advocated the return to home industry. 

In 1919 Parliament passed the Government of India Act. 

This was an indefinite ruling that promised self-government for India some time in the distant future. The act did not satisfy the people of India. There were many rebellions throughout the land. Finally Gandhi was arrested and sentenced to prison for six years. But the persecution of India's champion only 

Wide World Photo 

The India round-table conference in St. James's palace, London. Gandhi is wearing his native dress.

served to increase the spirit of revolt. The English saw their mistake and released him after a few years.

A round-table conference was held in London in 1931. The object of this meeting was to form some plan for the self-government of India, which had been assured the year previous. At first Gandhi refused to attend, but as the time for the conference approached his followers persuaded him to go and represent their interests. He was hurriedly driven over the rough Indian roads to a seaport, where he booked passage for England. 

Dressed in his scant native garb, as a symbol of India's determination to retain its own customs, Gandhi was the object of crowds of curious English people who turned out to see him wherever he went. His picture appeared in the daily newspapers of all nations. What he said, what he ate, and where he went were headlined as news of the day. 

The Fast of Gandhi Brings Relief to Sixty Million "Untouchables."A more definite understanding between England and 

India was reached at this conference. But Gandhi realized that his country could never become a great nation until its people were united. The caste system of the Hindus made this almost impossible. More than 60,000,000 of India's teeming population belonged to a class known as the "untouchables." These people were not members of any caste. They represented the lowest social order in the world, lower even than slaves. They had been outcastes for more than 2500 years, had no privileges, and were shunned by the rest of India. On his return home from 

England Gandhi, a Brahmin, that is, a member of the highest or priest caste, decided to reform the social system of his country. 

He informed his followers that these 60,000,000 outcastes should be represented and permitted to serve in the Indian legislature. They refused. Then Gandhi served notice that he would begin a fast which would either end in his granted request or his death. Day after day he refused to eat. The world was witnessing a great scene. This frail leader, weighing less than one hundred pounds, was staging the most revolutionary

Ewing Galloway

Street scene in Bombay, India. Notice the mosque in the background. 

What does the oxen-drawn cart tell you? social reform in all history. Could he break down the custom and tradition of ages? A week passed, and then it was announced that Gandhi had won. His request was granted, and 60,000,000 souls began to see the light of a new day. 

India had made its first great step toward national unity. 

This is, however, but one victory, with more to gain. There are still about 2300 castes in India, and an intense rivalry between Mohammedan and Hindu. Gandhi has now turned his attention to the difficult task of creating a better feeling and more sympathetic understanding among these groups. 

Russia's Red Army Maintains the Rule of the Bolsheviki. 

It was in Russia that the most drastic changes occurred after the war. We have seen in Chapter XXIV how the Communists or

Bolsheviki gained control of the government under Nicholas Lenin. In order to maintain this control, a standing army was organized. Leon Trotsky was appointed the "People's Commissioner for Military Affairs." Trotsky had been a political prisoner in Siberia, and when the World War began, he was living as an exile in the United States. Under his leadership, the "Red Army," as it was called, crushed all opposition. It supervised the elections, and none but the Bolsheviki was allowed to vote. It so frightened the majority of people, that the Communists were able to make the changes they desired. 

The Reign of Terror in Russia. For the next three years 

Russia witnessed a reign of terror. The nobles and large landowners were deprived of their property. All of the mines, factories, and industries were taken over by the Bolsheviki, and everybody was compelled to work for the government. A spy system called the Cheka was organized, and many Russian people were executed. Furthermore, the masses were facing starvation. The Soviets or workers' groups, who had taken charge of the factories and industries, were unable to manage them. The production of goods was halted, and the people were unable to secure enough food or clothing. The government could get no help from the outside world, because the other nations refused to trade with them. This was due partly to the fact that the Soviet Republic had repudiated (refused to pay) the war debts owed to those countries which had loaned money to the monarchy.

Besides this the Bolsheviki were calling on the workers of the world to overthrow their governments. Allied troops, which included regiments from the United States, invaded Russia. 

The Allies also financed anti-Bolshevist movements. All of these things added to the misery of the Russian people. They were now faced with unemployment, hunger, cold, civil war, and foreign invasions. Still Lenin retained his power. But he was compelled to change many of his communistic ideas.

The Russian Union of Soviet Socialist Republics. In 1924 

the Russian government ratified a new constitution. It created the Russian Union of Soviet Socialist Republics, which was to be governed by representatives of the people. The private ownership of land was abolished, and all natural resources, factories, railroads, and telegraph lines were to remain the property of the Union. However, a new economic policy was adopted. 

This has been called the "Nep" for short. The government began to lease its factories and mines to private individuals, and foreign experts were employed to assist Russia in its industrial recovery. After six years of bitter experience the Russian leaders had learned that individual initiative could not be crushed and a people prosper. 

The Five Year Plan. The Soviet Congress in May, 1928, adopted the now famous Five Year plan. An increase in production in all industries was to reach a stated amount within 

Soyuy photo

In Russia small farms have been united into very large farms and placed under government control. Here you see a line of tractors ready for work.

five years. There was an intense rivalry among the different industrial workers to complete their goal on time. There were three major projects in this plan: (1) the completion of the 

Dnieprostrory hydro-electric development. This would harness the power of the Dnieper River and supply electricity for many purposes; (2) the Turkistan-Siberian railroad was to be completed; (3) the Volga-Don canal was to be constructed. This would give the people living along the Volga River an access to the Black Sea. These projects were finished ahead of time. 

The plan was not confined to the improvement of agriculture and industry alone. The government realized that the future of Russia lay in the hands of their children, and so vast sums of money were spent for education. The first Five Year plan was followed by another. 1 Russia Today. - When the World War began, the great majority of Russian people were unable to read or write. But during the year 1929-1930 almost $800,000,000 was spent for education. New courses of study were prepared, libraries and reading rooms were opened, and art, literature, and music were encouraged. During this same year more than a million adults. attended school.

Many changes have taken place in Russia since the revolution. 

Many mistakes were made and had to be corrected. Many mistakes are still being made. Among these, according to thoughtful persons, is Russia's attempt to crush the church and destroy the home life of the nation. The home and religion are basic institutions in any social order. Just as Russia has been compelled to return to certain principles of the capitalistic system, students of Russian affairs feel assured that the government must and will return to those basic institutions of a sound social existence. In the early days of 1934 President 

Franklin D. Roosevelt began negotiations which led to the recognition of the Soviet Union by the United States. Our country was one of the last to recognize the government of new Russia.

RA-29

Conditions in Italy after the War. - The Communists in Italy tried to seize their government and follow the example of Russia. 

Conditions after the war seemed favorable for a revolution in that country. Factories were forced to close down, unemployment continued to increase, and prices rose. There were strikes, riots, and general discontent throughout the kingdom. 

Benito Mussolini Organ izes the Fascisti and Becomes Dictator of Italy. 

The situation was most critical when Benito Mussolini (moos-so-lē'nė) appeared upon the scene.

He was the son of a village blacksmith, a war veteran, and a former socialist. He organized the Fascisti, an organization composed of ex-soldiers and adventurous youth. The members dressed in green "knickers" and black shirts. They made war on communism and political corruption. Thousands of men joined their ranks. The Fascisti adopted as their emblem the old 

Roman fasces.

Wide World Photo 

Mussolini reviewing Fascisti troops.

On this occasion he presented the soldiers with a flag.

In October, 1922, they marched into Rome and took possession of the city. The king immediately appointed Mussolini as premier. As such he became sole ruler and dictator of the country. He silenced the Communists and suppressed the 

Bolshevist propaganda. He aided commerce and industry and improved the financial situation in his country. Conditions in 

Italy improved steadily, while Mussolini remained the dictator of the Italian government and continued to hold the confidence of the people.

Dictatorship in Germany. was threatened by a dictatorship similar to that in Italy. This movement was encouraged by the National Socialists, popularly called by the shortened name "Nazi." Their leader was Adolf 

Hitler, a war veteran. Referred to as the "firebrand of Germany," Hitler opposed the payment of any more war debts and reparations by his countrymen. He condemned the republic as responsible for Germany's present plight. In the elections of 1930 more than one hundred members of the Nazi party were elected to the Reichstag. Three years later the Nazis were able to overthrow the government, and Hitler was appointed After 1930 the German republic ELE

Wide World Photo 

Adolf Hitler greeting members of his staff. dictator of Germany. In January, 1935, the influence of 

Hitler was extended further, when the Saar Valley voted overwhelmingly in a plebiscite (vote of the people) to rejoin Germany.

The Kingdom of Spain Is Overthrown. Rebellions broke out in Spain after the war. These were caused by the hard times and religious discontent. The situation grew so serious that in 

1923 General Primo de Rivera was appointed dictator. He was unable to restore order, and in 1931 the monarchy with its dictatorship was overthrown. King Alfonso XIII fled first to 

France and then to England. A republic was organized, and a constitution drawn up and ratified. Thus crumbled the last throne of the old Bourbon dynasty. 

Civil War in China: "China for the Chinese." Europe,

India, and the Near East were not alone in witnessing great upheavals in government and social life. In southern China the people took up the cry of the dead patriot, Dr. Sun Yat-sen, "China for the Chinese." For two years, from 1927 to 1929, a civil war was waged between the people of northern and southern China. The former, with their capital at Peking, were in favor of allowing the unfair peace treaties (see Chapter XXIV) to continue until their terms expired. The leaders in the south wanted them to end immediately. The two factions saw the folly, waste, and misery of this war and in 1929 came to an agreement. The north and south united, with the capital at Peking. 

Its name was changed, however, to Peiping, the city of peace. 

The Manchurian Affair. Two years later China was at war with Japan. The Japanese and Russians about 1906 had been given spheres of influence in Manchuria. In 1931 Japan sent troops into that country to protect, as she said, her interests and her people. She further claimed that the Chinese government because of its civil strife was unable to check the lawlessness in Manchuria. The Chinese denied both of these statements and replied that the motive of Japan was to colonize and eventually seize that territory. Both sides prepared for war. 

The Council of the League of Nations hurriedly met in Paris. 

Japan was ordered to evacuate (leave) Manchuria, but refused. 

Later the Japanese government agreed to settle the matter by arbitration. Despite this agreement the Japanese army

mobilized, and war began in Manchuria. This was through no fault of the Japanese civil authority, which had promised to settle the question by arbitration. That body has no control over military affairs.

The Defense of Shanghai. The Japanese fleet sailed for 

Shanghai and landed their marines. Chinese towns were bombarded for weeks by their huge naval guns and the army was ordered to capture Shanghai. It appeared to be another lost cause for China. But the Chinese troops held their ground and fought like an army inspired. The world was amazed as the news was flashed from the battle front for weeks, telling of continued resistance of the Chinese on the outskirts of Shanghai. 

A wave of patriotic pride and enthusiasm spread throughout

China. But the League of Nations was bending every effort to end this conflict. Japan was criticized by the press of the world and again agreed to submit the question to arbitration. 

The Lytton Commission Reports. In the meantime, under 

Japanese influence, a new government was organized in Manchuria, which was given the name Manchukuo. Japan immediately recognized the new state, but China and the world powers refused to do so. The League appointed an investigating committee, known as the Lytton Commission. Their report, made public in November, 1932, was unfavorable towards the Japanese attitude in the whole affair. Japan replied to the report, stating among other things: "that China is in a condition of confusion bordering upon anarchy, and Japan because of its nearness suffers more than any other nation." Furthermore, Japan refused to remove its troops and denied that it was responsible for the creation of Manchukuo. Announcement of its withdrawal from the League was made. A truce was signed in the spring of 1933 between China and Japan, but early in 1935 fighting again broke out, near the western border of Manchukuo. 

The World Court to Settle Disputes among Nations. Thus we see that unrest, revolutions, wars, and rumors of war followed the signing of the armistice. But this is only one part of the

The seventieth session of the League of Nations.

Wide World Photo story. There has never been a time in all history when such world-wide and sincere efforts have been made to abolish war and settle disputes by peaceful methods. 

In February, 1922, the League of Nations established the Court of International Justice, better known as the World Court. 

It chose eleven eminent international lawyers from eleven nations which were members of the League and a number of deputy judges from the remaining members. While the United 

States had not joined either the League or the Court, Professor John Bassett Moore was chosen to represent us in the latter. 

The World Court meets at The Hague in Holland, and its duties are to settle disputes arising among nations. 

President Harding favored a movement to have our country join the World Court with certain reservations. The League refused to accept these in 1927. Proposals by President Hoover and President Roosevelt, that the United States join the World

Court, were rejected by the Senate in 1931 and 1935. 

The League of Nations and the World Court play a very important part in international affairs. Their work and actions are continually referred to in books, magazines, and newspapers. 

Without a clear idea of the departments and duties of these organizations, the references are often confusing and little understood. To make these clearer the following chart is given :

The League of Nations and the World Court 

I. Assembly 

1. Consists of not more than three delegates from each state. 

2. Meets every year first Monday in September at 

Geneva, Switzerland. 

3. Outlines the work of the League and controls financial matters. 

4. Each state has one vote.

II. The Council. 

1. Great Britain, France, and Italy are permanent members. Germany and Japan were also permanent members. In 1933, however, they announced their withdrawal, which will go into effect in 1935. Each allowed one representative. 

2. Nine non-permanent members chosen from the remaining states. 

3. The Council meets whenever the occasion demands and decides the course of action.

III. The Secretariat. 

1. Always in session. It files treaties and documents, collects information, and arranges for the meetings of the different organizations of the League. 

2. At its head is a Secretary-General, appointed by the 

Council and approved by the Assembly. 

3. It has about four hundred clerks and assistants. 

IV. The World Court. 

1. Council and Assembly appoint eleven prominent International Jurists and a number of deputies. 

2. Duties to settle disputes arising among nations in order to avoid armed conflicts.

The Washington Disarmament Conference. The first at tempts after the war to reduce armies and navies and to create a better understanding among nations occurred in 1921. At

that time the United States invited Great Britain, France, Italy, Japan, Portugal, Holland, Belgium, and China to send representatives to Washington. The conference began in November. 

The Secretary of State, Charles Evans Hughes, opened the meeting. He told the delegates frankly that the navies of the larger nations must be reduced in size. This was done, and it was agreed that for the next ten years each country concerned was to retain only a certain number of battleships. The Anglo- 

Japanese Alliance of 1902 (see Chapter XXIV) was annulled.

The nine powers agreed to the open-door policy in China. 

This meant that all nations were to enjoy equal trade rights in that country. France and England withdrew from certain 

Chinese ports, and Japan evacuated Shantung. This Washington Disarmament Conference helped to break down several of the mistakes of the past, for which the world had paid so dearly. 

The Briand-Kellogg Peace Pact. - During the next ten years governments and international organizations called meetings and discussed the question of world peace. In 1928 fifteen nations signed the Briand-Kellogg Peace Pact. This was a treaty drawn up by the Prime Minister of France, Mr. Briand, and our Secretary of State, Mr. Kellogg. The nations signing this pact agreed to renounce all aggressive warfare and to settle international disputes by peaceful methods. 

German Reparations: Inflation of the Currency and the 

Failure to Meet the Payments. The most perplexing problem that faced the nations was the question of war debts and reparations. The amount of indemnity money or reparation that 

Germany was to pay the Allies amounted to $50,000,000,000. 

This money was to be paid to the countries concerned by yearly payments for the next forty-two years. All of her exports were to be taxed, and a certain amount of this income was to be handed over to the Allies. 

To meet this demand the German people were heavily taxed. 

The government printed vast amounts of paper money. It was not backed by gold, silver, or any other valuable security.

People had little confidence in this money, and because there was so much of it prices rose and then soared. This inflation or expanding of the currency did not help matters. The people could not use it to pay their taxes, because the Allies would not accept worthless money in payment for the reparations. Industries failed, and thousands of men were without any means of making a living. Germany fell back in her payments and

Franco-Belgian troops marched in and occupied the coal and iron regions of the Ruhr Valley. But statesmen in other countries began to realize that the German government could not do the impossible. So a remedy was sought. 

The Dawes Report, 1924. — An international committee was appointed to study conditions in Germany and decide upon her ability to pay. General Charles G. Dawes of the United States was appointed chairman. The committee made its report and among other things recommended an international loan to the troubled country. Part of this loan was to enable the government to meet its expenses and balance its budget. 

The Young Plan, 1929. - The Dawes Plan did not prove a success, and in 1929 another conference was called to meet in 

Paris. This second committee reduced the amount of money to be paid as reparations and gave Germany a longer time in which to pay it. It also created a Bank for International Settlements, located at Basel, Switzerland. This bank was to receive 

Germany's payments and distribute them among her creditors. 

Its directors are able to give advice concerning world financial matters, just as the League of Nations aids the world in political affairs. Mr. Owen D. Young, another American, was an active member of this committee, and their report has been called the 

Young Plan. 

The Problem of War Debts. During this entire financial discussion the United States was placed in a peculiar position. 

The Allies owed us about $10,000,000,000, which had been borrowed by them to conduct the war. They began to complain that they were unable to pay us because Germany would or could

MABE 

Brown Bros.

Ex-President Hoover broadcasting a public address. 

not pay them. What were we to do? We could not accept goods in payment of these debts. If we did, our markets would be flooded, our own industries would have to close down, and American workers would have no employment. 

The "Merry-go-Round Policy." - We tried to aid our debtors by extending their payments over a longer period of time. This cut down the amount of their yearly payments. At the same time we decreased their interest rates. In the case of Italy this was lowered to less than one per cent. During these same years we were making loans to Germany. Germany would take the money and pay her reparations to the Allies. The Allies in their turn would pay us their war debts. So the money we loaned out to one country was returned to us by others. This has been called "the merry-go-round policy." 

The Moratorium of 1931-1932. The panic continued, and in 1931 financial conditions throughout the world became acute. 

President Hoover suggested a moratorium or postponement for one year of all war debts owed to the United States. The nations who desired to take advantage of this were to grant a moratorium

. for the same time and the same amounts to Germany. All agreed to this, and for a while confidence was restored. But 

Germany made another appeal to the International Bank to make a study of her ability to pay the reparations. This was the situation in the German republic when Hitler and his Nazis demanded that the government refuse to pay any more debts or reparations. 

President Roosevelt Attacks the Problem. By 1933 the economic conditions throughout the world had not improved. 

In the United States more than 13,000,000 persons were jobless. 

Banks were failing daily. Industries were forced to close down. 

People were unable to pay their taxes, and in many instances were compelled to give up their homes and farms. Communities were struggling to raise money to care for the hungry. The bread lines grew, and riots became common. President Roosevelt planned to fight the depression as we had fought the war. 

Billions of dollars were raised to aid business and industry. This was made possible by the National Industrial Recovery Act (NRA). More billions were raised to create jobs for those out of work. By these methods millions of Americans were enabled to live through the hard winter months of 1933. 

Wide World Photo President Roosevelt signing the National Industrial Recovery Act.

The government at Washington continued its interest in the welfare of everyone during 1934. Wages and salaries were increased. Prices went up. Confidence was slowly returning, but many millions of men and women remained unemployed. 

During 1935, people realized that unemployment may be a serious problem for many years. Loss of world trade and the use of much improved machinery were some of the causes. President 

Roosevelt included in his message to Congress a far-reaching program of social legislation. This included old-age pensions, unemployment insurance, and insurance against sickness. 

Radios and Transoceanic Telephones. - Besides these political, financial, and international problems, the post-war period was one of scientific advancement. The radio was invented, and national and international broadcasts were made. People in remote districts were able to tune in on important speeches and excellent operas. In 1927 the first regular transoceanic telephone was installed. These two inventions made it possible for the human voice to carry around the world. 

Airplanes and Airships.-The first airplane flight across the 

Atlantic occurred in 1919. This was accomplished by Lieutenant 

Commander A. C. Read of the United 

States Navy. He flew from Newfoundland to Portugal, a distance of 2150 miles, stopping at the Azores. 

Two members of the English Royal 

Air Force left Newfoundland that same year and flew to Ireland. This was the first non-stop flight across the Atlantic Ocean. Five years later (1924) two United States Army aviators circled the globe in three hundred sixty-five hours flying time. Then, in 1927,

Charles A. Lindbergh made his memorable non-stop solo flight from New York to Paris, in his small plane, the Spirit of St. Louis. These and other flights were followed by the 21-day voyage of the Graf Zeppelin, a huge German airship, around 

Spirit of St. Louis.

the world. It was commanded by Captain Hugo Eckener, and carried sixty-one passengers. Women, too, gained fame in aviation. In January, 1935, Amelia Earhart added to her laurels by a solo flight from Hawaii to California. Today air mail and air passenger service has been established in all leading countries. Some thirty years earlier (1904) Professor Samuel 

Langley, of Washington, D.C., had been ridiculed and humiliated by scientists, statesmen, and the press because he experimented and believed these things were possible. 

The Citizens and Leaders of Today and Tomorrow. - We have seen in Chapter XXIII how other scientific works and mechanical inventions have altered the life of mankind today. 

This is the end of our story. As we glance back at the events of the past, we can appreciate and understand the present. As we review the work and the problems in these backgrounds of world history, we can face the problems of our times with more courage. We realize, too, the complicated and difficult tasks that confront the men and women of this age your age. As we peep into the future, we realize that the people of today must be better prepared for the work of tomorrow if this civilization is to survive. Never in the history of the world has the need been greater than now for honest, sympathetic, and intelligent citizens. There must be justice in industrial affairs, justice between capital and labor, and justice between producer and consumer. There must be justice in political life and in international relations. Mankind must learn to work together for the good of all. This new age will demand leaders who understand our intricate problems of government, industrial life, and social needs. It will demand leaders with a world vision and leaders who stand four square.
